About this edition

An English rendering of al-Munammaq fī akhbār Quraysh. Arabic text: ʿĀlam al-Kutub, edited by Khurshīd Aḥmad Fāriq, Beirut, 1405 AH / 1985 CE.

The author’s introduction on the genealogy of Quraysh and its forefathers

In the name of God, Most Gracious, Most Merciful.

Al-Munammaq fī Akhbār Quraysh by Muḥammad ibn Ḥabīb al-Baghdādī.

Abū al-Ḥasan Muḥammad ibn al-ʿAbbās al-Ḥanbalī told us: Muḥammad ibn Ḥabīb told us: The first of the reports about Quraysh to be mentioned is what God specially bestowed upon them of excellence and favor above all other creation: He sent from among them the Prophet of Mercy and sent down the Qurʾān to him in their language. God Most High said: “We have sent no messenger except in the language of his people” (14:4). The language of Quraysh is the most eloquent of languages, and its lineage the soundest of lineages. Among the reports concerning this is the Messenger of God’s statement ﷺ: “Whenever two groups separated, I was in the better of the two.” His statement is true. This is because the people from Adam until Noah ﵈, passed away, and the descendants thereafter were Noah’s. The sons of Noah split into many groups, and God favored Shem son of Noah above his brothers, making the Arabs among his descendants, as well as all the prophets except Idrīs. Then the sons of Shem split into groups, and God favored Arpachshad son of Shem above his brothers because of the prophets He placed among his descendants. Among them are God’s intimate friend, the one offered in sacrifice, God’s confidant, God’s spirit, His word, and God’s beloved, may God bless upon them all. Then the descendants of Arfakhshadh divided into groups: among them were Qaḥṭān, Jurhum, Ḥaḍramawt, al-Salaf, al-Mawdh, and ʿAdnān. God favored ʿAdnān over Qaḥṭān and his brothers. Then the descendants of ʿAdnān divided into groups, and God favored Nizār son of Maʿadd son of ʿAdnān over them. Then the descendants of Nizār divided into groups, and God favored Muḍar over the rest. Then the descendants of Muḍar divided into two groups: Iyās and al-Nās—that is, ʿAylān—and God favored Iyās over al-Nās. Then the descendants of Iyās divided into two groups: Mudrika and Ṭābikha, and God favored Mudrika over Ṭābikha. Then the descendants of Mudrika divided into two groups: Khuzayma and Hudhayl, and God favored Khuzayma over Hudhayl. Then the descendants of Khuzayma divided into groups: Asad, Kināna, and al-Hawn, and God favored Kināna over its two brothers. Then the descendants of Kināna divided into groups, and God favored al-Naḍr over the rest. Then the descendants of al-Naḍr divided into two groups: Mālik and Yakhlud, and God favored Mālik over Yakhlud. Then the descendants of Mālik divided into two groups: Fihr and al-Ḥārith, and God favored Fihr over al-Ḥārith. Then the descendants of Fihr divided into groups, and God favored Ghālib over the rest. Then the descendants of Ghālib divided into three groups, and God favored Luʾayy over the rest. Then the descendants of Luʾayy divided into groups, and God favored Kaʿb over his brothers. Then the descendants of Kaʿb divided into three groups: ʿAdī, Huṣayṣ, and Murra, and God favored Murra over his two brothers. Then the descendants of Murra divided into three groups: Kilāb and Yaqaẓa, and God favored Kilāb over his two brothers. Then the descendants of Kilāb divided into two groups: Quṣayy and Zuhra, and God favored Quṣayy over Zuhra. Then the descendants of Quṣayy divided into four groups: ʿAbd Manāf, ʿAbd al-Dār, ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā, and ʿAbd Banū Quṣayy; God favored ʿAbd Manāf over the rest. Then the descendants of ʿAbd Manāf divided into four groups: Hāshim, ʿAbd Shams, al-Muṭṭalib, and Nawfal; God favored Hāshim over his brothers. Then the descendants of Hāshim divided into groups, but they all died out, and the surviving line was through ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib son of Hāshim. God then sent His Prophet ﷺ—who had four paternal uncles: Ḥamza, al-ʿAbbās, Abū Ṭālib, and Abū Lahab. Two followed him and two opposed him, and God favored the group that followed him over the group that opposed him.

Al-Kalbī said in his chains of transmission: “God favored the Arabs over the non-Arabs because they did not marry their daughters or sisters. God favored Muḍar son of Nizār over the rest of the Arabs because they knew best the practice of Abraham ﷺ Muḥammad, and his family—and adhered most firmly to his rites. God favored Quraysh over the rest of Muḍar because they did not wrong their neighbor, nor did they raid one another. God favored the Banū Hāshim over Quraysh because they were the most devoted to kinship ties and the most restrained from sins. God favored the Banū ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib over the rest of the Banū Hāshim through the birth of Muḥammad—God bless him and his family. And God favored Muḥammad ﷺ—over the rest of the Banū ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib because he was the best of them, the most dutiful, the most truthful, and the most devoted to kinship ties ﵌.”

Muḥammad ibn Sallām al-Jumaḥī said in his chains of transmission: “The Prophet ﷺ—said: ‘God, Mighty and Glorious, chose the Arabs from among the people, then chose Muḍar from among the Arabs, then chose Kināna from among Muḍar, then chose from among Kināna …’”

Quraysh, then chose the Banū Hāshim from among Quraysh, then chose me from among those to whom I belong.’”

Muḥammad ibn Sallām al-Jumaḥī said in another report: “The Messenger of God ﷺ—said: ‘Gabriel ﵇—came to me and said: I have traversed the earth, its east and west, its north and south, and found none better than Quraysh, nor found among Quraysh any better than Hāshim.’”

Hishām ibn Muḥammad al-Kalbī informed me, saying: Abū Zufar al-Kalbī told me, from his uncle ʿUmāra ibn Jarīr, from Athāl ibn Ḥaḍramī al-Asadī, who said: “I heard our elders mention that when Barra bt. Murra was presented to Khuzayma ibn Mudrika, she dreamed that she had given birth to two boys, one behind the other, with a sībāʾ between them. She said: While I was looking at them, one was like a shining moon and the other a roaring lion! I told Khuzayma about it, and he went to a soothsayer woman …”

There was a woman in Mecca called Sarḥa. She related the dream to her, and the woman said: “If her dream is true, you will give birth to a boy from her who will be the source of a people with valiant spirits and eloquent tongues. Then one of your descendants will take her after you, and she will bear him a boy whose descendants will have numbers and resources, lords of glory and might, until the end of time.” She bore him Asad ibn Khuzayma. Then Kināna took her after him, and she bore him al-Naḍr.

He said: “Kināna came while he was asleep in the Ḥijr, and it was said to him: ‘Choose, Abū al-Naḍr: neighing and bellowing, or the building of walls and the glory of time!’ He said:

‘No, Lord!’1

So God placed all of that in Quraysh.”

A number of people have transmitted, through different chains, that the Messenger of God ﷺ—said: “God chose Kināna from among the Arabs; Kināna is the might of the Arabs.” He ﷺ—also said: “I was shown the heartland of the Banū Kināna, and I saw a lamp among them that outshone all the lamps. I interpreted that lamp as Quraysh.”

Hishām ibn Muḥammad informed me, from ʿAbd al-Ḥamīd al-Majd ibn ʿAbs al-Anṣārī, from one of his people, from al-Shaʿbī, who said: “The Messenger of God ﷺ—said: ‘I was shown the fortunes, and I saw the fortune of Quraysh as a green garden from which water flowed. I interpreted this as abundance of wealth and an outpouring of generosity.’ When Ṣaʿṣaʿa ibn Nājiya came to the Messenger of God ﷺ—as a Muslim envoy, he asked him …”

The Messenger [of God] ﷺ—about his knowledge of Muḍar. He said: “Kināna is its face, with its hearing and sight; Tamīm is its shoulder; and Qays is its claws.” They say that Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān asked Laylā al-Akhyaliyya about Muḍar, and she said: “Boast with Kināna, fight with Qays, and multiply your numbers with Tamīm.”

It is reported that the Prophet ﷺ—said: “Quraysh is the salt of this community, like salt in food! Can food be good without salt?” It is reported from the Prophet ﷺ—that he said: “O God! You have made this Islam that I have brought a mercy for all the worlds and a reminder for Quraysh, so take care of Quraysh for me.”

The Messenger of God ﷺ—said: “People follow Quraysh: their believers follow its believers, and their wicked follow its wicked.” It is also reported from him ﷺ—that he said: “Quraysh is the backbone of the people; thus no one remains without a backbone.” He also said: “Quraysh are the leaders of the Arabs, in good and evil, until the Day of Resurrection.”

He ﷺ—said: “Do not go ahead of Quraysh, or you will go astray! Do not lag behind them, or you will perish! And do not teach them, for they know better than you.”

He ﷺ—said: “Am I not closer to you than your own selves?” They said: “Indeed—you are dearer to us than our fathers and mothers!” He said: “I shall be waiting for you at the Pool on the Day of Resurrection, going ahead of you, and I shall ask you about the Qurʾān and about my people. So do not go ahead of Quraysh, or you will go astray; do not lag behind them, or you will perish; and do not teach Quraysh, for they know better than you. Were it not that Quraysh might grow insolent, I would tell it what it has in store with God.”

He said: “Umāma bt. Yazīd ibn ʿAmr ibn al-Ṣaʿq came to Muʿāwiya, and he said to her: ‘Tell me about this branch of Muḍar.’ She said: ‘As for the forefront of Muḍar, these are the two clans descended from Ibn Khuzayma; as for …’”

“its claws, with which it grapples, this is the clan of Qays.” Muʿāwiya said: “And where are the Banū Tamīm?” She said: “Those are the shoulder that bears the load and the paunch that is eaten from.”

He said: “Tell me about Qays Muḍar!” She said: “As for the skull of Qays, it is Ghatafān; as for its molars, with which it eats, they are the Banū Sulaym; and as for its nostrils, through which it breathes, they are the Banū ʿĀmir.”

Laylā al-Akhyaliyya said to Muʿāwiya, when he asked her about Muḍar: “Quraysh are its leaders and chiefs; Tamīm is its shoulder and paunch; and Qays are its horsemen and its hooks.”

Ṣaʿṣaʿa ibn Nājiya said to the Messenger of God ﷺ: “O Messenger of God! I know Muḍar better than anyone. Tamīm is its crown and its mighty shoulder, which it struggles under and bears its weight; Kināna is its face, with its hearing and sight; Qays are its horsemen and its reins; and Asad is its tongue.” The Prophet ﷺ—said: “You have spoken truly.”

The Messenger of God ﷺ—said: “I have left among you the Book of God and my family. You will not go astray so long as you hold fast to them both.”

It is reported from the Prophet ﷺ—that he said: “If God grants me victory over Quraysh, I will overcome the rest of the Arabs.”

They said: “When the Messenger of God ﷺ—returned from the expedition to Badr, heading back to Medina, the Aws and Khazraj met him to congratulate him on the victory God had granted him. Salama ibn Salāma ibn …”

Waqsh al-Anṣārī said: “What are you congratulating us for? By God, we killed nothing but bald old women like tethered camels!” The Messenger of God ﷺ—said, and he heard him: “Those are Quraysh’s nobility. If only they had embraced Islam! If you were then to see them, you would fear them; if they commanded you, you would obey them; and you would consider your own deeds insignificant beside theirs.”

He said: “I saw myself in Medina, and whenever I met one of them on the road, I would move aside from his way, in awe of him, until he passed. Then I would say: ‘God and His Messenger spoke truly.’ Through Quraysh, God favored the Arabs over all other nations, gave them command over them, bequeathed to them their lands and wealth, and established them in the land. Quraysh are the most central in lineage among the Arabs, the tallest in pillars, the firmest in foundations, the most deeply rooted, the freshest in growth, and the highest in branches. In the pre-Islamic period, before God joined them to that distinction through the virtue of prophethood, they were called the People of God, the Dwellers of God, the People of the Sanctuary, and the Residents of the House of God.

“ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib said to Abraha al-Ashram, the master of the Elephant, when he asked him to return his camels: Abraha said to him, ‘Why did you not ask me to turn back from what I intended to do—to demolish your honor and violate your sanctuary?’ An exchange then took place between them, which we have recorded in the account of the Elephant at the end of this part. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib said: (al-ramal)

We are the people of God, within His sanctuary,

As we have always been, since ancient times.

The House has a Lord who protects it,

Who destroys whoever attacks it in sin.

Among their noble deeds in the pre-Islamic period was that, despite remaining in their state of polytheism, they contributed to the watering of the pilgrims, fed the people attending the pilgrimage season, carried any pilgrim who had been left without means, and helped him reach his destination. The person in charge of this in his time was Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf. During the pilgrimage season, Quraysh would gather their surplus wealth for him. It is said that he was responsible for a quarter of this from his own wealth, for the reason we have mentioned. Maṭrūd ibn Kaʿb al-Khuzāʿī said of him: (al-kāmil)

The noble ʿAmr broke bread for his people,

While Mecca’s men were famine-stricken and lean.

Both journeys were entrusted to him:

The winter journey and the summer journey.

O man who has shifted his baggage,why did you not alight among the family of ʿAbd Manāf?

May your mother be bereaved of you! Had you alighted among them,they would have protected you from hunger and disgrace.

Among Quraysh’s acts of generosity was that the House of God was in their hands, and its keys belonged to them: no one from the East or West could open it but them. These are acts of generosity by which they excelled the Arabs and non-Arabs. God Most High recounts Abraham’s words: “Our Lord, I have settled some of my descendants in a valley without cultivation, near Your Sacred House, our Lord, so that they may establish prayer. So make the hearts of some of the people incline toward them, and provide for them.” (14:37) They remained thus in the pre-Islamic age, with many acts of generosity, of which these are among the most renowned, until God ﵎ joined Islam, prophethood, and the caliphate to them.

In the pre-Islamic age, Quraysh were separate clans scattered among Kināna. Quṣayy ibn Kilāb gathered them from every direction in Mecca, and they were called Quraysh. Taqarrush means “coming together.” About this, al-Faḍl ibn ʿAbbās ibn ʿUtba ibn Abī Lahab says: (al-khafīf)

We have its spreading and its wholesome soil,and through us Quraysh was named Quraysh.

Among them, Ḥudhāfa al-ʿAdawī says: (al-ṭawīl)

Your father Quṣayy—by my life—was called “the Gatherer”;through him God gathered the tribes of Fihr.

Hishām ibn Muḥammad related, from Bishr al-Kalbī, from his father, who said: Before Quṣayy ibn Kilāb, Quraysh were called Banū al-Naḍr. They were scattered across the outskirts of Mecca, and none of them lived in al-Abṭaḥ.

When Quṣayy ibn Kilāb came of age, Khuzāʿa, Banū Bakr ibn ʿAbd Manāt ibn Kināna, and Ṣūfa—among them al-Ghawth ibn Murra—gathered around him. He sent for his maternal half-brother Razāḥ ibn Rabīʿa ibn Ḥarām ibn Ḍanna ibn ʿAbd ibn Kabīr ibn ʿUdhrā. Quṣayy’s mother was Fāṭima bt. Saʿd ibn Sayl, who was Khayr ibn Ḥamāla, ibn ʿAwf (ibn…

Ghanm— ibn ʿĀmir—who was al-Jādir, the first to build a wall around the Kaʿba—ibn ʿAmr ibn Jaʿthama ibn Yashkur ibn Mubashshir ibn Ṣaʿb ibn Dahmān ibn Naṣr ibn Zahrān ibn Kaʿb ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Maʿadd ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mālik ibn Naṣr ibn al-Azd.

About Saʿd ibn Sayl, Hawn ibn Abī ʿAmr al-ʿUdhrī says: (al-ramal)

I see no single person among peoplewho is like you, Saʿd ibn Sayl.

A horseman, keen and impetuous,who, when battle is met, stands his ground.

A horseman who draws out the horses asthe falcon draws out the partridges.

Jaʿthama left at the time the Azd departed from Maʾrib. He settled among Banū al-Dayl ibn Bakr ibn ʿAbd Manāt ibn Kināna, allied himself with them, married their women, and they married their women to him.

Fāṭima, Quṣayy’s mother, had been with Kilāb ibn Murra, and bore him Zuhra. Then she remained with him for a long time, until he grew old and lost his sight. Thereafter she bore him Quṣayy. Hishām said: He was named Quṣayy because his mother went far away with him to Syria. Rabīʿa ibn Ḥarām, the ʿUdhrī, came as a pilgrim and married her. She took the boy Quṣayy with her to Syria. She bore Rabīʿa Razāḥ and Ḥannā.

There was a dispute between Quṣayy and a boy from ʿUdhrā, and the ʿUdhrī disavowed him, saying, “By God, you are not one of us!” He went to his mother and asked, “Who is my father?” She said, “Rabīʿa is your father.” He said, “If I were his son, he would not have disavowed me.” She replied, “By God, your father is better and nobler than he is: your father is Kilāb ibn Murra, one of the people of the Sanctuary.” He said, “By God, I shall never remain here!” She said, “Then stay until the time of the pilgrimage comes!”

When that time came, she sent him with a group of Quḍāʿa. Zuhra was alive. Quṣayy came to him. Zuhra was the more eloquent, and Quṣayy the more eloquent.2 Quṣayy said to him, “I am your brother.” Zuhra said, “Come close to me!” He felt him and said, “By God, I recognize the voice and the resemblance!”

Then Zuhra died, and Quṣayy came of age. He wanted to gather his people, Banū al-Naḍr, in the heart of Mecca. Khuzāʿa, Bakr, and Ṣūfa joined him and made him numerous. He sent for his brother Razāḥ, who came with a host from Syria and groups from Quḍāʿa until he reached Mecca. Ṣūfa were the ones who sent the people onward. Razāḥ stood on the mountain pass and said, “Send Quṣayy through!” So he sent the people onward. The forwarding of the pilgrims has remained with the descendants of Quṣayy to this day. Then he brought all the clans of Quraysh into al-Abṭaḥ except Muḥārib ibn Fihr, al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr, Taym al-Adram ibn Ghālib, and Muʿayṣ ibn ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy. These are called the “outlying clans,” for they remained on the outskirts of Mecca. However, a group of Banū al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr—the group of Abū ʿUbayda ibn al-Jarrāḥ—settled in al-Abṭaḥ, and they are with the Muṭayyibūn.

The first property Quṣayy ibn Kilāb acquired was that a man among the Abyssinians’ notables came to Mecca with merchandise, sold it, and then set out for home. Quṣayy followed him, killed him, and took his property. He married Ḥubbā bt. Ḥalīl ibn Ḥabashiyya, who bore him four sons: ʿAbd al-Dār, ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā, ʿAbd Manāf, and ʿAbd of Quṣayy. Quṣayy used to say, “I had four sons: I named two after my gods, one after my house, and one after myself.”

Quṣayy was the leading man of Mecca; no one disputed his preeminence. He built the Council House (Dār al-Nadwa). Matters concerning Quraysh were handled there: whatever befell them, and whatever they wished to discuss of marriage, war, or counsel, and whatever might arise for them. A girl, when she reached the age to wear a robe, would have her robe torn only there, as a good omen, and out of reverence for it and to honor its position and status.

He said: When Quṣayy grew old and weakened, he assigned the custodianship, the Council House, the watering of the pilgrims, their provision, and the standard to ʿAbd al-Dār, his eldest son, who was weak and elderly. He singled him out for these duties so that he might bring him level with his brothers. The provision for the pilgrims was a levy that Quraysh took from their wealth…

for the hospitality of the pilgrims. When Quṣayy died, ʿAbd Manāf took charge of Quṣayy’s affairs and undertook the affairs of Quraysh. After his father’s death, Quraysh entrusted their affairs to him. He laid out residential plots in Mecca and acquired property in addition to what Quṣayy had allotted to his people. When ʿAbd Manāf died, the offices we have named passed to ʿAbd al-Dār.

Then the sons of ʿAbd Manāf wanted to take those offices from them and said, “We have a greater right to them.” But the sons of ʿAbd al-Dār refused. At that, Quraysh split apart and diverged; their affairs became scattered and their unity divided.

With the sons of ʿAbd Manāf were Banū Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā, Banū Zuhra ibn Kilāb, Banū Taym ibn Murra, and Banū al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr. With the sons of ʿAbd al-Dār were Banū Sahm ibn ʿAmr, Banū Jumḥ ibn ʿAmr, Banū Makhzūm ibn Yaqaẓa, and Banū ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb. Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy sided with neither party.

The sons of ʿAbd Manāf and their allies were called the Muṭayyibūn (“the Perfumed Ones”); the sons of ʿAbd al-Dār and their allies were called the Aḥlāf (“the Confederates”). ʿĀtika bt. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib brought out a bowl of perfume. They dipped their hands in it, and so were called the Muṭayyibūn. The others slaughtered camels, dipped their hands in their blood, and were called the Aḥlāf and “the Blood-Lickers,” because al-Aswad ibn Ḥāritha al-ʿAdawī licked the blood, and Banū ʿAdī licked it with him.

When they were on the verge of faltering and each tribe had been arrayed against another, Banū ʿAbd Manāf were arrayed against Banū Sahm; Banū ʿAbd al-Dār against Banū Asad; Banū Makhzūm against Banū Taym; Banū Jumḥ against Banū Zuhra; and Banū ʿAdī against Banū al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr. Then they set out to…

make peace, on the terms that Banū ʿAbd Manāf be given the watering of the pilgrims, Banū Asad the provision for them, and that the custodianship, the Council House, and the standard remain with Banū ʿAbd al-Dār.

The Muṭayyibūn had gone to a soothsayer woman in Mecca and told her their story and that of their companions. She said, “You acted like women when you dipped your hands in perfume; they acted like men when they dipped their hands in blood.”

Abū al-Mundhir said: Evil broke out between the groups until they nearly fought one another. The custodianship and the standard went to Banū ʿUthmān ibn ʿAbd al-Dār. At that time, Abū Ṭalḥa ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAbd al-Dār held them. The Council House went to ʿĀmir ibn Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn ʿAbd al-Dār.

In the time of Muʿāwiya, ʿIkrima ibn ʿĀmir ibn Hāshim sold the Council House to Muʿāwiya for one hundred thousand dirhams. It is today the residence of the governor. It was called the Council House because Quraysh used to deliberate there on good and evil, and regarded it as auspicious because it was Quṣayy’s house.

Ibn Qays al-Ruqayyāt said: (al-khafīf)

It belongs to ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayywhen he is summoned, and to ʿAbd Manāf.

It has forefathers among the Perfumed Ones,then the leaders of the Confederates came to possess it.

They also relate that Aktham ibn Ṣayfī entered the open valley of Mecca, and there he saw the sons of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib moving through it like silver towers, their turbans like banners atop men’s lances, their striped cloaks trailing over the ground. Aktham said, “O Banū Tamīm! If God wishes to establish a dynasty, He makes men like these grow for it. This is God’s planting, not the planting of men.” Hishām said: Among the Arabs there was no group more noble or more imposing than the sons of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. Every man among them had a prominent nose, with his nose projecting beyond his lips; he ate a young camel, and drank a faraq.

When God wishes to bring a dynasty into being, He makes men like these grow for it. This is God’s planting, not the planting of men.” Hishām said: Among the Arabs there was no group more noble or more imposing than the sons of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. Every man among them had a prominent nose, with his nose projecting beyond his lips; he ate a young camel, and drank a faraq.

On the day of Ajnādayn, Qurra ibn Ḥajal ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib said: (al-kāmil)

Count Ḍirār among the generous men,the lion Ḥamza, and count al-ʿAbbās.

Count al-Zubayr and then al-Muqawwim,al-Ṣatam, Ḥajal, and the young Dirfās.

“And Abū ʿUtayba—count him as an eighth; and the champion ʿAbd Manāf, our lion-clawed one.

“And the champion Ghaydāq, count him among the lords, the noble chiefs, who ruled the people despite the enemy.

“And al-Ḥārith, the bountiful, was a noble man

“in the days when the valiant champion challenged him to the cup.

“There are no uncles among mankind like my uncles,

“truly, nor people like our people, ever.”

He said: Al-faraq, with the rāʾ vowelled, is sixteen riṭls; al-farq, with the rāʾ vowelless, is one hundred and twenty riṭls. It is of this that ʿĀʾisha, may God have mercy on her, said: “I and the Messenger of God ﷺ used to bathe from major ritual impurity using that vessel”—and she pointed to a container that held a farq.

Of the notable men of the Banū ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, only Ḥamza and al-ʿAbbās ﵏ accepted Islam. He said: The surviving male line of the Banū ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib descended through al-ʿAbbās, Abū Ṭālib, al-Ḥārith, and Abū Lahab. Al-Zubayr, al-Muqawwim, and Ḥajal had sons of their own, but they died. Ḍirār ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib was among the Quraysh’s young men, outstanding in beauty, intelligence, dignity, and generosity. His mother, Nutayla, had lost him, and she was nearly— Her reason nearly gave way from grief for him. She was wealthy, and at the seasonal gatherings she would recite, saying:

(al-rajaz)

“I lost him—a fair-skinned, keen-witted youth,who was neither thickset nor of doubtful birth.”

And she said: (al-rajaz)

“I lost him, fair-skinned as a woven mat,the pride of the youths of the Banū Manāf.

“Then, by my life, the foremost of guests—this is for Fihr, a year of the īlāf.”

/ She promised a hundred camels to whoever brought him to her, and vowed to clothe the House if God returned him to her. Ḥassān ibn Thābit was passing by her as a pilgrim among a group of his people. He saw how she grieved for him and said: (al-ṭawīl)

“Abū Ḍirār’s mother calls out to the people, distraught.

“O Banū al-Najjār, what has she lost?

“Had what Nutayla endures at daybreakbefallen Mount Raḍwā, it could not have stood firm.”

A man from Judhām brought him to her. She paid him the promised reward, clothed the House in white garments, and said: (al-rajaz)

“Praise belongs to God, to whom praise belongs,

“and who eased my grief,

“when the Lord of the Throne returned my son to me,after I had searched among Maʿadd.

“I thank Him, then I fulfill my vow.”

The Merits of al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ﵁

Hishām al-Kalbī said: Abū al-Sāʾib al-Makhzūmī told me, from his father, who said: Al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib had a garment for the unclothed among the Banū Hāshim, a large bowl for their hungry, and a pillory for their foolish—or perhaps he said, “their ignorant.” He protected his neighbor, gave generously of his wealth, and supported those who had met with misfortune among his people. In the pre-Islamic period he was a drinking companion of Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb. A man from the Banū Sulaym had given shelter to a man from an unrelated Arab clan, but his hospitality was not praised. About this, al-ʿAbbās ibn Mirdās al-Sulamī said: (al-basīṭ)

“If your neighbor’s pledge did you no good,until you drank the breaths of death from a cup,

“then take refuge at the courtyard—the courtyard of God.No obscenity or trouble entered his gathering.”

“Go to the domed tents and stand apart among their people;you will meet Ibn Ḥarb and meet the man ʿAbbās.

“Quraysh’s champion, its ornament in its nobility,in glory and resolve, in what they possessed and governed.”

“‘O Messenger of God, it is strange: when Quraysh are talking, they reach a point of suspicion, and when they see me, they fall silent and speak of something else. I recognized disapproval on their faces.’ The Prophet ﷺ said: ‘By Him who sent me as a prophet with the truth, none of them will complete his faith until he recognizes— “‘Your merit, uncle.’” Hishām said: My father told me, from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Jaʿda ibn Hubayra, from Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ, who said: “A group of Emigrants, of whom I was one, gathered when the Prophet ﷺ was gravely ill. They said: ‘O Messenger of God, give us a charge to act upon after you!’ He said: ‘I am leaving among you my uncle, the same stock as my father. What will you do?’ Saʿd said:

“‘By God, I do not disclose to you what came to our minds.’”

Among al-ʿAbbās’s merits was that he alone, of all the pilgrims, was permitted to spend the nights of Minā in Mecca. Hishām said: My father told me, from al-Ṣalt ibn ʿAbd Allāh, from al-Mughīra ibn Nawfal ibn al-Ḥārith, who said: “I passed by Jābir ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Anṣārī, with a group of people around him, and greeted him. He said:

“‘Who is that?’ I said: ‘Al-Mughīra ibn Nawfal al-Hāshimī.’ He said: ‘May my father and mother be your ransom, Banū Hāshim! How can this community prosper, or hope for the intercession of its Prophet, when the Messenger of God ﷺ left his uncle among them, and they neglected him and gave others preference over him?’”

Hishām said, from his father: When the Messenger of God ﷺ was gravely ill, his wives, his family, and his uncle al-ʿAbbās gathered around him. The women said: “He has pleurisy. Come, let us administer medicine through his mouth.” When he came to, he said: “Do you think I have pleurisy? I am too honored by God for Him to punish me with it. Well then, let no one remain in the house without having medicine administered through his mouth—except my uncle al-ʿAbbās!” So each of them administered it to the others.” Hishām said: My father told me, from ʿIkrima, the freedman of al-ʿAbbās, who said: “Al-ʿAbbās said to the Messenger of God ﷺ: ‘May my father and mother be your ransom! Why is it that when men of Quraysh see us while they are talking, they break off and take up another subject?’ The Prophet ﷺ said: ‘Whoever honors me through you, may God honor him.’” Hishām said: My father told me, from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, who said: “I passed by my father as he was going around— “among a group of the Banū Umayya. They said: ‘He certainly struts as he walks, like a man who is sure he has been forgiven. Perhaps his kinship to the Messenger of God will not benefit him.’ He went to the Prophet ﷺ and said: ‘O Messenger of God, a man of Quraysh keeps saying things to me that I dislike’—and he told him what had been said. The Messenger of God ﷺ said: ‘Does he hope for my intercession if he is a Muslim from among the Turks and the Daylam, but not if he is my uncle? Do they not know that whoever harms you has harmed me, and whoever harms me, God will punish with a severe punishment?’ Then he said: ‘Uncle, we and this clan of ʿAbd Shams have always shared one line of descent, until ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib separated us from them. We were of purer descent than they, and more important in standing.’”

Al-Kalbī related that when ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās was buried, they heard someone reciting: “O tranquil soul, return”—the verse—to the end of the sūra. Al-Kalbī said: ʿAwāna told me, from someone who informed him, that ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵇ was asked about the Banū Hāshim and the Banū Umayya. He said: “The Banū Hāshim are more eloquent, more articulate, and more generous; the Banū Umayya are more cunning and more wicked.”

Abū al-ʿAbbās al-Ḥimyarī, from Asbāṭ ibn Muḥammad, from Hishām ibn Saʿd al-Madīnī, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās, concerning a water spout that al-ʿAbbās had over the route of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb: On a Friday, ʿUmar put on his clothes. Al-ʿAbbās had slaughtered two young birds, and when ʿUmar came to the spout, water poured from it and struck ʿUmar’s garment. He ordered the spout to be removed. Al-ʿAbbās came to him and said: “You removed my water spout, though that was not right of you to do!

“By God, it is in the very place where the Messenger of God put it!” ʿUmar said to al-ʿAbbās: “I adjure you to climb onto my back and put it back in its place!” Al-ʿAbbās did so.

The Account of the Īlāf

Abū Bakr al-Ḥalwānī told us: Abū Saʿīd al-Sukkarī told us: Abū Jaʿfar Muḥammad ibn Ḥabīb informed us, from Ibn al-Kalbī, who said: The account of the īlāf was that the Quraysh— “were merchants, and their trade did not extend beyond Mecca. Foreigners brought goods to them, and they bought from them, then traded among themselves and sold to the Arabs around them. Their trade continued in this way until Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf travelled to Syria and lodged with Caesar. Hāshim’s name at that time was ʿAmr. Every day he would slaughter a sheep, prepare a large bowl of bread soaked in broth, and invite those around him to eat. Hāshim, they say, had the finest sinews and was the most handsome of men. He was mentioned to Caesar, and it was said: ‘There is a man here from Quraysh who breaks up bread, pours broth over it, and lays the meat on top. The foreigners, however, put broth in dishes and eat bread with it.’ For that reason, ʿAmr was called Hāshim. Word of this reached Caesar, who summoned him. When he saw and spoke with him, he was impressed by him, and would send for him so that he might come in. When Hāshim saw how highly Caesar regarded him, he said: ‘O King, I have a people who are Arab merchants. If you would write them a document guaranteeing their safety and the safety of their trade, they could bring to you whatever is most prized among the leather goods of the Ḥijāz and its garments and sell it in your lands at a lower price for you.’

“He wrote him a document granting safe conduct to any of them who came. Hāshim set out with the document, and whenever he passed a clan of Arabs along the route to Syria, he obtained a pledge of īlāf from its notables. The īlāf meant that—”

“they would be safe in their lands without an oath. It was simply a grant of safe conduct for people, on the understanding that Quraysh would carry their goods for them and they would spare them the cost of transporting them, returning their capital and their profit. Hāshim obtained the īlāf from those between him and Syria until he reached Mecca. He brought them the greatest thing they had ever brought. They set out with great quantities of trade, and Hāshim went with them, escorting them and fulfilling for them the īlāf he had obtained from the Arabs. He continued to fulfill it, bringing them together with the Arab notables, until he reached Syria with them and settled them in its towns. He died on that journey at Ghazza in Syria. Al-Ḥārith ibn Ḥanish of the Banū Sulaym, Hāshim’s brother through their mother—he and ʿAbd Shams and al-Muṭṭalib were all sons of the same mother—said: Their mother was ʿĀtika bt. Murra ibn Hilāl ibn Fālij ibn Dhakwān, ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Bahtha ibn Sulaym. (al-basīṭ)

“My brother Hāshim is no ordinary brother.By God, Hāshim is no diminished, unsalable man.

“Goodness is in his garment, and in the grave of the tomb-maker—the one who took on the īlāf and went to the one who stayed behind.”

Maṭrūd al-Khuzāʿī said: (al-Kāmil)

Generosity died in Syria when he came to rest thereHe perished at Gaza—may he not be far away!May the lord of the house of destruction never be far away; we visit him,as a sick man is visited, and he gives generously among those who visit.His bowls overflow for whoever comes to him,and his help is given by tongue and hand.

When Hāshim died, al-Muṭṭalib ibn ʿAbd Manāf set out for Yemen and obtained from its kings a covenant for the Qurashī merchants who had traded with them before him. Then he went on, obtaining the ilāf from whichever Arabs he passed, until he reached Mecca, having done as Hāshim had done. Al-Muṭṭalib was the eldest son of ʿAbd Manāf; he was called al-Fayḍ. Al-Muṭṭalib died at Radmin in Yemen while returning from Yemen. ʿAbd Shams ibn ʿAbd Manāf set out for the king of Abyssinia and obtained from him a written covenant for the Qurashī merchants who had traded with him before. Then he obtained the ilāf from those between him and the Arabs until he reached Mecca. ʿAbd Shams died in Mecca and was buried at al-Ḥajūn. He was older than Hāshim. Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf set out. He was the youngest son of ʿAbd Manāf and had the same mother alone. His mother was Wāqida, daughter of Abū ʿAdī, of Banū Hawāzin ibn Manṣūr ibn ʿIkrima ibn Khaṣfa ibn Qays ibn ʿAylān. He set out for Iraq and obtained from Kisrā a covenant for the Qurashī merchants. Then he went on, obtaining the ilāf from whichever Arabs he passed, until he arrived in Mecca; afterward he returned to Iraq and died at Salmān in the land of Iraq.

These sons of ʿAbd Manāf were the first through whom God raised up Quraysh. The Arabs had never seen their like in generosity, forbearance, good sense, or beauty. They were like stars among the stars. Maṭrūd al-Khuzāʿī said, lamenting them—for he used to follow them and live under their protection. ʿAbd Manāf’s name was al-Mughīra: (al-Sarīʿ)

Al-Mughīra and their sonsare the best of the living and the dead.Four of them, each a chief,sons of chiefs, of chiefs.ʿAbd Manāf alone among themis saved from the blame of whoever blames.One grave is at Salmān, one at Radmin,and one at Ghazzāt.

And one who died was buried nearbyat al-Ḥajūn, east of the little buildings.O night that roused my nights,one of my bitter nights!You stirred in me grief for what had passedwhen I remembered the deaths,when I remembered the Manāf, descendantsof ʿAbd Manāf, and my needs were fulfilled.

Maṭrūd once came upon a man who was a neighbor among Banū Sahm. He and his daughters and wife were there during a year of hardship. They moved him on, finding it too much to bear, and told him to leave them. He departed carrying his belongings, with his wife and child; no one harmed him. Maṭrūd said: (al-Kāmil)

O guest whose baggage has been moved on,why did you not stay with the house of ʿAbd Manāf?May your mother lose you! Had you gone to them,they would have protected you from hunger and disgrace—those who secure covenants throughout its regions,and travel under the protection of the ilāf.

And fight the wind each winteruntil the sun sinks into the roaring sea.My eye has never seen their like: those whoearned the merit of ancient deeds and far-flung lands.

Maṭrūd also says that one day, after the Banū ʿAbd Manāf had died, while he was traveling, ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib encountered him. Maṭrūd was on a lean camel, with a worn saddle and in a wretched state. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib took him in with his own party, clothed him in fine clothing, and gave him a swift mount and a splendid saddle. Maṭrūd said: (al-Kāmil)

O Shaybat al-Ḥamd, to whomthe days have twice bestowed the finest hoard a hoarder can store!Glory endures as long as Iyād makes pilgrimage to His Houseand the dove calls above a fresh branch.He gave me shelter and then furnished my journeywith a swift, easy-riding she-camel and a splendid saddle.By God, I shall never forget you or your good deedsuntil I am hidden in the dust of a grave.

I shall surely love you for the praiseand far-spread renown you bestowed on your father.The full moon is Shayba, or a rising crescent,before whom the pilgrims stand in a hollow valley.

Maṭrūd also says: (al-Ramal)

Let no one blame Manāf:among them are al-Fayḍ and Hāshim,and my fair brother among them, Nawfal,with open hands and a keen sword.The dead one of the Sanctuary, of great renown,ʿAbd Shams, when hardship struck.

It is also transmitted: “ʿAbd Shams, the price of one who has no price.” He said: “I asked Ibn al-Aʿrābī about ‘the price of one who has no price,’ and he said: ‘I do not know it.’”

The Story of Zuhra and Umayya

The first discord to arise among Quraysh was this: Umayya ibn ʿAbd Shams was a handsome, charming man. He used to pass by Wahb ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhra. At that time Wahb had two wives. One was Ḍaʿīfa, daughter of Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf, mother of ʿAbd Yaghūth and ʿUbayd [Yaghūth], the sons of Wahb ibn ʿAbd Manāf. The other was Barra, daughter of ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAbd al-Dār ibn Quṣayy, mother of Āmina, daughter of Wahb, the mother of the Messenger of God ﷺ.

When Umayya began passing by Wahb frequently, Wahb took offense. He came back and said to him, “Cousin! Your passing by me troubles me. Take another route.” Umayya said, “By God, I shall pass only where I please!” Wahb ibn ʿAbd Manāf sat in wait for him with a sword and struck his buttock. Umayya’s buttock was large, and Wahb split it. Umayya left, and the Banū ʿAbd Manāf grew angry. They said to the Banū Zuhra:

“We shall drive you out of Mecca. Leave!” The Banū Zuhra began to depart by night. Qays ibn ʿAdī al-Sahmī heard the commotion. He was at the mountain’s summit on an intensely hot night, with a group of his people. The Banū Zuhra were his maternal uncles, and the mother of ʿAdī ibn Saʿd ibn Sahm ibn Qays ibn ʿAdī was Tamāḍur, daughter of Zuhra. When Qays ibn ʿAdī heard the departure and the noise, he said, “What is this?”

They said, “Zuhra is being driven out by the Banū ʿAbd Manāf.” He stood and shouted, “Let night turn to morning! The one departing is staying!” The Banū Zuhra recognized his voice and dismounted. At daybreak he went with the two sons of Huṣayṣ, Sahm and Jumāḥ. When the Banū ʿAbd Manāf saw that, they said, “By God, we will let no one come between us and our brothers!” They left them alone and did nothing to them. Wahb ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhra said: (al-Basīṭ)

Gently, Umayya, for oppression brings ruin;do not force yourself into a day of grievous evil.Its stars appear while the sun is still shining;from it, colocynth and bitter gourd pour into the cup.Do not think us like people whom you have toyed with,who will not resent humiliation until the wine is shamed.I am the son of ʿAbd Manāf—no concealment here—and the stallion bears the mark by which another stallion is known.I am the son of ʿAbd Manāf, beyond reproach,then the son of Zuhra, whose worth has never been matched.My paternal uncle is al-Ḥārith, who fulfills his pledge—not the sons of ʿIlāj, on the morning when Fihr was roused.

They came upon them before the sun’s crest, ablaze,the horsemen’s meteors, before which sight grows dim.A group of them poured forth to meet death;their foremost fled, and the scouts caught up with them.In Mecca’s valley, where their flocks were gathered,the Banū Judhayma—indeed, the sheep were driven forth.

This was the first thing that arose between them.

The Affair of the Perfumed Ones

The Banū ʿAbd Manāf, seeing their standing and their numbers, wanted to take the House from the Banū ʿAbd al-Dār. They sent to Abū Ṭalḥa, who was ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAbd al-Dār, saying, “Send us the key of the Kaʿba!” He went out from his place and came to the Banū Sahm. The mother of Sahm was Tamāḍur, daughter of Zuhra, and the mother of ʿAdī ibn Saʿd ibn Sahm was Hind, daughter of ʿAbd al-Dār ibn Quṣayy. He sought their protection from the Banū ʿAbd Manāf. They stood with him in the matter and said:

“By God, we will protect him!” The Banū ʿAbd Manāf came in the morning and said, “By God, we will take it from them!” Quraysh came to differ over the matter: some said that ʿAbd Manāf had the better claim to the House, others that ʿAbd al-Dār had the better claim. When the talk about it grew, Umm Ḥakīm, daughter of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ibn Hāshim—though it is said instead that ʿĀtika is better established than Umm Ḥakīm, and this is the consensus—took a great bowl, filled it with saffron perfume, and carried it until she set it down in the Ḥijr. She said, “Whoever perfumes himself from this bowl is one of us!” Asad stood and perfumed themselves; al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr stood and perfumed themselves; Zuhra [ibn Kilāb] perfumed themselves, as did Taym ibn Murra. These were the five tribes called “the Perfumed Ones”: ʿAbd Manāf, Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā, Zuhra, al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr, and Taym ibn Murra.

The Banū Sahm, for their part, slaughtered camels, then dipped their hands in its blood and said, “Whoever dips his hand in it is one of us!” Jumāḥ [and Sahm], ʿAbd al-Dār, Makhzūm, and ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb dipped their hands. Then they entered the House and swore by God that none of them would surrender another. They mixed their sandals in the courtyard of the Kaʿba, and so they were called the Confederates. They were five tribes: ʿAbd al-Dār, Sahm, Jumāḥ, Makhzūm, and ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb.

It was of their mixing their sandals and swearing an oath in the House that ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zibʿarā ibn Qays ibn ʿAdī ibn Saʿd ibn Sahm spoke, when ʿUthmān ibn Ṭalḥa ibn Abī Ṭalḥa of the Banū ʿAbd al-Dār, and Khālid [ibn al-Walīd] ibn al-Mughīra, emigrated to the Messenger of God ﷺ. ʿUthmān ibn Ṭalḥa recited: (al-Ṭawīl)

I appeal to ʿUthmān ibn Ṭalḥa by our pactand by the place where the people’s sandals were laid at the approach.And by the bonds our fathers made, every one of them,and by Khālid, who is no breaker of such a bond.Do you seek a key to a House other than your own,when all else is locked against it?

Abū Ṭalḥa ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAbd al-Dār said: (al-Wāfir)

My father’s pride bids me that the honor of the Banū Huṣayṣstand firm, and that I be their ally.When they set out to undertake a matter,I am neither slow nor weak behind them.

The Confederates said, as they gathered: “Who will suffice for us against the Banū ʿAbd Manāf?” The Banū Sahm said, “We will suffice against them! If they fight, we will fight them; if they send a delegation, we will send one; and if they act, we will act.” It was of this that Ibn al-Zibʿarā spoke in his boast: (al-Ṭawīl)

I am the son of those who passed Manaf by virtue of its might … and Manaf’s neighbor among the people is few.

A meeting is a meeting when they meet, and a delegation [is answered] with a delegation;

they act as others act, and a guarantor is a guarantor.

The clan of Jumah said, “We stand with Zuhrah”; ʿAbd al-Dār said, “We stand with Asad”; Makhzūm said, “We stand with Taym”; and ʿAdī said, “We stand with al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr.” The people nearly fought one another, and some were on the verge of attacking others. Then Quraysh, exercising good sense, brought matters to an end and held back.

They fell silent. This, then, is the affair of the Perfumed Ones and the Confederates.

The Pact of the Virtuous

The Pact of the Virtuous was an alliance such as people had never heard of before: none had been more honorable or more excellent. It began when a man from Banū Zubayd came to Mecca with merchandise. Al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil ibn Hāshim ibn Saʿd ibn Sahm bought it from him, then delayed paying what he owed. The Zubaydī went back to him repeatedly but he gave him nothing. The Zubaydī waited until Quraysh had taken their places in their meeting assemblies and the markets had opened. Then he stood on Abū Qubays and called out at the top of his voice: (basīṭ)

O people of Fihr, for a man whose goods have been wronged …in the valley of Mecca, far from his family and his kin, and a disheveled man in a state of consecration, who has not completed his ʿumrah.

O people of Fihr, between the Black Stone and the other Stone:

Is there anyone from Banū Sahm who will honor his obligation to protect? …Or has a pilgrim gone astray, with his wealth, on his journey?

The sacred is sacred to whoever upholds its sanctity;

but no sanctity belongs to the garment of a treacherous wrongdoer.

Then he came down. Quraysh regarded as grave what he had said and done; they feared the consequences, and the matter was discussed in their assemblies. Then Banū Hāshim, Banū al-Muṭṭalib, Banū Zuhrah, and Banū Taym gathered at the house of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān. He prepared food for them, and they made a pact among themselves that whenever anyone was wronged in Mecca, they would all stand with the wronged person against the wrongdoer until they secured redress from whoever had wronged him, whether one of their own—noble or lowly—or someone else. Then they went out.

The Messenger of God ﷺ was among those present at that pact and entered into it five years before revelation came to him. When he was in Medina, he used to say: “I attended a pact of the Pact of the Virtuous at the house of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān. I would not wish to have broken it, even in exchange for red camels. If I were called to it today, I would answer.” It was called the “Pact of the Virtuous” because it was an alliance that emerged from the Pact of the Perfumed Ones and the Confederates, and so it was a virtue above both of them. It has also been related that it was called the “Pact of the Virtuous” because when Quraysh heard what the parties had agreed to, they said, “By God, these are the virtuous ones!” They left their place and made their pact. They then went to al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil and said, “By God, we will not leave you until you pay him what he is owed!” He gave the man what he was owed. They continued in this way: no one was wronged in Mecca without their obtaining redress for him.

ʿUtbah ibn Rabīʿah ibn ʿAbd Shams used to say: “If a man were to leave his people, I would leave ʿAbd Shams so that I could join the Pact of the Virtuous.” ʿAbd Shams was not a party to the Pact of the Virtuous.

A man from Thumālah came and sold some merchandise to Ubayy ibn Khalaf ibn Wahb ibn Ḥudhāfah ibn Jumah. Ubayy wronged him and behaved outrageously toward him; he was ill-mannered and oppressive. The man went to the members of the Pact of the Virtuous and told them what had happened. They said, “Go to him and tell him that you have come to us. If he gives you what you are owed, fine; otherwise, come back to us.” The man went to Ubayy and said, “I have come to the Pact of the Virtuous. They told me to come back to you and tell you that I had been to them. I have now returned to you: what do you say?” Ubayy brought out what he owed the man and gave it to him. In connection with this, the Thumāli, Lamīs ibn Saʿd al-Bāriqī, said: (ṭawīl)

Would Ubayy wrong me in the valley of Mecca,

while I am wronged, with neither my clan nor my companions beside me?

I called my people, the Bāriq, to answer me—

yet between me and my people lie many deserts and open plains.

The Pact of the Virtuous will not allow you to violate my rights,

O Banū Jumah; the right is seized by force.

A merchant from Khathʿam came to Mecca with his daughter, called al-Qatūl. Nabīh ibn al-Ḥajjāj ibn ʿĀmir ibn Ḥudhayfah ibn Saʿd ibn Sahm became enamored of her. He would not leave until he had taken her away and prevailed over her father. Someone said to the girl’s father, “You should turn to the Pact of the Virtuous!” He went to them and complained of what had happened. They went to Nabīh ibn al-Ḥajjāj and said, “Let this man’s daughter go!” At the time, he was staying apart on the outskirts of Mecca, and she was with him. He said, “My people, let me enjoy her tonight!” They said, “By God, not for an hour!” He let her go and returned her to her father, and the Khathʿamī set out with them. For this reason Nabīh says: (khafīf)

My companions departed, and I did not bid farewell to al-Qatūl;

I did not give them a gracious farewell.

Do not think, as the caravan departed that evening,

that you have wronged me by keeping me from speaking.

I feared the Virtuous Ones when they came to me;

now I see that I have nothing to fear from the Virtuous Ones.

By the One to whom the shaven-headed pilgrims go,

the hands clapped, and they raised their voices in praise,

I am free of blame: a slain woman to the people—and do they seek anything but women to slay?

My need is fulfilled—except for the conversation; I am not withoutmy need for conversation and kisses.

I writhe with her, just as a water snake writhesin a vessel, for a long time.

And a three-night stay at Dhū al-Majāz,

and Minā, where our pilgrimage became permissible.

Then they went along beside Nakhlah, without—one band of them catching up with the next.

And young women, sheltered and modest,

and young men whom I kept awake through a long night.

They are neither camels of mixed breed nor base folk; and youwill never lack among them a distinguished and noble man.

Nabīh ibn al-Ḥajjāj also said of her: (kāmil)

Greet al-Dirīrah when she has gone farfrom us, with distance between us.

Neither by leaving nor by meetingdoes she grant us anything.

She seized the delight of his heart,and departed with what she kept hidden.

with its furnishings and its level ground.

They raised the canopy over her

and found her water sweet.

Were it not for the Virtuous Ones, and the factthat I am not safe from her distance,

I would draw near her dwellings

and slip around her tent,

and come to her, walking withouta guide, in her darkness,

and drink what remained of her saliva,

and cling to her in her innermost being.

Nabīh ibn al-Ḥajjāj was one of Quraysh’s horsemen, but he had little means. He had two wives from Quraysh: one was Umm ʿAmr, daughter of Asīd ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah, and the other was the daughter of Mālik ibn ʿUmaylah ibn al-Sabbāq ibn ʿAbd al-Dār ibn Quṣayy. He fed them only with what he earned day by day at Mecca’s market. The two women agreed to ask him for a divorce. When he returned to them, they said, “By God, we have been patient with you until this has gone on too long for us and making a living with you has become difficult. We ask you to part from us.” He said of this: (khafīf)

Those two wives of mine speak words of estrangement

and utter falsehood and nonsense.

They ask for divorce when they see that I have little means;

you have come to me with something reprehensible.

Perhaps my wealth will increase

and my back will be freed of debts;

we will drag our trains in a life of ease and distinction,

and you will say, “Lay down your staff, for a long while,”

and you will see servants for us, and measures of silver,and ten half-grown slave girls.

Do you not see: whoever has wealth is loved,and whoever is poor bites into a harsh life?

He keeps a confidant’s secret hidden, buta man of wealth is present at every secret.3

Not long afterward, he married the daughter of Qamṭah, the Roman, who was a wealthy merchant in Mecca. Qamṭah gave him, as a wedding gift, a qawsarah filled with silver. He traded, his wealth grew, and his standing in Mecca became great, until he was killed at Badr as an unbeliever.

Abū ʿUbaydah said: “The man in this story was Nabīh ibn al-Ḥajjāj, one of the young men of Quraysh. The poem that accompanies the story is by ʿAmr ibn Nufayl. ʿAmr ibn Nufayl was a maqtī; a maqtī is a man who marries his father’s wife after his father, and he is called a ḍayzan.”

The Account of the Gazelle, the Gazelle of the Kaʿbah

The account of the gazelle was as follows. Maqīs ibn ʿAbd Qays ibn Qays ibn ʿAdī ibn Saʿd ibn Sahm had a house that was a gathering place for Quraysh’s young men. They spent their money and drank there. Among them were Abū Lahab, al-Ḥakam ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ, al-Ḥārith ibn ʿĀmir ibn Nawfal, and al-Fākih ibn al-Mughīrah.

Mulayḥ ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Sabbāq ibn ʿAbd al-Dār; Abū Ihāb ibn ʿAzīz ibn Qays ibn Rabīʿa ibn Zayd ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Dārim; Qays ibn Suwayd—Qays was the maternal brother of ʿĀmir ibn Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf, for their mother was Kuhayfa of Banū Jandal ibn Abīr ibn Nahshal, and he was their ally; Abū Musāfiʿ al-Ashʿarī, an ally of Banū Makhzūm; and Dayk and Duwayk of Khuzāʿa, who served them. They gathered at the house of Maqīs, who had two singing slave girls called Asmāʾ and ʿAthma. Asmāʾ sang, when their drink had run out, verses by a man of Balī: (al-Ṭawīl)

O Būha, pass the cup around among my companions,

for my fellow drinkers beside you are thirsty.

Though a day’s delight may not be completed,

and its morning brightness fade, tears fall in showers.

How many a day and night have I witnessed,

with abundant intoxication and sustenance!

Alone with her, when the ill omen of her stars had died,

my fellow drinkers there were ʿĀmir and Khidāsh.

Abū al-Mundhir said: ʿĀmir and Khidāsh were the sons of Zuhayr ibn Janāb al-Kalbī. (al-Ṭawīl)

When wine overpowered their wits and stirred them,

their limbs and pleasures alike came alive.

I found the two of them showing no sign of the wine

when men’s wits were said to be but straw.

He had earlier told them: “Dayk and Duwayk, a caravan has come from Syria carrying wine. It has halted at al-Abṭaḥ.” Abū Lahab said, “Woe to you! Have you no money?” They said, “No, by God!” He said, “Then take the Kaʿba’s gazelle! It is my father’s gazelle.” They rose and set out, though they were apprehensive. A cold, dark, rainy night had come upon them. They reached the Kaʿba, where no one was around. Abū Musāfiʿ and Abū Lahab carried al-Ḥārith ibn ʿĀmir on their backs and lifted him onto the Kaʿba. He struck the gazelle, and it fell. Abū Lahab picked it up, and they brought it away. Abū Lahab said, “You know the gazelle is my father’s gazelle, and I have a quarter share in it.” They went to the house of Dayk and Duwayk, broke it open, and took the gold and its two eyes, which were made of ruby. They threw away its casing, which was on a wooden stand in the house of a shaykh of Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy. Abū Lahab took the neck, head, and horns, and handed the two earrings to them, saying, “These are for Asmāʾ and ʿAthma.” Then he left without approaching them. The men went and bought all the wine that was at al-Abṭaḥ. Then they brought it to their companions, drank, and adorned the two singing girls with the nose-rings and earrings. Quraysh went several days before they noticed the gazelle was missing. They spoke of it and made much of the matter. The most outspoken and most vehement among them was ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān. Quraysh spoke, but no one matched his vehemence. He would stand— He would say, “I testify that no one dared do this to you but yourselves, and no one stole the gazelle but you. By God, if your prudent men do not restrain your fools, punishment will surely befall you!” When he had gone on at length, Ḥafṣ ibn al-Mughīra said to him:

“You have said much about the gazelle, though you have no greater claim to it than the rest of Quraysh. It is only ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib’s gazelle. Here are al-Zubayr ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib and Abū Ṭālib, and they say nothing. And Abū Lahab, in my view, is no less entitled to it. So hold your tongue!”

Al-Zubayr and Abū Ṭālib grew angry and said, “You keep defending it as though you know who took it. By God, if we catch him, we will cut off his hand!” They continued drinking for a month or more. Then, about a month later, al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, a young lad, passed by the dwellings of Banū Sahm late in the day on an errand of his. The men were making a racket and were drunk, raising their voices. He listened and heard one of them say to the two singing girls, “Sing Abū Musāfiʿ’s verses”: (al-Basīṭ)

The gazelle, with all its adornment, which you

keep for the vicissitudes and reversals of time,

was circled by a band of the worst of their people,

men of high rank and generosity, and of the House with its veil.

They cast lots over it, hoping perhaps

to learn where the head and the tracks were.

Though I was a stranger, far from my homeland,

my alliance is with ʿImrān or ʿUmar.

The company’s sweet basil—I seek no alliance

in place of theirs, nor any living person instead.

The two girls sang. Al-ʿAbbās came over and said, “Abū Ṭālib, would you like to know who stole the gazelle?”

He said, “Who are they?” Al-ʿAbbās replied, “They are at Maqīs’s house. I haven’t seen them, but come and listen!” Abū Ṭālib, al-Zubayr, Ibn Judʿān, Makhrama ibn Nawfal, and al-ʿAwwām ibn Khuwaylid came over, until they drew near the door and heard them say, “We have sung!” Abū Musāfiʿ said, “Sing them these verses of mine”: (al-Basīṭ)

Tell the highest and lowest of Banū al-Naḍr

that, by God and the Corner, the gazelle

has come to be divided among the singing girls of Banū Sahm,

though it was never bought at a price by their drinking companions.

They kept passing around fragrant, choice musk,

layer upon layer, over their partings,

and wine, cool and clear, over which merchants vie …

soft, aged in the wine-jar for a long time.

Abū Ṭālib said, “These are surely the men who took the gazelle. If you go in now, you’ll find them drunk and unable to understand or comprehend what you say to them. We would prefer not to enter upon them unless we have with us some of the allies who made the pact after the first alliance, whom we can call as witnesses against them. Neither ʿAbd Shams nor Nawfal joined that pact.” So they put it off until the next day. In the morning they went to Banū Sahm and said, “Banū Sahm, you know that the gazelle of your sanctuary has been stolen by Maqīs’s drinking companions, who are at his house. Come in with us so we can search it!” They went in with them. They found Maqīs absent and found the gazelle’s body—its sheath, which was made of Arab leather. They said, “We need no other proof against him than this.” They took his two singing girls into custody. One of them was wearing the gazelle’s earring and the other was wearing a nose-ring made from its nose-ring. She said, “Will you keep us safe if we tell you what happened?” They said, “Yes.” The two girls told them, naming Abū Lahab. They suspected him because he had been away from them for those days and had not come to see them. They sought him, but he went into hiding. They learned that the gazelle had been broken open at the house of Dayk and Duwayk. Dayk fled, but they seized Duwayk and held him from behind. Ibn Judʿān reached out and brought the blade down on him. It was blunt, and he cut through his wrist until he severed it. He died only a day later. Then the Muṭayyibūn challenged the Aḥlāf, saying, “We will not be satisfied until you cut off their hands, or return the gazelle itself, or each man of them pays one hundred camels.” This situation continued for a while. Then al-Ḥārith ibn ʿĀmir was sent out. He had been given a robe by Muṭʿim ibn ʿAdī, had entered the state of consecration for a lesser pilgrimage, and had circumambulated the House, but no one spoke to him. Then he went on his way and spent ten years without entering Mecca. Abū Ihāb ibn ʿAzīz said, “What prevents you from doing to me what you did to your companion? Is it because I am an ally, and you hold me in contempt?” They did not grant his request, so he reproached them: (al-Mutaqārib)

Perhaps Banū Nawfal have risen

with the fire of one warming himself consuming them;

he was a young man who had not answered us before,

and I forbid Nawfal to entrust him.

O Muṭʿim, your glory came first;

you follow in the first footsteps.

Would you feed Taym and its followers,

while you are confounded and have gone beyond all bounds?

A band of our own flesh, whom you despise, …

while Ḥasl sits back, not entrusted with the task.

Ḥasl was ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy’s son. When they heard these verses, they grew angry, gave him a robe, and sent him out in the consecrated state for a lesser pilgrimage. He met Abū Musāfiʿ and said, “Abū Musāfiʿ, what of your words:

(al-Basīṭ)

Though I was a stranger, far from my homeland,

my alliance is with ʿImrān or ʿUmar.

I see that ʿImrān and ʿUmar did nothing for you. By God, if your alliance had been with this—meaning Muṭʿim or Nawfal—you would have been safe from alarm and your face would have been seen.” He said, “Did you not praise him when he gave you security?” “Indeed I did,” he replied. Abū Ihāb said: (al-Mutaqārib)

Tell Quṣayy, when you reach her:

what a fine young man Nawfal bore!

When he drinks wine, he pays dearly for it,

though reproachers may blame him fiercely.

Love for the slender-waisted, graceful woman

drew him to the nose-ring, the gazelle’s nose-ring,

for ʿAthma, when she appeared to him,

and Asmāʾ, more lovely without adornment.

Ibn Judʿān was the most vehement of the group in this matter, but he could act only with the support of Abū Ṭālib, al-Zubayr, and Makhrama. He went to them and said, “You there! The men who stole your gazelle are safe while you sit here.” Abū Ṭālib rose up in great fury, until the two men disappeared and feared they would be killed. Abū Ihāb said: (al-Basīṭ)

O men, what deluded wits—

if only prudence and experience could help them!

The house of Ibn Judʿān is refuge for every wanton woman …

so how can virtue and sin be gathered there?

Why do I see lions, their breasts boiling,

as though the shin-bones in them had grown weak?

And a house of excellence belongs to ʿAbd al-Dār, not to you,

while you are a band of dark, foul men.

“Jaʿbūb” means low and vile. He was alluding to the singing slave girls of Ibn Judʿān. The Umayyads then rose and supported the Aḥlāf, until they were nearly strong enough to prevail. ʿUtba and Shayba, the sons of Rabīʿa, Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb, Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ, Usayd ibn Abī al-ʿĪṣ, and a number of Quraysh’s elders came forward. They spoke, mentioned the gazelle, and urged one another to support the Aḥlāf. Abū Uḥayḥa said, “Obey me, and do not meddle in this matter of the gazelle, for I know something about it.” They said, “What do you know?” He said, “My father told me, on the authority of his father, that two Arab tribes settled in Mecca and perished over a gazelle that one of their men had killed. Their free and enslaved people were utterly destroyed.” They said, “We have never heard this.” He replied, “Indeed, and I have verses about it, composed by ʿAbd Shams.” They said, “Recite them to us.” He recited: (al-Ramal)

O men of Quṣayy, people of the town,

whoever seeks its pleasures through injustice

will soon bite his fingers in remorse,

when no excuse will help the remorseful.

Purify your garments; do not wrap yourselves

in them against God’s religion, in resentment.

Then rise together in this affair,

with the dignity of righteousness in the sacred month.

Have you heard of Arabs who survived,

or of a foreign tribe that escaped harm,

when they perished over a doe, pursued

by a dark fawn with a black-tipped eye?

It held him back from it, and he did not follow it,

where it had sheltered him beside the Sanctuary.

He struck it with the short side of his feathers,

and roasted some of it, then fed people and divided it up.

They said to him, “How did they perish?” He said, “A serpent, as large as a mountain, came upon them and began to blow at them. From its belly it cast forth things like spears of fire, and they began to burn until they all perished.” They said, “How could that be?” He said, “Have you not heard the words of ʿAbd Shams:

(al-ramal)

Then a serpent came upon him from behind,

crooked of fang, springing, devouring.

It struck him with a piercing meteor,

like what you saw at night, blazing.

They said, “By God, we will have no part in any of his affair!” At that point the power of the Allies [al-Aḥlāf] weakened, until they made peace with them on terms of fifty camels from each side. These were handed over to Abū Ṭālib and al-Zubayr, who used them to support the Kaʿba, the pilgrims, and others. Whoever did not give fifty camels remained in fear until God sent the Prophet ﷺ. During the days of Badr, Abū Musāfiʿ and his companions, who had fled, came back and said, “People of Quraysh! Why do you banish and drive us away? What do we have with you if we fight Muḥammad and his companions? If we are killed, that is what you want; if we survive, it will make amends for what we did.” They came and took part at Badr. Abū Musāfiʿ and al-Ḥārith ibn ʿĀmir were killed, while Abū Īhāb escaped. Al-Ḥārith ibn ʿĀmir had used to sit with the Prophet ﷺ before he went out, and he admired his speech. Quraysh said, “He has apostatized.” He was killed as an unbeliever at Badr, although the Messenger of God ﷺ had said, “Do not kill him; leave him for the orphans of Banū Nawfal!” Khubayb ibn ʿAdī al-Anṣārī killed him, and was later killed in his place and crucified at al-Tanʿīm. This is what Ḥassān ibn Thābit said:

(al-basīṭ)

Ḥārith, had you not been struck by what you were struck with,

how excellent you were in might and noble lineage!

You brought shame and disgrace upon your people,

a shame no Arab tribe can conceal.

You, who plundered the House of its ornament—

where is the gazelle? The one who stole it will not remain hidden.

Quraysh first sought al-Ḥakam ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ, but Banū Umayya protected him. When Abū Lahab heard that Quraysh were coming to him, he hid himself. He had ten maternal aunts from Khuzāʿa, who had borne many children among them. He grew bold and called out to them. A large group of his maternal cousins came to him from among the sons of his aunts, but no one came near him. They said, “Leave him to his brothers!” When Shaybān ibn Jābir al-Sulamī wanted to form an alliance with Banū Hāshim and mentioned the matter of Abū Lahab, he said:

(al-ṭawīl)

I will make with you an alliance whose bonds are firm,

like the alliance of Banū ʿAmr with your father, son of Hāshim,

to give support so long as a stone lies at Najd,

and so long as a dove coos among the karātim.

They protected the old man of ʿAbd Manāf after

he saw the poison of the knife above the knuckles.

The Account of the Elephant

The account of the Elephant was that a group of Kināna set out toward Yemen. When they entered Ṣanʿāʾ, they found a building constructed like the Kaʿba. It had been built by Abraha al-Ashram, the Abyssinian, who named it Qullays. Some of that group entered the building, and one of them defecated in it. Then they set out and went on their way. When Abraha found the trace, he grew angry and said, “Who did this?” They told him, “A group from the Arab House did it.” By his religion, he swore that he would not leave them until he devastated their land and demolished their House. He sent out and gathered the reprobates of the Arabs and their riffraff. Most of those who followed him were Khaṯʿam. They did not make pilgrimage to the House or regard the Sanctuary as sacred. Banū Munabbih ibn Kaʿb ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Kaʿb also followed him; they did not regard the Sanctuary as sacred or make pilgrimage to the House. Among them was al-Aswad ibn Maqṣūd, who said:

(al-rajaz)

O my horse, hurry me along

when you hear the pilgrims’ cry!

Before that, he used to waylay pilgrims and those making the lesser pilgrimage. Among those who followed al-Ashram was Nufayl ibn Ḥabīb al-Khaṯʿamī, along with many men of Khaṯʿam. The wicked al-Ashram said:

“When I have carried out my campaign in Tihāma, I will march until I raid the people of Najd.” This coincided with the words of Ṭarafa ibn al-ʿAbd, who was then at Najrān. When he saw that force and heard al-Ashram say that he would raid Najd, he composed some verses and sent them to Qatāda ibn Maslama al-Ḥanafī. They are these:

(al-ṭawīl)

Will you not deliver a message of good counsel to Qatāda?

For caution must surely save you,

while in Najrān the kings have carried out their decree.

Would that a raven in the sky were calling to you!

One party comes to visit the Kaʿba of God,

and another will come to you unless you cut across the sea.

Kulthūm ibn ʿUmays of Banū ʿĀmir ibn ʿAbd Manāt ibn Kināna was taken by al-Ashram and shackled in his presence. While in chains, he said:

(al-ṭawīl, with the initial foot omitted)

Would that God would hear a prayer

and send a caller between the two mountain ridges!

The troops of al-Ashram are coming toward you, with the elephant among them,

and dark men riding female ghouls.

A mighty host whose number cannot be reckoned—

by al-Lāt, they brandish thirsty, gleaming spears!

They come to you, they come, and the earth is choked by them,

as a sudden downpour flows and chokes a valley.

Two men of Banū Sulaym came with them; they were both dissolute and had joined them at Najrān. One was called Muḥammad, the other Qays: the sons of Khuzāʿī ibn Ḥazāba ibn Murra ibn Hilāl. Al-Ashram summoned Qays ibn Khuzāʿī and said, “Praise me and speak of my campaign.” He said:

(al-kāmil)

Greet wine and its cup,

for al-Ashram, the king, the mighty lord.

I was told that you had set out,

and said, “A man of no small renown is mentioned!”

The sons of Abyssinia around him,

cloaked in robes of marājil.

Fair of face, and dark of face,

their hair like falāfil.

Ibn Isḥāq said: He means “on the pulpits.”

Al-Ashram set out until he stopped at a place of his in Najrān. It happened to be a festival day when only the eunuch was eaten. He ordered the eunuch to be cooked and served to the people. The Arabs shunned it, except for Khaṯʿam, who ate it and said to al-Ashram, “O King! Those of Muḍar who are with you refused to eat any of this eunuch, and they taunt us for eating it.” Al-Ashram grew angry and sent for some men of Muḍar. Qays ibn Khuzāʿī and his brother were among those seized. He had ordered them to prostrate themselves to the cross, but none of the Muḍar who were with him did so. When they stood before him, Qays ibn Khuzāʿī said:

(al-ṭawīl, with the opening foot omitted)

If you are from a noble stock,

then you, O King—may no evil befall you—are the noblest of those who walk.

And we, O King—may no evil befall you—follow the religion of our people:

we do not worship the cross or eat the eunuch.

Al-Ashram said, “He speaks truly. Every people has its own religion. Let them go.” For this reason ʿAbd Allāh ibn Thawr ibn ʿAbbāb ibn al-Bakkāʾ ibn ʿĀmir ibn Rabīʿa ibn ʿĀmir ibn Ṣaʿṣaʿa taunted Khaṯʿam, saying:

(al-ṭawīl, with the opening foot omitted)

We went off, and Khaṯʿam went off in its finery

to a second dwelling, with many wood gatherers.

They brought to their gathering a broad shayzā platter,

with eunuchs in it like the heads of rabbits.

Al-Ashram sent Muḥammad ibn Khuzāʿī as a scout with a group. They climbed a mountain, and God sent a thunderbolt upon them, and they all perished. His brother Qays lamented him—Muḥammad was known by the kunya Abū Khuzāʿī—and said:

(al-kāmil)

O Abū Khuzāʿī, how many a horse had reached [them] together—

first to feed on torn-out spleens.

Would that death had protected him, since his shirt

was a zaghf coat of mail, doubled, like the pool of al-Abraq.

May my family be your ransom, in war and in peace,

O son of generosity, when generosity was not yet bestowed.

Al-Ashram went on until he passed by al-Azd and sent cavalry against them. They routed his cavalry. ʿAbd Shams ibn Masrūḥ al-Azdī said:

(al-ṭawīl, with the opening foot omitted)

We protected our land’s domain from the army,

and their affair was no near threat to us.

Whenever they shot at us with a shower of arrows,

I met them with every long-armed, noble steed.

How many young men—before their arrows had flown—

returned with no share of our property!

Then he marched on until he stopped at al-Ṭāʾif. He was told that there was a House here, venerated by the Arabs. When he arrived among them, Masʿūd ibn Muʿattib al-Thaqafī, who was shrewd, came out to meet him and offered him wine, raisins, and condiments. Then he said, “O King! This is not the House you seek. The great House you seek is the one whose people did what they did before you. We are under your rule, so go on! When you have finished, you can decide what to do with us.” He went on and left him. Quraysh heard of him and went out, leaving Mecca. No one remained there who was mentioned except that he feared for himself—except ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ibn Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf and ʿAmr ibn ʿĀʾidh ibn ʿImrān ibn Makhzūm. The two of them used to provide food each day. Al-Ashram sent al-Aswad ibn Maqṣūd with cavalry, and he seized camels belonging to Quraysh near a well, including two hundred camels belonging to ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. Then he sent a messenger and said, “Look to see who remains in Mecca!” He went, looked, and returned to him, saying:

“I found all the people there, and I found no one.”

“He found a man whose height and beauty I have never seen the like of, and a man whose shortness I have never seen the like of.” The handsome one was ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, and the short one was ʿAmr ibn ʿĀʾidh.

He said, “Go and bring me the tall one!” The man went and brought ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. When he entered, he pleased and charmed the king, who ordered a seat to be brought for him. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib sat upon it, spoke with him, and answered his questions. The king’s admiration for him grew. Then he said, “Ask me for whatever you wish!” ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib said, “You have taken camels of mine; return them to me!” The king said, “By God, you have made me think less of you after I admired you!” ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib said, “Why is that, O King?” He replied, “I came to destroy your honor and sanctity, yet you left off asking me to spare them and asked me for your property!” ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib said, “By God, my sanctity is dearer and more important to me than my property. But my sanctity has a Lord who, if He wishes to protect it, will protect it; and if He leaves it, He knows best. These camels belong to me alone, and I fear for them, so deal with them!” The king ordered his camels returned to him. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib rose and said:

(al-rajaz)

O Lord, bring low al-Aswad ibn Maqṣūd,

who seized the great herd adorned with collars.

Between Ḥirāʾ and Thabīr and the open plain ... Protect him, Lord, and You are praised

ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib stood in the courtyard of Mecca, supplicating, and said: (kāmil)

My Lord, the servantprotects his own encampment, so protect Your encampment.

Let not their cross prevail ...nor their stratagems; my Lord, Your stratagem.

If You leave themand our Kaʿba, then that is something You have willed.

They put on their equipment and covered their elephant, then advanced until, when they entered al-Mughammis, intending to enter the Sanctuary, the elephant turned back. They drove it on, but when it drew near, it turned back. They urged it on and shouted at it, but it knelt. They began putting iron into its trunk until they pierced it, but it would not move. This was on a Friday, and they spent the night until Saturday. When the sun rose, they heard something like the whistling of cold, then it rose over them, birds larger than locusts, which came from the sea. When they were above their heads, God unleashed the wind against them, and the birds pelted them with stones held in their feet. They abandoned their buildings and possessions, released the elephant, and fled. Those stones would strike a limb and pierce it until they severed the bone. Some died where they stood, while others escaped. What struck them caused smallpox and measles, and more died than survived. Among those killed by the sores were al-Ashram and his son al-Najāshī; he had been [at the head of] the army’s vanguard. Al-Aswad ibn Maqṣūd and Qays ibn Khuzāʿī were killed in the battle. Nufayl ibn Ḥabīb escaped, as did Akhnas al-Fiqaymī. He had been one of the elephant’s guides, and the one most opposed to the expedition. ʿAmr ibn al-Waḥīd ibn Kilāb said: (ṭawīl)

God struck the Abyssinians and the elephant a blow;I see every faint heart fearful.

The day of the sword’s buzzing was its warning,and the day beside al-Mughammis was one of dreadful gloom.

Their commander was a bird, unlike anythat ever fought among stones pouring down.

As though showers from the sky had fallen,while the plains were set ablaze with the assembled troops.

Al-nafnaf is the space from the top of a mountain to its bottom; al-nafnaf is also the stretch from one end of the land to the other.

We crushed them from behind and before them,while a blast of violent wind confronted them.

It stole their breaths and their lives,and none escaped save those who followed behind, trailing.

As though, after the eagle’s attack, they weredry brushwood scattered by the summer winds.

It would have been a cure had Nufayl stayedamong its claws—but the appointed term comes and passes.

Nufayl ibn Ḥabīb answered him, saying: (basīṭ)

What do you think my claws would have doneif I had caught you, Ibn al-Waḥīd, among signs and portents?

We spent one day and night at al-Mughammis,in ʿĀlij, amid the bleating of camels and cattle.

Then we saw the sun’s light hiddenby birds like a man-sized swarm of locusts, spread wide.
