They pelted us, advancing and then turning back,with a hailstorm from the black horizon.

The Abyssinians scattered, caring for no one,while battalions of wind assailed us from the left.

They hurled us down on our faces, and the wind drove us on;we could guard against neither wind nor stone.

A man of strength among us slipped, with no vigor in him,and most of that army died in hardship.

As though flocks of sheep lay sleeping,while on the backs of the Abyssinians were lumps like dung beetles.

Nufayl ibn Ḥabīb also said: (wāfir)

O ʿAyn, be greeted! O Rudayna,take comfort as I return to you.

Had you seen us, while the army was peltedwith stones, you would have pitied us, Rudayna.

I praised God when I saw birdsand a rain of stones falling upon us.

We were rained on without water, but witha punishment of vengeance, following one another for a time.

Everyone keeps asking about Nufayl,as though I owed the Abyssinians a debt.

Qays ibn al-Aslat said of this: (mutaqārib)

[And] of God’s blessings are our wealth,and our children, and the blessings we possess;

And among His favors was the day of the Abyssinianelephant, when each time they drove it on, it knelt.

Their goads beneath its flanks ...and they pierced its trunk, so it was cleft.

It quickly turned back on its tracks,and when they had routed its host, it was routed.

The alliance of ʿAdī and Banū Sahm

As for the circumstances that brought about the alliance of ʿAdī and the alliance of Banū Sahm: ʿAbd Shams ibn ʿAbd Manāf had a Bactrian camel—there was no other Bactrian camel in Mecca—and he lost it and searched for it. Its disappearance and loss distressed him. He continued searching for it until a caller stood atop Abū Qubays, when the people had quieted, and called out in a loud voice: (rajaz)

By God, it was no gift to us,O ʿAbd Shams, seeker of the Bactrian camel!

Nor do we seek anything from you;we have no blood-money due us, nor any gift.

But it was a wayward Bactrian camelthat came to us one evening for a time;

She drank among us, as a greeting,while a cup passed among them, unhurried.

Then, abased and humbled, she was slaughteredby young men whose faces were radiant.

Let the ill-fated Bactrian camel be far away!You will never see her again, until death’s end.

ʿAbd Shams awoke angered by what he had heard. He offered a herd of camels to whoever would guide him to news of her. A son of a sister of Banū ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb, from Banū ʿAbd ibn Muʿayṣ ibn ʿĀmir, came to him and said to him: “The one who slaughtered your Bactrian camel was ʿĀmir ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUwayj ibn ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb. The proof of this is that its hide is buried in a pit in the courtyard of his house.” ʿAbd Shams set out with his sons and some of his family until they entered the house of ʿĀmir ibn ʿAbd Allāh and found the matter as the man had said. He seized ʿĀmir, took him to his own house, and said, “I shall cut off his hand and take his property!” Banū ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb interceded with him, and they made peace on the terms that he take all of ʿĀmir’s property and that ʿĀmir leave Mecca. They did so: he sent for and took all of ʿĀmir’s property, then let him go. He then said, “Leave Mecca!” So they departed. Banū Sahm intervened, took them in among themselves, and said, “By God, you will not leave!” The mother of Sahm ibn ʿAmr was al-Alūf, daughter of ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb. They settled there as allies of Banū Sahm until the coming of Islam. ʿĀmir ibn ʿAbd Allāh said: (wāfir)

May my father and mother be ransomed for Banū al-Alūf,while throats were constricted with anguish.

We abandoned our clients, though they had shown us affection;there was no kinship to return to, nor friend.

They prevented our expulsion and gave us shelterin dwellings where no strait need be feared.

They stood between us and Banū Quṣayy;there is no road back to them.

The account of Quṣayy ibn Kilāb, his gathering Quraysh, and their settlement in al-Abṭaḥ

Hishām, from Bishr al-Kalbī, from his father, said: Before Quṣayy ibn Kilāb, Quraysh were called Banū al-Naḍr. They were scattered in the outskirts of Mecca, and none of them lived in al-Abṭaḥ. When Quṣayy came of age, Khuzāʿa, Banū Bakr ibn ʿAbd Manāt ibn Kināna, and Ṣūfa—namely, al-Ghawth ibn Mur—joined forces against him. He sent for his maternal brother, Rizāḥ ibn Rabīʿa ibn— Ḥarām ibn Ḍanna ibn ʿAbd ibn Kabīr ibn ʿUdhrā. Quṣayy’s mother was Fāṭima, daughter of Saʿd ibn Sayl, of al-Azd. Sayl’s name was Khayr ibn Ḥamāla ibn ʿAwf ibn ʿĀmir, known as al-Jādir, the first to build a wall around the Kaʿba. He was the son of ʿAmr ibn Jaʿthama ibn Mubashshir ibn Ṣaʿb ibn Dahmān ibn Naṣr ibn Zahrān ibn Kaʿb al-Ḥārith ibn Kaʿb ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mālik ibn al-Azd. Jaʿthama had set out when al-Azd left Maʾrib, and settled among Banū al-Dayl ibn Bakr ibn ʿAbd Manāt ibn Kināna. He made an alliance with them and married among them.

Fāṭima, Quṣayy’s mother, had been with Kilāb ibn Murra and bore him Zuhra. Then a long time passed, until he grew old and lost his sight; after that she bore Quṣayy. Hishām said: He was called Quṣayy because his mother took him far away [taqaṣṣat bihi] to Syria. Rabīʿa ibn Ḥarām al-ʿUdhrī came on pilgrimage and married her. She took Quṣayy, still a boy, with her to Syria. She bore Rabīʿa Rizāḥ and Ḥannā. A dispute arose between Quṣayy and a boy from Banū ʿUdhrā, and the ʿUdhrī disowned him, saying, “By God, you are not one of us!” He went to his mother and said, “Who is my father?” She said, “Your father is Rabīʿa.” He said, “If I were his son, he would not have disowned me.” She said, “By God, my son, your father is nobler than he: your father is Kilāb ibn Murra, one of the people of the Sanctuary.” He said, “By God, I will never stay here!” She said, “Then stay until the pilgrimage season comes!” When that time arrived, she sent him with a group of Quḍāʿa and with Zuhra, who was alive. He came to him; and Zuhra, they say, was more eloquent, and Quṣayy was also eloquent. Quṣayy said, “I am your brother.” He said, “Come closer.” He touched him and said, “By God, I recognize the voice and the likeness.” Then Zuhra died, and Quṣayy came of age. He wanted to gather his people, Banū al-Naḍr, in the valley of Mecca. Khuzāʿa, Bakr, and Ṣūfa united against him and grew numerous. He sent for his brother Rizāḥ, who came with a force from Syria and contingents of Quḍāʿa until he reached Mecca. Ṣūfa were the ones who conducted the people’s departure for the pilgrimage. Rizāḥ stood on the mountain pass and said, “Let Quṣayy proceed!” He then let the people proceed. From then on, the departure for the pilgrimage remained in the hands of Banū Quṣayy, down to the present day.

He brought all the branches of Quraysh into al-Abṭaḥ except Muḥārib ibn Fihr, al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr, Taym al-Adram ibn Ghālib, and Muʿayṣ ibn ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy. These were called al-Ẓawāhir, and they settled in the outskirts of Mecca. However, a group of Banū al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr settled in al-Abṭaḥ: they were the kin of Abū ʿUbayda ibn al-Jarrāḥ, and he was among them. Quṣayy’s name was Zayd; he was also called Mujammiʿ, “the Gatherer,” because he gathered Quraysh. That is what Ḥudhāfa ibn Ghānim said: (ṭawīl)

Your father Quṣayy was called Mujammiʿ;through him God gathered the tribes of Fihr.

The account of al-Arkāḥ

Al-Kalbī said: Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf had appointed his brother al-Muṭṭalib ibn ʿAbd Manāf as his heir. The Banū al-Muṭṭalib and Banū Hāshim have remained united to this day, and the Banū ʿAbd Shams and Banū Nawfal have likewise remained united to this day. When al-Muṭṭalib died, Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf seized some plots of land belonging to Hāshim, called al-Arkāḥ, and gave them to his son ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, who took possession of them. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib sought the support of his people, but none of their leading men answered him. When ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib saw that his people had forsaken him, he sent to his maternal uncles among the Banū al-Najjār. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib’s mother was Salmā bt. ʿAmr b. Zayd b. Labīd, one of the Banū ʿĀmir b. Ghanm b. ʿAdī b. al-Najjār b. Thaʿlaba b. ʿAmr of the Khazraj. The following verses were in ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib b. Hāshim’s letter to them: (basīṭ)

O how long my nights, my sorrows, and my cares!

Is there a messenger to my maternal uncles, the Najjār?

Let ʿAdī, Dhubyān, and their Māzin know,and Mālik, the protectors of their neighbors, of my plight.

I was once among you, fearing no wrongfrom any wrongdoer; I was mighty, secure, and at ease.

Then I journeyed to my people, and my uncle al-Muṭṭalibdrove me from that place by making the journey.

Al-Muṭṭalib was alive, and I lived in comfort and delight;I walked briskly, swinging the hems of my robes.

Al-Muṭṭalib has vanished into a dark grave,and Nawfal has risen to seize my property.

When he saw a man whose paternal uncles were absentand whose maternal uncles were far away, without a protector,

he turned on him, showing no regard for kinship.Who can protect a man between paternal and maternal uncles?

So rally to his aid and ward off the wrong done to your sister’s son.Do not abandon him: you are not people who abandon others.

No people among all the descendants of Qaḥṭān are your equalin protecting a neighbor, generosity, and graciousness.

You are gentle toward whoever is gentle in disposition;you are a refuge for those who seek your protection, and a deadly poison to the arrogant.

They set out across every difficult and easy terrain until they reached Mecca. They spoke with Nawfal, who returned ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib’s arkāḥ to him. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib then composed these verses: (wāfir)

Māzin and the Banū ʿAdīand Dhubyān b. Taym Allāt refused my wrong,

while Mālik warded it off until it came to an end,and after Nawfal, [they] turned aside from my sanctuary.

Through them God restored my arkāḥ to me;in seeking aid, they stood apart from my own people.

ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib also said to his maternal uncles, the Banū al-Najjār: (sarīʿ)

Tell the Banū al-Najjār, if you reach them,that I am one of them, their son, and [swear by] al-Khamīs.

I have seen them: when you come to them,they long to meet me and cherish my faintest sound.

He said: Ibn al-Kalbī told me: When ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib sent word to his maternal uncles, the Banū al-Najjār, eighty men came from among them. They had girded on swords, slung bows over their shoulders, and hung shields from them. They dismounted in the courtyard of the Kaʿba. When Nawfal saw them, he said, “Nothing but trouble has brought these men here.” He feared them, so he returned the arkāḥ to his nephew and treated him well. Shamir b. ʿUwaymir al-Kinānī said, praising the Banū al-Najjār for supporting ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib against his uncle:

(long)

By my life, the maternal uncles of Ibn Hāshim gave supportthat was better and nobler than that of his closest paternal uncles.

Though far away, they answered their sister’s son’s call,when Nawfal sought to wrong him and betray him.

They did not desist until his right was restoredand returned to him, after it had nearly been consumed.

May God reward with good a Khazrajite bandwho pledged themselves to righteousness—and the righteous are best.

The alliance of Khuzāʿa with ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib

The reason for Khuzāʿa’s alliance with ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib was that a group of Khuzāʿa said among themselves: “By God, we have seen no one among all creation more handsome in face, more perfectly formed, or greater in forbearance than ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. His uncle has wronged him, and he has sought the aid of his maternal uncles. We gave birth to him just as the Banū al-Najjār did. What if we offered him our support and made an alliance with him?” They agreed on this and went to ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. They said, “Abū al-Ḥārith, if the Banū al-Najjār gave birth to you, so did we; and we are also your neighbors here. Come, then, and let us make an alliance with you.” He accepted. Budayl Abū Warqāʾ b. Budayl al-ʿAdawī, Sufyān b. ʿAmr, Abū Bishr al-Qumayrī, and Hājar b. ʿUmayr b. ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā al-Qumayrī, Hājar b. ʿAbd Manāf b. Ḍāṭir, ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā b. Qaṭan al-Muṣṭaliqī, Khalaf b. Asʿad al-Malḥī, and ʿAmr b. Mālik b. Muʾammal al-Ḥabtarī came with a group of their people. They entered the Dār al-Nadwa and drew up a document between them. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib came with seven men from the Banū al-Muṭṭalib, al-Arqam b. Naḍla b. Hāshim, who was one of Quraysh’s leading men, and al-Ḍaḥḥāk and ʿAmr, the sons of Ṣayfī b. Hāshim. No one from the Banū ʿAbd Shams or Nawfal attended, because of the favor owed to them. They hung the document in the Kaʿba.

When they had sent ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib off, Hājar said, “By God, if you have said that, I once had a vision in Yathrib: his descendants will surely have a great destiny!” They asked, “What did you see?” He said, “I saw the Banū ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib walking above the tops of Yathrib’s palm trees, throwing dates down to the people. They will surely have a great destiny, and it will surely come from Yathrib.” Hājar said, “I replied, ‘By God, ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib has only one boy, named al-Ḥārith!’” He said: They made the alliance with him.

That day ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib married Lubnā bt. Hājar b. Ḍāṭir, and she bore him Abū Lahab. He married Mumanniʿa bt. ʿAmr b. Mālik b. Muʾammal al-Ḥabtarī, and she bore him al-Ghaydāq.

He said: They drew up a document for which Abū Qays b. ʿAbd Manāf b. Zuhra served as scribe. The Banū Zuhra honored ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib because of his marriage tie to them. The document read:

This is the covenant entered into by ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib and the leading men of the Banū ʿAmr of Khuzāʿa, together with those of Aslam and Mālik who are with them. They have formed an alliance for mutual support and assistance, a comprehensive pact that makes no distinctions: elders with elders, juniors with senior men, those present with those absent. They have pledged and made a covenant for as long as the sun rises over Thabīr, a camel bellows in open country, the two mountains stand, and any human being lives in Mecca. It is an everlasting pact for a long span; sunrise will strengthen it, and the darkness of night will extend it. It was made by ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib b. Hāshim and the men of the Banū ʿAmr. They became a single hand against the Banū al-Naḍr. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib is bound to support them against any person seeking vengeance, whether on land or sea, in easy or difficult terrain. The Banū ʿAmr are bound to support ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib and his descendants against all the Arabs, in the east or the west, in mountainous country or open plain. They have made God guarantor of this, and God is sufficient as surety.

Then they hung the document in the Kaʿba. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib said: (long)

I shall charge Zubayr, should my death come upon me,to preserve what lies between me and the Banū ʿAmr,

and to uphold the pact its elder established;let no one violate it through injustice or betrayal.

They kept the ancient bond of kinship and made an alliancewith your father, standing between your people and you, O son of Fihr.

He said: ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib entrusted [the matter] to his son Zubayr; Zubayr entrusted it to Abū Ṭālib; and Abū Ṭālib entrusted it to al-ʿAbbās. What confirms this is the statement of ʿAmr b. Sālim to the Prophet ﷺ—when the Banū Bakr raided them and killed some of the Khuzāʿa: (rajaz)

O God, I appeal to Muḥammadby the ancient pact of our father and his father.

We gave birth to him, and he was a son.Then we embraced Islam and did not withdraw our hand.

Abū Saʿīd said: Abū Bakr recited the rest of this poem to us. He said: ʿAbd al-Karīm b. al-Haytham b. Ziyād related it to us, with its chain of transmission, in a lengthy report: (rajaz)

Quraysh failed to keep the promise they made youand broke your confirmed covenant.

They claimed that you do not summon people to guidanceand set up a watch for me at Kadhāʾ.

They are weaker and fewer in number,yet they came upon us at al-Watīr by night.

They killed us while we were bowing and prostrating.So support the Messenger of God with steadfast support,

and summon God’s servants, that they may come as reinforcements—among them is the Messenger of God, now armed,

white, like the full moon, rising high,amid a host like the sea, surging with foam.

The Messenger of God ﷺ—said, “You have been supported, ʿAmr b. Sālim.”

What also confirms the alliance between the Banū Hāshim and Khuzāʿa is the statement of Shaybān b. Jābir al-Sulamī. He came to al-Muqawwim b. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib to make an alliance with him, and said: (long)

I make with you an alliance whose bonds are firm,like the alliance of the Banū ʿAmr with your father, Hāshim,

for mutual support as long as there is Wathīma in Najdand a dove sings among the Karātim.

They protected the shaykh of the Manāfīs afterhe saw the blade of the adze above his knuckles.

The contest of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib and Ḥarb b. Umayya

Abū al-Mundhir said: A Jewish man from Najrān, named Udhayna, was under the protection of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib b. Hāshim. He used to trade in the markets of Tihāma with his wealth, and Ḥarb b. Umayya was angered by this, so he incited some young men of Quraysh against him. He said to them, “This foreigner is crossing the land to reach you and traversing your country with his wealth, without protection or safe-conduct! By God, if you killed him, you would have no one to fear seeking vengeance for his blood.”

He said: Hāshim b. ʿAbd Manāf b. ʿAbd al-Dār b. Quṣayy and Ṣakhr b. ʿĀmir b. Kaʿb b. Saʿd b. Taym b. Murra charged at him and killed him. Ibn Maṭrūd b. Kaʿb al-Khuzāʿī was with them.

He said: ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib did not know who had killed him until later, when he learned where the deed had come from. He went to Ḥarb b. Umayya, reproached him for what he had done, and demanded vengeance for his protected neighbor. Ḥarb refused him. Their dispute and obstinacy led to a contest, and they appointed the Negus, king of Abyssinia, to judge between them. He refused to arbitrate. They then appointed Nufayl b. ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā b. Riyāḥ b. ʿAbd Allāh b. Qurṭ b. Rizāḥ b. ʿAdī b. Kaʿb, and they went to him.

Ḥarb b. Umayya said, “Abū ʿAmr, are you going to contend with a man taller than you, more handsome than you, greater in stature than you, with fewer followers than you, more children than you, greater in generosity than you, and longer in reach than you? And I say this even though you have—”

4 “You are slow to anger, renowned among the Arabs, steadfast in resolve, and beloved by your clan. But you have challenged a man whose victory is assured.” He said: “So ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib prevailed over Ḥarb.” Ḥarb was angered by this, and spoke harshly to Nufayl, saying: “It is an inversion of fortune that we made you a judge.” Nufayl then composed:

(al-basīṭ)

“May the people who have precedence among mankind rejoice: they bore the burden of hundreds, and surpassed those who have no restraint.

“God gave them a light by which one may seek illumination when the stars miss their rain-bringing constellations.

“They are the roots of the earth; from them comes our stock. What needy petitioner came today to their soil, weak and humbled?

“Decay cannot reach the pillars of their dwelling, nor can a fissure open upon the summit of their mountain.

“The sons of Shaybat al-Ḥamd, lords of glory, as is known: the foremost of Maʿadd, whenever restraint is shaken.”

“The wind blew with fine cloud, and set it moving, driving a rainless cloud swiftly on its way.

“Shaybat al-Ḥamd is a light by which one seeks illumination when terror comes upon the fire that has been kindled.

“The she-camels returned, their pastures barren, around the noble stallion, with a breeding male, but no follower.

“Ḥarb, your efforts have not reached the level of a pack donkey: you water the pilgrims, but what burden can the pack donkey bear?”

“Your father is one, but the branch between you differs: from him come the lean and the fresh, ripened shoots.

“Know the people who are your lords and masters; may no evil reach you that cannot be repelled.

“They are Quraysh’s highlands, in the very root of its stock, and they feed the people when drought touches it.”

Al-Arqam ibn Naḍlah ibn Hāshim said of this, recalling the challenge between Hāshim and Umayyah:

(al-ṭawīl)

“Before you, Umayyah had already brought Hāshim to ruin, and ʿAmr led him to the worst of destinations.”

“Ḥarb, you have competed without falling short, a keen contender, reaching the furthest goals.”

He said: Ḥarb ibn Umayyah intended to expel Banū ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb from Mecca. For that purpose, Banū ʿAbd Shams ibn ʿAbd Manāf and Banū Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf gathered together. Banū Hāshim, Banū al-Muṭṭalib, and Banū Zuhrah took the side of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, while Banū Sahm supported Banū ʿAdī because they were allies, and defended them. When Ḥarb ibn Umayyah saw this, he desisted.

The challenge between ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib and Thaqīf

Al-Kalbī said: ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ibn Hāshim owned property at al-Ṭāʾif called Dhū al-Haram. Thaqīf claimed it and came to dig there. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib took them to court over it before a soothsayer in Syria called ʿAzzā Salamah al-ʿUdhrī. A group of his people went with ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. His son al-Ḥārith was with him; at that time he had no other children. The Thaqafī who was disputing with ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, named Jundub ibn al-Ḥārith, set out with a group of Thaqīf. They all traveled together. When they were partway along the road, ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib and his companions ran out of water. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib asked the Thaqafīs to give them water from their supply, but they refused. When the people’s thirst became extreme and they thought they were doomed, ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib and his companions dismounted and made their camels kneel, believing that death was upon them.

God brought forth a spring for them from beneath the forequarters of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib’s camel. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib praised God for this and knew it was from God, exalted is He. They drank their fill from the water and took with them what they needed. He said: The Thaqafīs’ water then ran out, and they asked ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib to give them water. His son al-Ḥārith said to him: “By God, if you do, I will plunge my sword into its sheath, then lean over it until it comes out of my back.” He said to him: “My son, give them water, and do not do that to yourself.” So ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib gave them water.

Then they continued on to the soothsayer. They had hidden a small object for him: the head of a locust. They put it in a punctured waterskin and hung it from the collar of one of their dogs, named Suwwār. He said:

When they came to the soothsayer, they found two cows driving a baḥzaj between them. Each claimed it as her own calf. They had both given birth on the same night, but a tiger had eaten one of the two baḥzajs, so they were both grieving for the one that remained. When they stood before the soothsayer, he said: “Do you know what these two cows are saying?” They said: “No.” He said: “They are disputing over this baḥzaj and asking for another baḥzaj that was taken away by one with a gray body, a gaping mouth, a long tooth, and a loud throat. The smaller one has no claim to the offspring of the larger.” So he awarded it to the larger of the two cows. When they had gone, He turned to ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib and his companions and said: “What do you need?” They said: “We have hidden something; tell us what it is.” He said: “Yes. You hid something that flew, rose up, then came down, and fell to the ground, leaving a barren patch.” They said: “Lādah—that is clear.” He said: “It is something that flew: it took wing, with a trailing tail, and a head like a nail, and a leg like a saw.” They said to him: “Lādah.” He said: “If it is lādah, then it is lādah: it is the head of a locust, in a hole in a waterskin, around the neck of Suwwār, the one with the collar.” They said to him: “You have got it right.” Then they gave him their names and said: “Tell us about the matter over which we are disputing.” He said:

“I swear by brightness and darkness, and by the Sacred House: the property of Dhū al-Haram belongs to the Qurashī, the generous one.”

He said: The Thaqafīs grew angry. Jundub ibn al-Ḥārith said: “Judge in favor of the one of us who holds the higher rank, has the largest cauldrons, and strikes most fiercely with the spear.” ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib said: “Judge in favor of the man of the greatest good works, whose father was the lord of Muḍar and who gives drink to the pilgrims when they are numerous.” The soothsayer said:

(al-rajaz)

“But by the Lord of the tall she-camels that travel at a steady pace, carrying men, in barren lands, old and worn:

“Indeed, the exalted rank and noble qualities belong to Shaybat al-Ḥamd, the generous son of Hāshim.”

ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib said: “Judge between my people and his people: which are the better?” He said:

(al-rajaz)

“Here is my judgment; hear this testimony:

“Banū al-Naḍr are noble lords,

“Of red Muḍar, the jewel in its necklace,

“People of exalted rank, kings and leaders;

“To visit the House is their act of worship.”

Then he said: “Thaqīf is a runaway slave who was captured and freed; then he had offspring and ran away again, so he has no claim in the matter of lineage.… Abbaqa means ‘he had many children’; baqq derives from this.” He thus gave precedence to ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib and his people over the Thaqafīs and their people.

The challenge between Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf and Umayyah ibn ʿAbd Shams

He said: Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf had gone to Syria, where he stayed for some time. Then he set out from there for Mecca, bringing large sacks filled with broken bread, with camels carrying the sacks, until he reached Mecca. This was in a year of severe hardship, when people went hungry and their property and lives were lost. Hāshim took the camels that had carried the sacks, slaughtered them, and had cooks prepare them. Then he brought out the broken bread and filled large bowls with it. He ordered the pots to be turned over on top of them, and fed the people of Mecca and others. This was the first time they had plenty. A man of Quraysh said of this:

A man of Quraysh, Ḥudhāfah ibn Ghānim al-ʿAdawī, said of this:

(al-kāmil)

“ʿAmr the Exalted broke bread for his people, while the men of Mecca were gaunt from the barren years.”

Wahb ibn ʿAbd ibn Quṣayy ibn Kilāb said of it:

(al-wāfir)

“Hāshim bore what had become too much for them, what Ibn Bayḍ was unable to take on.

“He brought them sacks, filled to overflowing, from the land of Syria, with sifted wheat.

“He gave the people of Mecca their fill of broken bread, and mixed the bread with fresh meat.

“The people sat among platters of black shīzā wood, while their juices overflowed.”

It is also transmitted: “of shīzā wood, their jābir.”

Umayyah ibn ʿAbd Shams was wealthy. He undertook to do what Hāshim had done, but was unable and fell short. Some of Quraysh gloated over him, mocked him, and criticized him for what he had done and then failed to accomplish. This stirred up strife between him and Hāshim, involving hostility, boasting, and dispute. Umayyah then challenged him to a contest, and rallied his brothers. They rebuked and incited him. Hāshim disliked this because of his age. But Quraysh made so much of the matter and blamed him, that Hāshim said to him: “Since you insist on a contest, I will compete with you for fifty black-eyed camels, which we will slaughter in Mecca, and for exile from Mecca for ten years.” They agreed to this and appointed the Khuzāʿī soothsayer as judge between them. Abū Hamhamah ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ʿĀmirah ibn ʿUmayrah ibn Wadīʿah ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr went out with them. His daughter Amāh was married to Umayyah ibn ʿAbd Shams, and he went with them as a witness. They said: “What if we hide something for him and test him with it before we submit our case to him?” They found the bottom of a decayed wooden cup and Abū Hamhamah kept it with him. Then they came to the soothsayer, whose home was at ʿUsfān, and made their camels kneel at his door. They said: “We have hidden something for you; tell us what it is before we submit our case to you.” He said:

“I swear by light and darkness, by the livestock in Tihāmah, and the hill in Najd, you have hidden the bottom of a cup, with al-Filandaḥ, Abū Hamhamah.”

They said: “You are right. Judge between Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf and Umayyah ibn ʿAbd Shams: which is the nobler?” He said:

“By the shining moon, the radiant star, the rain-bearing cloud, the bird in the air, and the traveler who finds his way by knowledge, whether journeying from the highlands or— “...farther off. Hāshim had indeed outstripped Umayya in distinctions, first and last.” He said: Hāshim took the camels, slaughtered them, and fed those present. Umayya set out for Syria and stayed there ten years. Hence it is said that Umayya claimed Abū ʿAmr, his son—who was Dhakwān, a man from the district of Ṣaffūriyya—as his own. Afterward Abū ʿAmr married his father’s wife and fathered Abān by her; Abān was Abū Muʿayṭ. It is also said that he claimed Dhakwān as his own as well as Abān.

The contest of ʿĀʾidh ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm and al-Ḥārith ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā

He said: ʿĀʾidh ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn Makhzūm and al-Ḥārith ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Quṣayy disputed which of them was nobler and more distinguished in rank and glory. They appointed as arbiter between them a soothsayer who used to stay at ʿUsfān, and agreed that the loser in the contest would owe the winner fifty camels. They placed the camels in the keeping of al-Mughīra ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm, then set out to see the soothsayer. When they were near him, a man from Banū Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā, called Zarr ibn Ḥubaysh, found an ostrich egg. He said: “Shall we hide this egg from him? If he finds it, we will know he can judge your case correctly.” They said, “Yes.” He kept it with him. Then they came to the soothsayer, made their camels kneel at his door, and hobbled them in his courtyard. They called out to him, and he came out to them. They said, “Tell us what we have come to ask you about.” He said:

“I swear by the Lord of heaven and the Sender of the rainclouds that pour down water! You have come only to seek distinction.”

They said, “You are right. We have hidden something from you; tell us what it is.” He said, “You have hidden from me something rounded and smooth as a pestle, its color like a pearl, with ants slipping over it.” They said:

“Between them?”

He said, “I swear by the Lord of Mecca and al-Yamāma, and by whoever travels through the valley of Tihāma, whether for pilgrimage or to settle there: you have hidden an ostrich egg from me, with Zarr, the turbaned man.”

They said, “You are right.” They gave him their genealogies and said, “Judge between us: which of us has the greater claim to glory and nobility?” He said:

“I swear by gazelles of a dusty hue, in a barren plain bright with mirage, coming and going between salm and lote trees: the distinction of glory and pride belongs to ʿĀʾidh, until the end of time.”

He said: ʿĀʾidh took the camels, slaughtered them, and fed the people. Then he composed these verses (al-basīṭ):

I am a man from the heights of Fihr, when they trace their descent,While you, Ḥār, are traced to a spring.

You vie for glory with people you cannot overtake ...When the ostrich chick runs swiftly, or the old she-camels long for home.

So return disgraced, for you have met a calamity;I have outstripped you, and the defeated is defeated.

“Something rounded and smooth, like a pestle, its color like a pearl, with ants slipping over it.” They said:

“Between them?”

He said, “I swear by the Lord of Mecca and al-Yamāma, and by whoever travels through the valley of Tihāma, whether for pilgrimage or to settle there: you have hidden an ostrich egg from me, with Zarr, the turbaned man.”

They said, “You are right.” They gave him their genealogies and said, “Judge between us: which of us has the greater claim to glory and nobility?” He said:

“I swear by gazelles of a dusty hue, in a barren plain bright with mirage, coming and going between salm and lote trees: the distinction of glory and pride belongs to ʿĀʾidh, until the end of time.”

He said: ʿĀʾidh took the camels, slaughtered them, and fed the people. Then he began to recite (al-basīṭ):

I am a man from the heights of Fihr, when they trace their descent,While you, Ḥār, are traced to a spring.

You vie for glory with people you cannot overtake ...When the ostrich chick runs swiftly, or the old she-camels long for home.

So return disgraced, for you have met a calamity;I have outstripped you, and the defeated is defeated.

The contest of Mālik ibn ʿUmayla and ʿUmayra ibn Hājir al-Khuzāʿī

Hishām said: Mālik ibn ʿUmayla ibn al-Sabbāq ibn ʿAbd al-Dār ibn Quṣayy owned a horse that had won races, and ʿUmayra ibn Hājir ibn ʿUmayr ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Numayr al-Khuzāʿī owned a horse that had also won races. The two men met in Mecca and spoke about horses. ʿUmayra said, “My horse is better than yours.” They wagered their horses and entrusted the wager to ʿIkrima ibn ʿĀmir ibn Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn ʿAbd al-Dār: whichever horse won, its owner would receive a hundred camels. They released their horses from Ajyād. ʿUmayra’s horse came ahead, but Qāsiṭ ibn Shurayḥ ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAbd al-Dār intercepted and held it back. ʿUmayra demanded the prize, but Qāsiṭ refused him, until a quarrel nearly broke out between them. They then submitted the matter to a contest before a soothsayer: whichever one the soothsayer favored would receive a hundred camels and the horse. They made a binding agreement and set out, each accompanied by a group of his people. Each led twenty camels for the soothsayer. Arṭāh ibn ʿAbd Shuraḥbīl ibn Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn ʿAbd al-Dār ibn Quṣayy advised Mālik ibn ʿUmayla not to compete with him, but Mālik refused. They set out toward the soothsayer, accompanied by ʿAlqama ibn al-Faghwāʾ al-Khuzāʿī, from Banū Naṣr. They said, “If only we could hide something from him and test him with it!” On their way they found the carcass of a vulture and took it. Then they came to the soothsayer, ʿUzzā Salama al-ʿUdhrī—Salama was his name, and ʿUzzā the name of his jinn companion. They made the camels kneel at his door. He came out to them, and they said, “We have hidden something from you; tell us what it is.” They had put it in a woolen bundle and given it to ʿAlqama. He said:

“You have hidden from me a winged creature with a long neck,Long-legged, mottled; when it plunges deep, it circles,And when it swoops, it splits—a sharp-clawed creature ...”

“... with a sharp claw, living until it grows old.”

He said, “Between them?” They said, “You tell us.” He said:

“I swear by the light and the moon,By radiance and time,By the winds and the dawn!You have indeed hidden from me a vulture’s carcassIn a woolen bundle,With the young man from Banū Naṣr.”

They said, “You are right. Judge between Mālik ibn ʿUmayla and the son of Hājir.” He said (al-rajaz):

I swear by al-Marwa and the holy sites,And the place where sacrificial camels are slaughtered at al-Ḥazāwir,

And by everyone who makes pilgrimage on a powerful camel,Whether one who leaps or one who spreads his wings,

Heading to the House of God, with its curtains,The distinction of glory and proud deedsBelongs to the young man, ʿUmayra ibn Hājir.

So return, neighbor, with ill fortune.

ʿUmayra went to the camels, slaughtered them, and took the camels and the horse. Mālik began to recite (al-ṭawīl):

Ibn Hājir outstripped me when I raced him,He gladdened my enemies and stripped me of my wealth.

Would that, before my journey and my departureTo the tyrant soothsayer, my limbs had been severed

By a sharp, keen sword with a wavy blade,And that ʿUmayra’s good fortune had not come from my wealth.

I went astray as one goes astray in the night,Unable to see a fingernail in the place where he has stopped to rest.

Arṭāh said to Mālik about this (al-ṭawīl):

You will regret it too late, that you obeyed me,When regret can do you no good.

You raced a noble champion from a noble line,And fell short when advancing became too hard for you.

(“Too late”: after the opportunity has passed. Hence the saying of the Exalted: “And how will they reach it from so far away?” [Qurʾān 34:52].)

The contest between Banū Makhzūm and Banū Umayya

He said: A group of Banū Makhzūm and a group of Banū Umayya gathered by the Stone and spoke of power and strength. A man from Kināna, who was an ally of Banū Makhzūm, said, “Banū Makhzūm are more powerful and stronger.” A man from Zubayd, who was an ally of Banū Umayya, said, “Banū Umayya are more powerful and stronger.” Their argument continued until al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīra al-Makhzūmī and Asīd ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ became angry and began boasting against one another. The dispute grew heated. Al-Walīd said, “I am better than you in mother and father, and more firmly rooted than you in Quraysh by descent.” Asīd said, “I am better than you in rank, and more firmly rooted than you in Quraysh by descent. You are a man from Kināna, from Banū Shajaʿ, an outsider among Quraysh, an adopted newcomer among Banū Makhzūm. I am the noblest of Banū ʿAbd Manāf and the foremost of Quṣayy. Come, let me compete with you in boasting.” Then Asīd said (al-ṭawīl):

I am not of Shajaʿ, though my lineageIs to a noble family, not to one who claims it falsely.

Had you been one of us, you would not have wrought corruption among us;You would have acted generously toward us, as does the prudent man who keeps his dignity.

Unless you abandon the enmity between us,A bright, branded reproach will fall upon you.

He said: They submitted the matter to a contest, as the Arabs used to do, and said, “Let Satiḥ judge between us. He belongs to neither side, so we will accept his judgment.”

They both accepted him as judge and agreed that the loser in the contest would owe the winner fifty camels. [The account continues:] They set out toward him, accompanied by a group of their people, until they reached Satiḥ, who was then at Ṣaʿda in Yemen. On the way they found a lion’s claw and put it in a provisions bag with a black boy belonging to Asīd ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ. They said, “We will hide this from him and ask him about it; if he gets it right, we will take our case to him.” They came to Satiḥ, made their camels kneel at his door, and hobbled the camels belonging to the two men in his courtyard. A man from Banū Makhzūm sprang up and said, “O Satiḥ, we have come to you” (al-rajaz):

We come to you, Satiḥ,A company led to you by the open plain.

It is you alone we truly seek;May you be guided, for we have no way around you.

Give your judgment quickly and do not hesitate.

Satiḥ came out to them. They said, “We have hidden something from you. Tell us what it is so that we may then submit our dispute to you.” He said, “You have hidden a piece of wood, though it is no wood; rather, it is a stone, but not a boulder.” They said, “Explain.” He said, “It is crooked and sharp-pointed, in a basket or a provisions bag: a tawny lion’s claw, with the black boy.” They said, “You are right. Judge between al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīra and Asīd ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ.” He said:

“I swear by the highlands and the lowlands, then by the House of God with its pillars, and by everyone who makes pilgrimage on a wide-mouthed camel: I know the matter you have brought me. The son of Makhzūm is a man of noble deeds. So go back, Asīd, with a humbled face.”

Then he turned to the two of them and said, “As for you, al-Walīd ...”

“... O Walīd! You are like a mountain laden with water and trees, where people find a place to take refuge, and where the tribe finds protection and shelter. You hasten toward good and beware of evil. As for you, Asīd! You are like a rugged mountain, where those who seek fire find embers; there is neither a place to descend nor a way back up. Good is scarce with you, and evil is abundant. Al-Walīd prevailed and triumphed; Asīd failed and lost.”

Satiḥ took what had been allotted to him from the camels. Al-Walīd went to the camels, slaughtered them, and fed the people; they ate and carried some away.

The contest between Banū Quṣayy and Banū Makhzūm

Maʿrūf ibn al-Kharrabūdh, from Bashīr ibn Tamīm, said: A group of Quraysh held a gathering. Abū Rabīʿa ibn al-Mughīra, his son al-Mughīra, and the sons of al-Mughīra said, “Among us is Suwayd ibn Hurmā, of Banū ʿĀmir ibn ʿUbayd ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm.” Asīd ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayya said, “Away with you! It is Banū Quṣayy who are nobler. The nobility of ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar is due to his mother, Barra, daughter of Quṣayy; through her he attained what he attained.” Then he enumerated the men of Quṣayy, and said, “Among us are the watering of the pilgrims, the custodianship of the Kaʿba, the council, the feeding of the pilgrims, and the standard.” They submitted the matter to a contest. Asīd said, “If I defeat you, I will take you out of your property; if you defeat me, I will leave my property.” They agreed to have a soothsayer from Khuzāʿa judge between them. Ibn Abī Hamham and his mother, Tamāḍir, daughter of Abū ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd Manāf, said, “Wait, Abū Rabīʿa!” But he refused. They set out and drove camels to be slaughtered by the winner. On the way they found a dove—or a wild pigeon—and gave it to Usāma, the servant of Abū Hamham.

He put it in the feathers of an ostrich. When they came to the soothsayer, they said, “What have we hidden from you?” He said:

“Perhaps a cloud followed by another cloud,Flashing over the land of Tihāma,So that from its downpour every talḥ tree and thumām plant floated away.You have hidden from me a young dove,Or its sister, a wild pigeon,In an ostrich’s down,With your servant Usāma.”

They said, “Judge between them.” He said:

“I swear by the firmly rooted, lofty peaks,And by the black, rocky ground, with its hard stones;And by every ship that has sailed across the sea:Asīd is indeed the lord of abundance.Do not deny him his merit among the great multitude.”

“Indeed, I swear by heaven, earth, and water,And by what has appeared to us from Ḥirāʾ:Asīd has outstripped Abū Rabīʿa, beyond dispute.”

They said, “Which is better, Quṣayy or Makhzūm?” He said:

“I swear by the charging horses, snorting:A free man is not the equal of a lowly miser,One who settled his people in al-Abṭaḥ.”

Asīd slaughtered the camels and returned, taking Abū Rabīʿa’s property. Asīd’s sister was married to Abū Jahl, and she spoke to her brother until he returned Abū Rabīʿa’s property to him.

The contest between Banū Luʾayy ibn Ghālib

Abū Firās Muḥammad ibn Firās ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAṭāʾ ibn Khawli al-Shāmī said: Abū Ḥafṣ, the brother of Abū al-ʿAlāʾ al-ʿĀmirī, told me: Ibrāhīm ibn ʿAbd al-Malik al-ʿĀmirī, of Banū Ḥubayl, told me:

Luʾayy ibn Ghālib had a son named ʿAmr, who died young. This was what happened: he went on a journey with his brother ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy. When ʿĀmir was returning to Mecca, ʿAmr fell behind him on the road. A viper bit and killed him. Banū Luʾayy suspected ʿĀmir of killing him and wanted to kill ʿĀmir. Those among them with good judgment restrained them, and they demanded blood money from him. He said:

“I will not pay for someone I did not kill.”

They agreed to go to Suṭayḥ al-Dhiʾbī regarding the matter. ʿĀmir said to them, “If Suṭayḥ says, ‘I killed him,’ though I did not, will you kill me for it? And if he says, ‘I did not kill him,’ though I did, will you forgo your brother’s blood?” They said, “Then what do you advise?” He replied, “Do something during your journey. If he tells you what you did, he will speak truly about your companion.”

They left Mecca. After traveling for ten days, they slaughtered a young camel and hunted an eagle with it, then took ten of its covert feathers. They traveled for another month after those ten days, then slaughtered a young camel, hunted an eagle with it, and took ten of its covert feathers. Then they came to Suṭayḥ. He was told, “The Banū Luʾayy ibn Ghālib are at the door.” He said, “Admit the Banū Luʾayy.” They entered, and he said, “The Banū Luʾayy are people of eminence, nobility, lordship, and renown; authority will be among them in the future.” Then he said:

“You left your homeland after a dispute arose among you. You traveled ten days from your homeland, then slaughtered a young camel and hunted an eagle with it, and took ten of its covert feathers. ʿĀmir did not kill ʿAmr; a viper bit him.” ʿĀmir said to them, “The man is surely telling the truth: he had fallen behind me at such-and-such a place.”

They went to the place and found his head and bones by the viper’s hole.

The contest between ʿUtba ibn Rabīʿa and al-Fākih ibn al-Mughīra al-Makhzūmī

Abū al-Sikkīn Zakarīyā ibn ʿUmar ibn Ḥiṣn al-Ṭāʾī told me: my father’s paternal uncle Zaḥr ibn Ḥiṣn told me, from his grandfather Ḥumayd ibn Ḥāritha. Abū Saʿīd al-Sukkarī said: Abū al-Sikkīn al-Ṭāʾī also told me. Abū Bakr—that is, al-Ḥulwānī—said: Muḥammad ibn Aḥmad Abū Bakr also told me: Abū al-Sikkīn al-Ṭāʾī related to us, with his chain of transmission:

Hind, daughter of ʿUtba ibn Rabīʿa, was married to al-Fākih ibn al-Mughīra al-Makhzūmī. Al-Fākih was one of Quraysh’s young men, and he had a guesthouse that people visited without asking permission. One day the house was empty, and he and Hind were inside. Al-Fākih then went out to attend to some need. A man who used to visit the house came along and entered it. When he saw the woman, he turned and ran away. Al-Fākih called after him, then came to Hind, struck her with his foot, and said, “Who was that man who was with you?” She said, “I saw no one, and I did not wake until you woke me.” He said to her, “Go back to your father.”

People began gossiping about her. Her father said to her, “My daughter! Tell me what happened. If the man is telling the truth about you, I will secretly have someone kill him, and the gossip about you will cease. But if he is lying, I will take him before one of the soothsayers of Yemen.” She swore by the oaths they used that he was lying. ʿUtba said to al-Fākih, “You have accused my daughter of a grave matter. Take me before one of the Arab soothsayers to settle this.” Al-Fākih set out with a group of Banū Makhzūm, and ʿUtba set out with a group of Banū ʿAbd Manāf. Hind and some women went with them. When they drew near the region, Hind’s manner changed. Her father said to her, “I can see the change in your manner, and that can only be because of something troubling you.” She replied, “No, by God, father! It is not because of anything troubling me, but I know you are going to a human being who can be wrong or right. I am not sure he will not brand me with a mark that will be a disgrace to me until the Day of Resurrection.”

He said to her, “I will test him before we consider your case.” He took a grain of wheat, put it into the opening of his horse’s penis, and fastened a leather thong over it. When they reached the soothsayer in the morning, he slaughtered an animal for them and treated them generously. After they sat down, ʿUtba said to him, “I have hidden something for you; tell me what it is.”

He said, “A fruit in a purse.” ʿUtba said, “I want a clearer answer.” He said, “A grain of wheat in the penis of a stallion.” ʿUtba said, “You are right. Now consider the matter of these women.” The soothsayer began approaching them one by one, striking each on the shoulder and saying, “Rise,” until he came near Hind. He struck her shoulder and said, “Rise: neither flat-bottomed nor adulterous. You will give birth to a king named Muʿāwiya.”

Al-Fākih rose, took her hand, and she wrenched her hand from his. “Away from me!” she said. “By God, I will see to it that this comes to pass by someone other than you!” Afterward, Abū Sufyān married her, and she bore Muʿāwiya. Abū Jaʿfar said: Abū al-Sikkīn al-Ṭāʾī told me, “Abū Bakr ibn ʿAyyāsh traveled from Kūfa to the desert country, where he met my father’s paternal uncle and asked him about this report.”

The account of Banū Sahm killing snakes

Muḥammad ibn Ḥabīb, from Hishām, from Ibn al-Kharrabūdh, said:

Banū Sahm ibn ʿAmr were the most powerful people of Mecca and the greatest in number. They had a rock by the mountain called Muslim. Whenever they wanted something, their herald would call, “O morning alarm!” They would say, “Morning has dawned upon the night.” Quraysh would say, “What do these ill-omened people want?” and regard them as a bad omen.

Among them were people called Banū al-Ghayṭala. Eminence and aggression were theirs. She was al-Ghaylaṭa, daughter of Mālik ibn al-Ḥārith, of Banū Kināna, then of Banū Shanūq ibn Murra. Qays ibn Saʿd ibn Sahm married her, and she bore him al-Ḥārith and Ḥudhāfa. Hostility and aggression were among them.

He said: One of their men killed a snake, and in the morning he was found dead on his bed. They were angered, so they went to every snake in that house and killed them. In the morning they were dead on their beds. They tracked the snakes through the valleys and ravines and killed them. When morning came, a number of them had died equal to the number of snakes they had killed. One of them cried out, “Come out to us, O company of jinn!” A voice from among the jinn called out:

(al-Khafīf)

O Sahm, you have killed a mighty one,

So we have struck you with a sweeping death.

O Sahm, you grew numerous and grew arrogant,

But death reaches everyone of high rank.

He said: So they desisted and stopped. Al-Kalbī said: It was concerning them that “Rivalry in piling up riches distracts you, until you visit the graves” (Qurʾān 102:1–2) was revealed. Ibn al-Kharrabūdh said: They began counting those of their people who had died during the days of the snakes. This was before the revelation, because a dispute had arisen between them and ʿAbd Manāf ibn Quṣayy. They said, “We outnumber you,” and began counting those of their people who had died from the snakes. Then this verse was revealed.

Thereafter, concerning them, through the words of the Prophet ﷺ.

The account of the aggression of Banū al-Sibāq against the people of Mecca

Abū Muḥammad al-Murhabī related, from a shaykh of Mecca, of Banū Jumḥ, from his elders, who said:

The first of Quraysh whom God destroyed in Mecca were Banū al-Sibāq ibn ʿAbd al-Dār. When their aggression had gone on for a long time, they heard a voice in the middle of the night on Abū Qubays, saying:

(al-Basīṭ)

Look at the Banū al-Sibāq: before long

They will be without a trace or a sign.

These are Iyād, who were a people of distinction,

Yet they were destroyed when they wronged Muḍar through aggression.

A year passed, then they perished. No trace or sign of them remained, save one man in Syria who had descendants.

The account of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib dyeing his hair with wasma

Al-Kalbī mentioned that the first person among the people of Mecca to dye his hair with wasma was ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. He had come to Yemen and stayed with one of its kings. The king looked at his grey hair and said:

“ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, would you like to change this whiteness and become a young man again?” He said, “That is up to you.” The king dyed his hair with henna, then applied wasma over it. When ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib was about to depart, the king gave him a great deal of it. As he approached Mecca, he dyed his hair and entered the city, his head and beard as black as a raven’s beak.

The raven. Natīla daughter of Janāb al-Namriyya, the mother of al-ʿAbbās, said, “Shaybat al-Ḥamd! How handsome this dye is, if only it would last!” ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib said:

(al-Ṭawīl)

Had this blackness lasted, I would have praised it;

It would have replaced youth that has passed away.

I enjoyed it, though life is short,

And death, Natīla, or old age is inevitable.

What benefit does a man gain from ease

And prosperity, if his throne collapses one day?

A swift, sudden death, with no lesser injury,

Is dearer to us than their saying, “His term is ended.”

The saying “his term is ended” means that his lifespan has run its course. It is said that a man has ḥakama when his lifespan has run its course and he has reached maturity. The people of Mecca dyed their hair after [hāʾ].5

The account of what took place between Quraysh and Kināna on the day of Dhū Nakīf

What prompted Quraysh to expel Banū Layth from Tihāma was that the people of Tihāma were struck by a year of famine. Banū Layth traveled until they settled in lower Tihāma, toward Yalamlam and Yemen. They had a neighbor from al-Qāra, named ʿAwwāf, who held a position of distinction and was an ally of Hishām ibn al-Mughīra and al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil. Balʿāʾ ibn Qays set out with his companions on a raid against some of the Arabs, leaving his brother Qatāda ibn Qays among those of his people who remained. One day Qatāda went around the dwellings of the tribe, whose households were close together. He saw camels grazing belonging to their neighbor, ʿAwwāf of al-Qāra, and considered raiding them because of the famine that had struck them. He consulted ʿUmayr ibn ʿĀmir ibn al-Malūḥ and Maʿbad ibn ʿĀmir ibn al-Malūḥ. They strongly rebuked him, saying, “Do not raid your neighbor. He has people who will be angered on his behalf and will protect him: Abū ʿUthmān Hishām ibn al-Mughīra and al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil, and others like them.” He fell silent and bowed his head like a snake. Then they parted. ʿUmayr ibn al-Malūḥ said to his brother Maʿbad, “Do you see how he bowed his head? He is very likely to attack the man.” He replied, “Then what we dislike will befall us.”

When evening came, Qatāda summoned a man of his people named such-and-such ibn Ṣudūf al-Laythī, and a man of Banū Zubayd who was their neighbor. He called them to raid the camels of the man from al-Qāra, and they agreed. The man from al-Qāra knew nothing until they came upon him, drove off his herd—thirty camels—and killed a young son of his who had come out to confront them. When they brought the camels to their dwelling, Qatāda ordered ten of them slaughtered and their meat distributed among the tribe. He divided the rest among his people, one or two camels to each. He sent some to ʿUmayr and Maʿbad, the sons of ʿĀmir ibn al-Malūḥ, but they refused to take any and censured his judgment, saying:

“What you have done will have a bad consequence.” He said, “What consequence could there be?”

ʿAwwāf went and entered the house of Hishām and al-ʿĀṣ. He told them what Qatāda had done to him and that his son had been killed. Hishām and al-ʿĀṣ sent word to ʿUmayr and Maʿbad, the sons of ʿĀmir ibn al-Malūḥ, about what Qatāda had done to their neighbor, and asked them to exact retaliation from Qatāda for the son of the man from al-Qāra and to return the value of the camels he had lost. They said, “Balʿāʾ is away. Do not press us until he returns.” Balʿāʾ soon returned. Hishām and al-ʿĀṣ sent him word, saying, “Hand Qatāda over to us, so that we may kill him for the son of the man from al-Qāra.” Balʿāʾ refused and would not yield.

Quraysh gathered to fight them, and on that day they rallied the Aḥābīsh. The Aḥābīsh were Banū al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Manāt ibn Kināna; al-Qāra, the descendants of al-Hawn ibn Khuzayma, namely ʿAḍl and al-Dayṣ, who are al-Qāra and all its clans; and Banū al-Muṣṭaliq of Khuzāʿa. They joined them because they were allies of Banū al-Ḥārith ibn Manāt.

When the armies met at Dhāt Nakīf, in the direction of Yalamlam, the commander of the people that day was al-Muṭṭalib ibn ʿAbd Manāf, with a thousand men from Banū ʿAbd Manāf and the Aḥābīsh. With Banū ʿAbd Manāf were their allies among Quraysh. The commander of the Aḥābīsh was Ḥuṭmuṭ ibn Saʿd, one of Banū al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Manāt, along with Abū Ḥāritha and al-Ḥubaysh ibn ʿAmr, the two leaders of Banū al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Manāt. Among Banū Bakr were Balʿāʾ ibn Qays and his brothers Juthāma, Ḥumayṣa, and Qatāda, sons of Qays. They outnumbered Quraysh.

When they met, they fought fiercely. As they faced each other in battle, Balʿāʾ said to his people, “Shoot at them. When the arrows are spent, draw your swords”—a ruse against the opposing people. Al-Qāra, who were archers, said, “Al-Qāra has met its match in archery,” and the saying became a proverb. The people fought fiercely that day, and al-Muṭṭalib began urging Al-Muṭṭalib ibn ʿAbd Manāf urged his people on, while Ḥaṭmaṭ encouraged his companions. They smashed the sword scabbards, and Banū Bakr were routed. As they fled, they were slaughtered in a terrible massacre. Muṭʿim ibn ʿAdī was that day pursuing them with his sword drawn, saying, “Leave none of them breathing; cut off their strength!” Ḥarb ibn Umayya urged his companions on, saying, “Show them no mercy.” That day Quraysh slaughtered Banū Bakr so terribly that they entered the Sacred Precinct seeking its protection, and Quraysh drove Banū Bakr out.

That day ʿUbayd ibn al-Saffāḥ ibn al-Ḥuwayrith, the brother of al-Qārah, fought Qatādah ibn Qays, the brother of Balʿāʾ. ʿUbayd stabbed him with a wound that left him on the verge of death; he did not die until the people had dispersed after their battle, and he died afterward. A woman from Banū Bakr said: (kāmil)

Banū Bakr bit their father’s penison the day of battle, in disgrace,when every man with curled hair and a lock fled,from every helpless, lean hyena …

A man from Banū Shujaʿ named Aswad was killed along with Qatādah, as was a man from Banū Jundaʿ named Hilāl. Then Quraysh and the Aḥābīsh joined forces and drove Banū Layth out of Tihāmah. Banū Layth travelled until they settled among Banū Jaʿfar and entered into an alliance with Ṭufayl ibn Mālik ibn Jaʿfar. He said to them, “I have allied with you, and I will protect you from whoever seeks to harm you, though you are a fierce people.” So they approached them with the request that they not raise their hands against them. They said, “That is enough for us.” Banū Layth remained among Banū ʿĀmir for three years. Then a man from Banū Abī Bakr ibn Kilāb attacked a camel belonging to Balʿāʾ and stole it.

Ṭufayl rode out after him and found that the camel had been slaughtered, so he compensated him with two camels in its place. Then Ṭufayl became afraid of them and feared that trouble would arise between them and his people. He wanted to make his position clear to them and renounce his pact and protection of them. This was in the Sacred Precinct, so he wanted the sacred months to pass. Laylā daughter of Ṭufayl sent word to Balʿāʾ, telling him what her father intended to do to them. Balʿāʾ told his companions, and they agreed on a plan: they would wait, and when only one night of the month remained, they would send their women, baggage, and livestock toward Tihāmah, while the men stayed in the settlement. Then, when evening came and darkness had fallen, they would raid them. They did this when the month ended, then raided Banū Jaʿfar and Banū Hilāl that very night. They killed some of them, seized livestock, and turned back toward Tihāmah. Ṭufayl said, “Let no one pursue them,” and no one did. Balʿāʾ ibn Qays said of this: (wāfir)

Abū Laylā Ṭufayl threatens meand sends me fine words with the she-camels.

You threaten me while you are in the heart of Najd;I fear neither Najd nor Tihāmah.

We trampled your Najd until we leftthe rugged heights of Najd, which we took for soot.

The Account of the Day of al-Mushallal

He said: When Banū Layth came to al-Mushallal on their return from Najd, after doing to Banū ʿĀmir what they had done, Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah and al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil wanted to march against them with a force of Quraysh and those they had gathered from among the Aḥābīsh. Then Hishām and al-ʿĀṣ said to the leading men of Quraysh …

“Come with us to Abū Uḥayḥah, Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ.” Men from Banū ʿAbd Manāf went with them, including ʿUtbah and Shaybah, the sons of Rabīʿah; al-Muṭṭalib ibn al-Asad; Abū Ḥudhayfah ibn al-Mughīrah; Abū Umayyah ibn al-Mughīrah; and Nubah and Munabbih, the sons of al-Ḥajjāj. They told him that Banū Layth had settled at al-Mushallal and that they had resolved to march against them. They asked him to march with them, bringing Banū ʿAbd Shams. Abū Uḥayḥah said, “You know that Banū Layth are my maternal uncles, and I would be ashamed for the Arabs to say that I marched against them to fight them. I will not march with you, nor will anyone from Banū ʿAbd Shams.” Then Saʿīd said to Hishām, al-ʿĀṣ, and the Quraysh who were with them:

“You want to undertake a campaign that the Arabs will talk about tomorrow: you will go against a people who have been driven out and expelled from Najd, and then you want to drive them out of Tihāmah too. Where are they to go?”

Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah said, “Wherever they wish, provided they do not live beside us, after what they have done.”

Saʿīd said, “War shifts back and forth, and victory alternates between sides. I do not feel secure that they might gain the upper hand over you, bringing disgrace upon you. Which of you will take charge of carrying the banner when swords clash among the men, and not waver or grow faint and weak? A people’s ruin lies in their banner.”

The men were intimidated by what he said and fell silent. Al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil said, “I will take charge of carrying it.”

Saʿīd said, “And will you swear by Isāf that you will not flee?”

He said, “Yes.” They handed it to al-ʿĀṣ, and he carried it. Then he went to Isāf and swore there that he would not flee or would die. He then marched against Banū Layth with a force of …

Kinanah and the Aḥābīsh: ʿAḍl, al-Dayš, and al-Qārah. When the two sides met and looked at one another, al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil called out to them, “Stand firm! There is no way for you to escape!” They fought fiercely. Among Banū Saʿd ibn Layth was a young man named Khālid ibn Mālik, who was a boon companion of Balʿāʾ ibn Qays. Khālid ibn Mālik had fled on the day of Fakh, when Banū ʿĀmir raided them. Balʿāʾ had sworn not to speak to him until he lived to see a day on which his conduct would make amends. Khālid ibn Mālik charged al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil, stabbed him, and brought him down; then he took the banner from his hand. When Quraysh saw that the banner had been taken and their man brought down, Quraysh, the force of Banū Kinanah, and the Aḥābīsh fled. Banū Layth inflicted on them whatever they wished.

When Abū Uḥayḥah heard what al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil had done, he said, “What a disgrace! His people did not defend him; they fled from the banner and did not return to carry it.” Saʿīd said, “This is what I feared for you, and I told you that war shifts back and forth, and victory alternates between sides. You refused to accept my advice. How shameful it would have been if I had been there with you and then fled, trying to get into my house!”

Qudāmah ibn Qays al-Zubaydī, Balʿāʾ’s ally, spoke of what he had done against Banū ʿĀmir and what he had done against Quraysh. The beginning of his alliance with Balʿāʾ was that Balʿāʾ gambled with Qudāmah using arrows and won all his property. Qudāmah asked Balʿāʾ to gamble for his hand and fifty camels. Balʿāʾ played with him and won his hand. Balʿāʾ wanted to cut it off, but Qudāmah said to him, “Balʿāʾ, would you accept something better for you than cutting it off? Lend it to me on the condition that I never leave you, and that whenever a calamity befalls you in which lives are at stake, I will protect you with my own life. You are a man who often fights other men.”

Balʿāʾ accepted this and left the hand with him on loan, with the right to take it back whenever he wished. Qudāmah stayed with Balʿāʾ and never left his side, wherever he was. On the day of al-Mushallal, Balʿāʾ saw Qudāmah standing beside him and said, “Either return the hand I lent you, or charge the enemy and bring me a ransom for it.” So Qudāmah charged …

and did not return until he had killed some of them and taken a prisoner. This is what Qudāmah said to Balʿāʾ:

(basīṭ)

He scorned the wrong when wrong was imposed on him,and charged with the horses, their forelocks bound,

after he had made a fierce assault on Jaʿfar …they emerged through the dust, their forelocks reddened,

until they exacted from their enemy what he had pledged,crushing those at the rear after those at the front.

This Is the Day of Badr

He said: Then they turned back and travelled until they reached the water of Badr, where they divided what they had seized. As for Banū Layth, they departed without remaining at the water. Banū al-Dayl, however, remained. A clan of Ḥakam went out to pursue their property and caught up with Banū al-Dayl at the water of Badr. They recovered what was in their hands and killed three men from among them.

On the day of al-Mushallal, Ḥakam travelled with its rear guard. Balʿāʾ ibn Qays was informed of them, so he sent his brother Jathāmah with horsemen from Banū Layth to pursue them. They caught up with them and fought for a while. Then Ḥakam asked Jathāmah to grant them protection and take them to Balʿāʾ. He did so. When he brought them to Balʿāʾ, Abū Laqīṭ ibn Ṣakhr stood up and asked him to give them to him, so that he could kill them for the men they had killed from Banū al-Dayl. Balʿāʾ gave them to him.

Then ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn al-Bayyāʿ al-Laythī arrived and stayed with his sister’s son, Abū Uḥayḥah Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah. While ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā was sitting with Saʿīd ibn …

al-ʿĀṣ at the door of his house, al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil passed by, along with ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā and Ḥabīb, the sons of ʿAbd Shams. It seems there was a bond of brotherhood between ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn al-Bayyāʿ and al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil and ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā and Ḥabīb, the sons of ʿAbd Shams. ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā had instructed his son ʿAmr to meet al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil, ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā, and Ḥabīb, the sons of ʿAbd Shams, because of the bond of brotherhood between him and them. When they saw ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā sitting with Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ, they saw a handsome young man. They said, “Abū Uḥayḥah, who is this young man with you? We do not know him.”

He said, “He is a young man who claims to be the most powerful man in Tihāmah: this is ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn al-Bayyāʿ, whose father al-Bayyāʿ was named ʿAbd Shams.”

They said, “By your father, he is your maternal uncle!”

At that, the young man ʿAmr said, “The people of Tihāmah knew I was the most powerful among them before Saʿīd was born. The people of Tihāmah have acknowledged this to us and submitted to us.”

They were so angry that the anger showed on their faces, and Abū Uḥayḥah feared that trouble would break out. He said to al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil and ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn ʿAbd Shams, “ʿAmr’s father was once a friend to you.”

They said, “Yes, that was so, but hearts change, and that will soon be undone by the churlish one. Tell your father, when you return to him:

We have repudiated the bond of brotherhoodthat once joined us to him.”

The young man said, “Who are you, and to whom am I to deliver this message?” They gave him their genealogies and names. He said, “I will do so.”

When evening came, Abū Uḥayḥah feared that ʿAmr would be killed. He put him on a camel, then rode with him until he brought him safely to his destination. When ʿAmr reached his father, his father asked him about Saʿīd: “How did you find his kindness?” He also asked him about al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil and about ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā and Ḥabīb, the sons of ʿAbd Shams. ʿAmr told him the whole story: what had happened between him and them, and that …

he had seen no one among them like Saʿīd for forbearance and nobility. All of this took place during the sacred month.

When evening came, ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā gathered horsemen from Banū Layth, told them what had been said to him, and asked them to follow him on a raid in the heart of Mecca. They refused and said, “Woe to you! In the sacred month, and in the Sacred Precinct!” They rebuked him severely. He said, “By God, if you do not follow me, I will kill myself.” When they saw that, they went with him until they reached Mecca at night. He asked about al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil and about ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā and Ḥabīb, the sons of ʿAbd Shams. He was told, “They are with a group of Quraysh, talking at Ajyād.” They went toward them, and the group had no warning before they were raided. They killed two men from Banū ʿAbd Shams: al-Rabīʿ and ʿAmr. Al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil and his two companions, ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā and Ḥabīb, the sons of ʿAbd Shams, escaped with the rest of the group and made it to their homes.

Quraysh took this hard, and Banū ʿAbd Shams were angry with Abū Uḥayḥah. They said, “You knew that the young man intended to raid us. Why did you not warn us, so we could prepare for battle, before they came upon us unarmed, in the midst of our assembly, at our meeting place?”

He said, “I knew nothing of this, and what they did was against my wishes”—that is, it displeased him. They stayed there for some time.

Then ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ became fiercely angry on account of his father. He was a young man, and he rode out with horsemen from Quraysh to seek vengeance against Banū Saʿd ibn Layth. He met two men from Banū Saʿd ibn Layth, greeted them, and asked, “Which people are you from?” He wanted to use their answer to find Banū Saʿd. They said, “We are Saʿdīs.” He said, “I need not seek a trace when I have the thing itself.” He brought them forward and struck off their heads. Then he returned to Mecca. The two men were named Saʿd and ʿAmr.

The Account of the Day of Fakh

Then Banū Layth rode out in force to pursue al-ʿĀṣ. When Quraysh heard of their march, they went out to meet them and encountered them at Fakh. They fought, but neither side killed anyone; there were only wounds between them.

Then Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ and ʿAffān ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ rode out with a group of Quraysh elders. They kept working between the two sides until they were reconciled and appointed Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ as arbiter, accepting his judgment. He ruled that the dead should be counted, and set them as equivalent in retaliation, one side for the other. He paid from his own personal property the compensation for all the wounds. The people accepted Saʿīd’s judgment. The dead from Quraysh were two men from Banū ʿAbd Shams, al-Rabīʿ and ʿAmr; the dead from Banū Layth were two men. The blood compensation for the wounds on both sides came to one thousand three hundred camels, which Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ paid from his own property.

Then Came the Battle between Muḥārib ibn Fihr and Banū Ḍamrah

He said: The cause of the battle between Banū Ḍamrah ibn Bakr and Muḥārib ibn Fihr was this. It began when a man from Banū Ḍamrah named Masʿūd came with his camels, intending to water them. He brought them to a basin belonging to Abū ʿUthmān al-Muḥāribī. Abū ʿUthmān had plastered his basin with mud and was waiting for his camels to come to it. The Ḍamrī arrived with his camels, led them to the basin, and watered them. When Abū ʿUthmān saw what the Ḍamrī had done, he ordered that the man be seized. The man fled and evaded them.

He fled when he saw the danger, for he could not be caught. The Fahrite ordered that the camels be kept at the watering place until midday. Those that gave milk were milked, while the camels began to strain toward the watering trough and cry out. Abū ʿUthmān al-Fihrī said, “Whoever wants a share of the plunder may plunder the Ḍamrī’s camels; he has exposed them to plunder.” So they were plundered.

The Ḍamrī was waiting for his camels nearby, where he thought they would pass him when they returned from the water. When they were delayed, he looked out and saw that the camels had been plundered. He hurried toward them, calling on his people to help him against Abū ʿUthmān al-Fihrī, though they were close by. He found the encampment deserted, and found no one there except ʿAmr ibn Khālid. The two of them set out together and reached the tents of Banū Muḥārib ibn Fihr. There, with a young man from the tribe, they came upon a she-camel from their herd. When Abū ʿUthmān saw them, he hurried toward them. As he drew near, a stone lay in his path; as he ran, he struck his thumb against it and split his nail. He picked up the stone and threw it at ʿAmr ibn Khālid, striking him in the forehead and splitting it open. ʿAmr turned back, wounded, having achieved none of what he had set out to do. Abū ʿUthmān al-Fihrī said of this: (al-wāfir)

We denied Ḍamrah water on the day they came,

to make their drinking from Fihr’s trough.

When ʿAmr ibn Khālid returned to his people, wounded, and the camels had been plundered, he gathered his people and raided Banū Muḥārib. He took as much of their livestock as had been taken from his own, and killed three men: al-Ḥakam and Murrah ibn al-Ḥakam, who were the sons of Abū ʿUthmān’s brother, and a neighbor of theirs from Yemen named Rabīʿah. He also took weapons and horses from them.

This distressed Abū ʿUthmān and his companions. Abū ʿUthmān then gathered a large force and raided Banū Ḍamrah. He killed four men, wounded twenty, and took livestock, horses, and weapons. Then he returned to his people. His wife, who was a Kinanī woman, said to him, “By the Lord of the two sacred landmarks! Kinānah will not leave you alone; they will raid you.”

He said, “They will not do so.” ʿAmr ibn Khālid raided Banū Muḥārib ibn Fihr and found that Abū ʿUthmān had fortified himself against him. He killed one man and took no property, then returned. This was the last war between Quraysh and Kinānah, over a son of Ḥafṣ ibn al-Akhayf. This will be related later.

The account of the oath ordeal

As they related, the reason for the account of the oath ordeal was this: Khudāsh ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Qays ibn ʿAbd Wadd ibn Naṣr ibn Mālik ibn Ḥisl ibn ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy had set out for Yemen as a merchant. With him were his companion and hired man, ʿĀmir ibn ʿAlqamah ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn ʿAbd Manāf, a young lad. On the road they met a party of travelers and asked them for a rope for one of their needs. ʿĀmir ibn ʿAlqamah tossed them a rope that belonged to Khudāsh ibn ʿAbd Allāh, and they went off with it.

Khudāsh, who was an experienced old man, said to ʿĀmir, “You gave them my rope without my permission.” They argued until they had exchanged some sharp words. Khudāsh raised the staff in his hand and struck ʿĀmir ibn ʿAlqamah with it, splitting his head open. Some say it struck his kidney. ʿĀmir fell ill from the blow until he feared for his life. He passed a group of Arabs, told them his lineage, and informed them that Khudāsh ibn ʿAbd Allāh had dealt him this blow. “I think it will kill me,” he said. “If I die and do not return to you, tell my people among Banū ʿAbd Manāf what happened and inform them of my affair. If I live, I will come to you and tell you myself.” Before long, he died from it.

Khudāsh arrived and asked about him. He was told, “His appointed fate overtook him,” and they believed him, suspecting nothing else. They remained there until the Arabs’ pilgrims arrived for the season. The members of that group whom ʿĀmir had entrusted with his message came and asked where Banū ʿAbd Manāf were gathered. They were directed to them, came to them, and told them what had happened to ʿĀmir—while Khudāsh was circumambulating the House, unaware of what had occurred.

The men of Banū ʿAbd Manāf went to the portico of Zamzam, removed its columns, and went after Khudāsh as he circumambulated the House. They beat him with them until he was nearly dead. The people cried, “For God’s sake! For God’s sake, Banū ʿAbd Manāf!” Khudāsh said, “For God’s sake! What have I done to you?” They replied, “You killed our companion.”

“By God, I did not kill him!” When he said this, they stopped and dealt fairly with him. Eventually, the matter came to this: it was proposed that Khudāsh should have fifty men of Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy swear that he was innocent of ʿĀmir’s death, and that they would then pay you his blood-price. Banū ʿAbd Manāf agreed to this.

When men of Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy came forward to swear at the Kaʿbah, among them Ḥuwayṭib ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Abī Qays, his mother came, took him by the hand, and said, “By God, you will not swear with them today in this matter.” She led him away. They put another man in his place, and swore at the Corner that Khudāsh was innocent of ʿĀmir’s death. Then they paid the blood-price. Before a year had passed, not one of the men who had sworn remained alive. Most of their estates became the inheritance of Ḥuwayṭib ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā, and the men died. For this reason, Ḥuwayṭib became the owner of the greatest number of estates in Makkah, and the wealthiest among them. Abū Ṭālib said this to Khudāsh ibn ʿAbd Allāh: (al-ṭawīl)

Was it for the sake of a rope, you wretch, that a blow was struckwith a staff—then one rope brought ropes in its wake?

Come to the judgment of Ibn Ṣakhrah;he will judge between us and then be just.

As he used to judge in matters that arose for us,he would take up the weighty matter and settle it.

The account of Quraysh’s introduction of ḥamasiyyah

He said: Quraysh devised the practice of ḥums as an opinion they had formed and discussed among themselves. They said, “We are the descendants of Abraham, the people of the Sacred Precinct, the guardians of the House, and the inhabitants and residents of Makkah. No other Arabs have a right like ours or a standing like ours, and the Arabs acknowledge no one as they acknowledge us. So do not magnify anything in the permitted territory as you magnify the Sacred Precinct, for if you do, the Arabs will regard your sanctity lightly and say, ‘They have magnified the permitted territory just as they magnify the Sacred Precinct.’”

So they abandoned standing at ʿArafah and departing from it, even though they knew and acknowledged that it was one of the sacred sites and part of the religion of Abraham ﵇. They held that the rest of the Arabs should stand there and depart from there. But they said, “We are the people of the Sacred Precinct, so we should neither leave the sacred territory nor magnify anything besides it, just as we magnify it. We are the ḥums, and the ḥums are the people of the Sacred Precinct.”

They then granted those among the Arabs whom they had given birth to, whether residents of the permitted territory or the Sacred Precinct, the same standing as themselves: because of their birth among them: whatever was permitted to them was permitted to those people, and whatever was forbidden to them was forbidden to those people. Kinānah, Khuzāʿah, and Banū ʿĀmir ibn Ṣaʿṣaʿah all joined them in this, except Bakr ibn ʿAbd Manāh.

Then they introduced practices that had not existed before. They said, “It is not proper for the ḥums to make aqṭ or to clarify ghee while in consecration, nor to enter houses made of hair-cloth, nor to take shelter—if they do take shelter—except in leather tents while consecrated.” Then they went further in this and said, “It is not proper for people of the permitted territory to eat, in the Sacred Precinct, food they brought with them from the permitted territory when they come as pilgrims or visitors, nor to circumambulate the House on their first circumambulation unless they are wearing the clothes of the ḥums. If they cannot find any, they must circumambulate naked. If a man or woman among them is scrupulous and cannot find ḥums clothing, but circumambulates in the clothes they brought from the permitted territory, they must throw those clothes away when they have finished circumambulating. They may never use or touch them again, nor may anyone else.”

The Arabs called such clothes “cast-off garments.” They imposed these practices on the Arabs, who adopted them. The Arabs stood at ʿArafāt, departed from it, circumambulated the House naked, and followed what Quraysh had prescribed for them. Thus, when people of the permitted territory came as pilgrims or visitors, they would put down the provisions they had brought with them on entering the Sacred Precinct, buy food from within the Sacred Precinct, and seek out clothes belonging to the ḥums, either on loan or for rent.
