They would circumambulate wearing these. If they could not find any, they would circumambulate naked. The men circumambulated naked, while the women each removed all her clothes except a shift, and circumambulated wearing it. A woman of the Arabs, the daughter of al-Aṣhab al-Khathʿamiyyah, said as she circumambulated the House: (al-rajaz)

Today some of it—or all of it—will be seen;

whatever of it is seen, I will not make lawful.

Those among them who circumambulated in the clothes they had brought from the permitted territory threw them away afterward, and neither they nor anyone else could use them. One poet spoke of something he had left behind, though he loved it, and would not go near it: (al-ṭawīl)

Enough grief: a garment, though it was dear to him,like a forbidden cast-off among the circumambulators.

[It was—] a discarded garment from among the clothes of the people of the permitted territory. By his words, “[I left] that,” he meant, “I left it just as I left the clothes of the permitted territory.”

The account of Asad Shanuʾah and Banū ʿAdī, from al-Wāqidī: the day of Nakhla

He said: Asad Shanuʾah had killed a man from ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb. Every Qurashī clan had a leader who took charge of its affairs and sought vengeance for its blood, except ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb. When Asad killed that man, ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, then a young lad, still in the first years of his youth, went to ʿUtbah ibn Rabīʿah ibn ʿAbd Shams, then the elder of Banū ʿAbd Manāf and the elder of Quraysh. He spoke to him and said, “If you abandon us, our blood will remain unavenged among Asad.” ʿUtbah said, “We will not wrong you or forsake you, but we will stand with you until you obtain redress and take your revenge.”

ʿUtbah ibn Rabīʿah stood among Quraysh and said, “People of Quraysh! By God, if you fail to support one of your own in a matter like this, the Arabs will keep taking a man from among you and carrying him away.” Quraysh stood with him. He set out with those who followed him, and Banū ʿAdī went with them; among them were ʿUmar and Zayd, the young sons of al-Khaṭṭāb. Asad gathered its forces against them. They met at Nakhla and fought fiercely until wounds spread through both tribes. Then the people called for peace; Asad paid the blood-price for the man, and the two sides parted.

The account of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb and ʿUmārah ibn al-Walīd, from al-Wāqidī

He said: ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb went with ʿUmārah ibn al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah as a hired man to Syria or Yemen. ʿUmārah was an arrogant, ostentatious man. Before this, he had set out with an Arab man called Ṣabāḥ, tormented him, and left him on the road.

When they stopped at a place on the road on a hot day, ʿUmārah said to ʿUmar, “Prepare me some food.” ʿUmar slaughtered a sheep for him and cooked it. Then he crumbled bread into a dish, poured the broth and meat over it, and brought it to him. ʿUmārah found fault with him, intending to mock him. ʿUmar was a resolute man, and ʿUmārah was one of his maternal uncles: ʿUmar’s mother, Ḥantamah, was the daughter of Hāshim ibn al-Mughīrah.

“Do you feed me hot fat on hot bread on a hot day? You mean to kill me!” ʿUmārah stood up to strike him. ʿUmar drew his sword. When ʿUmārah saw that he was in earnest and realized that ʿUmar would strike him with his sword, he ran until he escaped him. ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb said: (al-rajaz)

By God, were it not for a measure of generosity,

and standing among the clan through uncle and father, evil would have joined me to the generous, the magnanimous, and Ṣabāḥ would have been cast beside the boundary marker,

though he had done no wrong and committed no injustice,

for mixing bread with the fat of sheep.

The account of Ibn al-Ḥafṣ ibn al-Akhayf, from al-Wāqidī

He said: A son of Ḥafṣ ibn al-Akhayf, one of Banū Muʿayṣ ibn ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy, went to Ḍajnan. At the time, it was the encampment of Banū Bakr ibn Kinānah. The chief of Banū Bakr there was ʿĀmir ibn Yazīd ibn ʿĀmir ibn al-Malūḥ. He was looking for a stray animal of his. The son of Ḥafṣ was a neat, handsome young lad, with a forelock, wearing a khurāqānī robe. He passed by ʿĀmir ibn Yazīd, who was looking for that stray animal, while ʿAmr ibn Yazīd sat among his people. ʿĀmir admired the lad’s elegance and said, “Who are you, lad?” He replied, “I am the son of Ḥafṣ ibn al-Akhayf, the Qurashī.”

When the lad had gone, ʿĀmir ibn Yazīd said, “People of Banū Bakr, have you no blood claim against Quraysh?” They replied, “Indeed, by God, we have blood claims against them.” He said, “Any man who kills this lad for one of our dead would have exacted his blood in full.” A man of Banū Bakr then rose toward the lad. He had a blood-feud among Quraysh, so he killed him. When Quraysh heard of this, they spoke about him, and ʿĀmir ibn Yazīd rode to them and said, “O assembly of Quraysh! There have been blood-feuds between us and you, which we have let pass. Then this young man was killed in connection with one of them. If you wish us to pay you blood-money and for you to pay us blood-money, we shall do so; otherwise, it is blood for blood.” A man of Quraysh, to whom the blood of that young man seemed of little account, said, “ʿĀmir speaks the truth: blood for blood.” So they disregarded the matter, did not pursue it, and left it alone.

One day, while ʿĀmir ibn Yazīd ibn al-Mallūḥ was riding on an errand at Marr al-Ẓahrān, he encountered Makraz ibn Ḥafṣ ibn al-Akhīf, the young man’s brother. Makraz recognized him, dismounted beside him—ʿĀmir ibn Yazīd had his sword on him—and struck him with the sword until he killed him. Then he took ʿĀmir’s sword, which had been hanging from his neck, and plunged it into his belly. At night he brought it and hung it from the coverings of the Kaʿba. When the people awoke, Quraysh saw ʿĀmir’s sword, recognized it, and said, “By God, this is ʿĀmir’s sword. Makraz ibn Ḥafṣ killed him.”

The account of the Day of Shahūra

This is the account of the Day of Shahūra, one of the greatest days of Banū Kināna. Quraysh went out from Mecca, led by Makraz ibn Ḥafṣ ibn al-Akhīf, a brother of Banū Muʿayṣ, accompanied by Banū al-Dayl and Layth, the two sons of Bakr. He raided the territory of Balī and Lakham, took much booty, and then— He continued on until, at the lower end of Yanbuʿ, he came upon women of Juhayna who were living as neighbors among a clan of Banū Ḍamra called Banū ʿAbbād. Their spokesman recited: (rajaz)

The women who are neighbors of Banū ʿAbbādHave become captives, stumbling in their fetters;The property of Banū Ḍamra has come to ruin.

He said: The army brought them to Dhāt al-Salīm and encamped among Banū Ṣakhr. The news had reached Banū Ṣakhr, who were at the lower end of Yalīl. In the evening, Banū Ṣakhr took them under their protection and asked for the women, but the army refused. They held them there for the night; there was no fighting between them. Banū Ṣakhr sought reinforcements from the surrounding clans of Ḍamra. At daybreak, the army set out. Makraz ibn Ḥafṣ intended to send the women back, when one of the sons of Abū Rāfiʿ—brothers of al-Barrāḍ—charged at Makraz and struck the rear of the mule beneath him with his sword. The mule threw Makraz, and one of his companions turned back and took him up behind him. He caught up with his companions and said: (ṭawīl)

Kaʿb ibn Ḍamra knew, when their swordsWere dyed, hands and foreheads,That, in hardship, I gave Makraz support …And brought the coward’s soul to its choking end.

I urged my two legs to race toward them;We would all die together, or return and catch them.

They say, “Leave him; death has come before him.”I said, “Today I refuse to let us part.”

Then Banū Fihr, Layth, and al-Dayl turned back and shot arrows at Banū Ḍamra. Ḍamra stood exposed— One of Banū Ḍamra was killed: ʿUbayd ibn Ḥudhayfa ibn Ṣakhr ibn Kaʿb ibn Khard was struck by an arrow, and Kulthūm ibn Maʿbad ibn Ṣakhr bled heavily. Ḍamra was routed. Hubayb ibn Maʿbad ibn Ṣakhr turned back toward the slain and wounded. Kulthūm said to him, “Call out!” He cried, “O Banū Ḍamra!” The reply came, “Stop, may God deprive you of your father!” He cried, “O Banū Kaʿb!” The same reply came. He cried, “O Banū Jābir!” Again the same reply. He cried, “O Banū Khard ibn Jābir!” They said, “Call now; call the men by name and the women by their nicknames!” Al-Ḥārith ibn Qays ibn Kaʿb ibn Khard, who belonged to al-Ḥaraqiyya—his mother was one of the women of al-Ḥaraqiyya—and Qays ibn Khālid ibn Mālik ibn Khard turned back. Then Ḍamra turned back too.

A man of Banū Qays ibn Jaddī said, “O Ḥārith, Maʿbad’s two sons are not yours, and the stone idols are yours to leave behind!” Qays said, “May his mother bite her clitoris, whoever does not fight when Ḍamra comes upon him!” He charged the enemy. Qurayshī Sharīk ibn Bishr met him. Qays ibn Khālid ibn Mālik struck Sharīk but did no harm; Sharīk struck Qays and sliced the skin of his head until it fell over his face. Qays then sprang up, seized Sharīk, lifted him, and threw him down. Farwa ibn Hubayb arrived; he was Qays’s sister’s son, his mother being ʿAfraʾ bint Khālid. He lifted Sharīk’s helmet and cut his throat. Then Sharīk’s brother came seeking vengeance for him. Qays lifted him and threw him down, and Farwa came again and killed him. Banū Ḍamra killed seven of them.

When the fighters became entangled, al-Dayl and Layth withdrew. Nawfal al-Daylī, who was [from] the household of Banū al-Dayl, cried, “O Banū Bakr! Bakr! Protect us!” He left Ḍamra to face Fihr. When Banū Fihr were routed, al-Dayl and Layth set out, fearing further fighting. Nawfal made his way—

…to Banū ʿAwf ibn Jaddī at a watering place on Yalīl. They barred his way and put him on a camel. Khārija ibn Khushāf al-Ḍamrī said: (ṭawīl)

May a people who yesterday denied Nawfal their helpBe bereft, as the water-carrying camels wentWith their heavy water-skins!

Alas for me—and grief is wasted—For Nawfal among them, and Nawfal’s companions.

Al-Ḥārith ibn Qays said: (kāmil)

I set out for Kulthūm and his companion,With a broad bow, firm at both curved ends, and powerful.

A gleaming sword, like a pool and rain,Polished by the sword-polishers, adorned with its marks.

I avenged myself upon their chiefsAnd removed the rancor from my breast—

Though they swore that they would leave the two of them,And I swore by the stone idols and the curtain.

I left him to Jaljal’s spears, whenThe sword-blades cut, glowing like embers.

I shall surely make their foremost men know it:I shall aim my arrows, and turn aside from Banū Bakr.

They were the steadfast friends on their rough mounts,They stood against us when the hour of endurance came.

A bold warrior, his incisors showing,I laid him low with a well-made arrow, keen-pointed.

I left him as a dwelling for the hyenas,Where songstresses’ ways are learned, their noses wrinkling.

I neither planned it nor knewThat the day they fought would be my doing.

I left him, his blood cooking within him,Like saffron in the place where the wound was made.

Then your man came to us at midday,Seeking aid, hastening with his band.

You saw your women, distraught,Fearing the lances and the force of the rebuke.

They met you with an army from Najd,Rough, with green spearheads.

Fihr made its way until, that day, they were at al-Farʿ, on the slope of Harshāʾ. There they encountered Mukhlid ibn Ḥudhayfa ibn Ṣakhr, brother of the slain man, and killed him. Then they went on until they found, at a watering place—

…a man of Banū Malḥa ibn Jaddī at a watering place called Dhū al-Asala, near Waddān. They killed him and returned with three captives, leaving them four men in excess. Ḍamra set out and encamped with them in the sacred territory, fearing that Fihr might seize them in the unrestricted territory and drive them into the sanctuary.

Banū Fihr had killed a daughter of Imāʾ ibn Raḥḍa al-Ghifārī, named Fāṭima. Banū Ṣakhr sought a grant of her blood, and obtained blood-money for her. They set out three marked spaces and said, “Whoever stands in one owes a young she-camel; whoever stands in two owes two. Whoever crosses all three owes three.” A young woman married to a man of Banū Ḍamra crossed all three. Her husband tried to hold her back, but her brother said, “By God, either you let go of her hand or your right hand will part from you!” He let her go. Ḍamra gave them three hundred she-camels.

On the day the Fihri killed Imāʾ ibn Raḥḍa al-Ghifārī’s daughter, he said: (rajaz)

A long day, among the blades of the swordsmen …And I have grown weary of life’s length.

Abū Julādhiyya ibn Sufyān said of the Day of Shahūra: (ṭawīl)

I spared Banū al-Jadhʿāʾ the scene of the battle,And gave them praise and a place in the story instead.

I am their uncle, Ḥarb, and I strive against them,As it pleased them that I should, though I stood alone.

When Khard laid a hand upon me in misfortune,I placed Banū al-Jadhʿāʾ at his side to help him.

I said to Khard’s men, “Stand ready! IfThis is your day, then tomorrow is ours.”

We left Banū Fihr’s women widowed,Their children orphaned, their fugitives driven off.

They bring their horses toward us, and whoever leadsThem to us, we leave him with no rein to hold.

He also said of that day: (rajaz)

They call for Khard, and I answer for them:Your concern is enough for me; what concerns her concerns me.

Al-Ḥārith ibn Qays, brother of Banū Kaʿb ibn Khard, was wounded. His wife was tending him and laughing at his fear. He said: (ṭawīl)

Had you seen the companions of Qays ibn KhālidAnd the lions, Zaynab, you would not laugh at wounds.

But she was away, and her people embalmed me …Saffron and zar­nab were strewn over me.

May my mother and father be ransom for thoseWhom I summon to face death, exposedAt the lower end of Dhū Waddān.

We turned them back, though, had we wished, our lancesWould have reached Usayd ibn Jaḥsh, who was guilty among the people.

Yet we spared them when we had power over them,Though we were resentful; and the guilty man reproaches.

Nawfal’s raid will be recounted among the campaignsWhen the people of the two mountain passes gather at al-Muḥaṣṣab.

Enough for you that you lost noble men among your dead:Scrawny camels, the offspring of Fihr’s tusks, and young camel calves.

I said to my people, “Strike, may you have no father!The last of our affection is disappearing.”

When we struck, the blows shook the chestsOf our grief; they could scarcely turn aside.

Among us, where ibn Maʿbad fell, stoodWarriors in battle, every one girded up.

We called Banū Bakr to make peace between us;For us, peace with Bakr is a bitter brew.

We fought them with spears day and night,Though a man’s day of good judgment may be hidden.

The account of al-Qurayya, from al-Kalbī

He said: Maʿrūf ibn al-Kharbūdh told me: The story of al-Qurayya, a watering place belonging to Hudhayl in the vicinity of al-Rajīʿ, was that Ḥarb ibn Umayya ibn ʿAbd Shams and Mirdās ibn Abī ʿĀmir al-Sulamī bought it from Khuwaylid ibn Wāthila ibn Maṭḥal al-Hudhalī. Mirdās said: (basīṭ)

I chose Ḥarb and his brothers for it,So that it might be said: “Mirdās transferred the charge.”

Then the foremost [is] in need above all people.

I am one of firm resolve, close-bound and discreet.

So the two of them set to work and cleared them out. While they were uprooting what was in it, they uncovered a white snake. They rushed at it with their whips and killed it. The place then took its revenge on them: Mirdās was strangled and died there, and was buried at al-Qurayya. Ḥarb was carried to Mecca and fell ill. He said to his sons, who were with him, “Attend to the jinn and give him water; keep looking after him. If he lives, your father will live.” They took the jinn and kept looking after him and giving him water, while Ḥarb remained in that state. The jinn died. Someone came to Ḥarb’s sons, while Ḥarb was at his last breath, and said, “The jinn has died.” One of Ḥarb’s sons said, “Then—” Ḥarb replied, “Then your father [will die],” and died there. They heard a woman lamenting the jinn, remembering Ḥarb. The jinn’s name was ʿAmr:

(rajaz)

Woe to Ḥarb, a horseman,a smiter, a plunderer!

Woe to ʿAmr’s mother, a horseman,when they donned their helmets!

Both of them have becomehopeless of life.

Ḥarb laid waste his fortressand demolished the churches.

For his death we shall surely killchiefs, noble and mighty,

and we shall lie in wait for their caravanand sit in their gathering places.

The ʿanābis were Abū Ḥarb b. Umayya, ʿAnbasa b. Umayya—who was Abū Sufyān and the eldest of the sons of Umayya—Ḥarb b. Umayya, and Sufyān b. Umayya. Al-Qurayya was abandoned, and its people dispersed.

When the time of ʿUmar b. al-Khaṭṭāb came, Kulayb b. ʿAhma, a brother of Banū Ẓafar b. al-Ḥārith b. Bahtha b. Sulaym, seized it. ʿAbbās b. Mirdās disputed with him, saying:

(kāmil)

O Kulayb, why are you unjust every day?Injustice is most hateful; its face is accursed.

Your people used to reckon you a lord,but I think you are a lord afflicted by the evil eye.

When you return to your women, apply oil to yourself;the man who makes peace is smooth and oiled.

Al-Qurayya’s affair would be clearif explanation were of any use to you.

You wronged us, then went off to mark its boundary,while Abū Yazīd lies buried in its hollow.

Do to your people what your namesake did to Wāʾilon the Day of al-Ghadīr, the one pierced by the spear.

I think you will meet its like:its sharpened point in your two sides.

Umayya b. ʿAbd Shams said, lamenting Ḥarb:

(wāfir)

Even if they had killed, in vengeance for Ḥarb, a millionof the noble jinn and humankind,

I would have deemed them a mere winnowingand said, “Show us one like Ḥarb among humankind.”

Al-waghl is what comes away from the sieve of the cloth used for food. The sons of Umayya were named— The four [were called] the ʿanābis after Abū Sufyān, who was ʿAnbasa b. Umayya, when they bound themselves together. The ʿanābis are lions; the singular is ʿanbas.

The account of the licentiousness of Banū al-Sabīʿa, from al-Kalbī

Ibn al-Kharbūdh said: After Banū al-Sibāq, Banū al-Sabīʿa, daughter of al-Aḥabb b. Zubayna b. Judhayma b. ʿAwf b. Naṣr b. Muʿāwiya b. Bakr b. Hawāzin, became licentious. ʿAbd Manāf b. Kaʿb b. Saʿd b. Taym b. Murra married her, and she bore him Khālid, whose descendants included al-Sharqī. Al-Sharqī was a violent man, given to licentiousness and wickedness. It was Abū al-Ghasham who removed the armor of the ʿĀmirī woman at ʿUkāẓ; that was the day called the Fijār of the woman. Their licentiousness became widespread, and at night they heard a voice from the jinn on one of the mountains of Mecca, saying:

(wāfir)

Say to Banū al-Sabīʿa: You have transgressed;taste the consequence of that before long,

as Banū al-Sibāq tasted it whenthey transgressed; transgression is a grievous burden.

He said: They desisted from it, though some of them remained. To Khālid, his mother al-Sabīʿa says:

(kāmil)

My son, do not wrong anyone in Mecca,neither the young nor the old.

The account of al-Fākih, from al-Wāqidī

He said: This is the account of al-Fākih b. al-Mughīra b. ʿAbd Allāh b. ʿUmar b. Makhzūm and ʿAwf b. ʿAbd ʿAwf b. [ʿAbd b.] al-Ḥārith b. Zuhra, and ʿAffān b. Abī al-ʿĀṣ b. Umayya. They had gone as merchants to Yemen. ʿUthmān, ʿAffān’s son, was with him, and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, ʿAwf b. ʿAbd ʿAwf’s son, was with him. On their return, they carried the property of a man of Banū Judhayma b. ʿĀmir b. ʿAbd Manāt b. Kināna to his heirs; he had died in Yemen. One of them, a man called Khālid b. Hishām, claimed it. He met them in the territory of Banū Judhayma before they reached the dead man’s family, and demanded it from them, but they refused. He and some of his people fought them over the property to take it, and they fought back. ʿAwf and al-Fākih were killed, while ʿAffān and his son ʿUthmān escaped. They seized the property of al-Fākih and of ʿAwf b. ʿAbd ʿAwf and went off with it. As they say, ʿAbd al-Raḥmān b. ʿAwf had killed Khālid b. Hishām al-Judhamī, his father’s killer. Quraysh then prepared to raid Banū Judhayma. But Banū Judhayma said to Quraysh, “The loss of your companions did not occur with our consent. A people acted against them in ignorance and killed them, and we did not know”—or words to that effect—“We will pay compensation for any blood or property for which we are responsible.” Quraysh accepted the compensation and called off the war.

The account of Qays b. Nashaba and his alliance with al-ʿAbbās b. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib

Aḥmad b. Ibrāhīm related to me, from Abū Ḥafṣ al-Sulamī, who was descended from al-Uqayṣir b. Qays b. Nashaba b. ʿĀmir, and whose lineage converged with that of al-ʿAbbās b. Mirdās b. Abī ʿĀmir. He said: Qays b. Nashaba entered Mecca and sold some camels of his to a man of Quraysh, who withheld what he owed him. He would stand and say:

(rajaz)

O people of Fihr, how can this happen in the sanctuary,in the sanctity of the House and the code of generosity?

I am wronged, and no one protects me from the wrongdoer.

The news reached al-ʿAbbās b. Mirdās, who composed some verses and sent them with the pilgrims to Qays b. Nashaba b. Abī ʿĀmir:

(basīṭ)

If your neighbor’s pledge did not help you,until you tasted breaths from the cup of humiliation,

go to the houses and keep apart from their people,and you will meet Ibn Ḥarb and meet al-Marʾa ʿAbbās.

The waterer of the pilgrims—and this is Yāsir Falaj—and glory are inherited in fifths and sixths.

When this poem became known, Abū Sufyān said, “He has made glory into fifths and sixths: he has assigned the fifths to al-ʿAbbās and the sixths to me. Go to al-ʿAbbās.” He went to al-ʿAbbās, who secured his due for him and said, “I am your ally whenever you enter Mecca; whatever is taken from you is my responsibility.” Al-ʿAbbās b. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib said of this:

(ṭawīl)

I safeguarded Qays’s right and his pledge,and thrust the spear into the nose of whoever resented it.

I shall support him while he lives, and, if I die,I shall urge Hāshim to support one another.

That bond between him and Banū Hāshim lasted until God sent the Prophet ﷺ. He said: Qays b. Nashaba came as a delegation to the Prophet ﷺ. Qays had studied the scriptures. He said to the Prophet ﷺ “God has never sent a prophet who was not a mediator among his people, accepted by them. We know that you are a mediator among your people, accepted by them. May I ask you what the prophets used to be asked?” He said, “Yes.” He asked, “Do you know Kaḥl?”

He replied, “It is the heaven.” He asked, “Do you know Maḥall?” He replied, “Yes, it is the earth.” He asked, “To whom do they belong?” He replied, “To God, exalted is He; God’s is the command, before and after.” Qays b. Nashaba then accepted Islam and began to say:

(kāmil) I followed Muḥammad’s religion and accepted it, for acceptance is [the path] to my trust and my religion.

That is a man whose word the enemies opposed,and I clasped his right hand in my left.

I had long hoped for him and awaited his time;God decreed that he should guide me.

I mean the son of Āmina, the trustworthy one; through himI hope for deliverance from the punishment of humiliation.

He said: The Messenger of God ﷺ used to call him “the best of Banū Sulaym.” Whenever he was missed, he would say, “What has become of your best one, Banū Sulaym?”

The account of Raqīqa

Yaʿqūb b. Muḥammad al-Zuhrī said: ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz b. ʿImrān b. Ḥuwayṣa related to me that Makhrama b. Nawfal recounted that his mother, Raqīqa bt. Abī Ṣayfī b. Hāshim, was a contemporary of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. She said: “Year after year of drought came upon Quraysh, drying out the highlands and wearing the bones thin. While I was asleep—O God!—or dozing, a herald cried out in a rough voice: ‘O assembly of Quraysh! The prophet who is to be sent is from among you, and this is the time of his rising. So hasten to the rain and abundance! Look, then, for a man among you who is of middle rank in lineage, tall, broad-boned, white and fair, with a prominent nose and smooth cheeks, possessing a distinction—’

—he keeps within himself, and an omen is directed to him. Let him go forth with his sons, then let one man from every clan come to him. Then let them pour water over themselves, apply perfume, touch the Corner, and climb Abū Qubays. Let the man pray for rain, and let the people say “Amen.” Then you will be relieved whenever you wish and live.’”

By God’s knowledge, I awoke in terror, dismayed; my skin bristled and my heart was distraught. I recounted my dream and went about the ravines of Mecca. By the sanctity and the sanctuary, there was no man of the valley there who did not say, “This is Shaybat al-Ḥamd! This is Shaybat al-Ḥamd!” Quraysh assembled around him, and a man from every clan came to him. They poured water over themselves, applied perfume, and touched the Corner. Then he climbed Abū Qubays, and the people hastened around him, as though you could not tell their swift movement from a measured pace, until he settled at its summit and they surrounded him on both sides. The Messenger of God ﷺ was with him; at that time he was a boy who had nearly reached maturity, or was close to it. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib stood and said, “O God, reliever of distress and remover of affliction, You know without being taught, You are asked without being denied. These are—”

“Your male and female servants in the courtyards of Your sanctuary complain to You of years that have eaten away at the hoofed and the padded beasts. Hear us, O God, and send us abundant, plentiful rain!” They had scarcely made their supplication when the sky burst with its water, and the valley swelled with its rushing flood. I heard Quraysh’s elders and leading men say, “Good tidings to you, Abū al-Baṭḥāʾ! Good tidings to you!” Raqīqa says of this:

(basīṭ)

Through Shaybat al-Ḥamd God gave our land to drink,when rain had failed us and its delay had grown long.

A dark cloud poured forth its streams,a rain that came down, and by it livestock and trees thrived.

A favor from God, through the blessed one whose omen is good,the best of those whom Muḍar was ever told would come.

Blessed in his affairs, through whom the clouds are asked for rain;among all humankind, none is his equal or match.

Ibn Ḥabīb said, mentioning Hishām b. al-Kalbī: al-Walīd b.— [ʿAbd Allāh ibn] Jamīʿ, from a descendant of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Mawhab, an ally of Banū Zuhrah, said: Makh­ramah ibn Nawfal ibn Uhayb al-Zuhrī told me: “I heard my mother, Raqīqah bt. Abū Ṣayfī—who was a contemporary of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib”—and he mentioned the report.

The report of the caller on Abū Qubays

Hishām, from his father, from ʿAbd al-Majīd, from Abū ʿAbs, his father, from his grandfather, said: An uncle of mine told me: “I heard a caller on Abū Qubays one night, saying:

(al-ṭawīl)

If the two Saʿds accept Islam, Muḥammad will be safein Mecca, fearing no opposition from an opponent.

When morning came, Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb and the Quraysh notables said: ‘Which Saʿds? Saʿd of Tamīm? Saʿd of Hawāzin? Saʿd of Hudhaym? Saʿd of Bakr?’ They counted up the Saʿds. On the second night, they heard his voice on Abū Qubays, saying:’/’

(al-ṭawīl)

O Saʿd, Saʿd of Aws, be a helper,and O Saʿd, Saʿd of the Khazraj, noble chiefs,

Answer the call to the religion of guidance, and hopefor a wish from God in the Firdaws, as one who knows.

For God’s reward for the seeker of guidanceis gardens in the Firdaws, with canopies.

The story of the origins of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān’s wealth

Hishām said: Al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jamīʿ, an ally of Banū Zuhrah, told me: I heard ʿĀmir ibn Wāthilah, Abū al-Ṭufayl, say: Some Quraysh elders said to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān, “Abū Zuhayr, where did this wealth of yours come from?” He was among the wealthiest of men. He replied: “You have come to the right person to ask. I went out with a group of Quraysh to Syria. While we were in one of its marketplaces, a man approached, so huge he nearly blocked the horizon. He said:

‘Who will take me to the land of Jurhum and load my two riding camels with gold?’

None of our elders answered him. He turned away, then returned on the second day and said what he had said the first day, then left without anyone answering. Then he returned on the third day and said the same thing. When I saw that everyone was silent, I said, ‘I will take you to the land of Jurhum.’ Ibn Judʿān said: ‘By the land of Jurhum, I mean the land of Mecca.’ So I loaded my camels, slaughtering a sheep for him each day and a camel each Friday, until we reached Mecca. I said, ‘This is the land of Jurhum.’ He said, ‘You are right, but go on—keep going.’ He took me through mountains and valleys I had never seen, until he came to a cave in the mountain, blocked up with stones. He said, ‘Make me kneel here.’ I made him kneel there. Then he said, ‘Take this cave apart, stone by stone.’ I did so and entered the cave. Inside were three beds. Two had dead men on them; the third was empty. There was a great deal of gold, and in one corner of the cave a basin containing ointments. He said, ‘Man, I am going to die as these two men died. A loud sound will come from me, but do not let it frighten you. There is a basin containing ointments, a bottle with a feather on the empty bed, and a great deal of gold in one corner of the cave.

“He took off the clothes he was wearing and said, ‘Anoint me with what is in this basin.’ I anointed him from head to foot, then wrapped him in clothes he had brought with him. He sat on the bed, took the feather, tickled his nose with it, then let out a cry more terrible than any I had ever heard. He fell dead, as though he had been dead all along. He had told me: ‘Take as much of this gold as you need, and put the cave back as it was. Beware of returning to what is here, for if you return, you will lose your wealth and your life.’ I did as he said. That was the origin of my wealth.”

The report announcing the death of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān

Hishām, from Maʿrūf ibn al-Kharbūdh al-Makkī, said: ʿĀmir ibn Wāthilah, Abū al-Ṭufayl, told me: A man from Mecca told me, on the authority of al-Aʿshā ibn al-Nabbāsh ibn Zurarah al-Tamīmī, of Banū Usayyid ibn ʿAmr ibn Tamīm, an ally of Banū ʿAbd al-Dār, who said:

“I went out with a group of Quraysh, intending to travel to Syria on a trading caravan of ours. We stopped in a valley called Wādī Ghawl and camped there for the night. I saw an old man on a rock saying:”

(al-ṭawīl)

Alas, al-Sayyāl, the rain of Banū Fihr, has perished,he of far-reaching strength, exalted glory, and renown.

“My companions were asleep, so I said, ‘By God, I will answer him,’ and said:”

(al-ṭawīl)

O herald of the death of the generous and renowned man,which man of Banū Fihr do you announce to us?

“He replied:”

(al-ṭawīl)

I announce the death of Ibn Judʿān ibn ʿAmr, the brother of generosity,of ancient, noble lineage and far-reaching standing.

I passed women scratching their faces,in the morning, handsome ones, between Zamzam and the Stone.

“I said:”

(al-ṭawīl)

By my life, you have proclaimed the death of a chiefwhose known excellence surpassed that of the descendants of al-Naḍr.

When had I last seen him? It was on ʿArūbah,nine days before the first of this month.

“He replied:”

(al-ṭawīl)

He has been dead for three full days,since nightfall; death came to him at dawn.

“He said: My companions awoke and asked, ‘Who are you speaking to?’ I said, ‘This man has announced Ibn Judʿān’s death to me.’ They said, ‘By God, if anyone could have been spared because of his nobility, great wealth, and generosity, it would have been Ibn Judʿān.’ The old man said:”

(al-wāfir)

I see that the days spare neither the mightybecause of their might, nor the lowly.

“He said: I replied:”

(al-wāfir)

They spare neither a single one of the two classes of beings,nor the mountains, nor the plains.

“We remembered that hour and that day, and found it to be as he had said.”

“So he threw off clothes he had on and said, ‘Anoint me with this from the basin.’ I anointed him, from head to foot, then wrapped him in clothes he had brought with him. He sat on the bed, took the feather and tickled his nose with it, then let out a cry such as I had never heard before. He fell dead, as though he had been dead all along. He had told me: ‘Take as much of this gold as you need, and restore the cave to how it was. Beware of returning to what is here, for if you return, you will lose your wealth and your life.’ I did as he said. That was the origin of my wealth.”

The report announcing the death of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān

Hishām, from Maʿrūf ibn al-Kharbūdh al-Makkī, said: ʿĀmir ibn Wāthilah, Abū al-Ṭufayl, told me: “A man from Mecca told me, on the authority of al-Aʿshā ibn al-Nabbāsh ibn Zurarah al-Tamīmī, of Banū Usayyid ibn ʿAmr ibn Tamīm, an ally of Banū ʿAbd al-Dār, who said:

‘I went out with a group of Quraysh, intending to travel to Syria on a trading caravan of ours. We stopped in a valley called Wādī Ghawl and camped there for the night. I saw an old man on a rock saying:’”

(al-ṭawīl)

Alas, al-Sayyāl, the rain of Banū Fihr, has perished,he of far-reaching strength, exalted glory, and renown.

“My companions were asleep, so I said, ‘By God, I will answer him,’ and said:”

(al-ṭawīl)

O herald of the death of the generous and renowned man,which man of Banū Fihr do you announce to us?

“He replied:”

(al-ṭawīl) I announce the death of Ibn Judʿān ibn ʿAmr, the brother of generosity, of ancient, noble lineage and far-reaching standing.

I passed women scratching their faces,in the morning, handsome ones, between Zamzam and the Stone.

“I said:”

(al-ṭawīl)

By my life, you have proclaimed the death of a chiefwhose known excellence surpassed that of the descendants of al-Naḍr.

When had I last seen him? It was on ʿArūbah,nine days before the first of this month.

“He replied:”

(al-ṭawīl)

He has been dead for three full days,since nightfall; death came to him at dawn.

“He said: My companions awoke and asked, ‘Who are you speaking to?’ I said, ‘This man has announced Ibn Judʿān’s death to me.’ They said, ‘By God, if anyone could have been spared because of his nobility, great wealth, and generosity, it would have been Ibn Judʿān.’ The old man said:”

(al-wāfir)

I see that the days spare neither the mightybecause of their might, nor the lowly.

“He said: I replied:”

(al-wāfir)

They spare neither a single one of the two classes of beings,nor the mountains, nor the plains.

“We remembered that hour and that day, and found it to be as he had said.”

The story of Rikānah

Hishām, from his father, from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, from the Prophet ﷺ—[he—] offered Islam to Rikānah ibn ʿAbd Yazīd ibn Hāshim ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn ʿAbd Manāf and called him to God. Rikānah was one of the strongest Arabs and had never been thrown in a wrestling match. He said, “I will not accept Islam unless you call that tree and it comes to you.”

The Messenger of God ﷺ—was then in the backlands of Mecca. He said to the tree, “Come, by God’s permission.” It was a ṭalḥ tree or a samurah tree, and it came forward. Rikānah said, “I have never seen such great sorcery as this today. Tell it to go back.” The Messenger of God ﷺ—said to it, “Go back, by God’s permission,” and it went back.

The Messenger of God ﷺ—said to him, “Accept Islam.” He said, “By God, not until you call half of it to come to you, while the other half stays where it is.” The Messenger of God ﷺ—said to half of it, “Come, by God’s permission.” It came forward, and Rikānah said, “I have never seen such great sorcery as this today. Tell it to go back.” The Messenger of God ﷺ—said to it, “Go back, by God’s permission,” and it went back to its place. The Messenger of God ﷺ—said to him, “Accept Islam.” Rikānah said to him, “No, not until you wrestle me. If you throw me, I will accept Islam; if I throw you, I will stop this talk.” The Prophet wrestled him and threw him. Rikānah accepted Islam after that.

The report about those among Quraysh who abandoned the worship of idols

He said: Those who abandoned the worship of idols and sought the religion of Abraham ﵇ before the Prophet’s mission were ʿUthmān ibn al-Ḥuwayrith ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al- ʿUzzā ibn Quṣayy; Waraqah ibn Nawfal ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā; Zayd ibn ʿAmr ibn Nufayl ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Riyāḥ ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Qurṭ ibn Rizāḥ ibn ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb; and ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Jaḥsh ibn Riʾāb, one of Banū Ghanm ibn Dūdān ibn Asad ibn Khuzaymah, an ally of Banū Umayyah ibn ʿAbd Shams.

Some of them said to others: “Do you know, by God, that your people have no true religion? They have strayed from the religion of Abraham ﵇. What is this stone that we walk around, which neither harms nor benefits, sees nor hears? O my people, seek a faith for yourselves, for, by God, you have no true religion.” So they dispersed through the lands, seeking the pure monotheism, the religion of Abraham ﵇.

As for Waraqah ibn Nawfal, he became a Christian, grew firm in Christianity, and learned the scriptures. Zayd ibn ʿAmr ibn Nufayl, however, remained undecided and entered neither Judaism nor Christianity. He abandoned the religion of his people and shunned idols, carrion, blood, and sacrificial animals slaughtered for idols. He forbade the killing of female infants. He said, “I worship the Lord of Abraham ﵇.” He openly reproached his people for what they practiced and would say, “O God, if I knew which way was most pleasing to You, I would worship You in that way, but I do not know.” Then he would prostrate himself on the palm of his hand. Zayd was the first to condemn what Quraysh were doing in worshipping idols. Then he set out seeking the religion of Abraham ﵇. He traveled through the lands of Syria until he reached al-Balqāʾ—which was named after Bāliq ibn Māb ibn Lūṭ. A learned monk there said to him, “You are seeking a religion, but today you will find no one to guide you to it. A prophet who will call people to it is soon to appear in your own land. He had looked into the beliefs of the Jews and Christians but had not approved of their religion.” So Zayd hurried toward Mecca in response to the monk’s words. When he had reached the middle of the land of Lakhm—or, it is said, the land of Judhām—they attacked and killed him. It is said that this Zayd will be resurrected as a nation on his own—and God knows best.

As for ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Jaḥsh, he accepted Islam and emigrated to Abyssinia. There he became a Christian and died a Christian.

The story of ʿUthmān ibn al-Ḥuwayrith with Caesar, from Hishām, Abū ʿAmr al-Shaybānī, and others

ʿUthmān ibn al-Ḥuwayrith ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā set out and came to Ibn Jafnah, king of Syria. He said to him, “Would you like Quraysh to submit to your authority?” He said, “Yes.” ʿUthmān said, “Then write for me, and appoint me ruler over them.” He said, “On condition that you make them submit to me.” In another version of his report, also in Abū ʿAmr al-Shaybānī’s book, he said: “Write me a letter and appoint me ruler over them.” Ibn Jafnah wrote for him, appointed him ruler, and imposed a levy on each tribe. ʿUthmān set out with Ibn Jafnah’s letter until he came to Mecca. When he came to Quraysh, they rejected what he had done. Some of their men rode to Ibn Jafnah. When they came to him, they spoke with him and said, “ʿUthmān is a foolish man. It is not like you to do this to us. We know your rights, and we are people of right and of the Kaʿbah.” Ibn Jafnah then brought ʿUthmān out and expelled him.

ʿUthmān set out and came to Caesar, intending to speak with him. When Ibn Jafnah heard of this, he sent word to the gatekeeper and interpreter [that—] they were not to admit him or tell Caesar about him. He ordered them to misrepresent what ʿUthmān said, so that Caesar would pay him no attention.

One day Caesar rode out. ʿUthmān intercepted him, called out and shouted to him, and spoke with him. Caesar said, “What is he saying?” The interpreter said, “This is a madman. He says there is treasure in my land on top of a mountain, and that if you give him money, he will strike the mountain for you until the treasure comes out.” The interpreter lied about him because of Ibn Jafnah’s letter. Caesar went on, leaving ʿUthmān wandering in the land of the Romans.

When ʿUthmān saw what had been done to him, he did not know what to do. As he sat near a teacher instructing some Romans in writing, he joined him and settled into conversation with him. The teacher recited a line of poetry, with all those present looking on. ʿUthmān seized his garment and realized he was an Arab. He said to him, “By God, I will not let you go until you tell me who you are! You are an Arab, and I am a man of your people.” When the teacher saw this, he said, “Woe to you! Do not speak to me, for Ibn Jafnah has written about you to every gatekeeper and interpreter. No one here can do anything for you. But if you give me a pledge, I will show you what will benefit you.” ʿUthmān gave him his pledge. The teacher said, “When the king passes by, say such-and-such words to him”—he taught him words from their religion—“and if the interpreter summons you, cling to him, tear your garment, and say, ‘This man has ruined me. Call another interpreter for me, one other than him.’”

“When the king passed him, ʿUthmān did as the teacher had instructed. When he did so, the king summoned another interpreter, since the first one had— “What happened?” He said to him, “I am one of the people of the Kaʿba and of the Sacred House of God, to which the Arabs make pilgrimage. I spoke with Ibn Jafna, asking him to give me authority over my people so that I might compel them to follow your religion. Men of my people wronged me, bribed him, and he expelled me. So I came to you. He wrote to the interpreter, ordering him to do me harm, so that you would not look favorably upon me. This is my story. If you write me a letter and give me authority over them, I will compel the Arabs for you until they follow your religion.” At that, Caesar wrote for him, clothed him, and mounted him on a she-mule saddled with a gold saddle. He said to him, “Ibn Jafna has no authority over you.” He handed him a sealed letter and recited verses about the land of the Romans, some of which have been transmitted, including the following (ṭawīl):

When we drew near the city of Caesar,

the people’s souls sensed some misgivings.

ʿUthmān then came with the letter until he arrived before Ibn Jafna and handed it to him. Ibn Jafna said, “Take whichever of your people you find here.” He took some men from Quraysh, among them Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayya and Abū Dhuʾayb ibn Rabīʿa, one of the Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy. He seized them while they were trading in Syria and imprisoned them. Abū Dhuʾayb died in irons, while Saʿīd remained until ʿUtba ibn Rabīʿa ibn ʿAbd Shams and Abū Umayya ibn al-Mughīra ransomed him. Some say that it was Hishām ibn al-Mughīra and Abū Umayya ibn al-Mughīra who ransomed him. Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ had a sister, the daughter of al-Mughīra, who was married to one of the two men; Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ praised them in his poetry. ʿUthmān ibn al-Ḥuwayrith died before he could leave Ibn Jafna’s court. Many people said that Ibn Jafna had given him poison, out of envy and fear that ʿUthmān would overcome him and take his kingdom. This reached ʿUthmān’s people, and Waraqa ibn Nawfal, ʿUthmān ibn al-Ḥuwayrith’s paternal cousin, the brother of his father, elegized ʿUthmān:

(kāmil)

Has news come to my two daughters that their father, ʿUthmān,

met his appointed end beside al-Mirṣad?

He rode the courier route, risking his life,

while the courier’s destination was a place of death, a place of peril.

I shall surely weep for ʿUthmān with all the weeping he deserves …

And I shall surely sing of ʿAmr, even if he does not sing.

Would that I knew of you, son of Ḥuwayrith:

Were you given poison in the raised vessel?

Or was it a death that came and then hastened you on?

Indeed, death surely finds its way to the fated end.

He was an adornment to his people in life—

ʿUthmān now lies in a grave, in a buried place.

My body has wasted away, and I said to our people,

when his death reached me, “May he not be far away!”

Ibn Jafna remains a king in this life,

while the beloved of my soul lies in a sealed grave.

By God, my Lord, if I am spared, I shall surely take vengeance …

upon him with a decisive blow, not aimed wide.

He said: The name of the Jafnid king was ʿAmr ibn Abī Shammir, brother of al-Ḥārith ibn Abī Shammir. When ʿAmr heard of this, he ordered an iron cauldron and said, “Boil the water in it.” He said:

“By Him by whom I swear, it shall remain on the fire until I boil Waraqa ibn Nawfal in it. By God, if his people do not bring him to me, I shall seize a man from Quraysh in Syria, and he shall not be released from the irons until Waraqa is brought to me.” Waraqa heard of this and fled until he reached the land of Ṭayyiʾ. He stayed there for some time, then went on to al-Baḥrayn. When he arrived in al-Baḥrayn, a Christian man said to him, “I shall show you something which, if you say it to the king, will secure your pardon.” The Christian taught Waraqa what to say: “When you come before the king, do not let him know who you are. Make your way to him, and when you reach him, grasp his garment and say:

‘I seek refuge in the Messiah from this king.’”

He went to him and entered his presence. “I have praised you, O king!” he said, then recited his verses and spoke with him. Then, trembling, he grasped the king’s garment and recited the following (wāfir):

Is there someone to deliver a message to ʿAmr?

I am wary for fear of him.

while around me the Banū Jarm bark loudly.

I seek refuge in the Lord of the House from him,

and in the Merciful, when the Messiah rose in the East.

I have left you the lands and the water of al-Baḥrayn,

to go far from you, if flight could avail me.

He said: “I have granted you protection; perhaps you are Waraqa ibn Nawfal.” He said, “Yes.” The king said, “I have granted you and your people protection. Put out the fire.” Christianity entered Waraqa ibn Nawfal’s heart that day. When he returned to Mecca and Quraysh had accepted Islam, the Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy said, “What of the blood of Abū Dhuʾayb? ʿUthmān ibn al-Ḥuwayrith killed him and shackled him in irons until he died.” Abū Dhuʾayb’s mother was Umm Ḥabīb bint al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayya al-Akbar, and Saʿīd was her brother. Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ set out and gave the Banū ʿĀmir his son Abān ibn Saʿīd as a pledge, for he did not want his brother’s blood to go unavenged. He said, “This is yours until I satisfy you regarding Abū Dhuʾayb.” Some men of the Banū Quṣayy opposed him, while others supported him. Among those who left him was al-Aswad ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn Asad, Abū Zamʿa. He said to him, “Saʿīd, why should we concern ourselves with the blood of a man who died in Syria, in the prison of one king or another?” For this reason al-Aswad said (wāfir):

Is there someone to convey from me to Saʿīd:

your making amends to your allies is enough for you.

Waraqa ibn Nawfal spoke about Abū Zamʿa, saying (wāfir):

Tell Abū ʿAqīl, if you can,

that there is no affection between you and me.

You censure my trustworthiness and disparage my family …

and make me food for town-dwellers and Bedouins.

I strive to sow discord within the tribe.

May he never find joy from a king,

and may his hands never cease to be in fetters.

The account of the Days of al-Fijār, connected with the reports about Quraysh; and an account of what provoked the First Fijār, from Abū al-Bakhtarī

Abū al-Bakhtarī related, from Mūsā ibn Muḥammad ibn Ibrāhīm ibn al-Ḥārith al-Taymī, from Abū Wajza al-Saʿdī, who said: The event that provoked the First Fijār between Quraysh and Qays ʿAylān was this: Aws ibn al-Ḥadathān al-Naṣrī sold a man of Kināna some of his camels, due a year later, when he was to come to the market. He came year after year, but the man did not pay him, and the Kinānī became insolvent. The Naṣrī came to the market of ʿUkāẓ with a monkey and stood it in the marketplace. Then he said, “Who will sell me the equivalent of this monkey for what so-and-so the Kinānī owes me?” He meant to disgrace the Kinānī by this. A man of the Banū Kināna passed by and struck the monkey with his sword, killing it, out of anger at what the Naṣrī had done. The Naṣrī cried out among Qays, and the Kinānī cried out among the Banū Kināna. The people argued until they nearly came to blows. Then they called for a settlement, the matter became easier for them to bear, and they refrained from attacking one another. Then the Second Fijār broke out.

The people argued until they were nearly at war, then called one another to make peace. The matter became easier for them to bear, and they refrained from attacking one another. Then the Second Fijār broke out.

An account of what provoked the Second Fijār, the Fijār of Boasting, also related as the Fijār of the Man

He said: What provoked this Fijār was that a man of the Banū Ghifār ibn Mulayl ibn Ḍamra ibn Bakr ibn ʿAbd Manāt ibn Kināna, called Abū Munīʿa, who was fierce and formidable in himself, came to the market of ʿUkāẓ. He stretched out his leg and said (rajaz):

My people are the Banū Mudrika ibn Khindif.

Whoever spears them in the eye will not blink;

whoever has them as his people will swagger …

as though they were a dark, swelling sea.

“By God, I am the mightiest of the Arabs. Whoever claims that he is mightier than I am, let him strike this with a sword.”

A man of the Banū Qushayr struck it, making a small, shallow cut. The people then argued until they nearly came to blows. They later drew back, seeing that there had been no major fighting or wounds. Ibn al-Ḍarība al-Naṣrī said (khafīf):

Ask Umm Mālik: what people

are my kin, throughout the annals of time?

We were the kings of the people of Najd,

and the protectors of honor when honor was at stake.

We protected the Ḥijāz from every clan,

and we upheld the boast on the day of boasting.

Laqīṭ said that a man of the Banū Naṣr ibn Muʿāwiya struck it, and he said (rajaz):

We are the Banū Dahmān, masters of swagger …

a sea of seas, surging and never drained.

Whoever comes to us among humankind draws from us.

We struck the foot of al-Mukhandif,

when he stretched it out in the months of the standing-place,

boasting before the people, against the customary place,

a free man’s blow, like a long gash in the crest …

a loud, unmistakable one, in truth, despite his pride. with a keen, sharp sword that cuts through the sinews,

passing through the weakened scabbard.

An account of what provoked the Third Fijār

He said: The beginning of the Fijār was this: a woman of the Arabs, descended from ʿIkrima ibn Khaṣafa ibn Qays, and then of the Banū ʿĀmir ibn Ṣaʿṣaʿa, came to ʿUkāẓ. She was beautiful, tall, and stately. The young men of Mecca surrounded her and looked at her. She wore a striped face-veil. They asked her to uncover her face, but she refused. Women at that time did not wear waist-wrappers; a woman would go out wearing only a faḍl garment, a shift without an undergarment. When she refused them, although they had seen her bearing and features, they persisted in pressing her. She sat down to buy some of what she needed. One of those young men, called Abū al-Ghashm ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn ʿĀmir ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Ḥāritha ibn Saʿd ibn Taym ibn Murra, came up while she was sitting and loosened the lower part of her shift with a thorn up to her back. When she had finished her shopping, she stood up and found herself naked. The young men laughed at her and said, “You kept your face from us, but we have seen your backside.” The woman uncovered her face, and it was radiant. They became [more] enamored of her than they had been. She cried out, “O men of Qays, look what has been done to me!” The people gathered, and her clan gathered around her. Some drew close to others, but then, after a little skirmishing and fighting, of no account, they drew apart.

This was the first such occurrence, and it was called al-Fijār because of the importance they attached to bloodshed, to the violation of what was inviolable, and to the severing of ties of kinship. Al-Qarābāt and ʿUkāẓ lay between Nakhlah and al-Ṭāʾif; Dhū al-Majāz lay behind ʿArafah; and Majannah lay at Marr al-Ẓahrān. These were the markets of the Arabs and Quraysh, and none of them was more important than ʿUkāẓ.

The cause of the Fourth Fijār, the Fijār of al-Barrāḍ

He said: Al-Barrāḍ, who was Rāfiʿ ibn Qays, had entered into an alliance with Banū Sahm. He attacked a man from Hudhayl and killed him. The Hudhaylīs then went to Banū Sahm, demanding blood-payment for their man. Banū Sahm said, “We have disavowed him and renounced responsibility for his crime.” Hudhayl said:

“Who will acknowledge this man?”

Al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil said, “I have disowned him just as one disowns a dog.” The Hudhaylīs fell silent, seeing no grounds for pursuing their claim. Then al-Barrāḍ went to Ḥarb ibn Umayyah, asking to enter into an alliance with him. Ḥarb said, “I have seen that your allies have disowned you and rejected you.” Al-Barrāḍ replied, “If you see the same from me as they did, you may choose: if you wish, you may remain in your alliance with me; and if you wish, you may disavow me.” Ḥarb said, “There is no harm in that.” So Ḥarb ibn Umayyah entered into an alliance with him.

Al-Barrāḍ attacked a man from Khuzāʿah, killed him, and fled through the land. The Khuzāʿīs sought blood-payment for him but could not catch al-Barrāḍ. He remained in Yemen for a year, then drew near to Mecca. The Hudhaylīs were looking for him, as were the Khuzāʿīs, and he had been disowned. He said, “What better refuge could there be than al-Nuʿmān ibn al-Mundhir? I shall go to him.” [He set out—] and reached al-Ḥīrah. There he found Arab delegations who had come to al-Nuʿmān ibn al-Mundhir. He waited with them, seeking permission to enter, but he did not reach al-Nuʿmān until he had spent a long time there, and had been treated harshly. The time came for al-Nuʿmān to dispatch the laṭīmah [which—] he sent to ʿUkāẓ. Al-Nuʿmān went out and sat before the people in his courtyard at al-Ḥīrah, with the Arab delegations there. Al-Nuʿmān’s caravans and laṭāʾim arrived at the seasonal markets when they entered Tihāmah. They had not been molested until al-Nuʿmān attacked and killed a brother of Balaʿāʾ ibn Qays. Balaʿāʾ ibn Qays then began lying in wait for al-Nuʿmān’s laṭāʾim in Tihāmah and plundering them. He had done this to them twice. Al-Nuʿmān grew anxious for his laṭīmah, and said that day, “Who will escort this caravan?” Al-Barrāḍ leapt up, wearing a small, narrow cloak, and carrying a sword whose scabbard had been worn away by its sharpness. He said, “I will escort it for you.” Then al-Riḥāl ʿUrwah ibn ʿUtbah ibn Jaʿfar ibn Kilāb said, “You will escort it past the people of shīḥ and qayṣūm? You are like a disowned dog. You are too narrow in the backside for that. But I, O king, will escort it for you past both tribes.” Al-Barrāḍ said, “You will escort it past the people of Tihāmah?” Al-Nuʿmān paid no attention to al-Barrāḍ, held him in contempt, and entrusted the laṭīmah to al-Riḥāl. [He] set out with the caravan, Al-Barrāḍ went after him. When they were partway along the road, al-Barrāḍ caught up with him, went ahead of his caravan, and drew lots with the divining arrows. Al-Riḥāl passed him and asked, “What are you doing?”

He replied, “I am seeking an omen about killing you.” Al-Riḥāl laughed and thought nothing of it. Then al-Riḥāl continued until he reached his people, just beyond al-Jurayb, at a watering place called Awārah. He unloaded the laṭīmah and turned the riding camels loose. Al-Barrāḍ had been seeking an opportunity to catch him off guard, but had found none. Then, at midday that day, he found him alone in a tent of hide. He entered and struck him with his sword until he was dead.

[He wrote to the people of Mecca, who were at ʿUkāẓ:]

(Basīṭ)

No doubt the offender brings ruin upon his ally, and the burden falls upon him;

When a clan refuses, the offender must bear it.6

“Now then: I killed ʿUrwah ibn ʿUtbah, al-Riḥāl, at Awārah on Saturday, when the crescent of Dhū al-Ḥijjah became visible. I fled. Whoever acts on what is at hand has done what is required of him. Tomorrow, when the wind rises, the ugly deed will turn against me he incurred liability to al-Nuʿmān for his crime.” Then he ran off until he reached Khaybar. He stayed there for several days, claiming affiliation with Fazārah and taking some of Khaybar’s produce. He remained as long as God willed him to remain.

Two men from Qays had set out after him to Khaybar, one from Ghatafān and the other from Ghanī, named Asad ibn Juwayn. They met him at Khaybar. When he saw them, he asked about their lineage. They traced themselves to Saʿd ibn Qays ibn ʿAylān and to Ghatafān. He claimed affiliation with Fazārah. They asked him, “Have you seen a man called al-Barrāḍ, of Banū Bakr?” Al-Barrāḍ replied, “You have asked about a thieving renegade. No one among the people of Khaybar will let him into his house. But stay here and be discreet; perhaps you will catch him.” They said, “Yes.”

He stayed there that day, then came to them and said, “I have tracked him down. Which of you will go ahead?” One of them said, “I will”—he was Asad ibn Juwayn al-Ghanawī. Al-Barrāḍ said, “Come on.” To the other he said, “Do not leave this place.” Then he took the first man out and led him into one of the Jewish ruins. He said, “Man of Ghanī, draw your sword and give it to me so I can test it.” He grasped the sword’s hilt and drew it, leaving its scabbard in the Ghanawī’s hand. Al-Barrāḍ raised the sword and struck him with it until he killed him. Then he returned to his companion and said, “I have never seen anyone more cowardly or more witless than your companion. I took him inside until he saw the man, and then he missed him like that. It seems to me he has now gone to the farthest part of Khaybar; if he escapes us now, when will we be able to catch him? Come with me.” The Ghaṭafānī said, “Take me wherever you like.” He went out with him until he reached another ruin, where he did the same to him as he had done to his companion, and killed him. Thus he killed them both.

Then he returned to their dwelling, took their two camels and their belongings, and fled. A Jewish man went out, intending to go to that ruin for a need, and found the Ghanawī slain. He went to the other ruin and found the Ghaṭafānī slain. He hurried back to his people, terrified and bewildered. They went out, looked at the two slain men, and searched for al-Barrāḍ. He learned that they were on to him and fled at once. He feared that the Jews of Khaybar would catch him and say, “This is a thieving renegade living among us.” He took the road of Najd toward Mecca and feared for his people on account of Qays. So he said—and warned them forcefully: “When Bishr ibn Abī Khāzim rides among them, tell him about the killing of al-Riḥāl, the Ghaṭafānī, and the Ghanawī. Make him keep it secret, and order him to carry this news to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān, Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah, Ḥarb ibn Umayyah, Nawfal ibn Muʿāwiyah, and Balaʿāʾ ibn Qays.”

Bishr ibn Abī Khāzim set out and reached the market of ʿUkāẓ. He found that the people had gathered there for the market, and that they were in the state of consecration for the pilgrimage. Bishr ibn Abī Khāzim told the men whom al-Barrāḍ had instructed him to inform. Quraysh said among themselves, “We fear Qays, and we fear that the market may not be held this year So let us go to Abū Barāʾ ʿĀmir ibn Mālik ibn Jaʿfar ibn Kilāb. We will tell him part of the story and conceal part, saying: ‘An incident has occurred between the people of Najd and Tihāmah, but no clear account of it has reached us. So keep the people apart and hold the market for them. Let no one depart before the market has been held, when they have come from every place, having driven their camels hard. You take charge of your people, and we will take charge of ours.’”

They went to Abū Barāʾ and told him what they had agreed to say. He agreed to what they wanted and said, “I will take care of this for you and hold the market.” The men went back, and some said to others, “This is no sound plan. If we stay here, we may fear that Qays will hear of it and confront us here when we are unprepared, while they are ready; they will outnumber us at this gathering and prevail over us. Go to your sanctuary.”

Quraysh departed, retreating toward the sanctuary in disarray. The news reached Qays later that day. Abū Barāʾ said, “Quraysh have done nothing but deceive us.” They set out after them. Quraysh formed their rearguard and hurried toward their sanctuary, entering the sacred precinct by night. Qays withdrew, though they were many. A man of Banū ʿĀmir ibn Ṣaʿṣaʿah, called al-Adram ibn Shuʿayb, shouted at the top of his voice: “Our appointed meeting with you is on these nights next year. We will spare no effort to gather.” And he said:

(Basīṭ) We have made a promise to Quraysh, though they are unwilling,

That they shall come to blows in bands of horsemen.

Khudāsh ibn Zuhayr said: (Basīṭ)

How fiercely we charged, and truly, uponSukhaynah—had it not been for night and the sanctuary!

When Hishām protected himself with al-Walīd,If we had caught Hishām, the servants would have scattered.

That year the market of ʿUkāẓ was not held. Quraysh and Kinānah gathered all the Aḥābīsh, as well as those of Asad ibn Khuzaymah who joined them, under Muhayr ibn Abī Khāzim, the brother of the poet Bishr. No one from Banū Tamīm attended except those allied to Quraysh: Āl Zurārah, Āl Abī Ihāb, and Umayyah ibn Abī ʿUbaydah ibn Hammām ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Bakr ibn Zayd ibn Mālik ibn Ḥanẓalah, an ally of Banū Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf. He was the father of Yaʿlā ibn Munyah; Munyah was the daughter of al-Ḥārith ibn Shabīb of Banū Māzin ibn Manṣūr. They appointed a chief for every tribe to organize its affairs. Over Banū ʿAbd Manāf was Ḥarb ibn Umayyah, [with] his two brothers Sufyān and Abū Sufyān—who was ʿAnbasah—the sons of Umayyah.

[From here, the account of Abū ʿUbaydah—] Over Banū Hāshim was al-Zubayr ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, with him the Prophet ﷺ and al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. With them were Banū al-Muṭṭalib, led by Yazīd ibn Hāshim ibn al-Muṭṭalib, whose mother was al-Shifāʾ bint Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf. Over Ḥarb ibn Umayyah were Banū Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf, led by Muṭʿim ibn ʿAdī ibn Nawfal. Over Banū Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā were Khuwaylid ibn Asad and ʿUthmān ibn al-Ḥuwayrith ibn Asad. Over Banū Zuhrah was Makhramah ibn Nawfal ibn Uhayb ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhrah. Over Banū Makhzūm was Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm. Over Jumaḥ was Umayyah ibn Khalaf ibn Wahb ibn Ḥudhāfah ibn Jumaḥ. Over Banū ʿAdī was Zayd ibn ʿAmr ibn Nufayl ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā. Over Banū as ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān had done: he equipped one hundred men fully. Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah equipped men and contributed a great deal of money. Ḥarb ibn Umayyah took men and armed them, placing Bishr ibn Abī Khāzim at their head among his people. No one from Banū Tamīm attended except those allied to Quraysh: Āl Zurārah, Āl Abī Ihāb, and Umayyah ibn Abī ʿUbaydah ibn Hammām ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Bakr ibn Zayd ibn Mālik ibn Ḥanẓalah, an ally of Banū Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf, the father of Yaʿlā ibn Munyah. Munyah was the daughter of al-Ḥārith ibn Shabīb of Banū Māzin ibn Manṣūr. They appointed a chief for every tribe to coordinate its affairs. Over Banū ʿAbd Manāf was Ḥarb ibn Umayyah, [with him] his brothers Sufyān and Abū Sufyān—who was ʿAnbasah—the sons of Umayyah.

[From here, the account of Abū ʿUbaydah—] Over Banū Hāshim was al-Zubayr ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, with him the Prophet ﷺ and al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. With them were Banū al-Muṭṭalib, led by Yazīd ibn Hāshim ibn al-Muṭṭalib, whose mother was al-Shifāʾ bint Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf. Over Ḥarb ibn Umayyah were Banū Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf, led by Muṭʿim ibn ʿAdī ibn Nawfal. Over Banū Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā were Khuwaylid ibn Asad and ʿUthmān ibn al-Ḥuwayrith ibn Asad. Over Banū Zuhrah was Makhramah ibn Nawfal ibn Uhayb ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhrah. Over Banū Makhzūm was Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm. Over Jumaḥ was Umayyah ibn Khalaf ibn Wahb ibn Ḥudhāfah ibn Jumaḥ. Over Banū ʿAdī was Zayd ibn ʿAmr ibn Nufayl ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā. Over Banū— Among the Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy was ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd Shams, Abū Suhayl ibn ʿAmr; over the Banū Muḥārib ibn Fihr was Ḍirār ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ibn Mirdās; over the Banū al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr was ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Jarrāḥ, Abū ʿUbaydah ibn al-Jarrāḥ. [The following, from here to the marked place, is not in Abū Bakr’s recension of Abū ʿUbaydah’s account.] Over the Banū Makhzūm was Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah; over the Banū Sahm was al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil; over the Banū Jumah was Muʿammar ibn Ḥabīb; over the Banū ʿAbd al-Dār ibn Quṣayy was ʿĀmir ibn ʿIkrimah ibn Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn ʿAbd al-Dār ibn Quṣayy. Abū ʿUbaydah omitted ʿĀmir, while Wahb mentioned him, saying “ʿĀmir.” Muʿammar said: “It was ʿIkrimah himself, son of Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn ʿAbd al-Dār ibn Quṣayy.” [Up to here is not in his account.] Over the Banū Taym was ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān ibn ʿAmr; over the Aḥābīsh—namely, al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Manāh, ʿAḍl, al-Qārah, al-Dayš, and al-Muṣṭaliq of Khuzāʿah, because of their alliance with al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Manāh—were al-Ḥulayṣ ibn Yazīd, a brother of the Banū al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Manāh, and Sufyān ibn ʿAwf; these two were their commanders. Over the Banū Bakr ibn ʿAbd Manāh was Balʿāʾ ibn Qays ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn ʿAwf ibn Kaʿb ibn ʿĀmir ibn Layth; over the Banū Firās ibn Ghanm ibn Mālik ibn Kinānah was ʿAmr ibn Qays, Jadhil al-Ṭaʿām; over the Banū Asad was Bishr ibn Abī Khāzim. Command of the people was entrusted to Ḥarb ibn Umayyah. It is said that they went out as separate contingents; it is also said that command was entrusted to Ibn Judʿān. Qays assembled, and Hawāzin and Sulaym assembled together, as did Thaqīf and their allies from Jisr ibn Muḥārib and others who joined them. They mustered all their forces except for Kilāb and Kaʿb, for neither took part in any of the Fijār days except the Day of Nakhlah. Then they met at the turning of the year, on the nights when Qays had promised to meet Quraysh the following year. Hawāzin reached the site before Quraysh and encamped at Shamaṭah, in separate contingents, each tribe led by its own chief. Abū Asmāʾ ibn al-Ḍarībah and ʿAṭiyyah ibn ʿAfīf al-Naṣrī led the Banū Naṣr; al-Khaysaq al-Jushamī led the Banū Jusham and the Banū Saʿd ibn Bakr. Wahb ibn Muʿattib ibn Mālik al-Thaqafī and his brother Masʿūd led Thaqīf. The Banū ʿĀmir ibn Rabīʿah, the Kaʿb ibn Rabīʿah ibn ʿĀmir ibn Ṣaʿṣaʿah, their allies from Jisr ibn Muḥārib, and the Abnāʾ—the sons of Ṣaʿṣaʿah—were led by Salamah ibn Saʿlāʾ, one of the Banū al-Bukkāʾ, together with Khālid ibn Hawdhah. The Banū Hilāl ibn ʿĀmir ibn Ṣaʿṣaʿah were led by— Rabīʿah ibn Abī Ẓabyān ibn Rabīʿah ibn Abī Rabīʿah ibn Nuhayk ibn Hilāl ibn ʿĀmir. This is Abū ʿUbaydah’s account. Abū al-Bukhtarī, who is more reliable, said that Abū Barāʾ would not have stayed away, nor would Kilāb have stayed away, since they were the ones who had suffered losses among the tribes of Qays on account of ʿUrwah ibn ʿUtbah ibn Jaʿfar. Abū al-Bukhtarī said that the Aḥābīsh were led by the man we have already named in the recension at the beginning of the account. These chiefs were thus in separate contingents, though Ḥarb ibn Umayyah, Ibn Judʿān, and Hishām were their supporters; Ḥarb was the most prominent of them, on account of Quṣayy and ʿAbd Manāf. He said: Mūsā ibn Muḥammad ibn Ibrāhīm told me, from his father, from ʿĀʾishah, who said: I said, “Messenger of God, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān used to bear the burden of the needy, welcome the guest, give to the petitioner, and provide food.” The Messenger of God said: “He died in the pre-Islamic period; he is in the Fire.” Then ʿĀʾishah would say: “Ibn Judʿān was one of the noblest of Quraysh. Whenever anything befell Quraysh and weighed heavily upon them, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān would be involved.” Then she would say: “There was the Fijār War, and no day among the Arabs was more widely remembered than it. The people spent a year gathering and preparing for battle. Quraysh set out from the house of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān, and on that day ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān was at their head: he led them, armed the men, and distributed the wealth. Then there was the Ḥilf al-Fuḍūl, which took place in Ibn Judʿān’s house.” Then ʿĀʾishah said: “I bear witness that I heard the Messenger of God say: ‘I was present at an alliance in the house of Ibn Judʿān. I would not wish to have betrayed it, even in exchange for red camels.’”

He said: Qays assembled and sought the support of Thaqīf. They gathered forces and led out the cavalry, which was numerous that day. He said: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yazīd al-Hudhalī told me, from Yaʿqūb ibn ʿUtbah, who said: Masʿūd ibn Muʿattib and Wahb ibn Muʿattib marched among Thaqīf, recruiting Thaqīf and those who obeyed them. Qays sent one man from each of its tribes to recruit it: Abū Barāʾ went to the Banū ʿĀmir, Durayd ibn al-Ṣimmah to the Banū Jusham, Subayʿ ibn Rabīʿah to the Banū Naṣr, and ʿAbbās ibn Ḥayy al-Aṣamm al-Riʿlī to Sulaym. They assembled and encamped at ʿUkāẓ two days before Quraysh. They disagreed over the command. The Banū ʿĀmir said, “We appoint Abū Barāʾ, ʿĀmir ibn Mālik ibn Jaʿfar, as our leader.” The Banū Naṣr ibn Muʿāwiyah, the Saʿd ibn Bakr, and Thaqīf said, “We appoint Subayʿ ibn Rabīʿah ibn Muʿāwiyah al-Naṣrī as our leader.” The Banū Jusham said, “Rather, we appoint Durayd ibn al-Ṣimmah.” They nearly fought among themselves, but Abū Barāʾ went between them and said, “Choose whoever you wish; I will be the first to obey and accept him.” The people desisted and agreed. They appointed Abū Barāʾ over the Banū ʿĀmir, and Masʿūd ibn Muʿattib al-Thaqafī—who was Thaqīf’s chief—over the Banū Naṣr, the Saʿd ibn Bakr, and Thaqīf, with his command subject to Subayʿ ibn Rabīʿah. ʿAwf ibn Ḥārithah al-Murrī led Ghaṭafān; ʿAbbās ibn Ḥayy al-Riʿlī, Abū Anas, led the Banū Sulaym; and Kudām ibn ʿUmayr led Fahm and ʿAdwān. These were the chiefs and commanders. He said: Subayʿah bint ʿAbd Shams ibn ʿAbd Manāf was married to Masʿūd ibn Muʿattib, and she bore him ʿUrwah ibn Masʿūd and al-Aswad ibn Masʿūd. He would gather shackles and chains, and she would say to him, “What are you doing with these?” He would say, “By God, I hope to fill your people with them.” She said, “That is up to you. But, by God, if you see them, you will know otherwise.” When Thaqīf was routed, Masʿūd fled. He fled without stopping for anything until he entered his wife Subayʿah’s quarters. He put his nose between her breasts and said, “I seek refuge in God, then in you.” She said, “No! You claimed…”

When they reached ʿUkāẓ and had spent the second day there, Subayʿ ibn Rabīʿah al-Naṣrī said, “People of Quraysh, your march against Quraysh amounts to nothing.” They said, “Why?” He said, “You will not see them muster this year.” Abū Barāʾ said, “What do you object to in that? Our market will take place, we will depart, and victory will be ours.” A man of the Banū Asad ibn Khuzaymah, who heard him, said, “Indeed, by God, Kinānah will surely come, will not stay away, and you will see no other outcome.” They argued until they wagered one hundred camels against one hundred and made a binding agreement. They had not yet left their gathering when a herald arrived and said:

“From Mecca have come the great masses,and the battalions come one after another.”

The Asadī rose up joyfully, reciting:

(rajaz)

“People, the host has arrived at the ʿUkāẓ fair,Ninety thousand, every one clad in armor.”

When Masʿūd ibn Muʿattib realized that Quraysh had come, he said to Qays, “Let me go and look among the people for you. If ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān is there, then none of Kinānah has stayed away from you of Kinānah.” Nothing startled him except the sight of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān on a camel, with a striped cloak wrapped around him. Masʿūd ibn Muʿattib returned to Qays and said, “Quraysh has come in its entirety. The people have made ready and drawn up their ranks.” Ḥarb ibn Umayyah stood arranging Kinānah’s ranks, with his brothers Sufyān, Abū Sufyān—namely ʿAnbasah ibn Umayyah—and Abū al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah beside him. That day they were called the ʿAnābis. Ḥarb ibn Umayyah put on two coats of mail and bound himself; Sufyān put on two coats of mail and bound himself; Abū Sufyān put on two coats of mail and bound himself; and Abū al-ʿĀṣ put on two coats of mail and bound himself. Al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib was with the ʿAnābis that day and bound himself with them as well. They said, “We will not leave until we die or prevail over them.” Qays drew up its ranks, which Abū Barāʾ ʿĀmir ibn Mālik ibn Jaʿfar arranged. Ḥarb ibn Umayyah took the banner, and Abū Barāʾ took Qays’s banner. Al-Ḥulayṣ ibn Yazīd, one of the Banū ʿAbd Manāh and then chief of the Aḥābīsh, came out and challenged them to single combat. Abū Ḥarb ibn ʿUqayl ibn Khuwaylid ibn ʿAwf ibn ʿUqayl ibn Kaʿb ibn Rabīʿah came out against him. They thrust at one another with their spears for a while, until the ʿUqaylī broke al-Ḥulayṣ ibn Yazīd’s arm. Then they separated, and the people advanced against one another and fought fiercely. Abū al-ʿĀṣ recited:

(rajaz)

“This is the time to strike at the backsWith every keen, sharp, battle-tested blade.”

At the beginning of the day, victory went to Qays over Kinānah, until the Banū Zuhrah and the Banū ʿAdī fled from Quraysh. Muʿammar ibn Ḥabīb and men of the Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy were killed, and a contingent of Quraysh was routed. But Ḥarb ibn Umayyah, his brothers, the rest of the Quraysh tribes, and the Aḥābīsh stood firm. As for the Banū Bakr, when they saw that the tide had turned in Qays’s favor against Quraysh, Balʿāʾ ibn Qays withdrew with them to Mount ʿUkāẓ and said, “Leave Quraysh—may God remove it! By God, if I had stopped you, not a single man of them would have escaped.” Ḥakīm ibn Ḥizām used to say, “I witnessed ʿUkāẓ. The Banū Bakr were harder on us than Qays: they turned away from us and left us.” Saʿīd ibn Yarbuʿ used to say, “I remember us that day: at the beginning of the day, the only ones who came at us were the Banū Bakr. They turned away from us and left us.” But at midday, Kinānah gained the upper hand over them and slaughtered them in great numbers. The Banū al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Manāh fought alongside Kinānah that day. They were in the vanguard, while Quraysh stood behind them; they were not with the Banū al-Ḥārith. One hundred men were killed that day beneath their banner, having stood firm against them. Qays was routed. Among its notables killed was ʿAbbās al-Riʿlī with a group of the Banū Sulaym. Thaqīf and the Banū ʿĀmir were routed, and ten men of the Banū ʿĀmir were killed. When a shaykh of the Banū Naṣr saw this, he called out, “People of Kinānah, you have gone too far in killing!” ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān answered him, “We are a people who go too far.” When Qays’s notables saw what their tribes were doing—fleeing—some of them bound themselves, including Subayʿ ibn Rabīʿah and others. Then he lay down and said, “People of the Banū Naṣr, fight for me or leave me.” The Banū Naṣr, the Banū Jusham, the Banū Saʿd ibn Bakr, and Fahm rallied to him, while the other tribes of Qays fled. They fought until midday. Then ʿUtbah ibn Rabīʿah called out; at that time he was a young man not yet thirty years old: “People of Quraysh, why are you killing one another? This is not sound judgment so young was he, that those advanced in years marveled at him that day. He had not yet come to understand, nor had he yet called for the peace he proposed. Then he sent word to Qays: “I will come and speak with you.” They said, “Yes”; Qays did not object. The tide turned against Qays at the end of the day. ʿUtbah went between them until they made peace. He said to Qays, “Withdraw, and this matter will be brought to its best and fairest outcome. You are in a sacred month; you have laid your market open to loss, your supplies have been cut off, and those near you are afraid.” Qays said, “We will never withdraw while we are avenged upon. Even if we all die, we will not withdraw.” ʿUtbah said, “They too have suffered losses. They have killed about as many as you have, and have wounded as many as you have.” Qays said, “Our dead are more numerous than theirs.” ʿUtbah said, “I propose a settlement that will be in your interest and fair to both sides: count the dead. If your number is greater, we will pay the difference; if theirs is greater, you will pay their difference.” Abū Barāʾ said, “Anyone who rejects this settlement will get something worse. We will accept it.” They agreed. He secured the agreement of Qays’s chiefs, Abū Barāʾ and Subayʿ ibn Rabīʿah; then he went to Ḥarb ibn Umayyah, Ibn Judʿān, and Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah and secured their agreement. The people separated and were safe. They counted the dead and found that Qays had the greater number— [They paid blood-money for] twenty men. On that day Ḥarb ibn Umayya gave his son Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb as a hostage; al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAlqama ibn Kilda gave his son al-Naḍr ibn al-Ḥārith as a hostage; and Sufyān ibn ʿAwf gave his son al-Ḥārith as a hostage, for the blood-money owed for the men—twenty blood-payments—until they should pay them. The people dispersed in every direction [and—] were saying, “ʿUtba ibn Rabīʿa kept the people apart.” He was remembered for this until the end of time, although he was a man of forbearance and calm among his clan. The war laid down its burdens between them, and they made a covenant and agreement that none of them would harm another over what had occurred between them concerning the affair of al-Bar­rāḍ, ʿUrwa, al-Ghaṭafānī, and al-Ghanawī. Quraysh departed, and they contributed toward the blood-money; they sent it to Qays and ransomed their men.

After that, Abū Barāʾ came on pilgrimage. Ibn Judʿān met him and said, “Abū Barāʾ, what weighed most heavily on you about your position that day?” Abū Barāʾ replied, “I kept thinking that the matter would not be brought to a settlement until I saw you. When I saw you, I knew that it would be settled. And it did come to a good outcome and a reconciliation.”

He said: al-Ḍaḥḥāk ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUrwa ibn al-Zubayr told me that Ḥakīm ibn Ḥizām said:

“I saw the Messenger of God ﷺ—at al-Fijār, where he was present.” He said: The Messenger of God ﷺ—mentioned al-Fijār and said, “I was present there with my uncles, and I shot arrows in it. I would not wish that I had not done so.” On the day he was present, the Prophet ﷺ—was twenty years old. Al-Fijār took place twenty years after the Elephant.

The remainder of the fourth Fijār, according to Abū ʿUbayda

He said: As for Abū ʿUbayda, he related that the Fijār of al-Bar­rāḍ, between Kināna and Qays, consisted of four days, with one day each year. Its first was the Day of Shammaṭa at ʿUkāẓ. The commanders on both sides were those we have mentioned, except for Abū Barāʾ. Hawāzin were beyond the ravine, Quraysh on this side of it, and Banū Kināna in the valley. Ḥarb ibn Umayya said to them:

“If Quraysh are overcome, do not leave your position.”

Hawāzin formed their ranks and took their positions; Quraysh formed their ranks, with Ibn Judʿān commanding one wing, Kurayz ibn Rabīʿa ibn Ḥabīb ibn ʿAbd Shams the other, and Ḥarb ibn Umayya in the center. Early in the day, the tide turned against Kināna in favor of Hawāzin. But when the day was nearly over and Kināna held firm, the killing among Quraysh grew severe. When those of Kināna who were in the valley saw this, they moved over to Quraysh and abandoned their position. When they did so, the killing among them grew severe, but they stood firm.
