Eighty men were killed beneath their banner. Others said: When Banū Bakr ibn ʿAbd Manāt saw this, Balʿāʾ ibn Qays said, “To preserve his people, [they joined Rukhm—]”; he withdrew with them to a mountain called Rukhm. He said, “Leave them; I wish not one of them had escaped.” The Day of Shammaṭa thus went in Hawāzin’s favor against Kināna, and no notable man of Quraysh was killed. At the end of the day Quraysh withdrew when Banū Bakr withdrew.

Then came the Day of al-ʿAblāʾ

Abū ʿUbayda said: These groups gathered, and the two sides met at the turning of the year, on the day the first of the days at ʿUkāẓ. They met at al-ʿAblāʾ, a white mountain beside ʿUkāẓ. Their commanders were the same men who had led them on the Day of Shammaṭa. Here too the tide turned in Hawāzin’s favor against Kināna.

Then came the Day of Sharab

He said: Then the two sides gathered at the turning of the year, on the second of the two days at ʿUkāẓ. They met at Sharab, in ʿUkāẓ, under the same commanders as before. No day was greater than this one. On that day Ibn Judʿān brought a thousand men mounted on a thousand camels. They met, after Hawāzin had twice prevailed over Kināna at the turning of the year, at al-Ḥarīra, a lava field beside ʿUkāẓ on the side facing the south wind. It then extends toward Mecca from the direction of the east wind, ending just below Qarn. Their commanders were the same, except that Balʿāʾ had died; the commander after him was Juthāma ibn Qays. That day Sufyān ibn Umayya was killed, along with eight men of Kināna, whom ʿUmar ibn Asīd ibn Mālik ibn Rabīʿa ibn ʿĀmir ibn Ṣaʿṣaʿa killed. Warqāʾ ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Mālik ibn Rabīʿa killed ʿUmar ibn ʿĀmir, Abū Kanaf, the two sons of Iyās, and ʿAmr ibn Ayyūb. Khidāsh ibn Zuhayr mentioned them in his poetry.

These were the five days of al-Fijār on which they clashed, over four years. The first was the Day of Nakhlah, when Hawāzin pursued them; it was an even contest, with neither side prevailing. Then came the Day of Shammaṭa, which went in Hawāzin’s favor against Kināna; then the first day at ʿUkāẓ, the Day of al-ʿAblāʾ, which also went in Hawāzin’s favor against Kināna; then the second day at ʿUkāẓ, the Day of Sharab, which went in Banū Kināna’s favor against— Hawāzin. No day between them was greater than that one. Then came the Day of al-Ḥarīra, the last of their days. He said: After that, a man among them might encounter one or two men, or more or fewer, and they would fight; sometimes they would kill one another. Ibn Maḥmiyya, a man of Banū al-Dayl ibn Bakr, met Abū Khirāsh Zuhayr at al-Ṣifāḥ. Zuhayr said, “I am under the protection of the sacred precinct; I have come on pilgrimage.” He replied, “You never meet anyone without saying, ‘I am on pilgrimage,’” and killed him. Then he regretted it and said:

(Rajaz)

O God, the man of ʿĀmir who came on pilgrimage—

I did not come here to offer an excuse.

Then the people called one another to make peace, on the condition that whichever side had the greater number of slain would receive blood-money for the difference from the other side. They agreed to meet at ʿUkāẓ to count the slain, made a pact, and gave one another assurances that they would carry this through. They established a truce between them under which they would meet for that purpose. Wahb ibn Muʿtab refused this, opposed his people, and would not accept it until they had avenged their losses. Umayya ibn Ḥarthān ibn Sukr said of this: (Kāmil) Wahb, the man of the house of Muʿtab,

The mischief-makers have grown weary, but you have not;

You strive to kindle it and feed its blaze... Whenever your people pursue peace, you hold back.

Wahb secretly stirred up Hawāzin against Kināna, when they were on the verge of making peace. Hawāzin sent a cavalry force led by Salama ibn Saʿlāʾ al-Bukāʾī and Khālid ibn Hawdha. Among them were men of Banū Hilāl, led by Rabīʿa ibn Abī Ẓabyān, and men of Banū Naṣr, led by Mālik ibn ʿAwf. They raided Banū Layth in the desert of al-Ghamīm while they were off guard, fought them, and Mālik fought while reciting:

(Rajaz)

A beardless youth, whose judgment is guided by gray-bearded men.

This is the first day on which Mālik ibn ʿAwf is mentioned. That day Banū Mudlij killed ʿUbayd ibn ʿAwf al-Bukāʾī and Subayʿ ibn al-Muʾammal of Jisr ibn Muḥārib. Then Banū Layth were routed, and the killing among Banū al-Malūḥ ibn Yaʿmar grew severe. Thirty of them were killed, their women were taken captive, and their livestock was driven off. The raiders then turned back, and Khuzāʿa confronted them, seeing an opportunity, and fought them.

When they saw there was no way to avoid fighting them, they said, “Give us compensation from your booty.” They refused, so the others let them pass. Mālik ibn ʿAwf said: (Ṭawīl)

We brought the horses from the valley of Layya,And from Jildān, lean and hard-hoofed.

The weaklings of Khuzāʿa threatened us with war;What war can a weakling wage, turning over a bread-board?

Then the people called one another to make peace, and hostages were given to guarantee payment of blood-money to whichever side had the greater number of slain. Peace was concluded, and the war laid down its burdens. This is the end of the account of the fourth Fijār according to Abū ʿUbayda.

Account of the Ḥilf al-Fuḍūl, from Ḥabīb, from Abū al-Bakhtarī

He said: al-Ḍaḥḥāk ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUrwa ibn al-Zubayr told me: I heard Ḥakīm ibn Ḥizām say: The Ḥilf al-Fuḍūl took place when Quraysh returned from al-Fijār. The Messenger of God ﷺ—was then twenty years old, and it was twenty years after the Elephant.

They said: Al-Fijār was in Shawwāl, and the pact was in Dhū al-Qaʿda. This pact was the noblest pact ever made. The first to speak of it and call for it was al-Zubayr ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ibn Hāshim. That was because a man from among the Arabs, or from among the non-Arab foreigners, who came to trade might be wronged in Mecca. The incident that prompted it was this: a man from Banū Zubayd came with goods and sold them to al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil al-Sahmī, who withheld the price from him. The Zubaydī demanded his due from him, but he did not give it to him. The Zubaydī then went to the confederates—ʿAbd al-Dār, Makhzūm, Jumah, Sahm, and ʿAdī—but they refused to help him, rebuked him, and drove him away. When the Zubaydī saw the danger, he went up to Abū Qubays before sunrise, while Quraysh were gathered in their meeting places around the Kaʿba, and cried out:

(Basīṭ)

O men, aid the man whose merchandise is wronged,

Here in Mecca, far from his clan and his people.

The sacred is inviolable to one who honors its inviolability,

But my garment has no sanctity for the wearer of treachery.

He said: Al-Zubayr ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib set about the matter and said, “This is intolerable.” Banū Hāshim, Zuhra, and Taym gathered at the house of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān, who prepared food for them. In Dhū al-Qaʿda, a sacred month, they formed an alliance while standing, clasping one another’s hands, and swore and pledged by God, saying: “We shall stand with the wronged until his right is restored, for as long as the sea wets a tuft of wool.” They also pledged mutual support in their livelihoods. Quraysh called this alliance the Ḥilf al-Fuḍūl. Al-Zubayr ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib composed these verses about it: (Wāfir)

I have sworn that we shall form an alliance among them,

Even though we all belong to this place.

We shall call it al-Fuḍūl when we make it,

By which a stranger under protection will be honored.

If an enemy seeks to wage war against it,

We shall stand with our swords, bent and drawn.

Those around the House will know that we

Refuse humiliation and shun all disgrace.

He said: Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh told me, from al-Zuhrī, from Ṭalḥa ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn— ʿAwf, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Azhar, from Jubayr ibn Muṭʿim, who said: The Messenger of God ﷺ said: “I would not wish to have red camels in exchange for the pact I witnessed in the house of Ibn Judʿān, nor to break it. Hāshim, Zuhra, and Taym swore an alliance to stand with the wronged for as long as the sea wets a tuft of wool. If I were summoned to it, I would answer. It was the Pact of the Virtuous.” Abū al-Bakhtarī said: Maʿmar related to me, from al-Zuhrī, from Muḥammad ibn Jubayr ibn Muṭʿim, who said: ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān said to Muḥammad ibn Jubayr, “What do you say about this pact”—meaning the Pact of the Virtuous? ʿAbd al-Malik was laughing. I replied, “I am not of it, Commander of the Faithful.” ʿAbd al-Malik said, “As for me and you, we are not of it.” I replied, “The Commander of the Faithful has spoken truly.” Then I said, “Ibn al-Zubayr claims it.” He said, “By God, he is in the wrong.”

Abū al-Bakhtarī said: Al-Ḍaḥḥāk ibn ʿUthmān related to me, from Yaḥyā ibn ʿUrwa, from his father, from Ḥakīm ibn Ḥizām. He said: Quṣayy had assigned the council house, the banner, and the provision of hospitality to his son ʿAbd al-Dār, because ʿAbd al-Dār was less distinguished than his brothers. His brothers had become eminent and had established themselves. He granted him these offices so that he might catch up with them, not because he considered him the best or most noble among them.7 When death came upon ʿAbd al-Dār, he assigned the offices to ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-Dār. Umayya ibn ʿAbd Shams said to ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-Dār, “Relinquish one of these three willingly.” He refused. Umayya said, “Then I shall throttle you.” ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-Dār called upon Quraysh for aid. Banū Makhzūm, Jumah, Sahm, and ʿAdī said, “We will protect these offices for you and form an alliance with you over them.” He said, “Yes.” So they swore an alliance and protected them for him. Ḥakīm said: We, Banū Asad, ʿAbd Manāf, Zuhra, Taym, and al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr, remained apart; there was no pact between us until Quraysh returned from the sacrilegious war. Banū Hāshim, Taym, Zuhra, Asad, and al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr came together to swear an alliance and protect every wronged person in Mecca, and to call that pact the Pact of the Virtuous. Ibn Judʿān gathered them in his house and prepared food for them. They swore by God, saying:

“We shall not break this pact for as long as the sea wets a tuft of wool, and we shall leave no wronged person in Mecca without aid.”

Ḥakīm said: I saw the Messenger of God ﷺ present at that pact that day in the house of Ibn Judʿān. The one who wrote it down for them was al-Zubayr ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. Ḥakīm said:

There was no pact among Quraysh except the first pact—that of Banū Makhzūm, Jumah, Sahm, ʿAdī, and Banū ʿAbd al-Dār—and this pact. They said: Elders of Quraysh from Banū Hāshim, Zuhra, and Taym used to say, “There had never been a pact among us until this Pact of the Virtuous.” The other alliances had been made earlier. There is a third transmission of this report, from Abū al-Bakhtarī, from al-Ḍaḥḥāk ibn ʿUthmān, from Yaḥyā ibn ʿUrwa. The beginning of this chain is: “Al-Ḍaḥḥāk ibn ʿUthmān related to me.”

The Affair of the Perfumed Ones and the Confederates: Ibn al-Kalbī’s Account

They said: Quṣayy was the most eminent of the people of Mecca, and no one disputed his position there. He built the Council House, where Quraysh would conduct their affairs and attend to matters of marriage, war, and consultation whenever something arose among them. A girl, upon reaching the age when she was to wear a shift, would have it cut there, as an auspicious act and in honor of the place. When Quṣayy grew old and became frail, he assigned the custodianship, the council house, and the provision of hospitality and the watering and the banner to ʿAbd al-Dār, who was his firstborn and was weak. He singled him out for these offices so he could catch up with his other brothers. The provision of hospitality was an expense raised by Quraysh to host the pilgrims. When Quṣayy died, ʿAbd Manāf took over Quṣayy’s affairs, and Quraysh’s affairs came under his authority. He maintained his father’s arrangements after him and laid out plots in Mecca beyond those he had already allotted to his people. When ʿAbd Manāf died, the offices we have mentioned belonged to Banū ʿAbd al-Dār. Then Banū ʿAbd Manāf sought to take them from them, saying, “We have a better claim to them.” Banū ʿAbd al-Dār refused, and Quraysh split over the matter. Zuhra, Taym ibn Murra, Banū Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā, and al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr sided with Banū ʿAbd Manāf; Sahm, Jumah, Makhzūm, and ʿAdī sided with Banū ʿAbd al-Dār. ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy withdrew from both factions. Banū ʿAbd Manāf and their allies were the Perfumed Ones; ʿAbd al-Dār and their allies were the Confederates. ʿĀtika bint ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib brought out a large bowl of perfume, and they dipped their hands into it. The others slaughtered camels and dipped their hands in their blood, so they were called the Confederates. A man of Banū ʿAdī named al-Aswad ibn Ḥāritha licked a drop of blood, and they licked some from him, so they were called the Lickers of Blood. When they were on the verge of fighting and each tribe had drawn up against another—Banū ʿAbd Manāf against Sahm, ʿAbd al-Dār against Asad, Makhzūm against Taym, Jumah against Zuhra, and ʿAdī against al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr—they then worked toward reconciliation. They made peace on the terms that Banū ʿAbd Manāf would receive the watering, Banū Asad the provision of hospitality, and the custodianship, council house, and banner would remain with Banū ʿAbd al-Dār. At that time, Abū Ṭalḥa ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAbd al-Dār held the custodianship, and the Council House became the property of ʿĀmir ibn Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn ʿAbd al-Dār. Muʿāwiya bought it from— ʿIkrima ibn ʿĀmir ibn Hāshim for one hundred thousand dirhams. It belongs to the emirate today.

Abū Jaʿfar said: Among the favors God bestowed on al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, along with his other merits, was that no one but al-ʿAbbās was permitted to spend the nights of Minā in Mecca during the pilgrimage. This was permitted to him alone because of the watering.

The Death of al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīra and His Bequest

Hishām said: Ziyād ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ṭufayl al-Bakkāʾī related to us, from Muḥammad ibn Isḥāq and Isḥāq ibn ʿAmmār—who was Ibn al-Jaṣṣāṣ, the transmitter. He said: Others claim that one day al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīra passed between the gates of Banū Qumayr ibn Ḥabashiyya ibn Salūl ibn Kaʿb ibn ʿAmr of Khuzāʿa, dragging his two cloaks. One of them shot an arrow at him, striking the muscle of his lower leg—the injury to which Gabriel alluded. They claim that it grew so large that it became like a great waterskin and filled with pus and blood. One night, while he was asleep and his daughter was with him, his leg burst. His daughter said, “Father, the waterskin has split!”

He said:

“My daughter, it is not a waterskin; it is your father’s leg.”

He said: Ziyād al-Bakkāʾī related to me, from Muḥammad ibn Isḥāq, with his chain of transmission: When death approached al-Walīd, he summoned his sons. There were three of them: Hishām, Khālid, and al-Mughīra, sons of al-Walīd. He said: My father related to me that he summoned his sons Hishām, Khālid, al-Walīd, al-Fākih, Abū Qays, Qays, ʿAbd Shams, and ʿUmāra, and said to them, “My sons, I charge you with three things; do not neglect them. My blood is owed by Khuzāʿa, so do not let them go unavenged. By God, I know they are innocent of it, but I fear that after today you will be reviled on account of it. My interest is owed by Thaqīf, so do not leave it until you collect it. My bridal due is owed by Abū Uẓayhir al-Dawsī, so do not let him escape you over it.” Abū Uẓayhir had married him to a daughter of his, then kept her from him and did not let her join him before he died.

Returning to Ibn al-Kalbī’s account: He said to them, “My blood is owed by Khuzāʿa, so do not let it go unavenged; my interest is owed by Thaqīf, so do not leave it until you collect it. My plunder, and the blood of my brother al-Fākih ibn al-Mughīra, are owed by Banū Jadhīma ibn ʿĀmir ibn ʿAbd Manāt ibn Kināna, so do not let them escape you. The bishop of Damascus, al-Muqawqis, owes me a thousand dinars, which Khālid knows about. My bridal due is owed by Abū Uẓayhir: he married me to his daughter and took her bridal gift from me, then kept her from me, slighting my right and my honor. Do not let him escape you over it. This is my bequest; carry it out.”

His sons said to him, “By God, we know of no Arab who ever bequeathed his sons anything worse than what you have bequeathed us.” Khālid ibn al-Walīd sent a thousand dinars to al-Muqawqis.

Al-Bakkāʾī said in his report: When al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīra died, Banū Makhzūm rose up against Khuzāʿa, seeking compensation for his blood. They said, “He was killed by an arrow from one of his companions.” Banū Kaʿb ibn ʿAmr had an alliance with ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ibn Hāshim. Khuzāʿa resisted them until they exchanged verses and the matter became grave. The man who struck al-Walīd with his arrow was one of Banū Kaʿb ibn ʿAmr of Khuzāʿa.

Ibn al-Kalbī said: Banū Makhzūm, together with al-Walīd’s sons, rose up against Khuzāʿa seeking blood money for al-Walīd. They said, “He was killed by one of your men.” Khuzāʿa refused them this and denied that their man had died from that wound, until they exchanged verses and the matter became grave.

He said: Isḥāq ibn ʿAmmār related to me, saying: Hishām ibn al-Walīd said about this: ( wāfir )

“Have Banū Kaʿb ibn ʿAmr gone awaywithout yet avenging al-Walīd’s blood?

“If you do not pay compensation, you will know usby the tent ropes, where the Black One is driven off.”

When hostilities broke out between them, some of Khuzāʿa admitted it. Al-Jawn al-Khuzāʿī said the following—or, it is said, it was spoken by Nabḥān ibn Hilāl ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Ḍāṭir ibn Ḥabashiyya ibn Salūl ibn Kaʿb ibn ʿAmr ibn Rabīʿa. Rabīʿa is Laḥy, and ʿAmr is the ancestor of all Khuzāʿa: ( ṭawīl )

“We slew your al-Walīd on the open ground;no one of his kin is beyond such a fate.

“He fell face down, humbled,and his death was of little consequence to us.

“If you, Makhzūm, seek our blood price,no bird will fly among you with good fortune.

“We refused what they hoped to obtain from us;at the tent ropes we have ready a firm defense.”

“When Ghobshān are summoned on a day of hardship,and lions hem in the edges of their thicket,

“we prevail and bring ruin down upon our enemywith a blow that leaves the base fellow disgraced.”

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Umayya ibn al-Mughīra al-Makhzūmī said: ( ṭawīl )

“Have you not seen the slave revile his lord,then go unpunished awhile, before his brow is split?

“I guarantee that you will march away and flee,and leave al-Ẓahrān, where its foxes howl.

“You will leave the water at Juzʿat Aṭraqā,and ask which arāk trees bear the choicest fruit.

“We are a people whose blood is not shed with impunity,nor does one we fight rise to power.”

Al-Jawn ibn Abī al-Jawn answered him: ( ṭawīl )

“By God, al-Walīd will not be denied justice,and you shall yet see a day when its stars are swept away.

“One of your well-fed men after another will fall,and after death their drinking places will be opened by force.”

“When you have eaten your bread and your sakhīna,

“every one of you will weep for al-Walīd and mourn him.

“Ibn Ḍirāb shot him, and his arrow did not miss;a little pus flowed, if you see it above his calf.

“He fell lifeless, sprawled on his face,while his kin gathered around him, screaming.”

Al-Jawn ibn Abī al-Jawn spoke, recalling his alliance with Banū ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib and insulting Banū Makhzūm: ( ṭawīl )

“Who can make the lone ape, when he claimsnobility, a welcome guest upon the perilous stallion?”

“Their faces are black and ugly, as though they werethe faces of he-goats scattered in pens.”

Al-Ḥārith ibn Hishām ibn al-Mughīra said the following to the Aḥābīsh, Quraysh’s allies, urging them on. The Aḥābīsh were al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Manāt ibn Kināna, ʿAḍl, al-Qāra, al-Ḥayyā, and al-Muṣṭaliq of Khuzāʿa: ( wāfir )

“Is there someone to carry a message from me to the two Laylāns,their allies and their dwelling places, the battlefields—”

Qumayr wrongs us, turning away from us,while we seek them and our adversaries seek a settlement.

The Banū Qumayr seek reconciliation,though they have not been frightened by an army or a great force.

A squadron of horses passes between us,bearing the white blades and thirsty spears,

And noble men are struck down between us,among them those whom the tall lances break.

Al-Bakkāʾī said: Then the people drew back from one another, and they understood that the tribe feared only the disgrace. Khuzāʿah paid them part of the blood-money, and the two sides departed with some matters still unsettled. ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zibʿarā said to Buṣr ibn Sufyān al-Qumayrī: (al-ṭawīl meter)

“Tell Buṣr ibn Sufyān this message;let the well-informed man who stands alone convey it from me.”

This is a poem included among his verses. When Buṣr ibn Sufyān heard what Ibn al-Zibʿarā had said, he took his son by the hand while Quraysh were seated in the Ḥijr, and said: “People of Quraysh! You are the people with whom we most strongly contend in war, and the people whose peace we most cherish. You have accused us of killing al-Walīd, as you have accused us, but we have neither paid his blood-money nor made amends for him. Here is my son, pledged to you for the blood-money.” Khālid ibn al-Walīd took him and said, “We accept.” He took the boy home, fed and clothed him in a fine garment, and perfumed him. Then he said, “Go to your father. If he owes us anything, he will discharge it for us.” When the boy came to his father, he told him what Khālid had said. His father replied, “Do as he says. By God, I shall discharge what he is owed.” The blood-money was customarily paid in installments over several years, but he paid it in one year. Then the Messenger of God ﷺ performed the Farewell Pilgrimage, and some of the blood-money remained unpaid. He set it aside among the claims arising from the pre-Islamic era that he annulled, and nothing more was paid after that. When the people made peace, al-Jawn ibn Abī al-Jawn—or ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Ḥabtar al-Khuzāʿī—said: (al-ṭawīl meter)

“Did the beautiful woman say, on the day I met her,words of good counsel to a man who was not ignorant?

She said to us, when we made peace, in astonishmentat what we had borne for al-Walīd—and she spoke:

‘Do you give al-Walīd his due, as though he were wronged,though you have not yet seen a day of great turmoil?’

We mingled war with peace, and the two became equal;every barefoot man and every shod man followed his desire.

Yesterday, when their chiefs stood forth,they wished upon me a boiling like that of cauldrons—

the Banū ʿAbd Manāf and Kinānah, who call themselvesthe Banū ʿAlī because ʿAlī ibn Masʿūd al-Ghassānī raised them, and so they were ascribed to him: (al-ṭawīl meter)

‘Had they brought forward what they put last,their tribes would have been exposed as all valiant and bold,

men of long reach—may God multiply his goodness—who grew into men of eloquence and bounty.

What did we seek among ourselves from his great standing,or from any lineage attributed to him thereafter?’”

Then al-Jawn did not stop there: he boasted of al-Walīd’s killing and claimed that they had struck him down. All of this was false. As a result, what they had feared befell al-Walīd, his sons, and his people. Al-Jawn said: (al-wāfir meter)

“Al-Mughīrah claims that Kaʿbhas great standing among them in Mecca.

Do not be astonished, Mughīrah, to see usthere, where the foolish pretender and the worthy man walk.

Our fathers are there, and there we were born,as Mount Thabīr is firmly set in its place.

Al-Mughīrah said that onlyto make our standing known, or to provoke us.

If al-Walīd’s blood has gone unavenged,we shall exact blood in return—of this you know.

The fortunate slayer shot him with a deadly arrow,as he was gasping, full of breathlessness.

He fell, belly-down in Mecca, stretched out,looking like a camel as he fell.

The great she-camels of Jaʿdah, with their thick fleeces and rich milk,will suffice me for the withholding of Abū Hishām’s due.”

“When we compete, you are a supporter of ʿAbd Shujaʿ,base in lineage, of short descent.”

The account of the killing of Abū Uzhayhir al-Dawsī

Abū Saʿīd related to us, from Ibn Ḥabīb, from Hishām, from his father, who said: Among the events concerning Abū Uzhayhir ibn Anīs ibn al-Khaysaq ibn Mālik ibn Saʿd ibn Kaʿb ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿĀmir—who was al-Ghuṭayrif—ibn Bakr ibn Yashkur ibn Mubashshir ibn Ṣaʿb ibn Dahmān ibn Naṣr ibn Zahrān ibn Kaʿb ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Kaʿb ibn Mālik ibn Naṣr ibn al-Azd was that he was an ally of Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb, and the Daws were his maternal uncles. He was known only as “the Dawsī.” He and Abū Sufyān would sit together in their days in a tent of theirs, settling disputes among those who came to the place where they were. Abū Uzhayhir had married his daughter ʿĀtikah to Abū Sufyān, and she bore him Muḥammad and ʿAnbasah. He married Zaynab, the daughter of Abū Uzhayhir, to ʿUtbah ibn Rabīʿah, and she bore him Rabīʿah and Nuʿmān. Then Abū Ḥabīb ibn Muhashsham ibn al-Mughīrah married her after him, and she bore him children. He married another daughter of his to al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm, then kept her from him and did not let her join him until he died. He said: After marrying her to him and taking the bride-price from him, Abū Uzhayhir learned that he was harsh toward women and beat them. So Abū Uzhayhir kept his daughter from him and withheld the bride-price. [The word “he said”] Ibn Ḥabīb said: Ibrāhīm ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Nuʿaym al-Azdī related, on the authority of Azd elders, that she had been sent to him as a bride. When she was brought to him, he said, “Am I nobler, or your father?” She replied, “No, my father is nobler, for my father is the lord of the people of al-Sarāh, and the Arabs defer to his judgment. You, however, are only the lord of your own people, among whom there are men who contest your nobility.” He raised his hand and slapped her, so she fled to her father. He swore that he would not see her and withheld the bride-price.

Ibn al-Kalbī said: When the people came to the market of Dhū al-Majāz, one of the Arab markets, Abū Uzhayhir stayed with Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb. The sons of al-Walīd came and killed him. The killer was Hishām ibn al-Walīd. Abū Sufyān had a daughter of Abū Uzhayhir, and Abū Uzhayhir was a notable among his people. He killed him in return for the bridal gift of al-Walīd that he had in his possession, in accordance with his father’s instruction to him. This was after the Messenger of God ﷺ had emigrated, the affair of Badr had ended, and those among the Quraysh notable polytheists who were struck down there had been killed. Ibn al-Kalbī said: The Messenger of God ﷺ summoned Ḥassān ibn Thābit and said to him: “Ḥassān! A quarrel has arisen between the Muṭayyabūn and their confederates, and a man has been killed. Compose verses about the killing of Abū Uzhayhir to incite the Muṭayyabūn against the confederates.”

The Muṭayyabūn were five clans: all the Banū ʿAbd Manāf—that is, the Banū Hāshim, ʿAbd Shams, al-Muṭṭalib, and Nawfal, all sons of ʿAbd Manāf; the Banū Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā; the Banū Zuhrah ibn Kilāb; the Banū Taym ibn Murrah; and the Banū al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr. The confederates were five clans, the “lickers of blood”: the Banū ʿAbd al-Dār ibn Quṣayy; the Banū Makhzūm ibn Yaqaẓah; the Banū Jumḥ ibn ʿAmr; the Banū Sahm ibn ʿAmr ibn Huṣayṣ; and the Banū ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb. The Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy, Muḥārib ibn Fihr, and the Banū al-Adram ibn Ghālib stood apart from the two groups. The Banū ʿAbd al-Dār followed the Banū Asad; Makhzūm followed Taym; Jumḥ followed Zuhrah; ʿAdī followed the Banū al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr; and Sahm followed the Banū ʿAbd Manāf. He said: Ḥassān rose up, urging vengeance for Abū Uzhayhir’s blood, reproaching Abū Sufyān for failing to protect his guest and calling him a coward. He said: (al-ṭawīl meter)

“Tomorrow, at dawn, the people of the two sides of Dhū al-Majāzwill depart, but the guest of Ibn Ḥarb at al-Mughammis will not depart.

Hishām ibn al-Walīd has clothed you in his garments;wear them out, and make new ones just like them.

“He has had his fill of him and become a man of standing;you have become slack, neither trotting nor running.

If the elders at Badr had witnessed it,the slain men’s necks would have been drenched in fresh, rose-red blood.

The farting camel did not protect his guest’s honor,nor did Hind protect her father from disgrace.”

When Yazīd ibn Abī Sufyān heard his words, he went out, gathered the Banū ʿAbd Manāf, and called to the Muṭayyabūn. They assembled, while Abū Sufyān was at Dhū al-Majāz. He said: “People! Abū Sufyān has failed to protect his guest and son-in-law; he will seek vengeance.” Yazīd prepared himself, assembled them, and went out at their head. When the confederates saw this, they gathered and pitched their tents nearby. When Abū Sufyān ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib saw this, he mounted his horse and went to Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb to inform him. the news. Abū Sufyān was forbearing and formidable, and loved his people intensely. He feared there might be war among Quraysh over Abū Uzhayhir. He called for his horse, laid a felt saddlecloth on it, mounted, took a spear, and set out for Mecca. The two forces were there, and Abū Sufyān ibn al-Ḥārith kept saying to Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb on the way, “May my father and mother be your ransom! Restrain the people.” But he would not answer him or comply in any way until he arrived among them. He stopped between the two forces, which had made ready to fight. He looked and saw that the standard was with his son Yazīd, who stood armed among his people, the Muṭayyabūn. He pulled the standard from his hand and struck him on his helmet with it, knocking him down. Then he said, “May God disgrace you! Do you want to set Quraysh against one another over a man of the Azd?” “We shall pay them the blood-money, if they accept it.” Then he called out at the top of his voice: “People! Our enemy is behind us, rejoicing”—meaning the Prophet ﷺ—“and when we have finished with him, we shall consider what lies between us and you. Let each of you return to his home.” They dispersed, and the affair was settled. Abū Sufyān heard what Ḥassān had said and replied, “Ḥassān wants to set some of us against others over a man of Daws. By God, that is a wretched opinion.”

He said: When the people of al-Ṭāʾif embraced Islam, the Messenger of God ﷺ spoke to Khālid about the usury due to al-Walīd, which was among the people of Thaqīf because his father had instructed him regarding it. We know of no vengeance for Abū Uzhayhir; Islam intervened between the people. However, Ḍirār ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ibn Mirdās al-Fihrī went with a group of Quraysh to the land of Daws, and stayed with a woman called Umm Ghaylān, a client of Daws. She combed women’s hair and prepared brides. She wanted to Daws avenged them for Abū Uzhayr. Umm Ghaylān and the women with her stood before them until they prevented the attack.

Al-Bakkāʾī said: Abū Sufyān sent for two hundred camels and tethered Abū Uzhayr to them. Then he sent them with a party of Quraysh, including Ḍirār ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, to Abū Uzhayr’s people in al-Sarāt. They brought the blood-money to Abū Uzhayr’s kinsmen, who accepted it from them. They then gave them time. But when they were about to depart, the Ghaṭārīf attacked them. These were the people of al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿĀmir al-Ghaṭrīf, along with al-Namir and Daws. They killed some of the party, while others escaped. Ḍirār ibn al-Khaṭṭāb fled and sought protection from a woman of Daws called Umm Ghaylān. She took him into her house and gave him protection. The Azd came, and when she saw them, she brought out her daughters, unveiled, and stood before him. When Daws came to demand him, she said, “I have given him my protection, and your inviolable rights stand unveiled before him. If you wish, then tear through the veil and violate his protected status.” They left him to her, and he departed, saying: (al-ṭawīl)

May God reward Umm Ghaylān well on our behalf,

and her women, who were disheveled and without ornaments.

They warded off death when it had nearly arrived,

though the weapons of those seeking vengeance had been drawn.

She summoned Daws, and its ravines streamed forth

with a man, followed by the receptive ravines.

And ʿAmr—may God reward him well—did not falter,

nor did his limbs grow slack on my account.

I drew my sword, then stood with its blade,

and what life would I fight for after my own?

They report that Ḥassān ibn Thābit said: (al-kāmil)

O Daws, Abū Uzhayr’s echoes have become

pledged to the wine-seller—so kindle

a war that makes the child grow gray;

only a base, shameless man accepts disgrace.

Mourn your brother with every slender, dusky sword,

and every white, broad, straight sword like lightning.

And with a swift, high-stepping mare, swift in her run,

like a wolf in a desolate, wide plain.

If you kill a hundred men in return for him, that is still too little—

for Abū Uzhayr, one of the men of al-Abṭaḥ.

The Azd were not satisfied with that. They raided Quraysh, killing many of them, and began setting ambushes along the caravans, killing whoever they could reach until Quraysh gave them satisfaction. For each camel-load that went in or came out, they paid a dinar, and the Azd accepted that. The Dawsī said: (al-ṭawīl)

/

“Convey to Ḥassān—I mean Ibn Thābit—

that we have avenged the slain man of al-Muḍayyaḥ:

thirty of the sons of Fihr ibn Mālik,

and twenty less one, who did not make it in time.

We left the leaders of the clan—Taym and ʿĀmir,

Sahm and Makhzūm—like sheep at slaughter.

And there must be another attack on their Meccan man,

to delight the eye of the afflicted, slaughtered one.

Here they are, Ibn al-Furayʿah: emaciated horses,

in scattered groups, like quail setting out at evening.

They will make Hishām ibn al-Walīd and his clan forget

Sukhaynah’s sale of the silk-striped cloak.”

Sukhaynah was what Quraysh were called in mockery, because they ate khanīz. Surāqah al-Akbar ibn Mirdās said this about the levy Quraysh imposed on the Azd after the Azd had killed some of them, and he named some of those killed: (al-wāfir)

The Banū Asad know well that we

entered the sacred precincts, bearing our marks.

At ʿAkkak we left the two sons of Hishām,

Ḥarb and al-Musayyab, when we met them, and ʿAwf; after him, al-ʿAwwām was a hostage.

We were not of Quraysh, nor did we flee in fear.

We left nine of them to the birds

at Mecca, and the beasts cast them aside.

When we had settled the debt, they said,

“We want peace.” We said, “We agree.”

We imposed a fixed levy on them,

to be paid as tribute each year.

For us, a stipulated dinar on every caravan;

with it, they ward off the cutting of throats.

Were it not for that, Quraysh would not have roamed

north and south through the lands.

They continued paying this to the Azd until the Prophet appeared and abolished it, along with the other customs of the Jāhiliyyah. Al-Musayyab ibn ʿĀbid ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm was killed; Abū Ṣafīḥ al-Dawsī, maternal uncle of Abū Uzhayr, had encountered them and killed them.

As for al-Walīd’s words to his sons, “My plunder among Banū Jadhīmah and my brother’s blood”: al-Walīd was returning from Abyssinia on a trading journey, accompanied by a party of Quraysh. Among them was ʿAwf ibn ʿAbd ʿAwf ibn ʿAbd [ibn—] al-Ḥārith ibn Zuhrah, father of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf, and ʿAffān ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah. With ʿAwf was his son ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, and with ʿAffān was his son ʿUthmān. Ibn al-Kalbī said: They had been returning from Yemen, carrying the property of a man of Banū Jadhīmah ibn ʿĀmir ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Kinānah to his heirs; he had died in Yemen. A man among them called Khālid ibn Hishām claimed the property and met them in the territory of Banū Jadhīmah before they reached the dead man’s heirs. He demanded it from them, but they refused. He then fought them, with his people, over the property, intending to take it. They fought him, and al-Fākih ibn al-Mughīrah and ʿAwf were killed. ʿAffān and his son ʿUthmān escaped, and they seized al-Fākih’s property and the property of ʿAwf ibn ʿAbd, and they departed with it. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, it is reported, had struck Khālid ibn Hishām al-Judhamī, his father’s killer, and escaped. They plundered al-Walīd’s property and took captive a number of Quraysh from Banū al-Mughīrah and another number of Quraysh, including Mālik ibn ʿUmaylah ibn al-Sabbāq ibn ʿAbd al-Dār ibn Quṣayy. Al-Bakkāʾī said, concerning al-Fākih ibn al-Mughīrah ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm and his death: Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah sent ransom for his companions, and they were freed. Mālik ibn ʿUmaylah, however, was not among those freed. Mālik said the following, reproaching Hishām: (al-kāmil)

Never forget, Abū al-Walīd, our service,

or what we did in days gone by.

We have wealth, and it is no small amount;

we have a share in nobility and good sense.

Even if time has changed those who live through it,

when it was harsh, we were not base men.

As for ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf, it is reported that he had struck Khālid ibn Hishām, the man of Banū Jadhīmah who killed his father, and killed him. When ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf killed him in revenge for his father, he composed these lines. Then Ḍirār ibn al-Khaṭṭāb went to Khālid ibn ʿUbayd ibn Jābir, Abū Qāriẓ, one of Banū al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Manāf, who was an ally of Banū Zuhrah, and said, “Help us recover our caravan, our blood, and what was taken from us.” He replied, “I will help you against them, but not them against you.” Ḍirār ibn al-Khaṭṭāb said about this: (al-mutaqārib)

I summoned Khālid to a course of action

that would have brought glory, but Khālid let it go to waste.

Quraysh then prepared to raid Banū Jadhīmah. When the latter heard of it, they said to Quraysh:

“Your companions’ loss did not come about by a decision among us, as far as we know—or so they said. We will pay you compensation for any blood or property lost before our time.” Quraysh accepted the compensation and called off the war. Some time afterward, the Messenger of God sent Khālid ibn al-Walīd to Banū Jadhīmah ibn ʿĀmir. He fought them at a watering place of theirs called al-Ghumayṣāʾ and killed four hundred young men. When Hishām ibn al-Walīd killed Abū Uzhayr, Banū al-Mughīrah sent people to ask what Banū ʿAbd Manāf were doing and what they were gathering to do. Their informant came to them and told them how angry Banū ʿAbd Manāf were. Abū Sufyān summoned Banū ʿAbd Manāf, and they gathered around him. Abān ibn Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah stood up and said:

“Abū Sufyān! Would Quraysh make war among themselves over a bald ram from the Azd, whom they would abandon to their fate?” Abū Sufyān replied, “Abān, are you trying to turn the people away from me? By God, I am the one when I am roused.” Abān said, “Be roused where it will do you good. But better than what you wish to do is to pay what you owe for violating his protection, to make amends to your in-law, and to put your clan back in good order.” Abū Sufyān left, saying, “No two goats will butt heads over his killing. These are the sons of Abū Uḥayḥah; they have risen up for their maternal kin.” Ṣafiyyah bint al-Mughīrah, the eldest of Hind’s sisters, was married to Abū Uḥayḥah; her sister Hind was also married to him. All of Abū Uḥayḥah’s children were born to these two women except Khālid ibn Saʿīd. Ṣafiyyah bint al-Mughīrah’s mother was Ṣakhrah al-Bajaliyyah, and Hind’s mother was Rayṭah bint Saʿīd ibn Sahm. He said: No one among Quraysh married two sisters except Abū Uḥayḥah. He said: Saʿīd ibn Ṣafīḥ al-Dawsī, the grandfather of Abū Uzhayr al-Dawsī, attacked Bujayr ibn al-ʿAwwām ibn Khuwaylid in al-Yamāmah. They met while trading. The grandfather of Abū Uzhayr tricked Bujayr until he had gone ahead of him, then struck off his head, saying, “This is for Abū Uzhayr.” Before his head was struck off, Bujayr said, “Let me say some poetry.” He let him, and Bujayr recited: (al-ṭawīl)

Carry a message to Laylā, with a sign I send—

a reply from a tongue afraid of an eye, stammering.

My sign is that I found a man who shuns friendship;

the worst of companions is the fickle friend.

And a white man, who loved his wine pure, I met at dawn,

when morning had put on its wide-sleeved shirt.

I found myself burdened by him, so I bore the burden,

and eased a distress that had seemed impossible to relieve.

Then he made him go ahead and struck off his head. Abū Uzhayr’s children were Abū Ḥanāʾah, Junādah, as far as we knew—or so they said. We will pay you compensation for whatever blood or property was lost before us.” Quraysh accepted the compensation and called off the war. Some time afterward, the Messenger of God sent Khālid ibn al-Walīd to Banū Jadhīmah ibn ʿĀmir. He fought them at a watering place of theirs called al-Ghumayṣāʾ and killed four hundred young men.

He said: When Hishām ibn al-Walīd killed Abū Uzhayr, Banū al-Mughīrah sent people to ask what Banū ʿAbd Manāf were doing and what they were gathering to do. Their informant came to them and told them of Banū ʿAbd Manāf’s anger. Abū Sufyān summoned Banū ʿAbd Manāf, and they gathered around him. Abān ibn Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah stood up and said:

“Abū Sufyān! Would Quraysh make war among themselves over a bald ram from the Azd, whom they would abandon to their fate?” Abū Sufyān said, “Abān, are you trying to turn the people away from me? By God, I am the one when I am roused.” Abān said, “Be roused where it will do you good. But better than what you wish to do is to pay what you owe for violating his protection, to make amends to your in-law, and to put your clan back in good order.” Abū Sufyān left, saying, “No two goats will butt heads over his killing. These are the sons of Abū Uḥayḥah; they have risen up for their maternal kin.” Ṣafiyyah bint al-Mughīrah, the eldest of Hind’s sisters, was married to Abū Uḥayḥah, as was her sister Hind. All of Abū Uḥayḥah’s children were born to these two women except Khālid ibn Saʿīd. The mother of Ṣafiyyah bint al-Mughīrah was Ṣakhrah al-Bajaliyyah, and the mother of Hind was Rayṭah bint Saʿīd ibn Sahm. He said: No one among Quraysh married two sisters except Abū Uḥayḥah. He said: Saʿīd ibn Ṣafīḥ al-Dawsī, the grandfather of Abū Uzhayr al-Dawsī, attacked Bujayr ibn al-ʿAwwām ibn Khuwaylid in al-Yamāmah. They met while trading. The grandfather of Abū Uzhayr tricked Bujayr until he had gone ahead of him, then struck off his head, saying, “This is for Abū Uzhayr.” Before his head was struck off, Bujayr said, “Let me say some poetry.” He let him, and Bujayr recited: (al-ṭawīl)

Carry a message to Laylā, with a sign I send—

a reply from a tongue afraid of an eye, stammering.

My sign is that I found a man who shuns friendship;

the worst of companions is the fickle friend.

[The text continues on the following page.]

the eldest of Hind’s sisters, was married to Abū Uḥayḥah, and her sister Hind was also married to him. All of Abū Uḥayḥah’s children were born to these two women except Khālid ibn Saʿīd. The mother of Ṣafiyyah bint al-Mughīrah was Ṣakhrah al-Bajaliyyah, and the mother of Hind was Rayṭah bint Saʿīd ibn Sahm. He said: No one among Quraysh married two sisters except Abū Uḥayḥah. He said: Ṭaghā Saʿīd ibn Ṣafīḥ al-Dawsī, the grandfather of Abū Uzhayr al-Dawsī, attacked Bujayr ibn al-ʿAwwām ibn Khuwaylid in al-Yamāmah. They met while trading. The grandfather of Abū Uzhayr tricked Bujayr until he had gone ahead of him, then struck off his head, saying, “This is for Abū Uzhayr.” Before his head was struck off, Bujayr said, “Let me say some poetry.” He let him, and Bujayr recited: (al-ṭawīl)

Carry a message to Laylā with a sign I send—

a reply from a tongue afraid of an eye, stammering.

My sign is that I found a man who shuns friendship;

the worst of companions is the fickle friend.

And a white man, who loved his wine pure, I met at dawn,

when morning had put on its wide-sleeved shirt.

I found myself burdened by him, so I bore the burden,

and eased a distress that had seemed impossible to relieve.

Then he made him go ahead and struck off his head. Abū Uzhayr’s children were Abū Ḥanāʾah, Junādah, and ʿAbd Allāh. Abū Ḥanāʾah had a son, Shumaylah, who married Mujāshiʿ ibn Masʿūd al-Sulamī. He was struck by a projectile on the Day of the Camel and died afterward. He had been with ʿĀʾishah ﵂. After him, she married ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās in al-Baṣrah, when ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵇—appointed him over it. This is what Abū Faswah said: (al-ṭawīl)

Had you been of Zahrān, you would have brought me close to your gathering;

but I am a client of Jamīl ibn Maʿmar.

He means Jamīl ibn Maʿmar al-Jumaḥī.

Account of the Day of al-Ghumayṣāʾ

The Messenger of God sent Khālid ibn al-Walīd to the Aḥābīsh, who were the Hūn ibn Khuzaymah, the Ḥayyu of Khuzāʿah, and Banū Mālik ibn Kinānah, who were— At the lower part of Mecca, a woman of Banū Jadhīmah, after many of them had been killed, said: (al-Ṭawīl)

By God, had not the people of Ghūth submitted,Sulaym would have met that day a formidable opponent.

They would have fought fiercely against them—Bisr and the men of Jaḥdam,and Murrah—until they left the camels crying out.

How many young men will you see on the day of al-Ghumayṣāʾ,struck down though he had not been wounded, though he himself had dealt wounds?

Khālid clung to the cause of the widows and divorced,that very morning, those among them who had husbands.

Khālid took captives from among them. Among those captives was a young man of Banū Jadhīmah. While his hands were bound, he said to one of the men guarding him, “Take me to these captive women, so I can greet one of them.” The man took him there. When he stopped before the women, he said, “Aslamī— Ḥubaysh as life runs out.” The woman said, “And you, may you live ten and seven, singly, and eight in succession.” The young man said: (al-Ṭawīl)

I showed you—when I sought you and found you—at Ḥalyah, or when I reached you at al-Khawāniq.

Was it not right that a loving man should be given provisions,when he had taken on the hardship of setting out at night and the scorching heat?

And I said, when my family were neighbors to yours:“Reward me with affection before one of the calamities.”

“Reward me with affection before the distance parts usand a chief departs from his beloved, separated from him.”

He said: “When the young man was brought forward and his neck was struck, she came and threw herself upon him until she died with him.” A boy of Banū Jadhīmah said on that day, as he was driving his mother and his two sisters: (al-Rajaz)

Raise up the hems of your garments and walk,walking like snakes, as though you had not been terrified.

If the three women are spared today, you will be spared.

A fair-skinned woman, yellow in the flanks, knowsthat a man with a flock and camels will take her.

Today I shall do as much as any man can.

The other said: (al-Rajaz)

A yellow woman knows how to beguile a bridegroom;his jaws are not filled by taking a bite of her.

I shall strike the people today with a mighty blow,a blow like that of men who have left the sanctuary, against pregnant camels that refuse to be led.

It is also transmitted: “a blow like that of the men on the move,” which is better. The third said: (al-Rajaz)

I swear, no lion, lurking with a mane,rough-fingered on a cold morning,

grim of face, with the color of a rose’s thorns,growls between a thicket and barren land,

accustomed to attacking men one by one and alone,

will show greater valor at daybreak than I.

In its chain of transmission, the account is related from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Ḥadrad al-Aslamī, who said: “I was with Khālid on the day of al-Ghumayṣāʾ. I captured one of their boys and bound his hands to his neck. When I passed near some women— “Some distance from them, he said to me, ‘Take me by the women. I have a need, if that is not too difficult for you.’ I brought him toward them. When he was at a place where they could hear him, he said: ‘Aslamī, Ḥubaysh, as life runs out.’ She said, ‘And you, live, Shuʿayth; may my Lord give you rain.’ The young man said: (al-Ṭawīl)

I saw you in those days when I met youat Ḥalyah, or when our days were at al-Khawāniq.

Was it not right that a lover should be grantedwhat he had earned by setting out at night and enduring the scorching heat?

I have no fault: I said it before you parted,‘Reward me with a gift before one of the calamities.’

‘Reward me with a gift before distance parts usand the chief departs from his beloved, separated from him.

For I did not betray a secret held in trust,nor has any pleasing sight caught my eye in your absence.

Except what the clan spread among themselves in talk,on the basis of their suspicion—that, after all our loving.’

She answered him and said, “And you, may you live ten and nine, singly, and eight in succession.” Then he went away and they struck off his head. When Ḥubaysh saw him, she came, threw herself upon him, and kept gasping until she died. The people had been preparing for battle against Khālid ibn al-Walīd. Khālid shouted to them, “Lay down your weapons, for the people have submitted.” A man among them named Jaḥdam said, “O Banū Jadhīmah! This is Khālid ibn al-Walīd. By God, after laying down your weapons, there is nothing but captivity— “and after captivity, nothing but the severing of necks. By God, I will never lay down my weapon.” Some of his people took hold of him and said, “Jaḥdam, do you want to spill our blood? The people have submitted; the war has laid down its burdens, and the people are safe.” They kept at him until he laid down his weapon, and his people laid down their weapons. Then Khālid put them to the sword and killed many. The news reached the Messenger of God ﷺ—and he paid them compensation for the blood and property they had lost, so much so that he even paid them compensation for a dog. Nothing remained of any blood or property that had not been compensated until ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵇—had paid it. Some money still remained with him. When he had finished, he said to them, “[Is there—] any blood or property for which compensation has not been paid to you?” They said, “No.” He said, “Then I give you this remaining money as a precaution for the Messenger of God, in case there is something of which he is unaware, or of which you are unaware.” He did so, then returned to the Messenger of God ﷺ—and told him what had happened. He said, “You have acted rightly and well.”

It is said that there was an exchange of words between Khālid and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān over this. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said to him:

“You acted according to the practices of the Jāhiliyyah during Islam.”

Khālid said, “I only avenged your father.”

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said, “You lie. I killed my father’s killer. But you avenged your uncle al-Fākih ibn al-Mughīrah.”

The account of Suhayl ibn ʿAmr during the apostasy

Ibn al-Kalbī said: “When the Messenger of God ﷺ—died, the people of Mecca considered withholding the alms-tax. Suhayl ibn ʿAmr, a brother of Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy, stood among them and addressed them, saying:

‘O people of Quraysh! O people of Mecca! You know that I have the most ships at sea and pack-saddles on land. So pay the alms-tax. If what you intend comes to pass, I will return to you from my wealth what you paid; otherwise, you will not have disgraced Islam or brought it into disrepute.’”

“They accepted what he said. God completed Islam and left His Prophet among them. This was the fulfillment of the Messenger of God’s words to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵁—on the day of Badr, when Suhayl ibn ʿAmr was taken captive. He had been the orator who urged the people of Mecca to mobilize toward Abū Sufyān and the caravan. ʿUmar said, ‘Messenger of God, let me pull out his two front teeth, so that he will never again stand up as an orator against you.’ The Messenger of God ﷺ—said, ‘Leave him. Perhaps he will take a stand that will please you.’ This was his stand. Suhayl ibn ʿAmr was aʿlam—“aʿlam” means one whose lip is split.

The account of the Prophet ﷺ—and Abū Lahab

Al-Kalbī said: “When God, mighty and exalted, revealed, ‘Warn your nearest kindred’ (26:214), he went out and stood on al-Marwah, calling, ‘O sons of Fihr!’ Quraysh came to him, and Abū Lahab said, ‘Here are Fihr, before you.’ He said, ‘O sons of Ghālib!’ The Banū Muḥārib and Banū al-Ḥārith turned back. Then he said, ‘O sons of Luʾayy ibn Ghālib!’ The Banū Taym al-Adram ibn Ghālib turned back. He said, ‘O sons of Kaʿb ibn Luʾayy!’ The Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy turned back. He said, ‘O sons of Murrah ibn Kaʿb!’ The Banū ʿAdī, Banū Sahm, and Banū Jumāḥ turned back. He said, ‘O sons of Kilāb!’ The Banū Makhzūm and Banū Taym turned back. He said, ‘O sons of Quṣayy!’ The Banū Zuhrah turned back. He said, ‘O sons of ʿAbd Manāf!’ The Banū ʿAbd al-Dār and Banū Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā turned back. Abū Lahab said, ‘Here are the Banū ʿAbd Manāf, before you.’ He said, ‘God has commanded me to warn my nearest kindred. You are the closest of Quraysh. I have no share of God’s favor and no portion in the Hereafter unless you say, “There is no god but God.” I will bear witness to it for you before your Lord, and the Arabs will make it their religion.’ Abū Lahab said, ‘May you perish! Is this what you called us for?’ Then God, mighty and exalted, revealed: ‘Perish the hands of Abū Lahab’ (111:1).

The account of the two journeys

Al-Kalbī said: “Quraysh had become accustomed to two journeys, one in winter to Yemen and the other in summer to Syria. They continued in this way until hardship became severe for them, while Tabālah and Jurash and the people of Yemen’s seacoast enjoyed abundance. The people of the coast transported goods by sea, and the people of the interior transported them on camels. The coastal people put in at Jeddah, and the people of the interior at al-Muḥaṣṣab. The people of Mecca provisioned themselves as they wished, and God sufficed them in place of the two journeys they used to make to Yemen and Syria. Then God, mighty and exalted, revealed: ‘For the accustomed security of Quraysh, their accustomed security in the winter and summer journeys’ (106:1–2). His words, ‘and made them secure from fear’ (106:4), mean fear of the enemy and fear of leprosy. There is no leper among the people of Quraysh. “The accustomed security of Quraysh” means Quraysh’s customary practice of the winter and summer journeys. Then years struck Quraysh, taking away their wealth. Hāshim went out to Syria and ordered a great deal of bread to be baked for him. He carried it in large sacks on camels until he arrived in Mecca. There he crumbled the bread, slaughtered those camels, cooked them, and poured those pots over the bread. He fed the people of Mecca and filled their stomachs. That was the first ḥayāʾ. He said of this:”

Wahb ibn ʿAbd ibn Quṣayy ibn Kilāb: (al-Wāfir)

Hāshim took upon himself what was too much for them,

and what Ibn Bayḍ was unable to undertake.

He brought them sacks, filled to the brim,

from the land of Syria, with pure wheat.

He gave the people of Mecca ample food from his crushed grain,

and mixed the bread with fresh meat.

The people remained among bowls crowned

with teakwood, while their contents overflowed.

Umayya envied him, and what happened between them was what we have written about their mutual challenge. It is said that the first enmity to arise between Hāshim and Umayya was for that reason. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib said: (al-Mutaqārib)

I return with my wealth to the impoverished among Quraysh,

though the Ḥums have grown accustomed to asking them for aid.

I give them food in times of drought,

when, if drought strikes, their wealth perishes.

When he resolves to be generous after holding back,

his soul takes no rein.

ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib was the handsomest of Quraysh, the tallest in stature, the most forbearing, and the most open-handed. No king ever saw him without granting his request.

The reason ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib married into Banū Zuhrah, and also married his son ʿAbd Allāh into Banū Zuhrah

He said: When ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib came to Yemen, he would lodge with one of its leading men. Once, on one of his visits, he lodged with a man who had lived to a great age and had studied the scriptures. The man said to him, “ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, allow me to examine a place on you.”

He replied, “I do not allow you to examine just any place on me.”

“It is only your nostril,” said the man.

“Then go ahead,” he replied.

The man looked at the hair in his nostril—the hair is called yār, a Yemeni word—and said, “I see prophethood and I see kingship. I see one of the two in Banū Zuhrah.”

ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib then returned and married Hālah, daughter of Uhayb ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhrah; and he married his son ʿAbd Allāh to Āminah, daughter of Wahb. She bore Muḥammad ﷺ. Thus God placed prophethood and the caliphate among the descendants of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib—and God knows best where to place them.

He said: When ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib set out with his son to marry him to Āminah, daughter of Wahb ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhrah—ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib had already sent word to her family to ask for her hand on his son’s behalf, and they had agreed—he went with his son. On the way, he passed a woman of Khathʿam in Mecca, named Fāṭimah daughter of Marr. She was among the most beautiful, the most accomplished, and the most chaste of people; she had studied the scriptures, and the young men of Quraysh used to talk with her. She saw the light of prophethood in ʿAbd Allāh’s face. She said, “Young man, who are you?”

He replied, “I am ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib.”

She said, “Would you lie with me? I would give you a hundred camels.”

He looked at her and said: (al-Rajaz)

As for what is forbidden, death stands before it;

and what is lawful—no, not lawful—until I see it clearly.

So what of the thing you intend?

Then he went on with his father, who married him to Āminah of Zuhrah. He stayed with her for three days; that year, when a man went to his wife at her family’s home ...

Then he mentioned the camels the woman of Khathʿam had offered him, along with the beauty he had seen in her. He went to her, but did not find her as welcoming toward him at the end as she had been at the beginning. He said, “Would you still have what you told me about?”

She said, “No. That was once; today, no.” And so it became a proverb.

She said, “What did you do after I saw you?”

He replied, “My father took me along and married me to Āminah. I stayed with her for three days.”

She said, “By God, I am not a woman of ill repute. I saw the light of prophethood in your face and wanted it to be in me, but God willed only that it be placed where He placed it.”

The young men of Quraysh heard what the woman of Khathʿam had offered ʿAbd Allāh and how he had refused her. They told her, and she composed these verses: (al-Kāmil)

I saw a cloud arise, ... then shimmer with rain-filled black clouds;

from its water a light shone

on all around it, like the dawn’s brightening.

I saw its rain as life for a land,

falling upon it, and as cultivation for the barren.

I saw in it a distinction that I might claim,

though not every striker of flint brings forth a spark.

What I had hoped for,

in the matter I offered him,

was not something that beauty led him to,

nor did he refuse to be chaste.

And she also said: (al-Ṭawīl)

O Banū Hāshim, your brother has left behind

Āminah, when the two contended for union.

/ As a lamp, when its flame has died away, leaves behind ... wicks steeped in oil;

not all that a young man inherits from his forebears ... is gained by resolve, nor is all that he misses due to delay.

So be gracious when you seek a thing, for

two fates will suffice for you, contending with one another.

One will suffice for you, whether a clenched hand,

or a hand outspread with its fingers.

When Āminah had taken from him what she took,

my sight turned away from him and my tongue grew weary.

When Āminah had taken from him what she took,

she gained from him a distinction without equal.

The account of Ṭulayb’s support for the Prophet ﷺ

Ibn al-Kalbī said: There was an incident between Quraysh in Mecca at the beginning of the time when God sent His Prophet ﷺ. ʿAwf ibn Ṣabrah al-Sahmī insulted the Prophet ﷺ. Ṭulayb ibn ʿUmayr ibn Wahb ibn ʿAbd ibn Quṣayy took a camel’s jawbone and struck ʿAwf with it until he fell. Ṭulayb’s mother, Arwāʾ daughter of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, said: (al-Rajaz)

Ṭulayb supported his cousin,

standing by him with his blood and wealth.

Ṭulayb was the first to support the Messenger of God ﷺ and that was the first blood shed in support of the Messenger of God ﷺ. Ṭulayb then accompanied him, fought at Badr, and was killed as a martyr at Ajnādayn. ﵀.

The story of Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah and Ḍubāʿah

Al-Haytham and Ibn al-Kalbī, from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, from al-Muṭṭalib ibn Abī Wadāʿah, who said: Al-Muṭṭalib told Ibn ʿAbbās:

Ḍubāʿah daughter of ʿĀmir ibn Qurṭ ibn Salamah ibn Qushayr ibn Kaʿb was married to Hawdhah ibn ʿAlī ibn Thumāmah al-Ḥanafī. When he died, she inherited a great deal of wealth from him. Then she returned to her people’s lands, and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān al-Taymī asked her father for her hand; he married her to him.

A paternal cousin of hers, named Ḥazn ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salamah ibn Qushayr, came to her father and said, “Marry Ḍubāʿah to me.”

He replied, “I have married her to Ibn Judʿān.”

Her cousin swore that she would never reach him and that he would kill her rather than let that happen. Her father wrote to Ibn Judʿān and told him of this. Ibn Judʿān wrote back, “By God, if you do this, I will raise a banner of treachery against you at the market of ʿUkāẓ.”

Her father said to his cousin, “You see how things stand; I must keep faith with this man.” He prepared her for the journey and sent her to him. Ḥazn rode after her, taking his spear, and followed her until he caught up. He placed the spearhead between her shoulders and said, “Ḍubāʿah, would you rather join people who go about acquiring wealth for trade, or people who stay settled?”

She said, “No; people who stay settled.”

“By God,” he said, “if you had said anything else, I would have driven it between your breasts.” Then he turned away from her. She was escorted to Ibn Judʿān, and she stayed with him as long as God willed.

While she was circumambulating the Kaʿbah, she was noticed by Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah al-Makhzūmī. She was beautiful and young, and he was captivated by her. He spoke to her at the House and said, “You are content for your youth and beauty to be with an old man. If you asked him to part with you, he would marry you.” Hishām was a handsome man of considerable means.

She returned to Ibn Judʿān and said, “I am a young woman, and you are an old man.”

He said to her, “What has brought this on? I have been told that Hishām spoke to you while you were circumambulating the House. I swear by God that I will not part from you until you swear not to marry Hishām. If you ever do that, you must circumambulate the House naked, sacrifice such-and-such camels, and spin camel hair between the two mountains of Mecca, though you are one of the Ḥums and are not permitted to spin camel hair.”

Al-Haytham said: The Ḥums were Quraysh, Kinānah, Khuzāʿah, and those born to Quraysh by women from the various Arab tribes.

She sent word to Hishām, telling him what had been imposed on her. He sent back: “As for your circumambulating the House naked, I will ask Quraysh to clear the mosque for you so that you can circumambulate before dawn, in the darkness of night, where no one will see you. As for the camels you are to sacrifice, I swear by God that I will sacrifice them for you. As for your spinning camel hair, that is a custom established by some men of Quraysh, not a religion brought by a prophet.”

She said to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān, “Yes, I will do what you said and swore to do if I marry Hishām.” He divorced her, and she married Hishām. Hishām spoke to Quraysh and asked them to clear the mosque for her.

They did so. Al-Kalbī said: Al-Muṭṭalib ibn Abī Wadāʿah said, “I was a boy among the boys of Quraysh. I came in through the mosque gate and was looking at her. She took off her clothes and circumambulated the House seven times, saying: (al-Rajaz)

Today half of it or all of it will appear;

whatever of it appears, I shall not make lawful.”

She finished, and the camels she had mentioned were sacrificed on her behalf, and she spun that camel hair. She bore Hishām Salamah ibn Hishām, who was among the best of the Muslims.

One night, while she was standing, Hishām heard a woman crying out and said, “What is that?”

He was told, “ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān al-Taymī has died.”

Ḍubāʿah said, “By God, he was a fine husband for an Arab woman.”

Hishām said, “Indeed, by God—and she was a close cousin’s daughter.”

Hishām later died while she was still with him. Then the Messenger of God ﷺ asked her son Salamah ibn Hishām for her hand in marriage. He said, “Salamah, marry Ḍubāʿah to me.”

He replied, “I will consult her, Messenger of God.”

He consulted her and said, “Ḍubāʿah, the Messenger of God ﷺ has asked me for your hand.”

She said, “Woe to you! What did you say to him?”

“I said I would consult you,” he replied.

“Would you consult me about the Messenger of God?” she said. “May God disgrace your judgment! Go back, in case the Messenger of God has changed his mind.”

He came back, after the Messenger of God ﷺ had been told of her advanced age, and said, “Messenger of God, I consulted her, and she ordered me to do as you asked.”

The Prophet ﷺ fell silent.

This is the account of the nasīʾah among Kinānah

Abū al-Bakhtarī said: Al-Ḍaḥḥāk ibn ʿUthmān told me, from Ibrāhīm ibn ʿAbd— ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Rabīʿa said: “The nasīʾa [postponement of the sacred months] was in the hands of al-Qalammas, the Kinānī, and then in the hands of his descendants after him. They would postpone the month, so that they would perform the pilgrimage in each month for two years: they would perform the pilgrimage in Muḥarram for two years, in Ṣafar for two years, in Rabīʿ I for two years, in Rabīʿ II for two years, in Jumādā I for two years, in Jumādā II for two years, in Shaʿbān for two years, in Ramaḍān for two years, in Shawwāl for two years, then in Dhū al-Qaʿda for two years, then in Dhū al-Ḥijja for two years. When they performed the pilgrimage in a month, they did not ensure that they set the Day of Tarwiya, the Day of ʿArafa, and the Day of Sacrifice in keeping with the month’s pattern, or that they stand for three days. If the pilgrimage fell in Muḥarram, the market of ʿUkāẓ opened on the morning of Dhū al-Ḥijja. They would stay at ʿUkāẓ for twenty days. When the twenty days had passed, they would depart for Majanna and stay there for ten days, with their markets open. When they saw the new moon, they would depart for Dhū al-Majāz and stay there for eight nights, with their markets open. Then they would disperse, and that was the last of their markets. They would not sell on the Day of ʿArafa or on the days at Minā, nor would they buy. They considered the most egregious wrongdoing to be performing the lesser pilgrimage during the months of the pilgrimage, and Quraysh and the other Arabs did not attend the market of Dhū al-Majāz except in the consecrated state for the pilgrimage. They considered it grave to commit any of the forbidden acts or for one group to raid another, because these were sacred months. The fighting was called al-Fijār because of the wrongdoing committed during it.

This is the pact of Quraysh with the Aḥābīsh

ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn ʿImrān ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz al-Zuhrī, known as Ibn Abī Thābit, said: “The event that initiated the pact of the Aḥābīsh was this: a man from Banū al-Ḥārith ʿAbd Manāt ibn Kināna came down to Mecca and sold some goods of his. Then he took shelter in one of the houses of Banū Makhzūm and asked for water. A woman from Quraysh came out to him. He said, ‘Why didn’t you ask one of the attendants?’ She said, ‘Banū Bakr have left us like ostriches with a distinctive trait, so that we have no choice but to remain in our sanctuary.’ The man went out and came to Banū al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Manāt. He said, ‘O Banū al-Ḥārith! Quraysh have been brought low by Banū Bakr. If you have support to offer, then offer it.’ They said, ‘Call your brothers, Banū al-Muṣṭaliq and al-Ḥayāʾ ibn Saʿd ibn ʿAmr.’ They rode to them, brought them back, and Banū al-Hawn ibn Khuzayma heard of them and rode out with them. This was after Banū Asad had left Tihāma. They went out until they gathered at the base of Ḥabashī, a mountain below Mecca.”

They swore an oath by God, saying: “We are a hand that breaks the oppression and spares blood, as long as Ḥabashī stands firm.” Ibn Abī Thābit al-Zuhrī said: “When Quṣayy prevailed over Mecca, and Quraysh prevailed, grew numerous, and were abandoned by those among Quḍāʿa and Asad who had supported them, Quraysh dwindled and feared Bakr. ʿAbd Manāf therefore sent word to al-Hawn ibn Khuzayma and al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Manāt, and they answered them. Banū al-Ḥārith sent word to al-Muṣṭaliq and al-Ḥayāʾ, and they answered them. Al-Hawn came forward, led by Abū Ḍirār ibn Mālik, and al-Ḥārith came forward, led by Shayẓan ibn ʿAmr, the brother of Banū Aḥmar. ʿAbd Manāf went out to them and made a pact with them. Ghālib ibn Yathīʿ said: (al-khafīf meter)

Shuḥab and ʿAbd Manāf spent the nightamong us, seated for the making of pacts.”

He said: When the Aḥābīsh had become numerous and strong, they said, “We will enter whichever Qurashī group we wish to join.” Al-Qāra entered into the pact. They are Banū al-Dayyish ibn Maḥlam ibn Ghālib ibn Yathīʿ ibn al-Hawn ibn Khuzayma, among Banū Zuhra ibn Kilāb. Qāriẓ also entered into it. Then some of them wanted to travel to Syria, so they made a pact with some people of Khuzāʿa, in order to be safe through them. Then God—mighty and exalted is He—sent down to His Prophet: “Do not be like the woman who untwisted her yarn after making it strong, taking your oaths as a means of deceit between you” (16:92).

“so that one community may be greater than another” (16:92). He said: The news reached them at al-Juḥfa, so they returned to Mecca. They were called the Aḥābīsh because they had made their pact at Ḥabashī, which lies ten miles from Mecca in the direction of al-Ramaḍa. Ḥammād al-Rāwiya said: “The one who led Banū al-Ḥārith and made a pact with Quṣayy was ʿĀmir ibn ʿAwf, who was called ‘the Tail’s Fastener.’ It is also said that ʿAbd Manāf made the pact with him and married him to his daughter Rayṭa.” Ḥudhāfa ibn Ghānim, one of Banū ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb, praised Banū Quṣayy and singled out Abū Ṭālib in praise. He said: (al-ṭawīl meter)

Abū ʿUtba, who bestows on me his gift …
a noble, pure white in complexion, from the Zuhra clan and the one who gives the pilgrims drink … then the elder Hāshim, and ʿAbd Manāf, that generous and noble lord

Their father Quṣayy was called “the Gatherer,”for through him God gathered the tribes of Fihr.

He gave his daughter to ʿAwf in marriage, so that he might protect usfrom our enemies, when Banū Bakr abandoned us

A mention of what has come down concerning the pacts of Quraysh with Thaqīf and Daws

He said: The reason for the pact between Thaqīf and Quraysh was that when Quraysh grew numerous, they desired Wajj, the valley of al-Ṭāʾif. They said to Thaqīf, “We will share the sanctuary with you, and you share Wajj with us.” Thaqīf said, “How can we share with you a valley that our father settled and dug into the rock with his own hand” he had not dug it with iron. About it he says: (al-hazaj meter)

I strike it with a boulderand it strikes me with a boulder.

I wear it away, and it wears me away,and all who perish are doomed.

He said: “You did not make the sanctuary; Abraham ﵇—made it.” Quraysh said, “Do not enter our sanctuary, and we will not enter your Wajj.” When they feared war, and Thaqīf feared Quraysh, Khuzāʿa, and Banū Bakr ibn ʿAbd Manāt, Thaqīf made a pact with Quraysh and called their brothers from Daws. He said: When Quraysh made a pact with Thaqīf, Quraysh said to Thaqīf:

“We ask Daws for the same partnership in the abode that we asked of you.” Thaqīf said, “Instead, Daws will make a pact with you.” ʿAbd Yālīl ibn Muʿattib and Masʿūd ibn ʿAmr, both from Thaqīf and specifically from the Aḥlāf, rode out with a group until they came to Daws. They said to them, “Quraysh asked us to let them into Wajj and to let us into the sanctuary. We refused, then made a pact with them. They desire what you have, so admit them, let them admit you, and make a pact with them.” Daws made a pact with Quraysh.

He said: When Najda al-Ḥarūrī sent Ḥuzāq al-Ḥarūrī, one of Banū Ḥanīfa, to collect alms from the Azd, Daws killed him. ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān said to Ḥuzāq’s daughter when she came before him, “Did Daws kill your father?” She said, “They killed him in the mountains. Had he come out onto open ground, they would not have stood against him.” Al-Muḥriz ibn Abī Hurayra al-Dawsī said, “By God, I kill more of them on the plain than in the mountains.” ʿAbd al-Malik said to her,

“Recite to me what you said about your father.” She said: (al-ṭawīl meter)

I ask the riders of al-Yamāma, have they seen …Ḥuzāq, while my eye is like a bubble from the rain?

Whoever seizes the livestock of Fayḥ and Muṣmatand kills Ḥuzāq will forever be held in high renown.

If I fail to avenge myself on Daws with a band of young men,swift to fight, whom the wars of time have not broken,

then Quraysh—Ḥāziq’s killingwas of their own kin, so seek vengeance for him from the cleaver of stone!

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān said: “You have seen what ʿUmar ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Maʿmar al-Taymī did. He is one of Quraysh, but he is not of its senior lineages, nor of its houses, nor of its rule, nor of its standing by that he means that ʿUmar ibn ʿUbayd Allāh sent him against Najda al-Ḥarūrī, and that he killed Abū Fudayk, who was ʿAbd Allāh ibn Thawr al-Ḥarūrī.

Ibn Shihāb al-Zuhrī said: “A man from among the polytheists gave the Prophet ﷺ—a gift, and he gave him something in return. The man was displeased, so the Messenger of God ﷺ—said: ‘Indeed, I will never again accept the gift of a polytheist, except from a Qurashī, an Anṣārī, a Thaqafī, or a Dawsī.’” Al-Zabad means “gift.” Those from Daws who made a pact with Quraysh [were—] Banū Salāmān ibn Mufarrij, Banū Munahhib, Banū Mālik, and the majority of Nabīsh. The rest of Daws did not make a pact.

The pact of the two sons of ʿIlāj

ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn ʿImrān said: “The first pact Quraysh entered into was the pact of the two sons of ʿIlāj, namely, Shurayq and ʿAmr, the two sons of ʿIlāj of Thaqīf, from the Aḥlāf. Shurayq was the son of Wahb ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn ʿIlāj, and their brothers were Banū Jāriya ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā. Their pact came about because they had killed ʿAmr ibn Ghayra al-Mālikī of Thaqīf. They then entered into a pact with Āl al-Ḥārith ibn Zuhra ibn Kilāb and stayed a year. Then ʿAmr returned to al-Ṭāʾif and said, ‘I choose my people—even their killing me or their pardon—over a pact of disgrace and humiliation,’

He wanted Sharīq to return after they had pardoned ʿAmr. ʿAmr said: [poem in the ṭawīl metre]

I turned away from the pact he had sought to make,

and returned to my stock, Sharīq, and to my birth.

ʿAmr and his son perished, and none of them lived to see Islam. All the family of ʿIlāj entered that pact. When Wahb ibn Munāf ibn Zuhrah did what he did to Umayyah ibn ʿAbd Shams—he struck him with a sword, in a separate story that I wrote at the beginning of the book—he recalled the pact of Banū ʿIlāj with the family of al-Ḥārith ibn Zuhrah:

[My] paternal uncle al-Ḥārith, who fulfilled his pledge

to the two sons of ʿIlāj on the morning when Fihr broke faith.

The pact of Ḥārithah ibn al-Awqaṣ, according to Ibn Abī Thābit

He said: Then, following the pact of the two sons of ʿIlāj, Ḥārithah ibn al-Awqaṣ al-Sulamī made a pact. His story was that Ḥārithah was a devout man. He composed a line of poetry: [in the ṭawīl metre]

Truly, everything between Zawr and Munawwar

comes to rest with God’s own essence—enough for me.

Ḥārithah would recite it while circumambulating around Ḍimār, a shrine containing one of their idols. It was said— He was told that there was a sanctuary in Mecca whose people worshipped there, as did everyone who came from among the Arabs. “That sanctuary has the greater claim over this one,” he said. “I shall go there.” They said, “You cannot stay there unless you make a pact with its people.” So he set out and came to Mecca, where he made a pact with Umayyah ibn ʿAbd Shams ibn ʿAbd Manāf. Ḥārithah used to worship around the House. He later had a son, and Ḥakīm was the son who most resembled him. Quraysh put him in charge of its foolish young men. ʿAdī ibn al-Rabīʿ ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn ʿAbd Shams, one of Quraysh’s young men—or, it is said, al-Ḥārith ibn Umayyah the Younger—said the following:

[in the wāfir metre]

Every day I circle the open spaces,

for fear that Ḥakīm will drive me away.

This was the first pact made in Mecca. The pacts that followed came after it.

The pact of Jaḥsh ibn Riʾāb

He said: Jaḥsh ibn Riʾāb was of Banū Ghanm ibn Dūdān ibn Asad ibn Khuzaymah. His story was that Faḍālah ibn ʿAbd Marārah al-Asadī killed Hilāl ibn Umayyah al-Khuzāʿī, and Khuzāʿah killed Faḍālah in retaliation for their man. Banū Asad sought aid from Kinānah, but they refused to help them. Banū Asad then left their homes as a group and made a pact with Ghaṭafān. It is reported that the Messenger of God ﷺ—said, “Ghifār and Aslam, of Muzaynah and Juhaynah, are better than the two confederate tribes, Asad and Ghaṭafān.” These two, then, were the confederate tribes. Riʾāb ibn Yaʿmar, Jaḥsh’s father, came to Mecca and sought a pact with Quraysh. Banū Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā invited him to make a pact with them. He was asked— “Would you make a pact with the most ill-omened clan in Quraysh?” He broke off the pact with them and made a pact with Banū ʿAbd Manāf. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz said: Some people claim that he made a pact with Banū Umayyah alone, and not with the rest of Banū ʿAbd Manāf. He said: ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān was riding in Mecca, and ʿUrwah ibn al-Zubayr accompanied him. ʿUrwah recited to him the verses of Abū Aḥmad ibn Jaḥsh: [in the kāmil metre]

Sons of Umayyah, how could I be wronged among you,

when I am your son and your ally in hardship?

Others called on me, but I refused them,

and kept you for the trials of time.

I bound my rope to your ropes

at the pebbles, on the evening of the Sacrifice.

You upheld my kinship by sparing my blood,

and protected my bones from being broken.

You are true to your pledge, and worthy of it,

when treachery dwells in other households.

Sleep was denied me even for a brief, closed-eyed hour …a distress that constricts my breast when I remember it.

The reason was that when Banū Jaḥsh emigrated, Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb wanted to sell their houses in Mecca. Abū Aḥmad recited these lines to move him and remind him of the pact. When he went ahead with the sale of their houses, Abū Aḥmad satirized him, but Abū Sufyān paid no heed to his appeal and took no notice of his satire; he went ahead with the sale of their houses. The houses of Banū Jaḥsh had stood empty because they had emigrated. ʿAbd al-Malik said, “Who were the people who called on him, Abū ʿAbd Allāh?” ʿUrwah replied, “You know them, Commander of the Faithful.” He said, “Tell me more.”

“Give me more details about them,” he said. ʿUrwah replied, “We called on them, but they refused and made a pact with you.” He said, “You speak the truth.”

Then came the pact of Qāriẓ.
