He said: The family of Qāriẓ were of Banū al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Manāh ibn Kinānah. They made a pact with the Aḥābīsh. Khālid ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿUbayd ibn Taym ibn ʿAmr ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Mabdhūl ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Manāh ibn Kinānah—Qāriẓ’s father—came to Mecca. He was handsome, eloquent of tongue, and a poet. Quraysh said, “He is our ally, our sworn partner, our brother, our supporter; all our hands are joined against those who oppose him.” Each of them invited him to stay with him or offered him a marriage. He said, “I dislike going to one of you rather than another. Give me three days.” He went out to Ḥirāʾ and devoted himself to worship there for those three days, on its summit. Then he descended, having resolved and determined to make a pact with the first man of Quraysh he met. The first man he met was ʿAbd ʿAwf ibn ʿAbd ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Zuhrah ibn Kilāb. He tied his garment to ʿAbd ʿAwf’s garment and took his hand. Then the two went out until they entered the Sacred Mosque and stood by the House, where the pact was formally concluded for him.

The pact of Banū Shaybān al-Sulamiyyīn

He said: The pact of Banū Shaybān al-Sulamiyyīn was that of Shaybān ibn Dibyah ibn Ḥarmas al-Sulamī. The story of their pact was that al-Ghayḍāq ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib had a mother who had no other son by her from among Banū ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. His maternal brother was ʿAwf ibn ʿAbd ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Zuhrah. Their mother was Mumanniʿah daughter of ʿAmr ibn Mālik ibn Muʾammil ibn Suwayd ibn Asʿad ibn Mashnūʾ of Khuzāʿah. When ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib died, Banū ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib withheld from al-Ghayḍāq the inheritance from his father ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. He spoke to his maternal brother ʿAwf about them. ʿAwf said, “I have no power against them, and my clan will not help me.” Al-Ghayḍāq went to Shaybān, who was encamped outside Mecca at a place called al-Mufajjir, where there was a well called Karādim. Al-Ghayḍāq had married Umm Ḥakīm daughter of al-Zubayr ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, the mother of Maʿbad, ʿUbayd, and ʿAbbād, Shaybān’s sons. Shaybān was a drinking companion of ʿAwf. He concluded a pact between Shaybān and al-Ghayḍāq. His brothers then gave him his inheritance, and the pact with them remained in force.

The pact of the family of Suwayd

He said: The reason for the pact of the family of Suwayd ibn Rabīʿah ibn Zayd ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Dārim al-Tamīmī was that al-Mundhir ibn Imruʾ al-Qays al-Lakhmī had Zurarah ibn ʿAdas ibn Zayd ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Dārim nurse a son of his named Mālik. The boy grew up among them. Suwayd ibn Rabīʿah ibn Zayd ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Dārim was Zurarah’s in-law: he had married a daughter of Zurarah, who bore him seven sons. Mālik ibn al-Mundhir went out hunting but returned empty-handed.

He turned back and passed by Suwayd’s camels. He ordered one of them—a fat young she-camel—to be slaughtered and roasted, while Suwayd was asleep. Suwayd woke, took a stick, and rushed at Mālik, striking him on the head without recognizing him. The young man died from the blow. When Suwayd saw what had happened, he fled to Mecca, knowing he would not be safe. He made a pact with Banū Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf. Zurarah withdrew, fearing ʿAmr ibn al-Mundhir, while Ṭayyiʾ was pursuing Zurarah on a pretext. When Ṭayyiʾ learned what Tamīm had done to the king’s brother, ʿAmr ibn ʿAttāb ibn Thaʿlabah ibn Radmān urged ʿAmr ibn al-Mundhir to go after Zurarah. He said: [in the kāmil metre]

Tell Abū Qābūs that …

man was not created to be stone,

and the events of time

leave nothing but stones.

The last-born of his mother is not …

on the slope, below Awārah,

as the winds blow through

his flanks, when they have stripped him of his garment.

So kill Zurarah; I see no one

among the people more deserving than Zurarah.

He said: When ʿAmr heard these verses, he mounted and went to Zurarah’s home, but did not find him. He seized Zurarah’s wife, who was pregnant, split open her belly, and departed. Zurarah’s people said to him, “By God, you are not the one who killed his brother. Go to him.” So Zurarah went to ʿAmr. ʿAmr said, “Bring me the sons of Suwayd ibn Rabīʿah.” Zurarah brought his sons, and ʿAmr slaughtered them. Later, ʿAmr ibn al-Mundhir raided them. He lit a fire for them at Awārah and swore that he would burn one hundred people of Banū Tamīm. He burned ninety-eight men and a woman, al-Ḥamrāʾ daughter of Ḍarrah ibn Ḍarrah ibn Jābir ibn Qaṭan ibn Nahshal ibn Dārim, and a man of al-Barājim who smelled the smoke of roasting meat. He came urging his camel on, unaware that ʿAmr had burned those he had burned; he thought it was the smoke from a group roasting meat. He made his camel kneel and ran toward them. ʿAmr asked him, “What brought you here?” He replied, “Hunger for food. I have gone three days without tasting food. When the smoke rose, I thought it was the smoke of food.” ʿAmr asked him, “Who are you?”

He replied, “I am one of al-Barājim.” ʿAmr said, “The ill-fated man is the rider of al-Barājim.” This became a proverb. He ordered the man thrown into the fire. ʿAmr ibn al-Mundhir was thus called “the Burner” for burning these people. This was the reason for Suwayd’s pact with Banū Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf.

The pact of Murthid ibn Abī Murthid al-Ghanawī

The story of Murthid ibn Abī Murthid al-Ghanawī’s pact was this: Kannāz ibn Ḥuṣayn al-Ghanawī, one of Banū Ḥallān, was Abū Murthid and a hunter. He killed a man of Ghanī, from Banū ʿAtrīf, so Banū Ḥallān handed him over to Banū ʿAtrīf. He spent the night as their prisoner. Murthid crept up to him with a brand of fire and burned through his bonds. Then the two escaped that night and hid in a cave. They later reached Mecca and made a pact with Ḥamzah ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, who was a hunter. He said: Muqaddam ibn al-Ḥajjāj al-Ghanawī recited to me a verse by Abū Hurayrah, the Prophet’s Companion ﷺ: [in the ṭawīl metre]

How many a long night, and its hardships …

yet it saved me from the creed of unbelief.

Muqaddam said, “This verse does not belong to Abū Hurayrah. Kannāz ibn Ḥuṣayn said it on the night he escaped.”

The pact of Banū Nasīb ibn al-Ḥārith

He said: Banū Nasīb ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAmr ibn Māzin ibn Manṣūr made a pact. Among them was ʿUtbah ibn Ghazwān ibn Jābir ibn Wuhayb ibn Nasīb ibn al-Ḥārith, who was allied with Banū Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf. I do not know the reason for their pact, though I think it was due to kinship. which was between them. They said: Tamīm ibn Aws ibn Khāritha al-Lakhmī entered into an alliance with al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. I do not know the reason for his alliance.

The alliance of the families of ʿĀṣim and Sibāʿ

He said: The families of ʿĀṣim, who were from Banū Saʿd ibn Bayāḍah ibn Subayʿ ibn Khathʿamah ibn Saʿd ibn Mulayḥ ibn ʿAmr of Khuzāʿah as well, were all allies of ʿAwf ibn ʿAbd ʿAwf ibn ʿAbd ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Zuhrah ibn Kilāb. Their maternal half-brother was Khabbāb ibn al-Aratt, the client of ʿAwf ibn ʿAbd ʿAwf. Khabbāb was the one who fought at Badr with the Messenger of God ﷺ—and whom he appointed, along with Kaʿb ibn Zayd, over the distribution of the spoils of Badr. The one who brought them into alliance with ʿAwf was their maternal half-brother Khabbāb ibn al-Aratt. Their mother was a woman who served as a circumciser; she was the woman whom Ḥamzah ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib meant when he said to Sibāʿ ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā on the day of Uḥud: “Come here, you son of the woman who cuts vulvas!” He said: Abū Bishr brought these Khuzāʿīs into Zuhrah’s alliance. Among them was Karāmah al-Bushrā, the poet of Khuzāʿah. They were not allies, but joined them because of their brotherhood.

The alliance of the family of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd al-Hudhalī

The circumstances were these: Masʿūd, the father of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd, came to Mecca with an Arab horse and a she-camel of the Mahrah breed. He said: “Who will take these two from me, and to whom shall I bind myself by an alliance? For I am a blood-debtor”—that is, one wanted on account of bloodshed. ʿAbd ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Zuhrah ibn Kilāb took them from him and married him to Umm ʿAbd, daughter of al-Ḥārith. She bore him ʿAbd Allāh and ʿUtbah, the sons of Masʿūd, and he bound himself to him by an alliance. He said:

Wahb ibn Rabāḥ al-Ashʿarī entered into an alliance with Abū ʿAmr ibn ʿAwf ibn ʿAbd ʿAwf ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Zuhrah. He said: I do not know the reason for his alliance.

The reason the families of ʿAbd ʿAmr of Khuzāʿah entered into an alliance was that ʿAbd ʿAmr ibn Naḍlah ibn Mālik ibn Sulaym ibn Ghibshān ibn Malkān ibn Afṣā married a daughter of ʿAbd ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Zuhrah ibn named Niʿam. An alliance was made between him and her father. She bore Niʿam Dhū al-Shimālayn ibn ʿAbd ʿAmr ibn Naḍlah and Rayṭah daughter of ʿAbd ʿAmr. Maʿẓūn [ibn Ḥabīb ibn] Wahb ibn Ḥudhāfah ibn Jumāḥ married Rayṭah, and she bore him ʿUthmān, Qudāmah, ʿAbd Allāh, and Zaynab, the children of Maʿẓūn. Zaynab was the mother of ʿAbd Allāh and Ḥafṣah, the children of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb. Rayṭah was nicknamed Miskhannah, and the family of Maʿẓūn used to taunt her with it.

The alliance of the family of Ṣuʿayr ibn ʿUdhrah

This was because Ṣuʿayr ibn Ḥazān ibn Kāhil ibn ʿAbd ibn ʿUdhrah ibn Saʿd came to Mecca and entered into an alliance with Banū al-Mughīrah ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm. He then renounced their alliance and entered into an alliance with the families of Banū ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhrah, making an alliance between himself and them. Among his descendants was Khālid ibn ʿUrfuṭah. Khālid ibn ʿUrfuṭah was a Companion of the Prophet ﷺ—and was in command of the Muslims at al-Qādisiyyah. Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ was ill, so he appointed him to that command. Ṣuʿayr said when he left Banū al-Mughīrah:

(Long metre)

If the affection of Bakr is exchangedFor our affection, O son of al-Mughīrah,

You will find in them a falsehood that endures, and hatred,And a snarling dog, the most wary of barkers.

He said: The family of Anmār, from al-Qārah, also entered into an alliance with Zuhrah. I do not know the reason for their alliance. He said: Abū Musāfiʿ al-Ashʿarī entered into an alliance with the family of ʿImrān ibn Makhzūm. They have died out and left no descendants. I do not know the reason for their alliance.

The alliance of ʿAmr ibn al-Aʿẓam

He said: Among the alliances in Banū Makhzūm, and then among Banū al-Mughīrah, was that of the family of ʿAmr ibn al-Aʿẓam, from al-Ḥayāʾ of Khuzāʿah. They are the family of ʿAlbāʾ, the descendants of al-Rabʿah, daughter of al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, who was their mother. I do not know the reason for their alliance.

The alliance of Abū Usāmah

He said: Among the alliances there was that Abū Usāmah al-Jushamī entered into an alliance with al-Sāʾib ibn ʿĀʾidh ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm. I do not know the reason for his alliance. In a report he transmitted, the Prophet ﷺ—looked at Abū Usāmah and said: “An ally like Abū Usāmah!”

The alliance of al-Nabbāsh ibn Zurārah

He said: Al-Nabbāsh ibn Zurārah of Banū Asīd ibn ʿAmr ibn Tamīm entered into an alliance with Banū Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf. I do not know the reason for his alliance. Al-Nabbāsh was Abū Hālah, who married Khadījah daughter of Khuwaylid before the Messenger of God ﷺ. She bore him Hālah and Hind, both of whom were men. Hind had descendants among the family of Khālid ibn Ḥizām ibn Khuwaylid ibn Asad; al-Mundhir ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Ḥizāmī was one of them.

The alliance of Masʿūd ibn ʿAmr

He said: Ibn Shihāb said: The family of Masʿūd ibn ʿAmr, from al-Qārah, entered into an alliance with the family of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān [of the Banū Taym]. When ʿAbd Allāh was near death, he said: “Abū Masāḥiq”—he was also Abū Zuhayr, and had two teknonyms—“you have no child, and we should not remain allied with someone who has no child. So return our alliance to us.” He returned it to them and released them from it. They then entered into an alliance with Banū Nawfal ibn Uhayb ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhrah. He said: After ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān’s death, Abū Mulaykah ibn Judʿān was born to him by al-Ḍayrabah daughter of Abū Qays ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhrah. He said: These, then, are all the alliances that have come down to us as having existed among Quraysh in the pre-Islamic period. Any others were claims of affiliation in Islam on account of friendship, kinship, or protection by a neighbor, or marriage ties.

Those who entered Quraysh in Islam without an alliance, but through marriage, friendship, kinship, protection by a neighbor, or clientage

Among them, in Banū Hāshim, were the family of Abū Masrūḥ ibn ʿAmr. They were from Banū Saʿd ibn Bakr and entered through their marriage ties with al-ʿAbbās and al-Muqawwim, the sons of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. A daughter of al-Muqawwim was married to Abū Masrūḥ and bore him ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Masrūḥ. ʿAbd Allāh married a daughter of al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib.

Among them were Jaʿūnah ibn Shaʿūb of Banū Layth, who entered Banū Hāshim because of the friendship between Abū Bakr ibn Jaʿūnah and al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib.

Among those in Khuzāʿah were the family of Kathīr ibn al-Ṣalt al-Kindī and the family of Abū ʿUmar al-Ghifārī. The Commander of the Faithful, al-Mahdī, brought both families in during his caliphate. The family of Kathīr ibn al-Ṣalt was among Banū Jumāḥ.

Among those in Banū ʿAbd Shams were the family of ʿAmr ibn Umayyah al-Ḍamrī, who entered Banū Umayyah because ʿAmr ibn Umayyah al-Ḍamrī married Sukhaylah daughter of ʿUbaydah ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib.

Among them were the family of Hubayrah of Banū Qumayr. Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAnbasah ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿUthmān arranged their affiliation during the caliphate of al-Mahdī, and he registered them with them.

Among them were the families of Salamah and ʿAmr, the sons of al-Azraq. They entered Banū ʿAbd Shams because Salamah married Āminah daughter of ʿAffān, ʿUthmān’s paternal sister ﵁. Al-Azraq was a Roman slave belonging to al-Ḥārith ibn Kaladah al-Thaqafī. He went down with Abū Bakrah and al-Munbaʿith to the Prophet ﷺ—at al-Ṭāʾif, and they became Muslims. He emancipated them because they had become Muslims.

Among them was Ibn Ukht al-Nimr of Kindah, including al-Sāʾib ibn Yazīd. They were not allies, and we do not know why they entered Banū ʿAbd Shams.

Among them was the family of Hāniʾ, who are affiliated with Hamdān and claim to be allies of ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān ﵁—but they are in fact his clients.

Among them were the family of Quʿayn of Banū Asad ibn Khuzaymah and the family of ʿAlbāʾ of Banū Asad, the clan of Ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Uqaysh. They had no alliance; they entered through Jaḥsh ibn Riʾāb.

Among those in Banū Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf were:

Banū Abī Tajzāʾah and the family of [Abī-] Fukayhah. They were two brothers, sons of Yāsār, the slave of ʿUmārah ibn al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah. They are affiliated with the Ashʿarīs of Yemen. ʿUmārah ibn al-Walīd said the following about Abū Tajzāʾah:

(Long metre)

Marry Abū Tajzāʾah—if his familyBe in Mecca, he will travel, though he loves the shade.

Their maternal half-brother was Ṣayyāḥ, a slave of ʿUmārah ibn al-Walīd, whom ʿUmārah killed in the affair of the Jew. He had a story, as follows: ʿUmārah was a life of luxury and a tyrant. During one of his journeys, he stopped at a place in intense heat. Ṣayyāḥ got up, slaughtered a sheep, baked bread, cooked, and then crumbled the bread into broth for him. When the food was brought to him, ʿUmārah said: “Hot broth and hot bread on a hot day! You meant only to kill me.” Then he killed him. For that reason Kaʿb ibn Saʿd al-Ghanawī says: (Long metre)

I am no companion to a dead man who has perished.

Among them was the family of Abū Thawr, affiliated with Banū Tamīm. They are al-Khayyār ibn ʿAdī ibn Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz said: Abū Thawr, the slave of al-Khayyār ibn ʿAdī, was brought to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar al-Zuhrī, a descendant of al-Miswar ibn Makhramah.

Among them were the families of al-Ḥārith ibn Muʿāwiyah ibn al-Ḥuwayrith, from the Murādī tribes of Yemen. He said: I think they entered among them through the marriage of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Muʿāwiyah ibn al-Ḥuwayrith to Ḥafṣah daughter of Azhar ibn ʿUjayr ibn [ʿAbd-] Yazīd ibn Hāshim ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn ʿAbd Manāf.

Among them are the confederates of the Muḥāribī family of Sayḥān, from Jisr.

The reason is that the Banū ʿAbd Manāf support them, and I maintain that they are counted among them. This is indicated to me by the words of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Sayḥān when Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam, while governor of Muʿāwiya over Medina, flogged him eighty lashes for wine. Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān wrote to Marwān:

“To proceed: You flogged ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Sayḥān for the nabīdh of the people of Syria, which they drink, although it is not forbidden, at a time when he was allied to Abū Sufyān. By God, if he had been an ally of al-Ḥakam you would not have flogged him. So remit the penalty from him before I flog, along with him, the man you seized with him: ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Ḥakam.” Marwān accordingly remitted it for him. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Sayḥān said: (al-ṭawīl)

My bond is to the best of humankind ... counted among them when the confederates’ staff is scattered.

From his words, it became clear that he was counted among them.

He is not an ally, since he acknowledged his affiliation in his poetry.

Among those in the Banū al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib:

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿīd ibn al-Qasb of Azd Shanuʾa. He said: I think he entered among them by marrying Baḥīna daughter of al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. She has long since died, and he left no descendants. He said: The family of Juhaym, from the Sakāsik, entered the Banū al-Muṭṭalib ibn ʿAbd Manāf through a marriage connection.

Among those in the Banū ʿAbd al-Dār ibn Quṣayy:

The family of ʿAllāṭ al-Bāhizī, from the Banū Sulaym ibn Manṣūr, the clan of Ḥajjāj ibn ʿAllāṭ. Their entry among them came about because Ṣafiyya daughter of Abū Ṭalḥa ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAbd al-Dār had been married to Ḥajjāj. She bore him Muʿriḍ ibn al-Ḥajjāj and his maternal half-brother. They entered the Banū ʿAbd al-Dār through marriage, and they had no alliance.

Among them are the family of Yaʿlā ibn Munya, from the Banū Tamīm. Munya was his mother; he was Yaʿlā ibn Umayya. I do not know why they entered the Banū ʿAbd al-Dār.

Among those in the Banū Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Quṣayy:

The family of Ḥāṭib ibn Abī Baltaʿa, a Companion of the Prophet ﷺ who had fought at Badr. Among them was a man from ʿAns of Yemen, attached to the Banū Asad without an alliance. ʿĀmir ibn Ṣāliḥ ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUrwa claimed him. He said: He was descended from al-Ḥārith ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā.

Among those in the Banū Zuhra ibn Kilāb:

The family of Yazīd, from the Jadra of al-Azd, entered the Zuhra through the marriage of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yazīd to the daughter of al-Aswad ibn ʿAwf ibn ʿAbd ʿAwf ibn ʿAbd [ibn-] al-Ḥārith ibn Zuhra. They had no alliance. Among them are the family of Abū Bishr, from Khuzāʿa, including the poet Karāma al-Bushrī. They entered through their kinship with Sibāʿ ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā of Khuzāʿa.

Among them are the family of ʿAbd al-Qārī, the Banū al-Hawn ibn Khuzayma ibn Mudrika. Among them was Masʿūd ibn ʿAmr al-Qārī, a Companion of the Prophet ﷺ; he fought at Badr and was killed at Khaybar. He said: I heard some affirm their alliance, and heard others question it, saying that they had entered the Banū Zuhra only through their kin and in-laws.

Among them is the family of Shuraḥbīl ibn Ḥasana. Shuraḥbīl was the son of Sufyān ibn Muʿammar ibn Ḥabīb ibn Wahb ibn Ḥudhāfa ibn Jumah. His mother, Ḥasana, was from the Ashʿarīs and was married to Sufyān ibn Muʿammar. He adopted her son, Shuraḥbīl, and she bore him Muḥammad ibn Sufyān. Thus she, the two of them, and Sufyān were among those who emigrated to Abyssinia. Some people said that he was Muḥammad ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Muʿammar. Muḥammad had vowed to abstain from meat until he saw the Prophet ﷺ. He set out from Abyssinia, and when he was between Jeddah and ʿUsfān on his way to the Prophet ﷺ and grant him peace, he was overtaken by death. He said, “I would hate to meet God, mighty and glorious, having forbidden myself something He has made lawful.” He called for meat and ate it, as did his brother Sufyān. The Banū Ḥuṭāb and the Jumahī Ḥāṭibs quarreled with ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Shuraḥbīl, who was well off, and insulted him through his mother. He said, “I am not one of you. I am a man of al-Ghawth ibn Mur, the brother of Tamīm ibn Mur. They are the ones to whom the Arabs used to say, when they halted between the two narrow passes: ‘Make way, Ṣūfa!’”

He said: ʿAffān ibn Shabba told me: The mother of al-Ghawth bore daughters. She swore that if she bore a boy, she would dedicate him to the Sacred House. The first child she bore was al-Ghawth ibn Mur, and he was her eldest son. She tied him around the House. His sister Takma daughter of Mur passed by him—she was the mother of Ghaṭafān and Sulaym, who were brothers through their mother—and said, “By God, my brother has become nothing but a tuft of wool in the heat of the sun!” For this reason he was called Ṣūfa. They used to lead the people on the pilgrimage, and the Arabs would say to them, “Make way, Ṣūfa!”

Rizāḥ ibn Rabīʿa al-ʿUdh­rī, the maternal brother of Quṣayy and Zuhra, mentioned this in these verses: (al-wāfir)

I took the pilgrimage rites from ʿAdwān by force;

Had I encountered Ṣūfa, I would have avenged myself.

When wrong is done to him, I offer my support;

And he does likewise when I am wronged.

Then the account returned to Shuraḥbīl. He said: ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Shuraḥbīl rode to Muʿāwiya and said, “I am a man of al-Ghawth ibn Mur.” He replied, “Consider what you are saying.” He said:

“My lineage is from them, so transfer my register.”

“Where should I put it?” he asked.

“In the Banū Zuhra.”

So he transferred it. I think he transferred it specifically to the Zuhra because of the friendship between him and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Zuhra.

Among those in the Banū Taym:

The family of ʿAlqama ibn Waqqāṣ al-Laythī. They entered among them because ʿAlqama ibn Waqqāṣ married, in Islam, a daughter of ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿUthmān, the sister of Ṭalḥa ibn ʿUbayd Allāh. They entered among them through this marriage connection.

Among them is the family of Abū Yaḥyā. They are clients who affiliate themselves with Ḥakam of Yemen. Among them is the family of al-Ṭufayl ibn al-Art. They entered the Taym through their kinship with ʿĀʾisha, the Mother of the Believers.

Among them is Ṣuhayb ibn Sinān ibn Yazīd ibn al-Namir ibn Qāsiṭ. He lived on the bank of the Euphrates, in a village called al-Thannī. When he was a boy, the Byzantines took him captive among a group of of the Khazraj, from al-Namir. He grew up among the Byzantines until he came of age. Kalb bought him and brought him to ʿUkāẓ, where ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān bought him. He was foreign-speaking and set him free. He was the brother of Mālik ibn Sinān, the Persian king’s governor over al-Ubulla. When Ṣuhayb was stolen, Mālik said: (al-rajaz)

I adjure by God the Namirite boy ... dajja while my family is at al-Thannī.

He said: This is how it has come down. I have heard more than one person recite it this way.

Among those in the Banū Makhzūm:

The family of al-Fuḍayl ibn ʿAfīf ibn Kulayb ibn Ḥabashiyya ibn Salūl ibn Kaʿb ibn ʿAmr.

Among them is the family of Khurāsh ibn Umayya. They entered in the early period of Islam through Khurāsh ibn Umayya’s marriage to Qudhdha daughter of ʿArfaja ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm.

Among them is a clan of the Banū Sāma ibn Luʾayy, whom Ibrāhīm ibn Hishām al-Makhzūmī brought among them by means of an allotment assigned to them by Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik.

Among them is the family of Abū Yāsir, from the Banū Tamīm. They entered through an allotment from ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān— Hishām ibn Ismāʿīl assigned it to them.

Among them is the family of ʿAmmār ibn Yāsir, a Companion of the Prophet ﷺ. Their story is that Yāsir, a man from ʿAns of Yemen, came to Mecca with his two brothers, al-Ḥārith and Mālik, in search of a brother of theirs. Al-Ḥārith and Mālik departed, while Yāsir stayed. He married Sumayya daughter of Khayṭ, a slave woman belonging to Abū Ḥudhayfa. She bore him ʿAmmār ibn Yāsir ﵁. Then al-Azraq, the slave of al-Ḥārith ibn Kalada, took her as his wife. He was among those freed at the conquest of al-Ṭāʾif in the Islamic period. She bore him ʿAmr and Salama, the sons of al-Azraq; they were maternal half-brothers. Abū Ḥudhayfa freed ʿAmmār. His affiliation with ʿAns is therefore sound, and he was a client of the family of Abū Ḥudhayfa ibn al-Mughīra.

Among them is Abraha ibn al-Ṣabbāḥ. It is said [that he-] was from Ḥimyar, and [he-] was Abyssinian. He became Muslim, and no one had a claim of favor over him.

Among those in the Banū ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb:

The Laythī family of Bukayr entered through an allotment assigned to them by ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵁. They claim that they had been neighbors of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵀—and this is more firmly established, because they were present at Badr and are counted among the Badr veterans of the Banū ʿAdī.

Among them is the family of ʿĀmir ibn Rabīʿa, the family of Qurayṭ. They are from ʿAnz ibn Wāʾil, the brothers of Bakr ibn Wāʾil. They entered among them because ʿĀmir emigrated to the Prophet ﷺ fought at Badr, and was a friend of ʿUmar. ʿUmar assigned him a stipend among his people, as one of the Badr veterans of the Banū ʿAdī. A more firmly established account is that al-Khaṭṭāb adopted him and that he inherited from al-Khaṭṭāb alongside his children. When God, mighty and glorious, revealed what He revealed about the case of Zayd ibn Ḥāritha, he was affiliated with his father, Rabīʿa. Rabīʿa had died, leaving him a young child.

Among them is the family of Wāqid ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Tamīmī, who was from the Banū ʿArīn ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Yarbuʿ. Wāqid had emigrated and fought at Badr, and was a friend of ʿUmar, who assigned him a stipend with his people of the Banū ʿAdī. This is contradicted by his being counted among the Badr veterans of the Banū ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb. It is said that he was an ally who committed an offense against his people, fled to Mecca, and allied himself with the Banū ʿAdī.

Among them is the family of Rāfiʿ. They affiliate themselves with Lakhm, and Rāfiʿ was a client of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵁.

Among them is the family of Numayr, the companions of Ḥuḍayr. Among them was the poet Abū Numayr. They affiliate themselves with Hamdān. They were clients of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb. One of them, ʿAkriz al-Fāʾid, claimed—

…to Hamdān, and disavowed the clientage of ʿUmar.

. Among those affiliated with Banū Jumah

The families of Abū Yasār, Abū Fakīhah, and Abū Tajzaʾah—the clients of ʿUmārah ibn al-Walīd. Ṣafwān ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣafwān ibn Umayyah ibn Khalaf had married a daughter of Abū Yasār. When Muḥammad ibn Ṣafwān ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣafwān came to ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān, the latter asked him, “Who is your mother?” He replied, “The daughter of Abū Yasār.” ʿAlqamah ibn Waqqāṣ said, “Your father had a keener eye for marriage than you, when he married the daughter of ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUthmān [and] the sister of Ṭalḥah ibn ʿUbayd Allāh.”

. Among those affiliated with Banū Sahm, who had no alliance in the Jāhiliyyah

The family of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Yazīd ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn Ḥabīb. They claim descent from Ghaṭafān, though some people assert that they are from Balī, from Irāshah. The Banū ʿAbs claim that Abū Yazīd, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar, was a Persian slave belonging to them who escaped from him; on the night he escaped, he was named Mulāṣ. He said: There was no alliance among Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy in the Jāhiliyyah. In Islam, the Ḥimyarites of Banū Janāb joined them by claim of kinship; they are from the Thamūd of al-Yamāmah. The family of ʿImrān ibn Abī Anas also joined them. They claim to be Ashʿarites, from Banū Asʿad, and say that Abū Anas was Nawfal ibn Bajjād. The Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy claim that Abū Anas was a slave belonging to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd ibn Abī Sarḥ. The family of Shurayḥ also joined them; they claim that they are from Lakhm. They brought their genealogy from Syria in a document from one of the judges of Syria to Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz al-Zuhrī, who [was] then serving as judge of Medina. Their genealogy is sound, for Shurayḥ had been a slave belonging to Abū ʿAmr ibn Ḥammās al-Daylī. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn ʿImrān ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz al-Zuhrī said: Among what has come down to us from the Prophet ﷺ—concerning confirmation of alliances from the Jāhiliyyah, and concerning the occasions after which he intended there to be no further alliances, is this. He said: ʿUrwah ibn al-Zubayr related it, tracing it back to the Prophet, who said: “There is no alliance in Islam; whatever alliance existed in the Jāhiliyyah, Islam only makes it stronger.” He said:

My maternal uncle ʿAdī ibn Thābit related to me that al-Aws wished to form an alliance with Sulaym. The Messenger of God ﷺ—said, “There is no alliance in Islam, and Islam only makes an alliance from the Jāhiliyyah stronger.” It was related from Zayd ibn Aslam, from al-Aʿmash, from al-Shaʿbī, who said: The Messenger of God ﷺ—said, “There is no alliance in Islam; the alliance of the Jāhiliyyah remains binding.” This is what reached ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz from the Prophet ﷺ—concerning confirming alliances of the Jāhiliyyah and weakening alliances formed in Islam. He said: The Banū al-Ghazālah, of Banū Sulaym and then of Banū Bahz, committed an offense among their people: they killed a man, then left and crossed the lava plain. They came down upon Abū Julayd, whose dwelling was at al-Sitārah, and formed an alliance with him. Their people pursued them until they caught up with them, but Ibn Abī Julayd protected them. He said, “My father made an alliance with them, and I shall pay their blood-money.” A man from Banū…

A man of Bahz said: (rajaz)

I brought them here, O son of Abū Julayd,

Low fellows, like black jerboas.

Ibn Abī Julayd said: (rajaz)

I brought them here, their flanks rising high…

Every onlooker takes delight in them.

He said: In the time of ʿUthmān ﵀ Bahz disputed with Ibn Abī Julayd over their alliance. They said, “They formed an alliance while the Prophet ﷺ—was in Mecca; this, then, was an alliance in Islam.” He ruled that every alliance formed while the Messenger of God ﷺ—was in Mecca belonged to the Jāhiliyyah, while any formed during the Hijrah belonged to Islam, and that there was no alliance in Islam.

Abū Rabīʿah, the grandfather of Isḥāq ibn Muslim ibn Abī Rabīʿah al-ʿUqaylī, had formed an alliance with Jaʿfī. Jaʿfī claimed that his lineage was from them, but Banū ʿUqayl denied it, saying, “They formed an alliance in Islam.” Jaʿfī denied this, saying, “Rather, their alliance was in the Jāhiliyyah.” ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵁ ruled that every alliance formed before the revelation of “For the alliance of Quraysh” was from the Jāhiliyyah, and every alliance formed after its revelation was dissolved. By this, ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵇ meant that anyone who made [an alliance] to enter Quraysh after its revelation would have that claim rejected. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz said: ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵁ said, “Every alliance formed…”

…before al-Ḥudaybiyah remains binding, while every alliance formed after it is dissolved. This is because, when the Messenger of God ﷺ—made a truce with Quraysh, he wrote an agreement between them and himself stating that whoever wished to enter the covenant and pact of Quraysh could do so, and whoever wished to enter the covenant and pact of Muḥammad ﷺ—could do so.

He said: Ibn ʿAbbās said, “Every alliance formed before the revelation of God Almighty’s words, ‘For each We have appointed heirs to what parents and near relatives leave; and to those to whom your oaths have bound you, give their share’ (4:33), remains binding, and every alliance formed afterward is dissolved.”

He said: Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAbd al-Qārī said, “Regarding alliances, these words were revealed: ‘O you who believe, fulfill your contracts. Grazing livestock are permitted to you’ (5:1),” through the end of the verse.

He said: Muḥammad ibn ʿAlī related from his father, from Yazīd ibn Rikānah, who said: The Messenger of God ﷺ—said, “O company of Quraysh, enter the Dār al-Nadwah, and let no one enter except you.” They said, “Messenger of God, there are others among us.” He said, “Who?” They said, “ʿUtbah ibn Ghazwān.” The Messenger of God ﷺ—said, “An ally of a people belongs to them; a sister’s son of a people belongs to them; and a client of a people belongs to them.” He said: A similar report was related from Ḥizām ibn Hishām, from his father, from the Prophet.

He said: Among those who entered into Quraysh’s alliances despite not being their allies were the Ḥaḍārimah. Their story was that Kisrā sent a caravan bearing perfumes and trade goods to ʿUkāẓ, and Banū Tamīm and Banū Shaybān intercepted it…

Banū Tamīm and Banū Shaybān seized it. Kisrā sent cavalry against them and appointed Wahriz over the force. They set out until Tamīm and Shaybān met them at Dhū Qār. They killed a horseman [and Wahriz] and seized the caravan. They sold them in al-Yamāmah, al-Baḥrayn, and ʿUmān. They brought back Buzur Mihr, sold him, and he was a skilled craftsman. Ṣakhr ibn Razan al-Daylī bought him. Then a man from Ḥaḍramawt came to him and took him to Ḥaḍramawt, where he ransomed him for four thousand dirhams and brought him back. He was called “the Ḥaḍramī” because he had come from Ḥaḍramawt. Ṣakhr ibn Razan said: (kāmil)

A mount whose pack-saddle’s base I wore away,

And which my journey and my travel wearied,

I seek to ransom Zar Mihr, for he

Has become a grave loss to us—know this.

The Ḥaḍramī was freed. He settled in Mecca, grew wealthy, and had handsome sons and daughters who proved distinguished. His sons married whomever they wished. They claim an alliance with Ḥarb ibn Umayyah, though they have no alliance with anyone among Quraysh.

Someone other than ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz said: The story of the Ḥaḍramī was that Kulthūm ibn Razan and his brother al-Aswad ibn Razan ibn Yaʿmar ibn Nufāthah ibn ʿAdī ibn al-Dayl went as merchants to Ḥaḍramawt. There they saw a Persian slave, a carpenter called Zar Mihr, who belonged to a man from Ḥaḍramawt known by the kunya Abū Rifāʿah. They admired him and his intelligence, and tricked him into escaping with them. They arrived in Mecca, where he stayed and worked. They told his master where he was, and the master came seeking him and seized him. Ibn Razan then bought him from the master, paid him part of the price, and stipulated that whenever the master brought him the rest, he would hand over the slave. The master came and gave him that amount, then set out on his return to Ḥaḍramawt. Ibn Razan continued until he had gathered the remainder of the slave’s price; then he set out toward him, saying: (kāmil)

Tell Abū Rifāʿah, if you reach him, that

From Ḥaḍramawt my messenger must deliver this:

By your life, I have not been remiss, and I have kept

Seeking his ransom by every means.

A mount whose pack-saddle’s base I wore away,

And which my journey and my travel wearied,

I seek to ransom Zar Mihr, for he

Has become a grave loss to us—know this.

He paid the price to the master, took possession of him, and brought him to Mecca, where he left him to work. The people there called him “the Ḥaḍramī,” until the name prevailed and he was known by no other. Then his master freed him, and he worked for himself until he prospered and grew wealthy. He sought the protection of Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb, who granted it to him. He attached himself to him. The Banū Nufāthah, it is said, were allies of Ḥarb ibn Umayyah; by this connection he joined them. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz said: Among those who became part of Quraysh’s alliances, though they had no alliance, were the family of Mālik al-Dār, a client of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb. They claim descent from Jublān in Yemen.

They only entered Quraysh’s alliances when they disavowed ʿUmar’s clientage and, during the caliphate of al-Mahdī, asked him to have them counted among Quraysh’s allies. He granted their request and enrolled them among them. They are clients of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵁.

Among them are the family of Abū ʿAwn al-Dawsī. They were people who had not formed an alliance; they are the Banū Nabīsh. They entered because of their brethren.

He said: The family of Abū Dhubāb entered the alliances through Daws, though they are not of Daws: they are Banū al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAmr and have no alliance. He said: The family of Muʿayqīb ibn Abī Fāṭimah, a client of Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ, also entered among them; they claim descent from Banū al-Ḥārith ibn ʿĀmir.

He said: There were alliances between clans of Quraysh, and alliances between clans of the Arabs; there were also alliances between clans of the Arabs, some with others. These were in addition to those we recorded at the beginning of this book. Those alliances were broken and abandoned. We have recorded what we were able to preserve of them. Among these was the alliance of ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb with Sahm. Ṣaddād ibn ʿAbd ibn Adhāh ibn Riyāḥ ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Qurṭ ibn Razāḥ ibn ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb stole a she-camel belonging to ʿAbd Shams ibn ʿAbd Manāf. Banū ʿAbd Manāf rose against Ṣaddād, intending to cut off his hand. Banū ʿAdī then formed an alliance with Sahm, who were the sons of their sister Umm Sahm and Jumāḥ, the sons of ʿAmr ibn Huṣayṣ. Al-Alūf was the daughter of ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb. ʿĀmir ibn ʿAbd Allāh said: (wāfir)

May my father and mother be ransomed for Banū Suhaym

When throats are choked with anguish.

He said: This is how the verse has come down to us. Banū Sahm prevented Banū ʿAdī from being seized by Banū ʿAbd Manāf. Later, Ḥārithah, the grandfather of Muṭīʿ ibn al-Aswad ibn Ḥārithah al-ʿAdawī, drank with a group of Banū Sahm, among whom was the grandfather of ʿAmr ibn Huṣayṣ al-Sahmī. Ḥārithah struck him a blow that split his head, and that blow brought to an end the alliance between ʿAdī and Sahm.

Among these was the alliance of Banū al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr with ʿAbd Manāf

He said: ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn ʿĀmirah ibn ʿUmayrah ibn Wadīʿah ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr married Ḥayyah, daughter of ʿAbd Manāf ibn Quṣayy. She was one of those who lived in al-Layth and Ajmah…

Ādām and she bore him Abū Hamhama. When he came of age, he said to his father, “Why should we remain in a land where the Banū ʿAbd Manāf are not?” He said, “What draws you to your maternal uncles, who dwell in the Sanctuary?” He said, “Either you send me to them, or I will join them.” He said, “Then go—may God cut off your line!” Abū Hamhama joined his maternal uncles, entered into an alliance with them, and married the daughter of Abū ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd Manāf, who was his maternal cousin. The Banū al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr came and entered into an alliance with him. The alliance of the Banū al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr thus endured to this day, while Abū Hamhama died out, leaving no offspring.

Among these was the alliance of the Aws and Quraysh, which did not last

He said: The Aws left as exiles from the Khazraj until they settled with Quraysh in Mecca and entered into an alliance with them. After they had made the alliance, al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīra said, “By God! Whenever one people has settled among another, they have taken their honor and inherited their lands. So break the alliance with the Aws.” They said, “How?”

He said, “These people are modest. Tell them: ‘We have forgotten something we did not mention to you. When our women circumambulate the House, if a man sees a woman he likes, he kisses her and touches her with his hand.’” When they said this to the Aws, they recoiled and said, “Break the alliance between us and you.” So they broke it. This alliance between Quraysh and the Aws then came to an end, except for the alliance between ʿUtba ibn Abī Waqqāṣ al-Zuhrī and ʿUtba ibn al-Mundhir ibn Uḥayḥa ibn al-Jallāḥ, for that alliance endured. He therefore took up residence—

…a house at Qubāʾ, where he would stay and reside. It was the house behind Biʾr Ghars on the right, built with lime plaster. He said: Ibn Abī ʿUbayda said:

The Banū ʿAbd al-Ashhal, Ẓafar, the Banū Muʿāwiya, and the people of Rātij set out for Mecca to enter into an alliance with Quraysh. They made it appear that they intended to perform the lesser pilgrimage, and no one interfered with anyone who intended to perform the pilgrimage or the lesser pilgrimage. When they entered the state of consecration, they would hang ropes from the tops of the āṭām and hang palm-leaf stalk bases from them. When these were seen, people would say, “So-and-so’s people have entered the state of consecration.” They tied ropes to the tops of their āṭām and hung palm-leaf stalk bases from them, and people said, “The Banū ʿAbd al-Ashhal have entered the state of consecration for the lesser pilgrimage.” ʿAbd Allāh ibn Maʿrūr, the brother of the Banū Salima, specifically of the Banū ʿUbayd, granted protection to their property after they had departed. His mother was a woman from the Banū— When they set out for Mecca, Qays ibn al-Khaṭīm composed this poem:

(al-wāfir)

Has Laylā, mother of ʿAmr, appeared to me in a vision,

though she has not appeared to us except for a purpose?

We urged on the palms and the fortresses until,

when they would not yield to our urging,

we resolved to stay, then set out,

like the march of Ḥudhayfa, the good, son of Badr,

with blame for the two soothsayers and blame for ʿAmr;

a sign of how they forgot every calamity.

My two female companions said as the caravan set out,

“Will you leave what you gathered at the end of the night?”

I said to her, “Let me be. My wealth

goes out at evening and comes in by morning.

You will not find me present unless you see us

fighting you as if we were drinkers of wine.

Quraysh will bear your host away from us,

as though their fingertips were rubbing unripe dates.

All ten of the confederates will meet,

and brace their broken resolve with strength.

We have held dominion over honor—Maʿadd knows it—

and in Yathrib we were humbled for no more than a month.

We abandoned them and left our allies,

and parted from those who called for aid, not from poverty.”

If we join Abraha of Yemen,

and Nuʿmān sends us forth, along with ʿAmr—

When they had entered into an alliance with them, they stayed for several days. Then Abū Jahl ibn Hishām returned from a journey, learned their situation, and said to Quraysh, “You made a mistake when you entered into an alliance with them. They are people of treachery and wrongdoing, and scarcely ever does one people settle among another without driving them from their land and taking possession of their homes.” They said to him, “How can we be released from their alliance?” He said, “I will take care of that for you. They are among the Arabs most zealous in guarding their honor and most fastidious. Perhaps I can approach them on that basis.” Then he went out and came to them. He said, “You entered into an alliance with my people while I was away. I have come to enter into an alliance with you, and to tell you something about us so that you may know where you stand. We take our women to our markets, where they buy and sell. A man among us may come upon one of them if she pleases him and strike her buttocks. If you are willing for your women to do as our women do, we will enter into an alliance with you. If you dislike that, return our alliance to us.” They said, “We do not accept this, and we have returned your alliance to you.” Thus that alliance came to an end, and this was the reason for its dissolution.

Among these was the alliance of Mirdās ibn Abī ʿĀmir and Ḥarb ibn Umayya

He said: Mirdās ibn Abī ʿĀmir al-Sulamī entered into an alliance with Ḥarb ibn Umayya ibn— ʿAbd Shams and Abū al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayya ibn ʿAbd Shams. Mirdās said of this:

(al-wāfir)

They have a lineage, and our father allied with them

in Mecca, where the spears cross.

He also said: (al-basīṭ)

I chose the Banū Ḥarb and his brothers;

I am bound by a tightly knotted, hidden cord.

I stand before the matter, making its case,

so that it may be said, “Mirdās is the one in charge.”

He said: Then this alliance was broken.

Among these was the alliance of the Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy and ʿAdī ibn ʿAmr

The first alliance of the Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy with ʿAdī ibn ʿAmr and his brother Kaʿb ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿĀmir ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Māzin ibn al-Asad was that they stayed among them until, some time after the Year of the Elephant, Ḥuwayṭib ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā went out with a group of his people and settled in Mecca. They did not choose any place to stay except the valley floor; they pitched their tents there and then settled in it. They broke the alliance with the Banū ʿAdī ibn ʿAmr, and they had belonged neither to the Confederates, nor to the Perfumed Ones, nor to the League of the Virtuous. When Mālik ibn Ḥisl left, the Banū ʿAbd ibn Muʿayṣ returned from among them. The Banū Muʿayṣ, al-Adram ibn Ghālib, and Muḥārib ibn Fihr then formed an alliance, and they have been called the Banū Fihr up to this day. They broke the alliance of the Banū ʿAdī. Then the alliance of the Banū Muʿayṣ was broken from—

…ʿAdī ibn ʿAmr. The alliance of ʿAbd ibn Muʿayṣ, Taym ibn Ghālib, and the Banū Muḥārib ibn Fihr endured, and they have been called the Banū Fihr up to this day.

What is reported about the alliance of the Perfumed Ones and the Confederates, in the account of Ibn Abī Thābit

He said: The affair of the Perfumed Ones and the Confederates was as follows. When Quraysh built the Kaʿba, they divided it into four parts. The Banū ʿAbd Manāf received the area from the Black Stone to the Corner of the Stone, including the entire open space of the House. Asad, ʿAbd al-Dār, and Zuhra received the entire Stone. Makhzūm and Taym received the rear of the House, and the rest of Quraysh received the area from the Yemeni Corner to the Black Corner. When they had built it and finished, they disputed which of them should raise the Corner. The Banū ʿAbd Manāf said, “It is our share,” while Quraysh said, “The Corner was not part of what we divided.” They were on the verge of fighting over it, until they agreed that the first Qurashī to come to them through Bāb al-Sayl—the gate of the family of Shayba—would arbitrate between them. The Messenger of God ﷺ came out to them, and they appointed him as arbitrator. He took his cloak, laid it down, and then raised the Stone with his hand ﷺ. He said to each group, “Take hold of one edge of the cloth.” They all raised it together. Then the Messenger of God ﷺ went beneath the Stone and built it into place with his own hand ﷺ.

When they had finished building, completed the House, and established the water service, the Banū ʿAbd Manāf said, “Our brothers, the Banū ʿAbd al-Dār, hold offices that we do not hold. In their hands are the provision of food for pilgrims, the banner, the council, and the guardianship of the Kaʿba. We hold only the water service.” They said to them, “Come, give us some of what you hold.” The Banū ʿAbd al-Dār said, “We will not give you what we inherited from our father and grandfather, as it has been since we came into being.” The Banū ʿAbd Manāf said, “Then let us submit the matter to arbitration by whomever you choose.” They said:

“We will submit the matter to Jābir ibn Muḥammad ibn Wāʾila ibn Shaybān ibn Muḥārib ibn Fihr, who was Abū Karz’s father. Abū Karz was Jābir, a Companion of the Prophet ﷺ who was killed on the day of the Conquest.” They disputed the matter before him. He was called “the devout worshipper of Fihr.” The Banū ʿAbd Manāf said, “[From—] our father Quṣayy’s inheritance, we hold only the water service.” The Banū ʿAbd al-Dār said, “Our father’s inheritance and what his father entrusted to him, to the exclusion of his other sons.” Jābir said, “Fortune is followed, justice is binding, and the one who came first deserves that you associate them with yourselves; associate them—be patient until you are released.”8

When they refused them, the Banū ʿAbd Manāf said, “Give the Banū Asad the provision for pilgrims, and keep the rest for yourselves.” The Banū ʿAbd al-Dār said:

We will not loosen a bond, nor cast away a tie, nor renounce a father.

When they refused them, Quraysh gathered together until they saw what the Banū ʿAbd Manāf had sought, and they desired to share authority with them. So they entered into alliances. The Banū ʿAbd Manāf, Asad, Zuhra, Taym, and the Banū al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr entered into an alliance. Umm Ḥakīm, daughter of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, brought out for them a bowl of black-and-white beads containing perfume. They dipped their hands in it, and so they were called the Perfumed Ones. The Banū ʿAbd al-Dār, Makhzūm, ʿAdī, Jumaḥ, and Sahm entered into an alliance. The Banū ʿAbd al-Dār brought out a large bowl containing blood, and they dipped their hands in it. They were called the Lickers, and they were the Confederates. Then they made—

…their alliance and prepared for battle. Then they drew back. The Banū Kilāb said, “They are our brothers, and they are closer to us than others; we should not kill them or sever ties with them. If they kill us, others will kill them.” So they refrained from fighting and left them with what they held. When they had come to fight, they had divided themselves into groups: they placed the Banū al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr with ʿAbd Manāf, and assigned the sons of Huṣayṣ—Sahm and Jumaḥ—to them; they placed Asad with ʿAbd al-Dār; they placed Makhzūm with Zuhra; and they placed Taym with ʿAdī.

Ibn al-Zibaʿrā said the following when ʿUthmān ibn Ṭalḥa ibn Abī Ṭalḥa al-ʿAbdarī, Khālid ibn al-Walīd, and ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ became Muslims, reminding them of that alliance: (al-ṭawīl)

I adjure ʿUthmān ibn Ṭalḥa by our alliance,

and by the sandals cast off to the right of the one approaching.

Do you seek the key of a House other than your own?

The door to the matter you seek is shut.

And by the covenant the fathers made in every alliance,

Khālid is no one to break a covenant like it.

ʿIkrima ibn ʿĀmir al-ʿAbdarī said of this: (al-ṭawīl)

By God, we will not do what you have desired,

while we stand united, or until we are stained with blood.

We are the guardians of the House—you know it;

so how can we be wronged knowingly?

What is reported about the League of the Virtuous, in the account of Ibn Abī Thābit, which came after the alliance of the Perfumed Ones

He said: The Perfumed Ones and the Confederates remained in their alliances for a long time. Then a man from the Banū Zubayd of Yemen came to Mecca with his merchandise and sold it to a man from the Banū Sahm, called Ḥudhayfa ibn Qays ibn Saʿd ibn Sahm. The Sahmī wronged him and withheld his due, so he appealed for help among Quraysh, but no one came to his aid. Someone said to the Zubaydī, “Go to the Confederates.” He went to them and spoke with them, but they did not help him. They said, “If we help him, trouble will arise between us and our brothers.” So they left him. He stayed for several days. Then Ḥanẓala ibn al-Sharqī, one of the Balqayn of Jusham, arrived and became the neighbor in Mecca of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān al-Taymī. He had camels with him. Some of the Quraysh clans attacked him and slaughtered some of his camels. When Ḥanẓala heard of this, he brought them three she-camels and said to them, “Slaughter these in place of the ones you slaughtered; you are their people.” They were ashamed. But later they returned and seized the rest of his camels and took them away. He began to say: (al-ṭawīl)

“Have you not heard the Mirqāl groan, and her master long ... remembering Armām and recalling my people?

She spent the night, while anguish lay beneath her throat ...

in thinness, because the wild beasts had not been slaughtered.

If she knew the turns of trade, she would be glad ... in Mecca to buy ḥamḍ for idhkhir.”

“It would delight you if we were beside ʿUnayza ... in ḥamḍ, Ḍumrān al-Janāb, and ṣaʿtar.

I hope for its milk within your bellies ... and what I have spread out of the hide of an unkempt, dust-covered beast.

If she found a land distasteful, I found it distasteful too;

and ruin awaits, if I do not change course.

The recompense of Sinimmār: repay them, and their lord;

and, by al-Lāt and al-ʿUzzā, the recompense of the denier.

Sons of al-Sharqī, is it truly the case that they ... whenever they take a neighbor from among people, they betray him?

When I said, ‘Fulfill it,’ its fulfillment came upon him ... O you who harm neighbors through oppression, desist!”

He said: Sinimmār was a man from among the people of Persia, though it is also said that he was from the Romans. He built a palace at al-Qādisiyya or al-ʿUdhayb for Kisrā. When he had finished it—though it is also said that he built Shanīf and Mārd at Taymāʾ—ʿĀdiyaʾ the Jew killed him. The Aws claim that he built Wāqim, the fortress of Ḥuḍayr al-Katāʾib, and that he killed him when he had finished. Abū Jaʿfar said: It is also said that Sinimmār built for Uḥayḥa ibn al-Jalāḥ al-Awsī the fortress of al-Ḍaḥyān. He said to him, “I know of a stone in it which, if moved, would bring down the fortress.” He said, “Does anyone besides you know it?” He replied, “Climb up and I will show it to you.” He climbed up and leaned out to show him, so Uḥayḥa pushed him headfirst and threw him down. It is also said that Sinimmār built al-Khawarnaq for Bahrām Jūr, son of Kisrā, who was under the protection of Dhū al-Qarnayn al-Lakhmī. When Sinimmār had finished it, they marveled at its beauty. He said, “Had I known you would give me my wages, I would have built you a structure that revolved with the sun.” They said to him, “We see that you are skilled: you could have built something more beautiful and better than this, yet you did not.” Then they threw him from the top down to the bottom. The Arabs made him a proverb. Then he returned to the account. When the Zubaydī saw this, he climbed Mount Abū Qubays and cried out at the top of his voice: (al-basīṭ)

“O men, come to the aid of a man wronged in his wares,

in the heart of Mecca, far from his family and his clan!

The inviolable is inviolable to the one who honors its sanctity;

but there is no sanctuary for my garment, worn by one who betrays.

When Quraysh saw this, they were deeply moved. Hāshim, Zuhra, and Taym set out and entered the house of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān. They told him of the injustice they had witnessed and made a pact among themselves to repel oppression and exact the right from every wrongdoer. Saʿīd ibn al-Musayyab said: “They swore an oath among themselves by God, saying, ‘We are one hand against the wrongdoer, until we take the right from him, for as long as the sea wets a tuft of wool; and we pledge to share our livelihood equally.’” The Messenger of God ﷺ—said: “I witnessed a pact in the house of Ibn Judʿān that I would not wish to break. Were I offered red camels for it, and were I called to it today, I would answer.” They called their pact the Pact of the Virtuous because they stood apart from the Muṭayyibūn and the Confederates. He said: “I heard someone say it was called the Pact of the Virtuous because they swore that they would not leave anyone with a claim against another who had wronged him without taking it from the wrongdoer. It is also said that Quraysh said, ‘This is an excess on their part,’ and so the participants in the Pact of the Virtuous were named accordingly. The verse was revealed concerning the Pact of the Virtuous: ‘For each We have appointed heirs to what parents and close relatives leave behind; and those with whom your right hands have made a covenant, give them their share.’” (Qurʾān 4:33) in particular. He said: “One account concerning the Pact of the Virtuous is that a man from Khathʿam came to Mecca with his beautiful daughter, whose name was al-Durayra. Nabīh ibn al-Ḥajjāj took her and carried her off to al-Ramḍa, where he kept her by force. Her father went to Banū Sahm, but they did not help him. He went to the Quraysh clans, but they refused. Someone said to him, ‘Why don’t you go to the Pact of the Virtuous?’ He went to them, and they set out with him until they reached Nabīh. They said, ‘Return his daughter to him.’ He said, ‘Let me enjoy her tonight.’ They said, ‘By God, we will not leave until you bring her here.’ He handed her over to them, and they gave her to her father. Nabīh said: (al-kāmil)

“Farewell, al-Durayra, when she went away

from us along her path.

Neither does separation grant me

anything, nor does meeting her,

except for many promises

made when she had no need of me.

She seized the joy of his heart,

then went away—how can he bear her absence?

They raised the encampment toward them,

and found its water sweet.

Were it not for the Virtuous, and for their being

a refuge from her harshness,

I would have gone to her, walking without

a guide through her darkness,

and crept around her tent,

and lain down at her side.

And Sulā of Mecca, tell her

that I am among those who keep their pledges,

their bonds—and the best of them in generosity—

my sufficiency lies with her equals.”

He said: “Another account of the Pact of the Virtuous is that Lamīs ibn Saʿd al-Bāriqī, of al-Azd, came to Mecca with merchandise. Ubayy ibn Khalaf al-Jumaḥī bought it from him and then wronged him over it. Lamīs sought help against him, but could find no one to help. He was told, ‘Go to the people of the Pact of the Virtuous.’ He went to them and spoke with them. They said, ‘Go to him and tell him: “The Virtuous say to you: give him his due.” If he does, fine; otherwise, return to us and inform us, and tell him you will return to us.’ He went to Ubayy and delivered their message, and Ubayy gave him what he was owed. Lamīs said of them: (al-ṭawīl)

“Who will keep my property from being taken unjustly in Mecca—

Abū, while I have neither clan nor companions at hand?

I called to my people of Bāriq to answer me,

but how many deserts and open plains lie between me and my people!

Yet the Pact of the Virtuous refuses to let my claim be oppressed,

O Banū Jumḥ—and right is taken by force.

He said: “It has reached me that ʿUtba ibn Rabīʿa ibn ʿAbd Shams said, speaking of the Pact of the Virtuous: ‘By God, it is astonishing! Were a man to leave his people and lineage for a pact, I would leave my people for the Pact of the Virtuous.’” He said: “I was told by al-Mulaykī, in a report he traced back to the Messenger of God ﷺ—that he said: ‘I was present at a pact in the house of Ibn Judʿān in the pre-Islamic age. If I were called to the like of it, I would answer: that grievances be returned to their rightful owners, and that no wrongdoer oppress the wronged.’”

The account of those who held the guardianship of the House, how it came about, and how it eventually passed to Quraysh

ʿĪsā ibn Dāʾab al-Kinānī said: “The key of the House was in the hands of Jurhum. One of them, called Isāf ibn Yaʿlā, fell in love with a woman of his people called Nāʾila daughter of Mazīd—or Zayd. They found a secluded place in the House and fornicated there. They were transformed into two stones and taken out, then set up beside the Kaʿba so that people might take warning from what had happened. Later, after Quraysh had moved the two stones, they placed Isāf on al-Ṣafā and Nāʾila on al-Marwa, and worshipped them along with the idols they worshipped.

“Ibn al-Kalbī said that [Banū] Jurhum suffered an illness, and eighty mature men among them died in a single night, apart from the young. They left Mecca and went to Iḍam, al-Ashʿar, and al-Ajrad, two mountains of Juhayna. It is said that God destroyed them with tiny creatures. The Jurhumites said: (al-rajaz)

“The tiny creatures destroyed us when Yaqdum was alive,

because of our oppression and our committing sins.”

It is also said that the flood of Iḍam swept them away. Then the guardianship of the House passed to Iyād. The affairs of the House came under a man of theirs called Wakīʿ ibn Salama ibn Zuhr ibn Iyād. He built a lofty structure in lower Mecca, near what is today the market of the grain sellers, and placed in it a slave woman of his called al-Ḥazwara. From her, the market of Mecca was named Ḥazwara. He set up a ladder in it, which he would climb, claiming, “I am communing with God, mighty and majestic.” He uttered many words of goodness, and the Arab scholars have said much about him. The most common opinion about him was that he was one of the truthful. He practiced soothsaying and would say: “By the suckling mother, by Fāṭima and Wādiʿa, by the destroyer and the severer of ties, by the calamity and the joining of kinship, and by fine speech: your Lord declares that He will surely reward good with recompense and evil with punishment.” He would say, “Those on earth are servants of those in heaven. Jurhum perished and Iyād multiplied; such are righteousness and corruption.” When his death drew near, he gathered Iyād and said, “Hear my testament: speech is two words, and a matter comes after its explanation. Follow whoever is rightly guided, and reject whoever goes astray. Every ewe is held by its own leg.” He was the first to say this, and he sent it forth as a proverb. Wakīʿ died, and his death was announced on the mountain peaks. Bishr ibn al-Ḥujayr said:

(al-Mutaqārib metre)

We are Iyād, worshippers of God,

and a company of His intimates in peace.

We were the custodians of the Veil of the Ancient House

in the time of the nukhāʿ among Jurhum.

Ibn al-Kalbī mentioned that God sent against the Jurhumites who lived near the House creatures resembling naghaf. Eighty of their elders perished in a single night, apart from the young men, until they left Mecca for Iḍam. A mourning woman for Wakīʿ stood on Abū Qubays and said:

(al-Wāfir metre)

Alas, Wakīʿ, the brother of Iyād, has perished;

the messengers’ greetings be upon Wakīʿ.

The one who communed with God has died; there is no immortality,

and every nobleman of a people is brought low.

Then Muḍar grew numerous after Iyād. The first among them to grow numerous were ʿAdwān and Fahm. A man of Iyād and a man of Muḍar went out hunting. A hare passed between them, and they surrounded it so they could shoot at it. The Iyādī shot, but his arrow went astray and pierced the heart of the Muḍarī, killing him. The news reached Muḍar, and they said, “He only missed his mark.” But Fahm and ʿAdwān insisted on killing him. The people then clashed in a prolonged battle at al-Madīd. Fahm was on the verge of victory over Iyād, when Muḍar said to them, “Give us three days, for we cannot live with you in your land.” They granted them three days, and they departed eastward. When…

After they had travelled for a day, Fahm and ʿAdwān pursued them until they caught up with them. They said, “Return to us the women of Muḍar who have married among you.” They replied, “Do not sever our kinship. Put the matter to the women: whichever woman chooses her people, you shall return her; but if she wishes to go with her husband, you shall leave her to us.” They said, “Agreed.” Among those who chose her family was a woman from Khuzāʿa.

When Iyād intended to depart, on the last night they went to the Corner and tried to load it onto their camel, but the camel would not stand. They transferred it to another, but that one would not stand either. They tried to load it onto anything, but each animal sank to the ground. So they buried it beneath a tree and left. When Muḍar discovered it was missing, they were greatly troubled. The Khuzāʿī woman said to her people, “Take an oath from Fahm and ʿAdwān and all of Muḍar that, if you show them where it is, they will give you custody of the House.” They came to Fahm and ʿAdwān and said, “If we show you where the Corner is, will you make us its custodians?” They said, “Yes,” and all of Muḍar also said, “Yes.” She showed them its location, and they searched for it, restored it to its place, and entrusted it to them. It remained in the hands of Khuzāʿa until Quṣayy arrived and what happened to him happened. We have written about this in the account of Quṣayy and his brother Rizāḥ al-ʿUdhrī.

Then Quṣayy married Ḥubbā, daughter of Ḥalīl ibn Ḥabashiyya. The key to the House was in Ḥalīl’s possession. Quṣayy settled in Mecca with his aqnān. Ḥubbā bore him ʿAbd Manāf, ʿAbd al-Dār, ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā, and ʿAbdā, the sons of Quṣayy. Then a severe death affliction struck Khuzāʿa in Mecca, and a nosebleed epidemic spread among them, so they left…

to the surrounding region and settled at al-Ẓahrān. When they left, the deaths ceased among them and the nosebleed epidemic ended. Ḥalīl ibn Ḥabashiyya remained in Mecca as custodian of the House, with a group of his people, among them Abū Ghubbshān. He sent his sons away with those of his people who left, among them al-Mukhtariš, Hilāl, ʿĀmir, and ʿAbd; they were the sons of Ḥalīl. Then Ḥalīl died and bequeathed the custody after him to al-Mukhtariš. He handed the keys to Ḥubbā, Quṣayy’s wife, and told her to send them to her brother al-Mukhtariš ibn Ḥalīl, so that she could hand over to him what he had held of the custodianship and other matters. He included with her in the bequest Abū Ghubbshān al-Malkānī and her son ʿAbd al-Dār ibn Quṣayy.

When Quṣayy saw that Ḥalīl had died, his sons were away, and the keys were in the hands of his wife and son, he asked Ḥubbā to hand the keys to her son ʿAbd al-Dār. He said, “If your brothers return to Mecca, this disease will befall them.” He kept pressing his sons to say, “Ask your mother to make you custodians of your father’s House,” until she yielded to him. She said, “What am I to do about Abū…”

Ghubbshān, when he is my witness and has been appointed with me?” He said, “I, Quṣayy, will take care of Abū Ghubbshān and satisfy him, so that he will keep this quiet and tell the people that Ḥalīl had bequeathed the keys to his daughter’s son, ʿAbd al-Dār ibn Quṣayy.” She did so.

Quṣayy ibn Kilāb called Abū Ghubbshān al-Malkānī and said to him, “Would you be willing to relinquish this matter, which was entrusted to Ḥubbā and ʿAbd al-Dār, and leave it to them, in return for a worldly compensation?” Abū Ghubbshān was amenable and agreed. Quṣayy gave him garments and camels. The people said, “No bargain was worse than Abū Ghubbshān’s,” and it became a proverb. Abū Ghubbshān was not Ḥalīl’s heir or guardian; he was only an executor. He said, “He betrayed his testamentary trust,” and Ḥubbā transferred custody of the House to her son ʿAbd al-Dār and handed him the keys. They remained with the descendants of ʿAbd al-Dār.

When God granted His Prophet ﷺ—the conquest of Mecca, he ordered ʿUthmān ibn Abī Ṭalḥa ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAbd al-Dār to bring him the key to the Kaʿba. It is said that he intended to give it to al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, joining custody of the House to the provision of water. ʿUthmān went to his mother, but she refused to hand it to her son. She said to him, “The matter is not as you think.” She then gave it to him, and he brought it to the Messenger of God ﷺ—and handed it to him, saying, “Take it, Messenger of God, in God’s trust.” The Prophet opened the House and prayed inside it. Then God, Mighty and Glorious, revealed: “Indeed, God commands you to deliver trusts to those entitled to them” (Qurʾān 4:58). The Prophet returned it to ʿUthmān.

According to a report from Abū ʿAmr al-Shaybānī, custody of the House passed to Khuzāʿa because Rabīʿa ibn Ḥārith ibn…

ʿAmr ibn ʿĀmir ibn Ḥāritha ibn Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Māzin married Muhīra,

daughter of ʿAmr ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Muḍāḍ al-Jurhumī. She bore him ʿAmr ibn Rabīʿa. When ʿAmr came of age, became a leader, and attained distinction, he sought custody of the House. At that point war broke out between them and Jurhum. They reported that ʿAmr ibn Rabīʿa lived three hundred and forty-five years, and that his descendants numbered one thousand fighters in his lifetime. Among his descendants were Kaʿb, ʿAdī, Saʿd, Mulayḥ, and ʿAwf, the sons of ʿAmr. A long war was fought between them—or he said: a fierce one—then Khuzāʿa overcame Jurhum in control of the House. Jurhum left and settled in the valley of Iḍam, where they perished.

ʿAmr ibn Rabīʿa was the first to alter the religion of Abraham ﵇. He went to Syria and appointed a man from Banū ʿAbd [ibn—] Ḍaḍm, called Ākil al-Marwa, as his deputy over the House. At that time ʿAmr and the people of Mecca followed the religion of Abraham ﵇. When ʿAmr arrived in Syria, he settled in al-Balqāʾ and found people worshipping idols. He asked, “What are these statues that I see you worshipping?” They replied, “They are gods whom we adopt, seeking their aid against our enemy, and they grant us victory; we also seek healing from illness through them, and they heal us.” Their words took hold in his mind, and he said:

“Give me one of them, that I may adopt it in my land, for I am the custodian of the Sacred House of God, and the Arab delegations come to me from every quarter…” from every region.” They gave him an idol called Hubal. He carried it back and set it up for the people in Mecca, called the people to worship it, and established a religion of his own invention, one that no one before him had introduced. He declared the sāʾiba consecrated, made the baḥīra, linked the waṣīla, and protected the ḥāmī. The Arabs pledged allegiance to him in this matter.

They reported—and God knows best—that Isāf was a man of Banū Quṭūrāʾ who fell in love with a woman of Jurhum named Nāʾila. He fornicated with her in the Kaʿba, and God turned them both into stones. ʿAmr was angered by this and expelled the sons of Muḍāḍ, who were his maternal uncles and had expelled them from Mecca. They went to Yemen and dispersed among the tribes. Bakar…

Bakar ibn Ghālib ibn ʿAmr ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Muḍāḍ said this, recalling Mecca after he had left it: (al-Ṭawīl metre)

As though no people had ever dwelt between al-Ḥajūn and al-Ṣafā,

nor had any night wanderer passed the night in Mecca.

Indeed, we were its people, but the turns

of the nights and adverse destinies destroyed us.

ʿAmr drove us out from it to a land

where the wolf howls and the enemy lies in wait.

He also said: (al-Ṭawīl metre)

We were the custodians of the House and the people who dwelt there,

to which every pilgrim in the sacred month comes to fulfil his vow.

We dwelt there before the Ẓibāʾ, by inheritance,

which came to us from the sons of Hayy ibn Bayy ibn Jurhum.

We were driven away from it, though we were its keepers;

tribes of Kaʿb, ʿAwf, and Aslam.

Ḥalīl ibn Ḥabashiyya said: (al-Rajaz metre)

A sacred valley, its birds and beasts protected;

we are its custodians, and will not violate it.

Ibn Muḍāḍ stands there, brandishing his weapon.

Ḥalīl also said: (al-Rajaz metre)

We are the sons of ʿAmr, custodians of the Sacred Monument;

we repel the people of wrongdoing with what is right.

We are Ḥums, and not prey for the raiders.

Naṣr ibn al-Aḥabb al-ʿAdwānī answered him: (al-Rajaz metre)

You are the ones who speak obscenely and utter wrongdoing;

truth is with us beneath the blow of the dust-laden charge.

We have come upon you in a host wearing sannawr,

each a renowned man, resolute in battle.

He said: Then the House passed to ʿAbd al-Dār through the first account.

The Cause of the Conversion to Islam of Khālid and ʿAmr, the Sons of Saʿīd

Al-ʿAbbās related, on the authority of ʿAbd Allāh al-Hāshimī, who said:

The cause of that was that Khālid ibn Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ saw a vision before the Prophet’s mission began. It was as though darkness had enveloped Mecca, so that he could see neither plain nor mountain. Then he saw a light rise from Zamzam, like a lamp; it ascended, and he heard a voice in the light saying: “Glory be to Him! Glory be to Him! The son of Mārij has perished in the blazing fire of al-Ghaḍā, between Adhruḥ and al-Akama. Glory be to Him! Glory be to Him! The unlettered Prophet has been sent. Glory be to Him! Glory be to Him! The people of this town will call him a liar; he will be tortured twice, and you will perish the third time.” The light rose until I saw the date palms of Yathrib, bearing clusters of dates.

Khālid ibn Saʿīd went to his brother ʿAmr, who was the one he favored among his brothers, and recounted his vision to him. ʿAmr said, “My brother, if your vision is true, something noble will happen among the descendants of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib.” They were partners in their trade: one would stay home for a year while the other traveled. ʿAmr set out for Syria on his turn, and God sent Muḥammad ﷺ. Khālid believed in him. When he heard that his brother was approaching, he met him at a place where he would not ordinarily have met him. When ʿAmr caught sight of him, he was alarmed and said, “My brother, you have met me in a place where you would not ordinarily meet me. Has something happened?”

“Has anything happened?” he asked. “Nothing but good,” he replied. Then he took him aside and said, “My brother, do you remember the vision you had recounted to me?” He said, “What? Remind me of it.” He said:

“God has sent Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib as a prophet, calling people to God.” ʿAmr believed, and the two of them entered Islam together, concealing their faith.

He said: The Prophet ﷺ visited Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ during the illness from which he died. Saʿīd had lost consciousness, and the Prophet had a cloth in his hand. He placed it on Saʿīd’s forehead, and Saʿīd came to. He saw the Prophet ﷺ at his head and said, “Are you the one who disparages our gods and deems our judgment foolish? If God raises Saʿīd up, he will surely drive you out of Mecca.” Saʿīd’s leg was resting in Khālid’s lap, and his head in ʿAmr’s. They cast aside his head and leg and said, “May God not raise you from your swoon!” Then they turned to the Prophet ﷺ and said, “We believe in you and affirm you.”

It is said that this verse was revealed concerning the two of them: “You will find no people who believe in God and the Last Day showing affection to those who oppose God and His Messenger” (58:22), through the end of the verse. Saʿīd ordered that they be imprisoned, and they were imprisoned; his illness grew worse. He said, “Take me out to my estate at al-Ṭāʾif.” They took him out, and he died in a place called al-Ḍarībah.

Their brother Abān ibn Saʿīd had not yet become Muslim. He composed these verses: (Ṭawīl)

Would that a dead man at al-Ḍarībah could witnessThe lies ʿAmr and Khālid tell about religion!

To help our enemies with their stratagems.

ʿAmr answered him, saying: (Ṭawīl)

“My brother—what a brother! I do not insult his honor,Nor does he fall short in speaking ill.

“When his affairs become difficult, he says:‘Would that a dead man at al-Ḍarībah were raised!’

“Leave a dead man who has gone his way,And turn to the living, who is poorer.”

When the Prophet ﷺ came within sight of al-Ṭāʾif, there was a built-up grave. Abū Bakr ﵁ was on his right, and Khālid ibn Saʿīd ﵀ was on his left. Abū Bakr said, “May my father and mother be your ransom! This is the grave of my father, Abū Uḥayḥah Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ, the idolater—may God not have mercy on him!” Khālid said, “Rather, it is Abū Quḥāfah’s grave—may God not have mercy on him! By God, he would neither provide hospitality to a guest nor prevent an injury! I would not like Abū Quḥāfah to be my father even if Abū Uḥayḥah were in the highest of ʿIlliyyūn.” The Messenger of God ﷺ laughed and said, “Abū Bakr, do not revile the dead, lest you anger the living.”

The Wars of Banū ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb ibn Luʾayy in Islam

Ibrāhīm ibn al-Mundhir ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Ḥizāmī said: ʿUmar ibn Abī Bakr al-Muʾmilī told me, from Saʿīd ibn ʿAbd al-Karīm, from ʿAbd al-Ḥamīd ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Zayd ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, from his father, who said:

Among the accounts of the war that took place between ʿAdī …

ibn Kaʿb in Islam was that Abū al-Jahm ibn Ḥudhayfah ibn Ghānim was one of the leading Qurashīs in the pre-Islamic age. Before his conversion to Islam, he rivaled ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb in his hostility toward the Messenger of God ﷺ and his opposition to him. God honored ʿUmar with Islam, answered His Prophet’s prayer concerning him, and strengthened His religion through him. Abū al-Jahm delayed accepting Islam until the Conquest. He then moved to Medina and remained close to the Prophet ﷺ.

We have been told that the Messenger of God ﷺ was brought two black khamīṣahs. He wore one and sent the other to Abū al-Jahm. The Messenger of God’s khamīṣah had a woven design, and when he stood for prayer he would look at the design. He disliked it for that reason, so after wearing it a few times he sent it to Abū al-Jahm, and asked for Abū al-Jahm’s khamīṣah. He wore that after Abū al-Jahm had worn it a few times.

During ʿUmar’s caliphate, Abū al-Jahm used to sit at a place called al-Balāṭ in Medina, among older men of his standing from Mecca, talking with them. A young man from Quraysh would pass by, and they would taunt him about the failings of his parents in the pre-Islamic age. When this reached ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, he forbade them from gathering at that place.

When ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān was killed, a number of Qurashīs took him out at night to pray over him and bury him. Jablah ibn ʿAmr al-Sāʿidī came and prevented them from praying over him. Abū al-Jahm, who was among the group, said, “By God, if you do not pray over him, the Messenger of God ﷺ prayed over him.”

Abū al-Jahm was married to Khawlah bt. al-Qaʿqāʿ ibn Maʿbad ibn Zurārah ibn ʿAdas. She bore him Muḥammad ibn Abī al-Jahm. He also had …

He had Ḥumayd ibn Abī al-Jahm, whose mother was Ḥabībah bt. al-Junayd ibn Jumānah ibn Qays ibn Zuhayr ibn Judhaymah al-ʿAbsī. He had Ṣakhr and Ṣukhayr by a concubine. He had ʿAbd al-Raḥmān by a concubine, and the younger ʿAbd Allāh and Sulaymān by a concubine called Zujājah, who was a captive from Ghassān.

The sons of Abū al-Jahm were fierce and hardy, men of mischief and roughness. No one would challenge them without their hurting him, and the authorities had to contend with trouble and hardship because of them.

ʿAmr ibn al-Zubayr would stretch a rope across the road. It was held by Ḥabshānah, and whenever someone passed, they would snag him with it so that he fell face-first. When al-Ḥasan ibn ʿAlī ﵇ passed by, Ḥabshānah said to him, “Son of the Messenger of God, we have been ordered to do this.” He replied ﵁ “A fool, if he found someone to make a fool of.” He turned aside from them onto another road.

Abū al-Jahm passed by them while blind. They snagged him and he fell. When he reached his house, he gathered his sons, then took out his penis, spat on it, and said, “If a free man had come out of this, he would not have done to me what was done.” His sons went to ʿAmr’s house and set fire to his door, hoping he would come out to them, but he did not.

Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam, who was governor of Medina during Muʿāwiyah’s caliphate, came out to them while on pilgrimage. One day, as he was riding in his procession on the road, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muṭīʿ ibn al-Aswad approached and spoke to him. Marwān replied, Ibn Muṭīʿ answered him, and Marwān spoke harshly to him. Muṣʿab ibn …

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf, who was then in charge of Marwān’s police force, struck Ibn Muṭīʿ’s camel in the face with his whip and said, “Move aside.” He moved aside. Ṣukhayr ibn Abī al-Jahm made his way through the procession until he came close to Muṣʿab. He struck his nose with his whip, then rode away on a young Mahri camel. Muṣʿab held his nose and then approached Marwān, told him what had happened, and called on him to take action against Ṣukhayr. Marwān stopped, grew very angry, and said, “Bring him to me! By God, I will cut off his hand!” Ibn Muṭīʿ said, “You nearly multiplied the number of Quraysh’s amputees.” Some men pursued Ṣukhayr, but they could not catch him or seize him before he escaped.
