When the group arrived in Mecca and completed their pilgrimage, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muṭīʿ sent one of his slave-girls, called Khayrah, to ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān, who was then a youth at Ṭarfah. She was alluring, intelligent, and eloquent; he had bought her for four thousand dirhams. He said to her, “Present yourself to the chief of police. If he speaks to you, speak to him and jest with him.” The slave-girl set out and did as he had ordered.

When she passed by Muṣʿab ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, he asked whose she was and what her purpose was. She answered him and talked back with him. He was taken with her and sent to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muṭīʿ to negotiate her price. ʿAbd Allāh sent her to him; Muṣʿab took her and sent him her price. ʿAbd Allāh refused to accept it, saying, “A man like me does not sell someone like you.”

When the departure came, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muṭīʿ and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣafwān ibn Umayyah al-Jumaḥī rode to Muṣʿab ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān and asked him to waive the claim for the blow that he was bringing against Ṣukhayr ibn Abī al-Jahm.

He waived it for them. When they reached Medina, Ṣukhayr ibn Abī al-Jahm sent some verses in rajaz meter about the matter. They reached Muṣʿab, who regretted what he had done, but found no choice except to go through with it. Those are Ṣukhayr ibn Abī al-Jahm’s words: (Rajaz)

We struck Muṣʿab with the stickOn the day we broke his nose, to make him angry.

Perhaps war will break out between us,For a slave has climbed up to a watchtower.

There was a man of mixed blood among the group, from a foreign land;I struck him with the whip until he wailed.

I care nothing for what the bigot says,When ʿAdī walks around me in anger.

Khayrah rode with him,And played and sported with him.

Then we refused a reprover when he reproved us,So that he could find no recourse but arms.

Then Khawlah bt. al-Qaʿqāʿ grew old, became ill, and her joints ached; her legs grew heavy. After her pain had lasted a long time, Abū al-Jahm came to visit her one day. She said to him:

“I am bewitched, and Zujājah is the one who bewitched me. I have been told that my cure lies in the marrow of her legs, if I rub myself with it. Once I have done that, nothing will stand between me and my recovery.” Abū al-Jahm, who still retained some of the blindness of the pre-Islamic age, said, “Yes, that will be yours, and little will be asked of you.” Then …

He left her, and the news reached his concubine and her two sons, ʿAbd Allāh and Sulaymān. They went to their father and told him what had reached them. They found that he was resolved to do it, and he told them that he would. They strongly reproached him, reminding him of God Most High, Islam, and what was right. He refused and said, “Your mother is not to me as Khawlah is, nor are you to me as her children are.”

When the two could not persuade him, they went to Khawlah and spoke with her. They said, “You have not been bewitched. What you suffer is one of the ailments that afflict people. This is the talk of women and of those with no judgment or reason. Fear God, desist, and do not make our father do something for which he should punish us.” She said to them, “Your mother bewitched me. I had suspected it, and what you have now told me has confirmed my suspicion.”

They left her, went to their brothers, told them what their father and Khawlah had said, and asked them to help them put a stop to the evil course they had taken. Muḥammad, Khawlah’s son, said, “Whatever our father and mother command us to do, whether good or bad, we will obey them in it.” His other brothers—Ṣakhr, Ṣukhayr, and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān—agreed with him, for they held the same view.

Ḥumayd was in Iraq, absent. The two spoke harshly to them and said, “If we excused an old man, or an old, sick, foolish woman, because her judgment was the judgment of women, what excuse could we offer for you? By God, this will never happen—though we be killed. And by God, we will not be killed until some of you are killed, so you will have no choice but to look to your own survival.”

Conflict broke out among the sons of Abū al-Jahm. They became preoccupied with one another and left other people alone; their fighting was among themselves. The two sons of Abū al-Jahm, ʿAbd Allāh and Sulaymān, went to ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, recounted the matter to him, and asked him to protect and support them. He said, “Glory be to God! This is not something that can happen. Islam has forbidden this and the like.” They kept repeating the account to him, telling him what they had said and what had been said to them.

He would not believe that this could happen. They left him and met al-Miswar ibn Makhrama al-Zuhrī, who asked them about their situation. They told him what had happened and mentioned what they had said to ʿAbd Allāh and how he had answered them. He said to them, “Ibn ʿUmar has refrained from becoming involved in disputes within Muḥammad’s community ﷺ—so how could he become involved in a dispute among the Banū Abī al-Jahm? Go to someone more accessible to you, and to what you seek.” They set out and entered upon ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Zayd ibn al-Khaṭṭāb. They recounted their story to him, and when they reached that point, he was alarmed by what they had brought him. He said, “Wait. I shall consider this matter, examine it carefully, and learn what is true in it and what is false.”

He summoned his son ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, whose mother was al-Thaqafiyya. Because of his good looks and beauty, he was called al-Muṣawwir. He had once come before Muʿāwiya and stayed with him for a month. One day he went up to him and said, “Commander of the Faithful, grant me my request.” Muʿāwiya replied, “I grant you that you are the best-looking of men.” Then he granted his request, gave him a gift, and bestowed a generous reward on him.

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said to his son, “My son, go to your uncle Abū al-Jahm and ask about him, his condition, his companion, and her illness. Then go in to the daughter of al-Qaʿqāʿ, greet her, sit with her, and ask about her illness and what she is experiencing. Then record what each of them tells you, and come back to me.”

The young man went and did as his father had instructed. When he asked Abū al-Jahm about his wife, he said:

“She is ill; she cannot move a hand or a foot, or turn over except when she is turned. It has been said to her that she is bewitched, and that her cure is near me.”

Then he went in to Khawla, greeted her, and sat down. and asked her about her illness. She told him the same thing and said, “You have bewitched me. Abū al-Jahm promised me that he would slaughter her and extract the marrow from her shins, so that I could anoint myself with it.”

ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān went back, frightened and dismayed by what he had heard; he had not known of the matter before. He reported to his father what had been said and what he had been told. ʿAbd Allāh and Sulaymān were sitting with him. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said to them, “I can only think that the matter is true. By God, they will never get what they want from you or your mother, God willing.” He ordered them to bring their mother and whatever family and property they had, and then move to him. They did so, and he lodged them in the house of his freedman ʿUbayd ibn Ḥunayn, who was the freedman of their mother, Lubāba daughter of Abū Lubāba al-Anṣāriyya. She was among the captives of ʿAyn al-Tamr whom Khālid ibn al-Walīd had taken captive during the caliphate of Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq ﵁.

ʿUbayd ibn Ḥunayn was intelligent, learned in jurisprudence, and deeply knowledgeable. When ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Zayd governed Mecca, he appointed him judge over the people of Mecca.

ʿAbd Allāh and Sulaymān, the sons of Abū al-Jahm, went to ʿĀṣim ibn ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb. They recounted their situation and told him what ʿAbd al-Raḥmān had decided for them. He said, “I am with you, and nothing you dislike will reach you.” They then went to Zayd ibn ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, whose mother was Umm Kulthūm daughter of ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib—may God ennoble his countenance. They told her what had happened and asked for her support. He answered them, saying:

“No injustice shall be done to you, nor shall you suffer oppression.”

They also went to the elder sons of ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb: ʿUmar, Muḥammad, ʿUthmān, and Abū Bakr, whose mother was Asmāʾ daughter of ʿAṭṭārid ibn Ḥājib ibn Zarāra. They told them what had happened and asked for their support, and they promised it. They went to the two sons of Saʿīd ibn Zayd ibn ʿAmr ibn Nufayl, Zayd and ʿAbd Allāh, whose mother was Julaysa daughter of Suwayd ibn Ṣāmit al-Anṣāriyya; and to Muḥammad and Ibrāhīm, sons of Saʿīd, whose mother was Ḥazma daughter of Qays al-Fihrī, sister of al-Ḍaḥḥāk ibn Qays. They promised them support. They went to the Banū Surāqa and the Banū al-Muʾammal, and they all agreed to support and assist them.

When the elder Banū Abī al-Jahm saw what their two brothers had done, they went to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muṭīʿ ibn al-Aswad. They told him about their brothers and how they had sought help from the Banū al-Khaṭṭāb and others among their kin, and from those of their people who had taken their side. The Banū Abī al-Jahm had been the supporters and opponents of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muṭīʿ in every difficult matter that had befallen him and every undertaking he had pursued. He said to them, “As for what you have done concerning the woman under your protection and your father’s umm walad, I do not think I should learn about it or get involved in it with you. As for anything else, by God, even if my brother, my mother’s son and my father’s son, opposed you, I would support you against him.”

Then they went with their group, the Banū ʿUwayj ibn ʿAdī. When they learned that ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muṭīʿ had joined and supported them, they turned toward him. None of them held back—not even Sulaymān ibn Abī Ḥathma ibn Ḥudhayfa or Ḥakīm ibn Muʾriq ibn Ḥudhayfa. They were brothers through their mother, al-Shifāʾ daughter of ʿAbd Allāh [ibn Shams ibn Khalaf ibn Ṣadād ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Qurṭ ibn Razāḥ ibn ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb—] ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥafṣ ibn Khārija ibn Ḥudhāfa ibn Ghānim, [and—] ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Masʿūd ibn al-Aswad ibn Ḥāritha, Nāfiʿ ibn ʿAbd ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Naḍla ibn ʿAwf, Ibrāhīm ibn Nuʿaym, and Ṣāliḥ ibn al-Nuʿmān ibn ʿAdī, whom ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb appointed over Dastumaysān, the owner of the ruined palace. As far as we know, he appointed no one from the Banū ʿAdī except him.

Thus the Banū ʿAdī split into two factions, and conflict broke out and hostility took hold among them. Their middle-aged men would stay in their homes, while their young men went out at night to fight with sticks and throw stones. They parted only after suffering head wounds, other injuries, and broken arms and legs. This went on for a long time.

The conflict between them became severe. When they did not go out, they would shoot arrows and throw stones at one another from the rooftops at night. One of their fiercest clashes was on a night when they met at Ḥarrat Wāqim: Nāfiʿ ibn ʿAbd ʿAmr lost an eye, and Ṣāliḥ ibn al-Nuʿmān’s leg was broken.

The elder Banū Abī al-Jahm were troubled by the support their fellow clansmen, the Banū al-Khaṭṭāb [and—] their brothers, had given. They wanted to gain support from some of them, so they went to Wāqid ibn ʿAbd Allāh and Sālim ibn ʿAbd Allāh, who were then young men, and told them how their clan had taken sides against them. They complained about the Banū ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿUmar and said, “We trusted them because of our kinship through our maternal uncles, as well as the affection and kindness between us. Now they have become a single force against us, giving support to our opponents.” There had been some friction between the Banū ʿAbd Allāh and the Banū ʿUbayd Allāh, as can happen between cousins. They spoke to them and appealed to their sympathies, but could not get their assistance, out of respect for their father. They left them, satisfied.

Ḥumayd ibn Abī al-Jahm arrived from Iraq accompanied by al-Ḥurr ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿUmar, whose mother was an umm walad. The Banū ʿAbd Allāh had been fending him off, but he supported ʿAbd Allāh and Sulaymān. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī al-Jahm spoke of what had happened between them at Ḥarrat Wāqim:

(al-Ṭawīl)

By the aid and power of God, the Mighty,we turned the Banū al-ʿAjamāʾ and their aggression away from us,and the red men of ʿĀd, among the wicked misguiders.

With the aid of God, the Mighty, and His strength,and victory over the wrongdoer, the defender of sins.

I recall that Ibn Zayd, of many virtues,has a customary practice: he works to ward off injustice.

He set up for us a firm lance,and would not heed any slanderer’s words about us.

ʿĀṣim the good brought his aid to our cause,and no suppliant ever failed to find ʿĀṣim unshaken.

We went to Zayd; he welcomed us and did not shrink back,from the moment we called upon him, son of the best of the Fāṭimas.

The family of Saʿīd have requited us with their might,and the family of ʿUbayd Allāh are the adornment of the gatherings.

If you meet me one day, you will find me supportedby God’s aid and by the mature, generous leaders.

The Banū Surāqa are all around me, and all the Banū al-Muʾammal;among them, since ancient times, have been noble deeds.

We refused, and did not grant the enemy any claim of wrong;we guard our sanctuary with keen swords.

Does what befell your leaders not warn you—all at once, when we met you at Ḥarrat Wāqim?

You encountered men who did not fear your blowsand did not falter in the close-pressed field of battle.

Ṣakhr ibn Abī al-Jahm answered him:

(al-Ṭawīl)

Tell ʿUbayd from me that hewill take back what he said, a man full of regret.

You left the honour that once placed youamong its foremost people, and came to abiding shame and disgrace.

When will you call upon your mother’s kin, the Banū al-Khaṭṭāb,with words of certain truth, not the words of a pretender?

Ibn Zayd will not defend you,nor will ʿĀṣim, whose forbearance is firmly grounded.

Nor will the man who holds your protection in contemptdefend you; among us, your supporter is the son of the best of the Fāṭimas.

But as for the Saʿīdīs, the good among them,and the sons of the glorious family of Dārim—

We are closer to them, and their affection inclines toward us,bound by women’s noble kinship ties.

In difficult matters, we always turnto Wāqid, our man of merit, and Sālim.

I am a man who has never left the son of Ghānimunanswered when he spoke openly among those who took part in the raid.

Around me are peers from the noblest family,when the people of virtue are counted among the living.

The Banū Naḍla are the best; no clan is like them,nor the family of Nuʿaym, the nobles and the chiefs.

Do you forget what you suffered through your own misery,and your cowardice before us at Ḥarrat Wāqim?

Then they clashed one night at Aḥjār al-Zayt and parted after suffering head wounds, other injuries, and ugly scars. Ṣakhr ibn Abī al-Jahm spoke of it:

(al-Ramal)

Turn back the omens of war for the allies:did they rise under ill luck, or under good fortune?

They brought you ill fortune, while we seizedvictory from them, fortunate in every undertaking.

Though we charged against you with a bladeand towered over you with a great host in array,

it was without rancour or deceit;kinship with you will come to an end.

Have you seen a Qurashī like Ibn Hind? …May God make abundant the good fortune of the resourceful Ibn Hind!

Among them, on the day he grew up for battle,he was like a lion with its manes and its tawny coat—

[And-] as wonder filled me, when Ḥumayd,a man of bounty, came forward in attack and retreat—

Whoever supplies praise to Ḥumayd [and-]has provision brought to him without praise.

He brought them to us from the direction of the two Iraqs,between the Babylonian al-Ḥarr and ʿAbd.

And ʿUbayd longs for my death …Who will there be for you, son of Zujāja, after me?

If I die, remember the service I rendered in my place,and, son of Zujāja, you will know the loss of me.

ʿAbd Allāh answered him, saying: (ramal)

Ṣakhr of folly spoke in ignorance, and hisignorance will keep coming without intent.

Let the slanderous talk that came from him be left aside;I have a fitting recompense for it.

That is a war for you and against you—two courses, neither of them right-guided.

When the host gathers, there is noguide in it to direct and lead to a course.

Our birds are birds of good fortune; from themwe decree that what comes to you will not be good fortune.

You took no pride over us through Ibn Hind.Indeed, Ibn Hind met ruin.

When the assembly from among you withdrew, dispersed,from a band of pampered youths and striplings—

Ungrateful for favors was Ḥumayd, though he had beensteadfast in the quarter on the day it was steadfast.

The people held back from him when he fell—evil is the gratitude of the distressed man who falls.

You tasted punishment there,and we met you with force and anger.

Then one day ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muṭīʿ rode out to inspect some sheep of his. Word reached ʿAbd Allāh and Sulaymān, the sons of Abī al-Jahm, and they went out to lie in wait for him on his return. News reached their brothers, who went out to join them. The two parties summoned one another, and Ibn Muṭīʿ turned back. They met at al-Baqīʿ and fought. Ibn Muṭīʿ was struck with a staff; it hit the back of his saddle and broke it. Then…

Zayd ibn ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb came to intervene and restrain them from one another. He mingled with them, and a man among them struck him in the darkness, not knowing who he was, on the head, splitting it open. He fell, and the people called out, “Zayd! Zayd!” They scattered, and their hands were tied [in helplessness]. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muṭīʿ came forward. When he saw him lying there, he dismounted and bent over him, calling, “O Zayd! May my father and mother be your ransom!”—twice or thrice. Then Zayd answered him. Ibn Muṭīʿ bent down and carried him on his mule until he brought him home. Zayd was treated for that wound until he recovered, and it was said that he had healed. He would ask who had struck him, but they would not name the man. Al-Ḥizāmī said: “I heard that Khālid ibn Aslam, the client of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵁—wounded him with a spear-thrust, without knowing who he was. This account is more firmly established than the first.” ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿĀmir ibn Rabīʿa al-ʿAnazī, an ally of the family of al-Khaṭṭāb, said of this: (rajaz)

The Banū ʿAdī, on the night of al-Baqīʿ,scattered from a man lying fallen,

A man distinguished by noble lineage;the ill omen of the Banū Muṭīʿ overtook him.

ʿĀṣim ibn ʿUmar said to his brother Zayd, recalling what they had gone through: (ṭawīl)

A strange matter has passed—what happened between us—and what we are in now is stranger still.

The perpetrators of evil have transgressed after our closeness;we hoped, yet among us there is division and faction.

They are ill-omened, bringing evil into the open,and summon misguidance among those led astray.

Whenever we saw their breach, they would not make peace,and among them there was no refuge for one who would separate from them.

Their fierce tempers forbid them from it,and each of them is harsh by nature, difficult to deal with.

So, Zayd, be patient, seeking your reward, and face itfor the sake of recompense: in reward there is a mount for the one who faces it.

Do not conceal what has befallen you today, for among …your young men there is one who will strive for that and seek it.

Do not take blood-money from the people, for Isee that the wound remains while the compensations disappear.

It will be as though you were never afflicted or struck by hardshipif you obtain what you were seeking.

Muḥammad ibn Iyās ibn al-Bukayr, an ally of the Banū ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb, said:

(ramal)

My night has grown long, though the night is short;it has lasted until dawn has nearly ceased to shine.

Recall the days when reprehensible things befell us,when event followed event.

There folly increased in ignorance and spread;forbearance yielded in humiliation and did not return.

He who commands misguidance is obeyed,and he who commands what is right is driven away.

War has been kindled among ʿAdī by the ropes;the millstones of their war are turning today.

Ṣakhr and Ṣakhīr have burdened uswith grievous calamities of the evil, evil ʿAqaba.

Every day they cast us intoa quarry with rocks tumbling down behind it, and boulders.

Then the wound in Zayd ibn ʿUmar reopened. He remained ill from it, and he was afflicted with an abdominal disease, from which he died ﵀. Some learned men have reported that he and his mother, Umm Kulthūm daughter of ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵏—who was married to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar ibn Abī Ṭālib, fell ill together and became gravely ill. The illness came upon them, and men went between them to find out which would die before the other, so that the survivor might inherit from the one who died. They died in the same hour, and it was not known which of them died first, so they did not inherit from one another.

ʿAmr ibn Jarīr al-Bajalī reported that Zayd was struck on the face at the dawn prayer. His mother came out saying, “Alas for me! What have I suffered from the dawn prayer!” For her father, her husband, and her son had each been killed at the dawn prayer. Then she fell upon him, and they were both carried away dead. Al-Ḥasan ibn ʿAlī ﵉ both—and ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ﵃—attended their funeral. Ibn ʿUmar said to al-Ḥasan, “Go ahead and perform the prayer over your sister and your sister’s son.” Al-Ḥasan said to ʿAbd Allāh, “No, you go ahead and perform the prayer over your mother and your brother.” Ibn ʿUmar went forward and performed a single funeral prayer over them, saying four takbīrs. Muḥammad ibn Iyās ibn…

Al-Bukayr mourned Zayd and recalled their affair: (wāfir)

Would that my mother had never borne me,and that I had not been among the straying at al-Baqīʿ,

and that I had not seen the place where the good man Zayd fell,struck down there with another fallen man.

He was the man whose loss was great and grievousfor all the people of the quarter.

Noble in lineage, encompassedby the branches of glory and noble descent;

the patron of generosity—generosity truly hasno patron besides him when he is gone.

A calamity that was overwhelming and dreadfulstruck the people, the people of Banū ʿAdī;

and singled them out in particular for misfortune,because of the evil they do.

Through the ill omen of the Banū Ḥudhayfa, for among themthere is wretchedness together with the ill omen of the Banū Muṭīʿ.

How many a meeting place had pebbles stainedby the wounds of men, with fresh blood!

Then, when news of them kept reaching Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān, he was deeply troubled by what had come to pass. He sent for Abū al-Jahm ibn Ḥudhayfa, who came to him in Syria. He received him warmly, honored him, and reproached him concerning what had reached him about his sons and his people. He charged him to restrain them from what they had been doing, so that what was between them might be put right and affairs return to a good course. He sent him a gift of one hundred thousand dirhams. When…

When the money reached him, he considered it too little and said, “O God, change it!” Then he returned to Medina, bringing that affair to an end. The people made peace, and each refrained from the other.

When Muʿāwiya died and Yazīd succeeded him, Abū al-Jahm went with a delegation of Quraysh to visit him. When Yazīd was about to order his gift, he asked how much Muʿāwiya had given him. He was told, “One hundred thousand,” so he deducted ten thousand and sent him ninety thousand. When the money reached him, he considered it too little and said, “O God, change it!” When Yazīd died, Abū al-Jahm went to ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr, asking him to assign him a stipend. He ordered five thousand dirhams for him. When it reached him, Abū al-Jahm said, “O God, do not change it! For if You change it, You will bring us apes and swine.” Al-Ḥizāmī said: “I heard that Ibn al-Zubayr gave him ten thousand dirhams.”

Al-Ḥizāmī said: When ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr rebelled and gained control of Mecca, and al-Ḥusayn ibn ʿAlī ﵉ both—set out for Iraq, news reached Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiya that ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muṭīʿ intended to rise up in Medina. He feared this, and wrote to al-Walīd ibn ʿUtba ibn Abī Sufyān, who was then his governor in Medina, ordering him to seize Ibn Muṭīʿ and confine him in the prison before him, then write to him about it so that he could send back his instructions concerning the man. Al-Walīd seized him and put him in prison. He remained there for several days. Then ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb came and sat at the place of the biers by the mosque gate. Men of the Banū ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb gathered around him concerning Ibn Muṭīʿ. Then he sent word to al-Walīd ibn ʿUtba: “Come to us, so we may discuss some of our concerns with you.” Al-Walīd came and sat down. ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar spoke, praised God, extolled Him, and bore witness to His oneness. Then he turned to al-Walīd and said: “Seek God’s help and uphold the truth in establishing your religion and in pursuing what will set your worldly affairs right. Do not seek to establish and set those things right by wronging the innocent, humiliating the righteous, and frightening them. If you act uprightly, God will help you; but if you act unjustly, you will be left to yourselves. Refrain from harming our companion and release him, for we know of no offense on his part for which you should imprison him.”

“If you claim you imprisoned him on suspicion and accusation, we will not accept leaving our companion wronged and oppressed.” Al-Walīd said: “We seized and imprisoned him only on the order of the Commander of the Faithful. We shall consider the matter, and you shall consider it; we shall write, and you shall write. What you want will be the outcome.” Abū al-Jahm said: “We shall consider it and you shall consider it; we shall write and you shall write, while Ibn al-ʿAjamāʾ is imprisoned in the jail! By God, until none of us or you are left but the lowliest, that will not happen!” Al-Walīd stood and left. Some young men of the Banū ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb went out and broke into the prison. When Ibn Muṭīʿ heard their voices, he thought al-Walīd had sent someone to kill him. He leapt up, looking for something with which to defend himself and fight, but found nothing except a stone the size of his palm. He picked it up and went in. When his companions came to him, he recognized them, threw the stone down, and cried “Allāhu akbar!” They carried him out, and he made his way to Ibn al-Zubayr.

We have been told that Abū al-Jahm ibn Ḥudhayfa lived to see the Kaʿba being built by ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr, and worked on it along with the Qurashī men who were building it. Then he said:

“I have worked on the building of the Kaʿba twice: once in the pre-Islamic era with the strength of a youth, and once in Islam with the strength of an old, frail man.”

Udhayna ibn Maʿbad al-Laythī praised the Banū ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb and recalled their rescue of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muṭīʿ from prison: (basīṭ)

ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb showed its might in the stratagem,and those for whom ʿAdī was worthy as ally and supporter.

ʿAdī rescued its brother after deathhad closed its teeth and claws around him.

Authority resists nothing but the oppression of its chiefs,but God refuses to let them accept oppression.

Whoever among ʿAdī settles among them,

away from harm, or as a neighbor under their protection,

how many times you see among them, when they gather,

men of insight, righteous in good works;

leaders distinguished by glory and noble achievement,

who governed with forbearance, with regard for lineage and standing;

their generosity reaches all the living,

like the Nile, which flows through lands and regions;

through them their brother attains his ambition,

and through them grievances find their satisfaction.

Al-Ḥizāmī related from Ibn Shihāb that Abū al-Jahm ibn Ḥudhayfah said: One night, he came to find his son Muḥammad ibn Abī al-Jahm slain, after Musrif had killed him. This happened because, when Muslim ibn ʿUqbah al-Murrī had killed the people of al-Ḥarrah and gained control of Medina, he made the people pledge allegiance to Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiyah on the condition that they were Yazīd’s slaves, owned outright. Ibn Abī al-Jahm refused to pledge on the condition that he was Yazīd’s slave, so Muslim brought him forward and struck off his head. When the people saw that, they pledged on those terms. ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib was then brought before him, and he said to him: “Pledge that you are a slave, owned outright.” Al-Ḥuṣayn ibn Numayr al-Kindī, of the Sakāsik, rose up; four thousand men of Kindah were with him. He said: “By God, the son of our sister will never pledge allegiance on those terms!” Abū Muslim feared that his authority would slip from him, so he accepted ʿAlī’s pledge as the son of ʿUmar, Commander of the Faithful, and Muslim sent him back to his home on his mule and asked him to bring any needs he had to him. The rest of the people pledged that they were slaves. “By God, I have never been so wronged as I was tonight.” Present with him were some Umayyads, among them his son-in-law— Umayyah ibn ʿAmr ibn Saʿīd, married to his daughter. Present that day was Saʿdā daughter of Abū al-Jahm. Abū al-Jahm said: “You Umayyads think that my blood is owed to Banū Murrah. No, by God, my blood is not owed there. I can find no better comparison for you and me than the words of the poet:” (al-ṭawīl)

“We are thoroughbreds whose sire is one,

noble steeds, with no inferior beast among them.

“You have no merit over us that we count,

except that you say to us, ‘We are more numerous.’

“You are not richer in numbers than we,

for we are young, and the young may grow and flourish.”

He said: When the Umayyads set out on their final departure for Syria, Ḥumayd ibn Abī al-Jahm gathered men from Quraysh and others, brought them into the house of his father, Abū al-Jahm ibn Ḥudhayfah, and said: “You will take your vengeance on the Umayyads”—meaning vengeance for the blood of those among them whom Muslim ibn ʿUqbah had killed on the day of al-Ḥarrah. He gathered them until they numbered nearly a hundred men. Among them were ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵈; ʿAbd Allāh ibn Rabīʿah ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib; Salamah ibn ʿUmar ibn Abī Salamah; Muḥammad ibn Miʿqal ibn Sinān al-Ashjaʿī; ʿUmar ibn Shuwaifiʿ ibn ʿUthmān ibn Ḥakīm al-Sulamī, an ally of Banū ʿAbd Shams; Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Saʿd; and many others. He brought them into the house at evening, clad in armor. Abū al-Jahm came back from the evening prayer—he was then over a hundred years old—and said: “Tomorrow morning, honor Quraysh and fatten them up. Do not kill for your brother except a fat man.” Then he went inside and waited for a while, but heard no uproar. He came out again and called: “Ḥumayd! Ḥumayd!”

“May your mother’s clitoris be bitten! Why do I hear no uproar?” He said: “Father, do not be hasty! By God, I am searching for them and trying to find them.” Then he went back. After a while, Abū al-Jahm could not stay inside, so he came out and called: “Ḥumayd! May your mother’s clitoris be bitten!” (al-wāfir)

“[And if] I were the one slain, and he were alive …I would fight, neither shrinking nor growing weary.”

That remained their way: they walked through the lanes, seeking an opportunity to catch the Umayyads off guard, but could not find one. Then the Umayyads sent Ḥassān ibn Kaʿb al-Mukhannath, the client of Abū al-Jahm, and said: “Find out what is in Abū al-Jahm’s house for us.” He went and saw the armed company in the courtyard of the house, then returned to the men wailing and said: “Disaster is in Abū al-Jahm’s house! Take the Baṭḥān route.” They took that road. Ḥumayd raided the house of Yaʿqūb ibn Ṭalḥah at al-Balāṭ, where there were five Syrians, and the house of Ibn ʿĀmir at Rūmah; he plundered all of it. Then, when Ibn al-Zubayr heard of this, he wrote to Ḥumayd: “I have heard that no one in Medina was left alive except you. Go out with whoever among the people follows you, and pursue their tracks, for they are dropping away like ripe fruit. Seek them between yourself and Wādī al-Qurā, and seize as many of them and as much of their property as you can.” While he was making preparations, another letter from him arrived, saying: “Hold back from them today until I write to you, for I have been informed that ʿAmr and ʿUmar, the two sons of ʿUthmān, have turned their allegiance toward Ibn al-Zubayr.” This made him turn back from pursuing the Umayyads.

Ibn Shihāb said: Muḥammad ibn Abī al-Jahm and Abū Yasār ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Shaybah ibn Rabīʿah fought. Muḥammad ibn Abī al-Jahm threw him down, then trampled his belly and made him defecate. Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam, who was then governor of Medina, imprisoned him and said: “You made our master and one of our men defecate. By God, you will not escape me until I make you defecate.” He had men trample his belly. Muḥammad shouted:

“Marwān! My anus is stopped up, and I am not one of your arses.”

Umm Abān said: “Do not trample his belly! By God, he has not defecated.” So Marwān released him.

He said: Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam sought Abū al-Jahm’s daughter Saʿdā in marriage for his brother Yaḥyā ibn al-Ḥakam. Among those who intervened in the matter were Malikah daughter of Khārijah ibn Sinān ibn Abī Ḥārithah, and Sāriyah daughter of ʿAwf, Saʿdā’s sister and the mother of Yaḥyā. Abū al-Jahm refused. ʿAmr ibn Saʿīd was governor of Medina at the time. He sent Ibn Qaṭan, the client of Abū al-Jahm, and instructed him to learn Abū al-Jahm’s opinion about his daughter’s marrying Umayyah ibn ʿAmr. He feared Abū al-Jahm would reject him as he had rejected Marwān. Ibn Qaṭan went and sounded out Abū al-Jahm’s opinion. Abū al-Jahm said: “I will consider the matter.” Abū al-Jahm called his son— Ḥumayd and said to him: “ Do you prefer Ibn Abī Uḥayḥah, or your maternal cousin Yaḥyā ibn al-Ḥakam?” He replied: “You know best and are wiser.” Messengers then went back and forth between them until Abū al-Jahm gave them his promise. He sent for ʿAbd Allāh and ʿĀṣim, the two sons of ʿUmar, and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muṭīʿ, along with a group of men from Banū ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb. ʿAmr ibn Saʿīd came with men from the family of Saʿīd and the Umayyads. He sat with Abū al-Jahm on the couch and said: “Are you waiting for anyone?” Abū al-Jahm replied: “We are waiting for Muḥammad ibn Abī al-Jahm. Go, boy, and summon Muḥammad for us.” The boy went to him. Muḥammad said: “No, by God! I will not attend it or its marriage.” ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muṭīʿ was at Abū al-Jahm’s feet, and Ṣakhr ibn Abī al-Jahm was at his head. Abū al-Jahm sent for Muḥammad, saying: “I charge you to come.” Muḥammad came walking until he stood among the people and said: “Marry off your daughter, sir. By God, I will have no part in this and will not witness her marriage.” This was because of something that had happened between him and ʿAmr ibn Saʿīd. ʿAmr then spoke, recalling what had taken place between Abū al-Jahm and the family of Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ, and extolling the standing and nobility of Abū al-Jahm’s household. Abū al-Jahm then spoke about them, saying: “You were the leading house among your people, and your likeness among them was like smoke inside its casing.” Ibn Muṭīʿ took him by the leg and said: “That is enough, may God have mercy on you!” Abū al-Jahm said: “Leave me, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muṭīʿ! By God, I am not one of those who begrudge their clan anything, nor one who aspires to them.” Ibn Muṭīʿ kept at him until he restrained him from some of what he was saying. ʿAmr ibn Saʿīd kept looking at Ṣakhr ibn Abī al-Jahm and saying: “Ṣakhr, look at this and what he is doing!” Then he married her.

Ibn Shihāb said: Abū al-Jahm ibn Ḥudhayfah came to Muʿāwiyah. There had been a dispute between him and Thaqīf Muʿāwiyah said to him: “Abū al-Jahm, what is the matter between you and Thaqīf? They are complaining about you to me.” He replied: “How strange of you! By God, I will not make peace with them until they say, ‘Quraysh and Thaqīf are two wives of Wajj,’ and among us, only a fool would love them, just as among them only a fool would love us. By that, we count you among our fools.”

On another visit, when he came as a delegate, Muʿāwiyah said to him: “Abū al-Jahm, have I not settled your need?” He replied: “Yes, except for one thing I mentioned that I must have.” He said: “Then tell me.” Abū al-Jahm said:

“Banū Bakr are increasing their numbers in our land. So send for Banū Sāmah ibn Luʾayy, allot them land beyond the trench, make them the neighbors of Banū Bakr, and grant them the villages of Khaybar, Fadak, and Wādī al-Qurā as their stipend.” Muʿāwiyah said: “Yes. And what else did you have in mind?” He said: “Thaqīf are increasing their numbers against us at Wajj, so increase the number of the Romans and Persians; increase the number of the Romans and Persians until you consume them through those people.” Muʿāwiyah said: “Welcome! By God, I have been eager to accommodate you in what you asked. As for Banū Bakr, I have filled your territory with fighting men and battalions, to the point that any one of you among you, if angered, can send for one of them, have him led to you, and do with him as you wish. So go back and see. If you seek an increase, I will increase it for you; if you are content, may God make you content. As for Thaqīf, you have seen what I have done to them: I drove them from their settled land and joined them to the highlands of the Sarāt. They asked me to allot them land in Iraq, but I refused them that and said: ‘By God, only Syria—the land of those who obey readily—so that I might relieve you and myself of them.’ I did so until I made all their property Quraysh’s and filled the land with horses and mules. Go back and see. If you find it satisfactory, may God make you content; otherwise, write to me and I will give you more.”

Al-Ḥizāmī said: Ibn Shihāb related: Ismāʿīl ibn [Khālid ibn—] ʿUqbah ibn Abī Muʿayṭ met ʿĪsā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Shatīm and struck his head with a club, inflicting a skull wound. Then Ismāʿīl passed by Sālim, the client of Ibn Muṭīʿ. Sālim snatched from him the club with which he had struck ʿĪsā ibn ʿAbd Allāh, and struck him with it. Then the Banū ʿUqbah ibn Abī Muʿayṭ went to the house of Banū Masʿūd ibn al-ʿAjmāʾ in the market, where Sālim Abū al-Ghayth was. They informed Banū ʿAdī that they had besieged Sālim. The two sides met in the market and fought; the fighting grew fierce. Then they were separated, and some time passed. Later, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muṭīʿ went out to the market. Ismāʿīl ibn Khālid confronted him with a drawn sword and struck his head a blow that reached the bone. Then the Banū— The Umayyads brought Ismāʿīl to Ibn Muṭīʿ and said: “Here he is. We will satisfy you and place him in your power.” Ibn Muṭīʿ said: “I will do nothing until I consult Abū al-Jahm.” He sent to Abū al-Jahm: “What do you think I should do with him? They have put him in my power, and I have my due.” Abū al-Jahm sent back to him: “If they have given you his hand to cut off, accept their offer and take custody of him until you decide what to do. I think that, if they do that, you should give him a robe and a shirt, pardon him, and send him away.” They agreed to this. Ibn Muṭīʿ released him that very evening and gave him a robe. Years passed. Then [Ibn—] Sulaymān ibn Muṭīʿ came from Egypt and entered the bathhouse of Ibn ʿUqbah. There he found al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Ḥakam. They quarreled and traded insults at length. Al-Ḥārith said to him: “I see you insulting me, though we struck your uncle the blow that made him like a cow’s backside.” The other replied: “By my life, after that I cannot trade insults with you.” When [Ibn—] Sulaymān left the bathhouse, he went to Ḥumayd ibn Abī al-Jahm and said: “Did you see what al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān did to me?” He told him what had passed between them in the bathhouse and what al-Ḥārith had said. They went out and entered the house of Muḥammad ibn Abī al-Jahm, and Sulaymān told him what had happened. Muḥammad said to him: “May God banish you and your uncle! By God, I thought they would exact that from you. Ḥumayd, send for my sword, the ‘Standing-Seated’ one, and give it to this man so he can strike Khālid ibn—”

ʿUqba today—and it was a Friday—in his chest. When he passed the house of Abū al-Jahm, Ibn Sulaymān ibn Muṭīʿ came out against him and struck him with the sword, a blow like that of Ismāʿīl. Muḥammad ibn Abī al-Jahm said of this: (al-mutaqārib)

Two swords: a sword for a maʾmūmah wound,

and a sword named al-Qāʾim al-Qāʿid.

Take it upon your head—a maʾmūmah wound—

and beware, beware, Khālid!

Ibn Sulaymān ibn Muṭīʿ said: (al-basīṭ)

I am the young man who left this markon your old man’s head, until I weakened the sinews.

I am the one who sent Ismāʿīl back crippled:

he hears the thunder, and nearly dies or is overcome.

The fighting grew fierce that day between the Banū Umayya and ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb. Saʿīd and al-Walīd, the sons of ʿUthmān, supported the Banū ʿUqba of the house of ʿUthmān, as did all the Banū Abī ʿAmr and the Banū al-Ḥaḍramī. ʿAbd Allāh, Sulaymān, Ṣakhr, and Ṣukhayr of the Banū Abī al-Jahm, however, sided against the Banū Muṭīʿ. On the day of the house, when they stormed toward him, they numbered four or five thousand.

When it was afternoon, the Mother of the Believers sent word to them: “By God, you will leave it, or I shall come out in broad daylight.” Marwān went out with the people and stood between them. ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Umayya said of this: (al-wāfir)

[And-] Abān does not support the client,nor does ʿAmr, the two stragglers of a runaway camel.

She bore Yazīd so that he might be of help to her,but she bore nothing but grievous pain.

Marwān confers with them against us,and that ʿAmr is the man who lies asleep.

The clans of the house of Shams have forsaken us,while Saʿīd and al-Walīd stood by us.

The lineage of Sharḥabīl ibn Ḥasana among Quraysh

Al-Ḥizāmī, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ibrāhīm ibn Qudāma al-Jumaḥī, said: My father told me, from his father, that Sharḥabīl ibn Ḥasana was attributed to Sufyān [ibn-] Muʿammar ibn Ḥabīb. Then an inheritance in Egypt came to his descendants, and al-Ḥārith ibn— Ḥāṭib ibn Muʿammar said to them: “What you see has come to pass. If your claim that you belong to us is as you assert, then this property is a matter between us; otherwise, you are disavowed from our lineage. If you wish, we will share it with you.” They chose the property, severed their connection, and abandoned that lineage. They remained thus until the middle of the period. He said: A group of them met Qudāma ibn Ibrāhīm ibn Muḥammad ibn Ḥāṭib, reminded him of the lineage they had once claimed, and asked him to restore it. He said: “Welcome! I well remember what you have mentioned. But I have a partner in this matter, and I will not decide without him”—meaning his brother ʿUthmān ibn Ibrāhīm, who was then in Kūfa, where he lived. Qudāma said: “I will write to him and tell him of your affair.” He wrote, and the group departed. The news spread among their maternal uncles’ families, who said: “Was what you did not enough? Every day we are at a distance from you, and subject to estrangement.” So they stopped seeking this, and a letter from ʿUthmān ibn Ibrāhīm came back to his brother Qudāma:

“I have read your letter and understood its contents. There is no way to reclaim anything from which your uncle al-Ḥārith ibn Ḥāṭib withdrew.”

That was the end of their affair. I have also heard, from another account, that the family of al-Muʿallā ibn Lawdhān, the Anṣārī, had claimed them and disputed over them. I do not know; perhaps this was in the time of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵁.

The story of the idols at Mecca

He said: ʿAmr ibn Rabīʿa, who was of Khuzāʿa, was a soothsayer with a familiar spirit among the jinn. ʿAmr was called Abū Thumāma. His familiar came to him and said, “Answer, Abū Thumāma!” He replied, “At your service, from Tihāma.” It said to him, “Set out without weariness.” He said to it, “A neighbor, not a resident.” It said: “Go to the plain of Jidda, where idols are ready; bring them to Tihāma, and do not fear. Then summon the Arabs to worship them, and they will answer.”

ʿAmr went to the coast of Jidda and found there Wadd, Suwāʿ, Yaghūth, Yaʿūq, and Nasr—the idols worshipped in the time of Idrīs and Noah ﵉. Then the Flood cast them there, and the sand drifted over them and buried them. ʿAmr dug them up and carried them to Tihāma. When the pilgrimage season came, he summoned the Arabs to worship them, and they responded. ʿAwf ibn Kināna ibn ʿAwf ibn ʿUdhrā ibn Zayd al-Lāt ibn Rufayda ibn Kalb took Wadd and set it up at Dūmat al-Jandal; it belonged to Quḍāʿa. Al-Ḥārith ibn Tamīm ibn Saʿd ibn Hudhayl ibn Mudrika took Suwāʿ, which was at Ruhāṭ, where Muḍar worshipped it. Anʿam ibn ʿAmr al-Murādī took Yaghūth, which was at a hill in Yemen called Madhḥij, where it was worshipped— Madhḥij and those allied with them. Mālik ibn Marthad ibn Jusham ibn Ḥāshid ibn Jusham ibn Khayrān ibn Nawf ibn Hamdān took Yaʿūq, which was at a village called Khaywān, where Hamdān and their allies worshipped it. Maʿdīkarib, one of the Ḥimyar and one of Dhū Raʿīn, took Nasr, which was at a place in the land of Sabaʾ called Balḫaʿ, where Ḥimyar and their allies worshipped it.

It is related of the Messenger of God ﷺ that he said: “Hell was raised before me, and I saw ʿAmr ibn Luḥayy—Luḥayy was Rabīʿa—a short, red, blue-eyed man, dragging his intestines through the Fire. I said, ‘Who is this?’ It was said, ‘ʿAmr ibn Luḥayy: the first to declare the baḥīra and the waṣīla, to set the sāʾiba free, to protect the ḥāmī, to change the religion of Ismāʿīl ﵇ and to summon the Arabs to worship idols and images.’”

The baḥīra was a she-camel that had borne five litters. When it bore the fifth, if the calf was not a male, they slit her ear; that was the baḥīra.

No one shears her wool, and God’s name is not invoked over her. As for the sāʾiba, it was whatever of their property they set free for their gods. The waṣīla was a ewe that had borne seven litters. When she bore the seventh, they appointed it: if it was male, they slaughtered it; if female, they left her among the sheep. If it was a male and a female, they said, “She has joined her brother,” and left both forbidden, their use reserved for men and not women. As for the ḥāmī, it was the stallion camel when he became a grandfather; they said, “This one has protected his back,” and left him unridden and unburdened. Neither the baḥīra, the sāʾiba, the waṣīla, nor the ḥāmī could be kept from water or pasture, even if these belonged to people other than their owners. Their milk was for men and not women, but if one of them died, men and women alike could eat its meat.

Ibn al-Kalbī said: While people were going around the Kaʿba, they saw a creature whose head appeared to loom over them. The people fled in terror, but it called out to them:

“Do not be alarmed!”

They came toward it, and it said: (al-rajaz)

O God, Lord of the House of the Shoulders,

You bestowed the tall young men,

a herd at which the milker is bewildered,

and a flock like locusts on the move. goods for a time, all of them passing away.

They looked and saw that it was a woman. They asked her, “Are you human or jinn?” She said, “I am a human woman from Jurhum”: (al-rajaz)

Our people perished in the time of Yaqdumthrough crushing calamities and a death that cuts like a blade,

until we left behind a smooth, barren plain, parchedfrom wrongdoing and the commission of sins.

Then she said: “Whoever slaughters a camel for me every day, prepares provisions and a mount for me, and takes me to a prosperous land—I will give him much wealth.” Two men from Juhayna ibn Zayd volunteered. They traveled with her for many nights and days until she reached a mountain of Juhayna. She came upon a village of ants and tiny creatures and said:

“You two! Here my people perished. Dig in this place.”

They dug and uncovered a great store of gold and silver, and loaded both their camels. She said to them, “Take care not to look back, lest what you carry be snatched away.” The tiny creatures came until they covered her. The two men went a short distance and looked back; what they had carried was snatched away. They called to her, “Is there water?” She said, “Yes, in the place of these hills.” As the tiny creatures covered her, she said: (al-rajaz)

Alas for me, alas, because of my fate!

I see the tiny creatures seeking my ruin.

They have been unleashed, tearing at my mount’s load.

When I saw that there was no escape for me,

I sought refuge where I could secure my stronghold.

/ The tiny creatures entered her nostrils and ears, and she fell on her side and died. The two men of Juhayna found water where she had said. The water is called Masīḥa and lies in the vicinity of Farsh Malal, beside Mishʿal. It belongs to Juhayna today.

The chiefships of Quraysh

In the days of ʿAbd Manāf, the chiefship belonged to ʿAbd Manāf ibn Quṣayy. He managed the affairs of Quraysh and was the one to whom they looked. After him, it passed to his son Hāshim, who held it through his good stewardship. No one in Quraysh was his equal or matched him. Then the chiefship passed to ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. There were chiefs throughout Quraysh, but they acknowledged ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib’s merit, precedence, and nobility. When ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib died, the chiefship passed to Ḥarb ibn Umayya ibn ʿAbd Shams. When Ḥarb died, the chiefships and the honor were dispersed among the Banū ʿAbd Manāf and other Qurashī clans. Among the Banū Hāshim, the leaders were al-Zubayr, Abū Ṭālib, al-ʿAbbās, and Ḥamza, the sons of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib; among the Banū al-Muṭṭalib, it was ʿAbd Yazīd ibn Hāshim ibn— Al-Muṭṭalib—he was pure, with no impurity in him. Among the Umayyads, Abū Uḥayḥa Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayya; among the Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf, al-Muṭʿim ibn ʿAdī ibn Nawfal; among the Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā, Khuwaylid ibn Asad and ʿUthmān ibn al-Ḥuwayrith ibn Asad; among the ʿAbd al-Dār, ʿIkrima ibn Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn ʿAbd al-Dār; among the Zuhra, Makhrama ibn Nawfal ibn Uhayb ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhra; among the Taym ibn Murra, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān ibn ʿAmr; among the Makhzūm, Hishām ibn al-Mughīra, who was a man of distinction and great standing among Quraysh, so that they made his death their date; among the ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb, ʿAmr ibn Nufayl ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā; among the Sahm, al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil; among the Jumāḥ, Umayya ibn Khalaf; among the ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy, ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd Shams, Abū Yazīd Suhayl al-Aʿlam; among the Muḥārib ibn Fihr, Ḍirār ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ibn Mirdās; and among the Ḥārith ibn Fihr, ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Jarrāḥ, father of Abū ʿUbayda ibn al-Jarrāḥ.

. The story of al-Zubayr and the Bedouin

He said: A Bedouin had a claim against al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām. He came seeking al-Zubayr, then attacked and insulted him. Ṣafiyya, who was sitting in the courtyard of her house, said, “Do not say that. He will give you your due and pay you in full.” He said, “By God, if I meet him, I will hurt him.” The Bedouin met al-Zubayr, spoke abusively to him, and wronged him. Al-Zubayr beat him so severely that he could not get up. His companions carried him until they brought him to Ṣafiyya, who was sitting at her door. She said: (rajaz)

How did you find Zubayr—

curds, or dates,

or bitter Ḥaḍramī?

What truthful visions occurred among Quraysh, including the vision of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib when he dug the well of Zamzam

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muʿādh al-Ṣanʿānī related from Maʿmar, from al-Zuhrī, who said: While ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib was asleep—his son al-Ḥārith had been born and had grown up—a figure came to him in a dream and said, “Dig up Zamzam, the hidden treasure of the greatest elder.” He awoke and said, “O God, make it clear to me.” Then, another time, he was visited in a dream and told, “Dig up Taknum between the dung and the blood, [at] the place where the crow searches, in the village of ants, facing the red standing stones.” ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib went and sat in the Sacred Mosque, waiting for the signs that had been named to him. A cow was slaughtered at al-Ḥazwara, but it broke free from its butcher at al-Ḥashāsha and, overcome by death, came into the Sacred Mosque. at the site of Zamzam. That cow was slaughtered where it was, and when its meat could be carried away, a crow came searching, swooped down, landed in the dung, and searched for the village of ants. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib began to dig. Quraysh came and said to him, “What is this you are doing? We did not think you foolish. Why are you digging in our mosque?”

It is also related from ʿAbd al-Aʿlā ibn Abī al-Masāwir, from ʿIkrima, from Ibn ʿAbbās, who said: ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib was visited in a dream and told, “Dig up Barrah.” He said, “What is Barrah?” He was told, “The precious one, kept from the people and given to you.” When morning came, he gathered his people and told them. They said, “Why didn’t you ask what it was?” When night came, he was visited in his dream and told, “Dig.” He said, “Where? Where is it?” He was told:

“Where the ant’s path and the crow’s landing are, between the dung and the blood.”

When morning came, he gathered his people and told them. They said, “This is the place where the idols of Khuzāʿa have been set up; they will not let you do it.” His sons were away, except al-Ḥārith. So he and al-Ḥārith began to dig. They dug until they brought out Qalʿī swords wrapped in a cloak. Then they dug until they brought out a gold gazelle with a pair of earrings in its ears. Then they dug until they brought out gold ornaments. Then they dug until they drew up water. His people came and said, “ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, give us our share of the spoils.” He said, “Bring me three arrows: a black one, a white one, and a red one.” He assigned the black one to his people, the white one to himself, and the red one to the House. He cast them, and the black arrow fell to the gazelle, so it went to his people. It is said that they told him, “Give us a share of what you found.” ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib said, “Rather, it belongs to the House…” for the House of God.” Then he dug until he reached the bottom. The water flowed abundantly and he pierced its mountain so that its water would not run dry. Then he built a basin over it, and he and al-Ḥārith drew water and filled the basin, from which the pilgrims drank. Some Qurashīs envied him and, whenever night fell, broke the basin. When morning came, ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib repaired it. When they repeatedly ruined it, ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib prayed to his Lord. He was visited in a dream and told, “Say: ‘O God, I do not permit it for bathing; it is permitted only for one who drinks, both sound and healed.’” “Then I will suffice you against them.” When Quraysh had gathered in the mosque, ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib stood up and called out as he had been commanded in the dream, then went away. After that, whenever anyone of Quraysh damaged his basin, a disease struck his body, until they left his basin and his provision of water alone.

The vision of Umm Ḥakīm—al-Bayḍāʾ—daughter of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib

He said: When Umm Ḥakīm, Arwā daughter of Kurayz ibn Rabīʿa ibn Ḥabīb ibn ʿAbd Shams, gave birth, she heard someone say in a dream: “A noble man of pure stock, a dignified and forbearing leader, a generous dispenser, and a bold poet is in the womb of Umm Ḥakīm.” She gave birth to ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān, who was the forbearing leader. “The dispenser” was al-Muṭarrif, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿUthmān, who was the most handsome man of his time. The bold poet was al-Walīd ibn ʿUqba ibn Abī Muʿayṭ. And he saw— Zuhra ibn Kilāb ibn Murra, who could scarcely have children. He married ʿAqīla daughter of ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Ghayra al-Thaqafī. She bore him three sons, all of whom died young. He swore that if she bore him a daughter, he would bury her alive. She bore him a daughter, and he ordered that she be buried. Quraysh said to him, “The Arabs did this only for fear of poverty, and you have great wealth.” He told them why he had made this vow about her and ordered that she be buried, but her mother concealed her. Zuhra was visited in a dream and told: “A generous young man and horseman, a devoted leader and valiant champion, a giver of food in a time of denial, is in the womb of the daughter who has been buried alive.” He awoke, spared her, and named her al-Sawdāʾ. She married ʿAmr ibn Kaʿb ibn Saʿd ibn Taym ibn Murra and bore him children.

He said: When ʿUmayra daughter of ʿAmr ibn Zayd ibn Labīd, mother of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, gave birth, she heard someone say in a dream: “A bold man of blooming splendor, truth and goodness, a destroyer of evil, is in the womb of Salmā daughter of ʿAmr.” Salmā gave birth to ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, and he was indeed a leader and a man of distinction until he died.

When Māwiya daughter of Ḥawza ibn ʿAmr ibn Murra gave birth to ʿĀtika daughter of Murra ibn Hilāl ibn Fālij al-Sulamī, she heard someone say in a dream: “How many a leader who gathers wealth, a king of the sea, and a generous master…” a generous man, a noble falcon, is in the womb of the daughter of Murra.” She married ʿAbd Manāf ibn Quṣayy and bore him Hāshim, ʿAbd Shams, and al-Muṭṭalib, the sons of ʿAbd Manāf.

He said: When Naʿja daughter of ʿUbayd ibn Ruwās gave birth, her father heard someone say in a dream: “A man of great numbers and strength, warriors and champions, leaders who are not weaklings, kind yet fierce, is in the womb of the daughter of ʿUbayd ibn Ruwās.” She married ʿAbd Shams ibn ʿAbd Manāf and bore him Umayya the elder and Ḥabīb.

. The vision of ʿĀtika daughter of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib

ʿĀtika had a vision before the arrival of Ḍamḍam ibn ʿAmr. She saw this vision, and it alarmed and frightened her. She sent for her brother al-ʿAbbās and said, “My brother, by God, I saw a vision last night. I saw a rider on a camel come and stop at al-Abṭaḥ. He cried at the top of his voice, ‘O people of treachery, hasten to your places of death!’ He cried it three times. Then the people gathered around him. He entered the mosque, with the people following him, and then his camel appeared on the roof of the Kaʿba. He cried the same words three times. Then his camel appeared on Abū Qubays, and he cried them the same way three times. Then he took a rock from Abū Qubays and sent it down. It came hurtling until, when it reached the foot of the mountain, it broke apart. Not a house in Mecca or a dwelling…” in the city remained without a fragment of it falling inside. It is related from ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ that he said: “I saw all of this. I even saw in our house a piece of the rock that had been cast down from Abū Qubays. There was a lesson in it, but God did not will us to become Muslims that day. Rather, He delayed our Islam until the time He willed.” The vision was interpreted as Ḍamḍam ibn ʿAmr summoning them to war and their leaders being killed at Badr. Her vision was fulfilled at Mecca. Abū Jahl said, “Sons of Hāshim, was it not enough that your men claim prophethood, that your women now claim it too?”

. The vision of Juhaym ibn al-Ṣalt ibn Makhrama ibn al-Muṭṭalib

Al-Wāqidī said: When Quraysh reached al-Juḥfa in the evening, Juhaym ibn Abī al-Ṣalt fell asleep. He said, “I saw myself between sleep and wakefulness, looking at a man who came riding a horse with a camel beside him. He stopped before me and said, ‘ʿUtba, Shayba, Zamʿa ibn al-Aswad, Umayya ibn Khalaf, Abū al-Bakhtarī, Abū al-Ḥakam, and Nawfal ibn Khuwaylid have been killed,’ naming a number of Quraysh’s leaders. ‘Suhayl ibn ʿAmr has been taken captive.’” He said, “One of them said, ‘By God, I think you are…’” going out to your places of death.” He said, “Then I saw him strike his camel on the chest and send it through the army. Not a single tent among the tents of the army escaped a drop of its blood.” Its interpretation came to pass just as he had seen, on the day of Badr.

. The vision of Āmina daughter of Wahb ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhra

They related that she spent the night in the Ḥijr and saw someone say to her, “Make a firm knot, for you have been granted a son whom you will name Aḥmad.” She gave birth to the master of Adam’s descendants ﷺ.

Al-Sukkarī related from someone other than Ibn Ḥabīb: Āmina said this when she gave him back to his wet nurses: (rajaz)

May He keep him safe—then come back to him,

keep him safe; his Lord is his protector.

God has shown me that none but he

is a light; his vision will not fail me.

The dawn will not fail the one who saw him.

The reason for the conversion of Ḥamza ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ﵁

In a report with a chain of transmission, Ibrāhīm ibn Saʿīd related from Muḥammad ibn Isḥāq, from a man of Aslam, who said: Abū Jahl passed by the Messenger of God ﷺ as he sat at al-Ṣafā. He insulted and abused him, and said some things about his religion and belittled him in ways he disliked. The Messenger of God ﷺ did not speak to him. A woman who belonged to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān was in a dwelling above al-Ṣafā and heard this. Abū Jahl then left him, went to an assembly of Quraysh at the Kaʿba, and sat with them. Before long, Ḥamza ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib came along, his bow slung over his shoulder, returning from…

He had gone out hunting, for he was a hunter who would shoot game and bring it back for him. Whenever he did this, he would pass by a Quraysh gathering, stop, greet them, and converse with them. He was the most powerful of Quraysh and the most resolute in standing up for himself. When he passed by the woman—after the Messenger of God ﷺ had got up and returned to his house—she said to him, “Abū ʿUmārah! If only you had seen what your nephew Muḥammad had just suffered, before you came, at the hands of Abū al-Ḥakam b. Hishām! He found him sitting here, insulted and harmed him, and said against him what he disliked; then he went away without Muḥammad speaking to him.” Ḥamzah’s anger was stirred by what God had willed for his honor. He went out quickly, stopping for no one as he usually did,[/], intending to circumambulate the Kaʿbah and ready to confront Abū Jahl if he met him. When he entered the mosque, he saw him sitting among the group. He went toward him, and when he stood over him, raised his bow and struck him with it, giving him a dreadful wound to the head. Then he said, “Would you insult him while I follow his religion and say what he says? Strike back at me, if you can!” Men of Banū Makhzūm rose against Ḥamzah to defend Abū Jahl. But Abū Jahl said, “Leave Abū ʿUmārah alone. By God, I did insult his nephew shamefully.” Ḥamzah ﵁ remained steadfast in his Islam. When Ḥamzah accepted Islam, Quraysh realized that the Messenger of God ﷺ had become powerful and protected, and that Ḥamzah would protect him. So they refrained from some of the things they had been doing to him, and Abū Jahl’s wound went without retribution.

From the account of Banū Hāshim

Ibn al-Kalbī related from his father, who said: A man of Banū Sulaym, from among the people of Baṣrah, told me, from his father and his paternal uncle, who said: We set out on pilgrimage in pre-Islamic times, when hardship had afflicted the people so we came to Mecca and completed our pilgrimage. We sought food to buy, but found none, nor anyone to host us. We went to the seasonal markets, but there was no food for sale and no one giving food. We remained for three or four days. He said: While we were in the Sacred Mosque, we saw about a hundred men leaving the mosque. We asked, “Where are these men going?” They said, “For food.” I said to my brother, “Come with me, for by God, all we want is food.” They entered the ravine of Banū Makhzūm. There was a large house with a great hall and two doors. In it was a couch, with a man sitting on it: dark-complexioned, with sparse side-whiskers and a long, well-proportioned face. He wore a black robe and held a rod in his hand. There were large bowls, and one could scarcely see the fine flour beneath the liver and humps piled on them. He said: We were the first to enter and the last to leave. I was full before my brother, so I said, “Get up—may God never satisfy your hunger!” The man on the couch raised his head and said, “No man should get up before he is full. Food is made to be eaten.” He was cross-eyed. We went out through the other door and found slaughtered camels standing there. We asked, “What are these camels for?” They said, “For what you just saw.” We asked, “Who is that man?” They said, “That is ʿAmr b. Hishām, Abū al-Ḥakam.”

More of their reports

Abū al-Qāsim Aḥmad b. Muḥammad b. Isḥāq al-Musayyibī informed me, saying: My father told me, from a shaykh, one of our respected associates. He said: Al-Waqqāṣī told me, from al-Zuhrī, from Abū Ḥayyah from Abū Dharr, who said: I came to Mecca to perform the lesser pilgrimage and asked, “Is there a host?” They said, “Yes, many. The nearest of them is al-Ḥārith b. Hishām.” So I went to his door and asked, “Is there any food for a guest?”

The servant girl said, “Yes.” She went inside and brought out raisins in her hand. I said, “Put them on a platter.” She realized that I was a guest, and said, “Come in.” There I saw al-Ḥārith on a chair, with large bowls of bread and meat in front of him, and hides spread out with raisins upon them. He said to me, “Help yourself.” I ate, and then he said, “This is yours for as long as you stay.” I stayed three days, then returned to Medina and told the Prophet ﷺ of what al-Ḥārith had done. The Prophet ﷺ said, “He is a noble man, son of a noble man. I wish he would accept Islam.”

The report of the Council House

Among the reports about Quraysh is that some men of Banū Quṣayy entered the Council House to discuss a matter of theirs. ʿAbd Allāh b. al-Zibaʿrā wanted to enter with them and listen to their counsel, but they barred him. He wrote a poem on the door of the Council House facing the Kaʿbah. When Banū Quṣayy came out, they saw the writing and read it. It said: (basīṭ)

The legends distract Quṣayy from glory,

And bribes, just as brokers take bribes.

They inherited the root of disgrace, their forebears—

So no glory or goodness can be found in them.

A man of Quṣayy said, “Come, let us go to the wretch, so that we may call him to account for his offense.” One of the men said, “Do not do that! Instead, send word to his people. If they accept what you ask of them, then that is settled; otherwise, decide what you will do, and you will have excused yourselves in your dealings with them.” The one who made this final suggestion was Abū Ṭālib b. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. Banū Sahm were kinsmen who had standing, influence, strength, and protection. When there was hostility, dispute, or disagreement between al-Muṭayyabūn and al-Aḥlāf, they would support all of Banū ʿAbd Manāf. The men sent ʿUtbah b. Rabīʿah b. ʿAbd Shams to Banū Sahm about Ibn al-Zibaʿrā’s satire against them. They were in their meeting place when he arrived. He said, “Your people have sent me to you concerning this fool, who has satirized them without their having committed any offense against him.” News of Ibn al-Zibaʿrā had reached them before ʿUtbah came. ʿUtbah said, “If he did what he did on your advice, then your advice was a bad one. If he acted without your advice, hand this fool over to them.” The men said, “We disavow before God that this was on our advice, nor with our approval, nor with our knowledge.” He said, “Then hand him over to us.” The men said, “If you wish, we will do so on the condition that if a satirist among you satirizes us, you hand him over to us.” ʿUtbah said, “The only thing preventing me from doing what you ask is that al-Zubayr b. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib is away in al-Ṭāʾif. I know that he will take up this matter, and I would not make al-Zubayr a match for Ibn al-Zibaʿrā.” A man among them said, “Men, hand him over to them. By my life, you have the same claim against them as they have against you.” Talk and clamour grew among the men. Present that day were Nubayh and Munabbih, sons of al-Ḥajjāj b. ʿĀmir al-Sahmī, wearing two robes that they had bought earlier from a cargo of fine goods that Kisrā had sent to al-Nuʿmān. Al-Nuʿmān had sent them to be sold at the market of ʿUkāẓ. Banū Yarbuʿ b. Ḥanẓalah seized them on the way and sold them at the market of ʿUkāẓ. When al-ʿĀṣ b. Wāʾil saw the talk and clamour growing, he called for a rope, bound Ibn al-Zibaʿrā with it, and handed him over to ʿUtbah b. Rabīʿah. ʿUtbah brought him, bound, to his people. They gathered by the Black Stone, and Ibn al-Zibaʿrā praised al-ʿĀṣ b. Wāʾil: (ramal)

Convey to all of Sahm,

A lord among them, and one who has yet to be a lord;

A speaker whose words reach the whole gathering,

Saying, “You are my support and my strength.”

Then repeat the words, if you have understood them,

To those who keep their sworn pledges.

That is al-ʿĀṣ, son of Salmā,

Who raised his fame—so praise him and add to it.

The needy grew up under his protection …

Like the wild lote tree, rooted among the sidr, in thickets.

May death ransom him, if he is challenged—

A hard man, of firm nature, steadfast of heart.

ʿAbd Allāh b. al-Zibaʿrā said, praising Quṣayy and seeking its favor: (ṭawīl)

Quṣayy, take from me a message:

You are the summit of glory among the descendants of Ghālib.

You are the refuge of people in every winter,

When a harsh, hard-shouldered age afflicts them.

The highborn of Maʿadd know that you

Are their refuge in crushing calamities.

If you set me free, you set free a kinsman,

One who praises you truthfully, not as a liar.

So convey a message from me to Abū Sufyān,

And convey it to Asīd, the generous man of many acquisitions.

And convey it to Abū al-ʿĀṣī, and do not forget Zamʿah, … and Muṭʿim, do not forget the bridle for the troublemaker.

For in hardship and ease you are the best of us,

When the stars’ day is bitterly cold.

Quraysh’s rocking songs for their children

Salmā bt. ʿAmr b. Zayd b. Labīd said, rocking her son ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib:

(r ajaz)

My son has no stammer,

And no pretender or slave bore him.

One who recognizes him knows the goodness in him;

A noble man, laughter-loving, of lofty aims.

If God delays the death of the sons of the clan, …

He will thrust forward through the crowd, forcing it aside.

I speak the truth, not as the sinners speak.

ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib rocked his son al-ʿAbbās, saying: (rajaz)

My expectation of my son ʿAbbās, when he grows,

Is that he will give the pilgrims water when the pilgrims gather in great numbers.

The mother of ʿAbd Allāh b. al-ʿAbbās, Lubābah bt. al-Ḥārith b. Ḥazn al-Hilālīyah, used to rock her son, saying: (rajaz)

May I be bereaved of myself, and so be bereaved of my firstborn,

If he does not become lord of Fihr and those beyond Fihr,

Through ancient nobility and abundant generosity,

Until he is laid in the hollow of the grave.

Hind bt. Abī Sufyān b. al-Ḥārith b. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib rocked her son ʿAbd Allāh b. al-Ḥārith b. Nawfal, saying: (rajaz)

By the Lord of the Kaʿbah,

I shall marry Babbah

To a maiden in a nuqbah,

Honored and beloved,

Who loves those whom he loves.

Ṣafiyyah bt. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib rocked her son al-Zubayr b. al-ʿAwwām, saying: (rajaz)

By your father, Zubayr—he is no base, foolish man,

But a noble falcon, of distinguished stock;

Defender of what must be defended, a lord, true to his word,

He strikes the leader of the people squarely on the crown.

He is neither feeble nor clumsy.

She also rocked ʿAbd Allāh b. al-Zubayr, saying: (rajaz)

My youngest son is a beloved, stocky lad;

I fear he may disobey me and be stingy.

O Lord, let me enjoy my firstborn son,

the noble, generous, hopeful one.

Hind bt. ʿUtba sang while dancing her son Muʿāwiya b. Abū Sufyān to sleep: (rajaz)

My son is noble in stock,

dear to his family, forbearing,

neither foul-mouthed nor base,

neither a weakling nor weary.

Ṣakhr is the chief of the sons of Fihr;

he never disappoints expectation nor loses heart.

She also sang while dancing her son ʿUtba to sleep: (rajaz)

My son is one of the men of al-Ḥums,

noble in lineage and noble in spirit,

not fainthearted nor contemptible.

ʿUtba is a man of Badr, and his father is Shams.

Fāṭima bt. Naʿja al-Khuzāʿiyya sang while dancing her son Saʿīd b. Zayd b. ʿAmr b. Nufayl b. ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā to sleep: (rajaz)

My son is the chief of the clan,

chaste, steadfast, of good character;

ample in generosity, his hand pours forth.

He gives in times of ease and hardship.

Maysūn bt. Baḥdal sang while dancing her son Yazīd b. Muʿāwiya to sleep: (rajaz)

Yazīd is the best of the young men of the Arabs,

the most forbearing in contentment and in anger.

He hastens to give, and when asked, he gives;

my life, then my mother and father, be his ransom,

and all my family, from ruin.

Māwiya bt. Kaʿb b. al-Qayn sang while dancing her son Sāma b. Luʾayy to sleep: (rajaz)

[And—] my expectation of my son is the best expectation:

that he buys praise and pays dearly for it,

and routs the army when the army grows heavy,

and quenches the thirsty man with pure milk;

he fills the shīzā with the fattened beast, rich in flesh …

When the people are roused, whenever it is said,

he is the one called upon—not this one or that.

Al-Zubayr b. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib sang while dancing the Prophet ﷺ—to sleep: (rajaz)

Muḥammad b. ʿAbd,

may you live the most blessed life.

May you always live in a life of plenty,

and in power and booty,

which will make you independent of all the world;

and may you live until you grow old.

He also sang while dancing his brother al-ʿAbbās to sleep: (rajaz)

My brother al-ʿAbbās is chaste and generous;

he turns away from an indecency, and if you speak plainly, he is firm.

He delights in glory and fulfills his pledges;

he slaughters the great she-camel on a bitterly cold day.

How noble your forebears, on your mother’s and father’s sides!

He sang while dancing his brother Ḍirār b. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib to sleep: (rajaz)

/ My expectation of the proud lion Ḍirār is the best expectation:

that he buys praise by paying the highest price,

and slaughters for his guests the she-camels rich in fat …

More distinguished than Dhū Yazan and Dhū Jadan.

He also sang while dancing his daughter Ḍubāʿa to sleep: (rajaz)

How lovely is Ḍubāʿa,

honored and obeyed;

she does not steal the merchandise,

nor does she know wantonness.

He also sang while dancing his daughter Umm al-Ḥakam to sleep: (rajaz)

How lovely is Umm al-Ḥakam,

as though she were a white gazelle, blackish-white.

O her husband, what share …

He drew a lot for her, and the lot was his.

He also said: (rajaz)

My daughter is a white beauty among white ones,

like an egg in a sandy hollow, in a nest.

She amazes whoever circles the corners of the Stone.

He also said: (rajaz)

My daughter is indeed a freeborn woman of noble birth;

she does not withhold the firewood or the extra firewood.

Umm al-Banīn al-Waḥīdiyya sang while dancing her son al-ʿAbbās b. ʿAlī b. Abī Ṭālib ﵉ both—to sleep: (rajaz)

I commend him to the One,

against the eye of every envier—

the one standing and the one sitting,

their Muslim and their disbeliever,

/ their departing and their arriving,

their newborn and their parent.

Umm Ḥabīb bt. al-ʿĀṣ b. Umayya sang while dancing Jubayr b. Muṭʿim b. ʿAdī b. Nawfal to sleep: (rajaz)

Protect Jubayr, Lord, in the expedition;

do not leave me seated in a wretched place,

and bless his sons, O Lord.

She also said: (rajaz)

Protect Jubayr from the swords of Persia,

and keep him clear of the onset of misgivings.

Protect him from every fierce, striking enemy …

Adorn the gatherings with him, O Lord.

Ḍubāʿa bt. ʿĀmir sang while dancing her son Salama b. Hishām b. al-Mughīra to sleep:

(rajaz)

Hishām raised him to the heights,

long ago, and he had noble forefathers—

mighty chiefs, generous men, great men …

From the house of Makhzūm, they are the leaders,

the foremost branch, the chief, and the peak.

Umm Ḥakīm bt. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, known as al-Bayḍāʾ, sang while dancing her daughter’s son ʿUthmān b. ʿAffān to sleep: (rajaz)

My expectation of him is truthfulness and goodness;

he commands and obeys.

He is one of the white, steadfast young men,

who guard the womenfolk at the rear,

and strike the ram that bellows;

they strike it until it falls,

with every keen, sharp blade.

Report of the Night Caller with an Elegy for Hishām

Ibn al-Kharbūdh al-Makkī said: Quraysh heard a caller in the night, one of the jinn, saying: (basīṭ)

Hishām has perished, though the sons of Fihr

used to seek his protection whenever time bore down on them.

Who is there for the orphans and the guests when they arrive,

now that events and the shroud have come before him?

/ The women of Malāḥ weep for him whenever

the sun of day rises, and the aged man laments his grief.

I mean the son of Rayṭa, whose forefathers were of Sahm:

there is no breach in their spear-shaft, nor flaw.

Report of the Day of Dhū Ḍāl, Which Was the Day of al-Quṣayba

Abū Mūsā related from ʿAbd Allāh b. ʿAmr al-Madanī, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān b. Muḥammad al-Taymī, one of the descendants of Abū Bakr ﵁—who said: And Abū al-Ḥasan ʿAlī b. Muḥammad related to me; he said: My father related it to me, from his shaykhs and family. Abū Bakr said: And Abū …

Saʿīd al-Sukkarī said: ʿAlī b. Muḥammad al-Nawfalī related it to me; he said: My father related it to me, from his shaykhs, who said:

Al-Ḥārith b. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib set out with a hundred and twenty-odd men from Quraysh and others among their allies, intending to go to Syria for trade. On his return, he stopped at a place called Dhū Ḍāl, also known as al-Quṣayba, a watering place belonging to Banū Saʿd of Tamīm. As he stopped at the watering place, two men from ʿIjl and Shaybān, one named ʿAmr and the other ʿAwf, raided it with men of their tribes. They raided the watering place while its people were away—only the women and boys were there. They took captives and drove off the livestock.

A woman of Banū Saʿd, named ʿĀtika, whose head-covering had fallen from her head, came to al-Ḥārith and his companions and adjured them by their kinship with Khandaf to come to her aid. Al-Ḥārith called upon his companions, and they answered him. He fought them fiercely. The men of ʿIjl and Shaybān were perplexed by their dialects, and said: “By God, you are not of Banū Saʿd. Who are you?” Al-Ḥārith said to them: “We are Quraysh.” They said: “Men of Quraysh, what have we to do with you? We are people of your religion; we make pilgrimage to your sanctuary and your House.” Al-Ḥārith said: “Then do not make us sin against our religion, for our religion requires us to protect the neighbor. The livestock is yours; release the captives.”

They refused, so he fought them even more fiercely. That day, al-Ḥārith received twenty wounds and captured ʿAmr, one of the two chiefs. The men fled, and al-Ḥārith found a man of Banū Saʿd among the slain; he had been left behind with the women. Al-Ḥārith then handed over to the Saʿdīs the chief he had captured in exchange for the man of theirs who had been killed. Then he began to say:

(basīṭ)

Tell Quraysh, when you come to them from us,

that bravery and generosity are their nature.

Were it not for the horsemen of Kaʿb, men of distinction,

on the day of al-Quṣayba, eyes would have reddened.

The women of Banū Saʿd were led away,

by a lion, unmatched among his peers in war.

How many wailing women you might have seen that day,

their eyes drowned in tears.

When they saw us at Dhū Ḍāl, standing against them,

blows split open the heads of their chiefs.

A band of Shaybān turned and fled, driven on …

by lean, fleet horses, their flanks sleek and swift.

The man ʿAwf escaped, though he was not free of danger;
