a swift runner, strong in both legs, carried him off.

After his attack, the man ʿAmr was left

humiliated among them, a captive whose fetter chafed.

ʿĀtika al-Saʿdiyya said: (ṭawīl)

May God reward with good—and reward is in His hand—

the horsemen of the clans of ʿAbd Shams and Hāshim,

and the people of nobility, the Taym b. Murra; they

are the leaders in noble deeds and momentous affairs.

/ They drove away all of Rabīʿa from us

with stout spears and keen, flashing swords.

And ʿAmr has become a captive in our lands …

a prisoner tormented by the rings of iron fetters.

Do not deny [the merit of] Saʿd, the chiefs of Ghālib ...Quraysh of exalted rank, as long as the people of the pilgrimage seasons perform the pilgrimage.

Al-Ḥārith came to ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib in Mecca and told him what had happened. He was pleased, slaughtered camels, and fed the people.

The Arrival of Aws ibn Ḥajar in Mecca and His Lodging with Abū Jahl

He said: Aws ibn Ḥajar al-Tamīmī came to Mecca to visit Abū Jahl ibn Hishām al-Makhzūmī and praised him. Aws said to him, “I would like to see your people.” Abū Jahl sent word to the young men of his clan that none of them should come to the mosque except wearing the finest garments he could obtain. They put on al-qaṭr, al-ataḥmī, and garments whitened with al-muwarris. Aws saw no fine robe or splendid garment without asking about it, and they would reply, “It belongs to the Banū al-Mughīra”. The Banū al-Mughīra thus became highly esteemed in his eyes, and his regard for them increased.

Then Abū Jahl ordered food to be prepared and invited Aws and his people. They went ahead, then came out to the mosque. While they were circumambulating the Kaʿba, ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ibn Hāshim appeared in a litter, with his sons around him. Aws looked at an old man, white as silver, whose face was a cubit long; young men carried his litter, white and tall as spears. He had never seen figures like them. He kept looking at them, while Abū Jahl tried to distract him from them with conversation. Aws kept craning to look at them, struck by the old man’s bearing, beauty, and perfect form, and by the young men’s completeness, tall stature, handsome faces, and fine appearance. He said, “Abū al-Ḥakam, who is this old man, and who are these young men? By God, I have never seen an old man more handsome, more complete, or taller, nor young men more eloquent, more radiant, or more judicious—”

“—than these.” Abū Jahl said, “You have seen him. This is ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, and these are his sons. These are the people alongside whom Quraysh can claim no nobility for as long as they remain. May God not let them remain!”

The Alliance of Jaḥsh ibn Riʾāb with Umayya and His Marriage into ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib’s Family

He said: When Jaḥsh ibn Riʾāb ibn Yaʿmar al-Asadī came to Mecca, he entered into an alliance with Umayya ibn ʿAbd Shams. He was told, “You have left aside the noblest among them, and the one held in greatest esteem by Quraysh: ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ibn Hāshim.” He said, “By God, if I have missed the chance to ally with him, I shall not miss the chance to marry into his family.” He then asked for the hand of Umayma, daughter of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, and Umayya married her to him.

The Account of the Necklace Assembly

He said: Notables from Quraysh and elsewhere used to gather in an assembly attended by the sons of the Emigrants. That assembly was called the Necklace, likened to a necklace strung with jewels for the beauty and splendor of the gathering and the nobility of its members. Whenever someone arrived in Muʿāwiya’s presence, he would ask about the Necklace Assembly, out of his concern for it.

It is related that a merchant held a debt of six thousand dirhams against Ibn Abī ʿAtīq, and came to demand payment. Ibn Abī ʿAtīq said to him, “I do not have it [right now], but when you sit in the Necklace Assembly, ask me about the household of the Banū ʿAbd Manāf.” Ibn Abī ʿAtīq came and sat beside al-Ḥasan ibn ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵉ both. The merchant said to Ibn Abī ʿAtīq, “Abū Muḥammad, tell me about the household of the Banū ʿAbd Manāf.” He replied, “The Āl Ḥarb: they entered into alliance, and the people entered into alliance; they submitted to Islam, and the people submitted to Islam.” The man said, “And then who, may God preserve you?” He replied, “The Banū al-ʿĀṣ: among them are more martyrs and men of noble standing than among anyone else.” The man said, “Glory be to God! But where are the Banū ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib?” He said to him, “Fool! You asked me only about the households of human beings. Had you asked me about—”

“—the faces of the angels, I would have told you about the Banū ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. Among them is the Messenger of God ﷺ—and among them is the Lion of God, and among them is the one who flies in Paradise.” Al-Ḥasan ﵇—said, “I adjure you by God: you have a need, Abū Muḥammad?” He said, “Yes, by God! I owe this man six thousand.” Al-Ḥasan said, “God has paid it for you; it is our responsibility, not yours.”

That assembly continued to meet, attended by ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās, ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿAdī ibn al-Khiyār ibn Nawfal, ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Rabīʿa al-Makhzūmī, Abū Yasār [ibn—] ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Shayba ibn Rabīʿa ibn ʿAbd Shams, Mūsā ibn Ṭalḥa ibn ʿUbayd Allāh, and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAbd al-Qārī. The leading men and notables of the people would sit with them. Muʿāwiya said, “Medina will remain flourishing as long as the Necklace Assembly continues.”

One night they gathered as usual. ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿAdī spoke, and, as they discussed the Companions, he said, “I have seen no one equal to ʿAlī ﵇—in eloquence and jurisprudence.” Abū Yasār said:

“It is as though you have never seen Muʿāwiya! By God, Muʿāwiya saw only a human being, and his heart was only a human heart”—and he spoke at length in praise of Muʿāwiya. ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar said to him, “It is as though you have never seen ʿUmar, his justice, and his perfection.” ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Rabīʿa said, “It is as though you see merit only in the Emigrants. By God, once they had become Muslims, what were they? Have you not seen al-Ḥārith ibn Hishām?” Mūsā ibn Ṭalḥa said, “And you are sitting here mentioning them alongside—”

“—the Emigrants? By God, they were only their slaves, whom they emancipated after they had overpowered them and gained control of them.” They leapt up against one another, and the people intervened to keep them apart. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān swore that he would tell Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam that he had called him a slave and had called Muʿāwiya a slave.

Mūsā went to ʿĀʾisha’s house, fearing only Marwān. Barīra opened the door for him. He entered while ʿĀʾisha was asleep. ʿĀʾisha was his aunt through nursing: Asmāʾ had nursed Mūsā ibn Ṭalḥa, and he was married to the daughter of her brother, ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Abī Bakr.

When morning prayer was over, and ʿĀʾisha still did not know that he was there, Marwān prayed and sat on the pulpit. He said, “Where is the man who claims that the Commander of the Faithful is an emancipated slave? I shall certainly do this and that!” ʿĀʾisha would not speak until sunrise. When the sun rose, she said, “Barīra, what is Marwān saying?” Mūsā came out to her and said, “He means me,” then told her what had happened. She said, “Alas for me! Does Marwān deny that the Messenger of God ﷺ—cast the shelter of his pardon over them, then gave them their own freedom? Marwān, you wretched little man!” She raised her voice and said, “Go to your home.” He said to her, “I fear Marwān.” She replied, “ Is he going to set upon you with all his might?” Mūsā left. When Marwān heard what ʿĀʾisha had said, he wrote to Muʿāwiya, reporting the whole affair. When Muʿāwiya read it, he said, “By God, the Necklace Assembly has been ruined. May God curse Marwān!” He wrote to him: “May God curse you, your sermon, and your sitting on the pulpit of the Messenger—”

“—of God ﷺ—by claiming that we are slaves. When this letter reaches you, do not mention any part of this story again, nor speak of it, and leave its subject alone.” They dispersed that night and never again returned to that assembly.

The Killing of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Khālid ibn al-Walīd and Its Cause

Ibn al-Kalbī related, from Khālid ibn Saʿīd, from his father, that when Muʿāwiya intended to secure the pledge of allegiance for Yazīd, he said to the people of Syria, “The Commander of the Faithful has grown old and his end is near. I intend to appoint a man to govern after me. What do you think?” They said, “Choose ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Khālid ibn al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīra. He is a man of merit.” Muʿāwiya was silent and kept it in his mind.

Then ʿAbd al-Raḥmān fell ill. Muʿāwiya summoned Ibn Athāl, one of the leading Romans, who was a physician and used to visit Muʿāwiya. He said, “Go to ʿAbd al-Raḥmān and devise a way to deal with him.” Ibn Athāl went to ʿAbd al-Raḥmān and gave him a potion. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān’s insides were torn apart, and he died. When Muʿāwiya heard of his death, he said, “There is no true worth except in one who strikes down for you someone you dislike”.

The news reached his nephew Khālid ibn al-Muhājir ibn Khālid ibn al-Walīd. He said to a client of his named Nāfiʿ, who was a Roman and one of the stoutest-hearted of men—Khālid ibn al-Muhājir was then in Mecca and thought ill of his uncle ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, because al-Muhājir had been with ʿAlī, may God ennoble his face, and was killed at Ṣiffīn, while Khālid ibn al-Muhājir was with the Banū Hāshim in the ravine during Ibn al-Zubayr’s time—“Come with me,” he said to his client Nāfiʿ.

They set out and reached Damascus at night. They asked about Abū Athāl, and were told, “He is with Muʿāwiya, and he only goes out in the middle of the night.” They waited for him until he came out with a group. Khālid charged; they parted to make way for him, and he struck Abū Athāl with his sword and killed him. The two men left and went into hiding.

When morning came, they told Muʿāwiya the story. He said, “By God, this is Khālid ibn al-Muhājir!” He ordered that he be sought, and they searched until they found him along with Nāfiʿ. When Khālid was brought in before Muʿāwiya, Muʿāwiya said, “Did you kill him? May God not reward you for coming to visit!” Khālid replied, “The one who was ordered has been killed, while the one who gave the order remains.” Muʿāwiya said, “By God, if you had uttered the profession of faith even once, I would have killed you.” Khālid said, “Indeed, by God, if we were on equal terms—” Muʿāwiya said, “By God, if we were on equal terms, you would be Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān ibn Ḥarb ibn Umayya, and I would be Khālid ibn al-Muhājir ibn Khālid ibn al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīra. My house would be between the two mountain passes, with the valley opening onto it, and your house would be in Ajyād, its lower part stone and its upper part mudbrick.”

He ordered that Nāfiʿ be given a hundred lashes but did not strike Khālid. Then he ordered the two men expelled from Damascus. He decreed twelve thousand [dirhams] for Ibn Athāl, and the Banū Makhzūm paid the blood money. Muʿāwiya took six thousand from it and put the sum in the treasury. The blood money remained divided in this way for protected non-Muslims until ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ﵀—became caliph and abolished the half [that] the ruler [had been] taking.

Kaʿb ibn Juʿayl al-Taghlibī, who had been a friend of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Khālid ibn al-Walīd, came to Muʿāwiya. Muʿāwiya said, “This man was a friend of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān. What have you said about him?” He replied, “I said:” [al-wāfir]

Should you not weep? Quraysh have not been wrongedby the loud lamenting for their young man.

Were Damascus, the land of Ḥimṣ,and Buṣrā asked who gave you their villages,

[they would say:] “The Sword of God brought death into them,destroyed their fortress, and protected their sanctuary.

“Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥarb settled them there,though it was land other than his own.”

Ibn al-Kalbī said: ʿUrwa ibn al-Zubayr often taunted Khālid ibn al-Muhājir for killing his uncle ʿAbd al-Raḥmān but not avenging him. When Khālid killed Ibn Athāl, ʿUrwa composed these verses: [al-ṭawīl]

The Sword of God’s son was judged justly by his sword,and his mounts were left without one to bear them.

If it was justice, then it was justice he meted out;if it was suspicion, then suspicion was what drove him.

Ask Ibn Athāl: have you avenged Khālid’s son?Here is Ibn Jarmūz—are you going to kill him?

ʿUrwa said, “Where is Ibn Jarmūz, so I can kill him?”

The Alliance of al-Miqdād ibn al-Aswad ibn ʿAbd Yaghūth

Hishām related that ʿAmr ibn Thaʿlaba al-Bahrānī, Abū al-Miqdād, the Companion of the Messenger of God ﷺ—had been involved in a blood feud among his people, so he fled to Ḥaḍramawt. He married a woman of al-Ṣadaf, from a branch called the Banū Shakl. She had six or seven children by a paternal cousin. She bore al-Miqdād, and a quarrel arose between him and his maternal brothers, on the one hand, and Abū Shammar Ḥijr ibn Murra, on the other. Ḥijr was a qayl—one of the aqyāl of Ḥaḍramawt—and was known as al-Adhmarī. Al-Miqdād charged Abū Shammar and struck him with his sword on the leg, leaving him lame. Al-Miqdād fled to Mecca. Abū Shammar and his companions captured al-Miqdād’s companions. Abū Shammar said: [al-ṭawīl]

We routed the army, the army of Ibn Ḍajʿam,and we killed ʿĀmir and Mālik’s son.

We killed those who would choose our best men,and the captives of Saʿd and Māsiq came to us.

Al-Miqdād escaped us while the night was pitch-dark,as though menstrual blood covered his clothes.

“If escape saves you today, then flight from me has never ceased to inspire fear in your heart.”

Al-Miqdād entered Mecca and saw a man circumambulating the House with two swords slung at his side. He said, “This man would not carry two swords unless he were well protected.” He asked about him and was told, “This is al-Aswad ibn ʿAbd Yaghūth ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhrah.” Al-Miqdād went to him, told him his story, and asked to enter into an alliance with him and for his protection. Al-Aswad agreed. Thus al-Miqdād was called “al-Miqdād ibn al-Aswad,” until the Prophet ﷺ—ordered that they be affiliated with their fathers. He meant Ḍajʿam ibn Ḥamāṭah ibn Saʿd ibn Sulayḥ ibn Bahrāʾ, and Mālik ibn Sulayḥ: they were the two leaders on that day. Saʿd ibn Sulayḥ and Māsik ibn Sulayḥ were the drinking companions among Quraysh.

Quraysh’s drinking companions

ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib was a drinking companion of Ḥarb ibn Umayyah until the two submitted their dispute to Nufayl ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā. When Nufayl gave his judgment in favor of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, they parted ways. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib died before the sacrilegious war, at the age of one hundred and twenty. Ḥarb then became the drinking companion of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān al-Taymī. Abū Uḥayḥah Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah was a drinking companion of al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah al-Makhzūmī.

Al-Makhzūmī was a drinking companion of Maʿmar ibn Ḥabīb [ibn Wahb] ibn Ḥudhāqah ibn Jumāḥ. ʿUqbah ibn Abī Muʿayṭ ibn Abī ʿAmr ibn Umayyah was a drinking companion of al-Aswad ibn ʿAbd Yaghūth al-Zuhrī. Abū Ṭālib ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib was a drinking companion of Musāfir ibn Abī ʿAmr ibn Umayyah. When Musāfir died, Abū Ṭālib became the drinking companion of ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd Wudd ibn Naḍr ibn Mālik ibn Ḥisl ibn ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy. ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib killed him on the day of the Trench. ʿUtaybah ibn Rabīʿah ibn ʿAbd Shams was a drinking companion of Muṭʿim ibn ʿAdī ibn Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf. Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb was a drinking companion of al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. Al-Fākih ibn al-Mughīrah was a drinking companion of ʿAwf ibn ʿAbd ʿAwf ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Zuhrah. Zayd ibn ʿAmr ibn Nufayl ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā was a drinking companion of Waraqah ibn Nawfal ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā. Shaybah ibn Rabīʿah ibn ʿAbd Shams was a drinking companion of ʿUthmān ibn al-Ḥuwayrith ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā. Al-ʿĀṣ ibn Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah was a drinking companion of al-ʿĀṣ ibn Hishām al-Makhzūmī. The two were called “the two fools of Quraysh.” ʿAlī ﵇—killed al-ʿĀṣ ibn Hishām on the day of Badr. He had gone out in place of Abū Lahab. When Quraysh set out for their caravan, they sent the Banū Hāshim out to fight the Messenger of God ﷺ—against their will; anyone among them who did not go was replaced by another man. Abū Lahab had gambled with al-ʿĀṣ ibn Hishām— Abū Lahab won his property, making him his slave, and then made him a blacksmith. He sent him out as his substitute, and he was killed on the day of Badr. Abū Lahab was a drinking companion of al-Ḥārith ibn Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Quṣayy. Al-Walīd ibn ʿUtbah ibn Rabīʿah was a drinking companion of al-ʿĀṣ ibn Munabbih al-Sahmī; ʿAlī ﵁—killed both of them on the day of Badr. Ḍirār ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ibn Mirdās al-Fihrī was a drinking companion of Hubayrah ibn Abī Wahb al-Makhzūmī. Abū Jahl, who was ʿAmr ibn Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah, was a drinking companion of al-Ṭarīd, that is, al-Ḥakam ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah. Al-Ḥārith ibn Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah was a drinking companion of Ḥakīm ibn Ḥizām ibn Khuwaylid. Ḥakīm was born to his mother in the Kaʿbah. Al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil ibn Hāshim ibn Saʿīd ibn Sahm was a drinking companion of Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah, the father of Abū Jahl ibn Hishām. Nabīh ibn al-Ḥajjāj ibn ʿĀmir al-Sahmī was a drinking companion of al-Naḍr ibn al-Ḥārith, one of the Banū ʿAbd al-Dār. The Messenger of God ﷺ—executed him after the battle of Badr. He was a heretic who harassed the Messenger of God. ʿUmārah ibn al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah al-Makhzūmī was a drinking companion of Ḥanẓalah ibn Abī Sufyān. Ḥanẓalah was killed as an unbeliever on the day of Badr. Al-Zubayr ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib was a drinking companion of Mālik ibn ʿUmaylah ibn al-Sabbāq ibn ʿAbd al-Dār. Al-Arqam ibn Naḍlah ibn Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf was a drinking companion of Suwayd ibn Harmī ibn ʿĀmir al-Jumaḥī. Suwayd was the first to—

…set up the couches and served milk and honey in Mecca. He left no descendants. Al-Ḥārith ibn Ḥarb ibn Umayyah was a drinking companion of al-ʿAwwām ibn Khuwaylid ibn Asad. Al-Ḥārith ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā was a drinking companion of ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAbd al-Dār. Abū al-Bakhtarī al-ʿĀṣ ibn Hāshim ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Asad was a drinking companion of Ṭalḥah ibn Abī Ṭalḥah ibn ʿAbd al-Dār. Al-Mujadhdhir ibn Dhiyād al-Balawī killed Abū al-Bakhtarī on the day of Badr, and ʿAlī ﵇—killed Ṭalḥah on the day of Uḥud. Munabbih ibn al-Ḥajjāj ibn ʿĀmir al-Sahmī was a drinking companion of Ṭuʿaymah ibn ʿAdī ibn Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf; Ṭuʿaymah was killed on the day of Badr. Abū Sufyān ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib was a drinking companion of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil al-Sahmī. Abū Umayyah ibn al-Mughīrah al-Makhzūmī was a drinking companion of Abū Widāʿah ibn Ḍubayrah ibn Saʿīd ibn Sahm. The two of them served honey in Mecca after Suwayd ibn Harmī. Abū Qays ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhrah was a drinking companion of Qays ibn ʿAdī ibn Sahm. The poet says of him: (rajaz)

In his house, in his house, generosity is sought;

In nobility, he is like Qays ibn ʿAdī.

He would go to the wine seller with a whip in his hand. He would ask him to show him some wine; if it was good, so be it, but otherwise he would say, “I find your wine [good],” strike him on the head with the whip, and leave. Their number was fifty-eight men.

Judges among Quraysh

Among the Banū Hāshim were ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ibn Hāshim, al-Zubayr, and Abū Ṭālib, the sons of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. Among the Banū Umayyah were Ḥarb ibn Umayyah and Abū Sufyān Ṣakhr ibn Ḥarb. Among the Banū Zuhrah ibn Kilāb was al-ʿAlāʾ ibn Jāriyah al-Thaqafī, an ally of the Banū Zuhrah. Among the Banū Makhzūm was al-ʿAdl, namely al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm. Among the Banū Sahm were Qays ibn ʿAdī ibn Saʿd ibn Sahm and al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil ibn Hāshim ibn Saʿīd ibn Sahm. Among the Banū ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb was Nufayl ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Riyāḥ ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Qurṭ ibn Rizāḥ ibn ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb.

Provisions for travelers from Quraysh

When they traveled, no one traveling with them would bake bread or cook. They were al-Aswad ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Quṣayy; Musāfir ibn Abī ʿAmr ibn Umayyah ibn ʿAbd— Shams; Abū Umayyah ibn al-Mughīrah ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm; and Zamʿah ibn al-Aswad ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn Asad.

The story of Musāfir and Hind

Musāfir ibn Abī ʿAmr was passionately in love with Hind bint ʿUtbah ibn Rabīʿah ibn ʿAbd Shams. He traveled as an envoy to al-Nuʿmān ibn al-Mundhir al-Lakhmī, who honored him and kept him as a companion. A visitor arrived and informed him that Hind had married Abū Sufyān. He fell ill from grief, and fluid accumulated in his belly, so he was cauterized with fire. When the physician who was to cauterize him saw the branding irons and Musāfir’s endurance, he farted. Musāfir said: (basīṭ)

Even a lout may fart while the branding iron is in the fire.

It became a proverb. Musāfir also said: (ṭawīl)

Would that Hind had become forbidden to you,

And I had become a father-in-law to her nearest in-laws.

I have become like a man robbed of his sword’s sheath,

Turning a bow and arrows over in his hands.

Then he set out toward Mecca and died at Hobalah. Abū Ṭālib mourned him, saying:

(khafīf)

Would that I knew of Musāfir ibn Abī ʿAmr—

Would that the grief-stricken could say it—

How many a true companion have we seen,

And a cousin, whom death overtook!

So I consoled myself with fortitude and patience,

Though I am loath to part from my companion.

Will the people ever return to us,

While my friend lies buried in a tomb?

Blessed be the stranger who has died, as blessed

As fresh basil, and olives.

A spokesman who repels opponents with his hands—

And with a face whose bridge adorns it.

Would that I knew whether grief will bring my heart

To morning in such a state—what a trial I have suffered!

A dead man, laid in the valley of Dhū Raw, near Hobalah;

Before him are Ṣiḥār and mountain ridges.

Yet whenever I recall him to my heart,

My tears overflow, and my veins overflow.

The generous men of Quraysh

Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf—we have recorded his story at the beginning of the book—and Umayyah ibn ʿAbd Shams, whom Hāshim surpassed; their story has already been related. Among the Banū Taym ibn Murrah was “Gold-Drinker,” namely ʿUthmān ibn ʿAmr ibn Kaʿb ibn Saʿd ibn Taym, who was one of those who fed people. His father was al-Sayyāl, namely ʿAmr ibn Kaʿb ibn Saʿd ibn Taym, who was generous and fed people. Also among them was ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān ibn ʿAmr ibn Kaʿb ibn Saʿd ibn Taym. When he grew old, his people placed him under restriction, so that whenever he gave something away, they would take it back from the recipient. When he saw this, he would say to a petitioner who came to ask him, “Sit close beside me, within reach of my hand, so that I may slap you— If you do so, say: “I will not be satisfied until I slap ʿAbd Allāh as he slapped me, and you are satisfied with your judgment from my property.” ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Qays al-Ruqayyāt says of him: (al-khafīf)

And the one who, if he points toward you with a slap,

follows the slap with a gift and a grant.

He had two criers, one who called out in the lower part of Mecca and the other in the upper part. The two criers were Abū Sufyān ibn ʿAbd al-Asad and Abū Quḥāfa. One of them would call out: “Whoever wants fat and meat, let him come to the house of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān.” He was the first to serve fālūdhaj in Mecca. The poet says of him: (al-wāfir)

He has a caller in Mecca, alert and active,

and another calling from above his house,

to a great dish of walnut wood, upon it …

wheat flour, kneaded with honey.

Among the Banū Makhzūm was Hishām ibn al-Mughīra ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm. He was noble and generous in feeding others. / Quraysh made his death a date by which to reckon time. The poet says of him: (al-wāfir)

The valley of Mecca awoke desolate,

as though the earth held no Hishām.

His two sons, Abū Jahl and al-Ḥārith, were generous. A report about al-Ḥārith has already been related. Also among the generous were Khalaf ibn Wahb ibn Ḥudhāfa ibn Jumah, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣafwān ibn Umayya ibn Khalaf, and ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣafwān ibn Umayya ibn Khalaf. Khalaf was generous, his son Umayya was generous, his son Ṣafwān was generous, his son ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣafwān ibn Umayya was generous, and his son ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣafān ibn Umayya was generous. Thus ʿAmr was the son of a generous man, the son of a generous man, the son of [a generous man, the son of a generous man—]. Of all people, ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣafwān ibn Umayya ibn Khalaf was the most deeply rooted in generosity—except for Qays, the generous son of Saʿd, the generous son of ʿUbāda, the generous son of Dulaym, the generous son of Ḥāritha, the generous son of Ḥuzayma, the generous son of Thaʿlaba, the generous son of Ṭarīf, the generous son of al-Khazraj. He was the son of a generous man, the son of a generous man, the son of a generous man, the son of a generous man, the son of a generous man, the son of a generous man, the son of a generous man. This one was the most deeply rooted of all people in generosity, while ʿAmr was the most deeply rooted in generosity among Quraysh. and Ṭalḥa ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿUthmān ibn Kaʿb ibn Saʿd ibn Taym ibn Murra. A man who was related to him came to him and asked him for help. He said to him: “This is my garden wall at such-and-such a place. I have been offered six hundred thousand dirhams for it, and the money will be brought to me this evening. If you wish, take the money; if you wish, take the garden wall.” [And—] ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. An account of his generosity relates that a money-changer became insolvent in Medina, and his creditors pressed him. He asked them for time so that he could try to arrange matters for them. They said:

“We will not give you time unless ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās guarantees you.”

They went to his door and asked permission to enter. He gave them permission, while his hand was in a trough, stirring the seed for the sheep. The money-changer said to him: “These people are owed nine thousand dinars by me. I asked them to give me time so I could try to arrange matters for them, and they asked me for a guarantor. I offered them one, but they refused to be satisfied with anyone but you. I ask you to guarantee me.” He said to them, “Bring me your notes.” They handed them over to him; he tore them up and ordered that their debts be paid from his own property.

And ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar ibn Abī Ṭālib. Among the accounts told of his generosity ﷺ is that a client of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muṭīʿ ibn al-Aswad al-ʿAdawī came to him and said: “We hear things about ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar such as have never been heard of anyone else.” ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muṭīʿ said to him:

“It is true. Everything you hear about him is less than the truth.”

He said, “I would like to see some of it.” “Bring me a sheet,” he replied. The man brought one, and he said: “Write: ‘A debt owed by so-and-so to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar, three hundred dinars, payable at once.’ Then go to him, greet him, and say to him: ‘This is a debt owed to me by you, Abū Jaʿfar.’” The client went to him and did as instructed, showing him the sheet. He ﷺ asked: “Is it yours?” “Yes,” he replied. “How much?” “Three hundred dinars.” He said: “Boy! Pay it to him.” He did not take the sheet. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muṭīʿ’s client brought him the dinars and told him what had happened. “By God,” he said, “I have never seen anything more astonishing than this.” Ibn Muṭīʿ said to him: “Keep the dinars.” He left him for ten days, then said: “Go to him and say the same thing.” The client said to him: “May I be your ransom, are you making a fool of me? After the first time, does he not know that I am the same man?” He replied: “Go, as I tell you.” He went, and the conversation between them proceeded as it had before, so ʿAbd Allāh ordered that the money be given to him. The client came to Ibn Muṭīʿ, full of astonishment. Ibn Muṭīʿ said to him: “Keep it.” When a month had passed, Ibn Muṭīʿ said to him: “Go back to him.” When he returned, he said to him what he had said on the two previous occasions, and ʿAbd Allāh ordered that the money be given to him. The client came to Ibn Muṭīʿ, who said to him: “Gather everything you have received and bring it here.” He brought it to him. Ibn Muṭīʿ rode over to ʿAbd Allāh with his client and the money, and said to him: “Abū Jaʿfar, fear God and consider your own welfare and your obligations, for you have an afterlife to face.” He said: “What is that?” Ibn Muṭīʿ replied: “This client of mine came to you with a note stating that you owed him three hundred dinars, although there had been no business dealings between you. All you did was ask him, ‘How much?’ and give it to him, until you had given him nine hundred dinars.” ʿAbd Allāh said: “It seems you are saying I do not know what I own and what I owe.” “That is indeed so,” he replied. ʿAbd Allāh said: “I know every dirham I have, and I knew that I did not owe him that. But I gave myself the choice: I could say, ‘No, it is not yours,’ and he could say, ‘It is mine.’ Someone might hear about it, and I would be caught between one who believed me and one who did not. So giving it to him was easier for me.” Ibn Muṭīʿ said: “Fear God and consider your own welfare. Boy! Bring what you have with you.” He brought the money. Ibn Jaʿfar, God bless them both, said: “What is this?” “This is your money,” he replied. “May God forgive you! Should anything that has left me return to me? It is yours, lawful and wholesome.”

He said: An old woman came to Ibn Jaʿfar, God bless them both, with a hen she had fattened. She said: “Abū Jaʿfar, I have fattened this hen until it reached its full size, and I wanted you to eat it.” He said: “Take it in. Boy! Give her a thousand dirhams.” She said: “May God preserve you!” He said: “Give her another thousand.” She said: “May God keep you safe!” He said: “Give her another thousand.” She said: “May God let me enjoy your company!” He said: “Give her another thousand.” She said: “May I be your ransom!” He said: “Give her another thousand.” She said:

“That is enough, you extravagant man!” He said: “Had you kept going, I would have kept giving you…”

It is related from Ibn Sīrīn that a landowner asked Ibn Jaʿfar to speak to ʿAlī ﷺ on his behalf about a need. ʿAbd Allāh spoke to him about it, and ʿAlī took care of it. The landowner sent ʿAbd Allāh forty thousand, but he returned them, saying: “We, the people of the House, do not accept payment for our good deeds.”

He said: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar sought a safe-conduct from ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān for ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Qays al-Ruqayyāt, who had praised Ibn al-Zubayr and urged people to oppose ʿAbd al-Malik. When Muṣʿab died, ʿAbd Allāh sought a safe-conduct for him, and ʿAbd al-Malik granted it. Ibn Qays entered before him and asked permission to recite to him. ʿAbd al-Malik gave him permission, and he recited his poem, which includes these lines: (al-kāmil)

Listen, Commander of the Faithful,

to my praise and its commendation.

You are the son of the intertwined ravines,

Kudayy and Kudāʾ.

/ ʿAbd al-Malik said to him: (al-khafīf)

Muṣʿab is but a meteor from God,

from whose face the darkness has lifted.

He said: “Commander of the Faithful, I am the one who says: (al-khafīf)

They found fault with the Umayyads only

because they are forbearing when angered.”

ʿAbd al-Malik said to him: (al-khafīf)

How can I sleep upon my bed while

a fierce raid sweeps across Syria?

“I have granted you safety, but, by God, you will never again receive a stipend with the people.” When he left, Ibn Jaʿfar said to Ibn Qays: “You heard his oath, so do not worry; live out your life.” He said: “Sixty years.” “How much is your stipend?” he asked. “Two thousand,” he replied. Ibn Jaʿfar ordered that he be given one hundred and twenty thousand dirhams. Ibn Qays was then about sixty years old.

He said: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar, God bless them both, came to Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiya. Yazīd said to him: “How much did Commander of the Faithful Muʿāwiya give you when you visited him?” He replied: “One million dirhams.” Yazīd said: “Then you shall have two million dirhams.” He said: “May my father and mother be your ransom!” Yazīd said:

“You said, ‘May my father and mother be your ransom.’” “Yes,” he replied, “and I have said that to no one before you except the Messenger of God ﷺ and I shall say it to no one after you.” Yazīd said: “Then you shall have twice that: four million dirhams.” Someone said to Yazīd: “You gave ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar four million!” He said:

“Woe to you! I gave it to the people. ʿAbd Allāh does not keep a single dirham.” When ʿAbd Allāh left him and took his leave, he saw a black she-camel at his door. Badīḥ said to him: “This is one that the people of Medina would admire.”

He said: “Take it.” The boy refused / to hand it over, so he returned to Yazīd and said: “There is a black she-camel at your door that Badīḥ wanted the people of Medina to admire.” Yazīd said: “Boy! Give it to him, along with every black she-camel you have.” There were seven hundred black she-camels. He wrote to the [governor—] of Adhriʿāt, ordering him to send them all to him as oil. When he could not find enough for all of them, he was given its price. Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik said to Badīḥ: “How many of the seven hundred camels reached Medina?” Badīḥ replied: “Thirty camels.” Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik asked Badīḥ about ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar. He said: “If I spent my whole life describing him, I would only fall short in describing his generosity and liberality.” “Then tell us about him,” Hishām said. Badīḥ replied: “A man from Quraysh came to him and asked him to pay the cost of his mount. ʿAbd Allāh asked: ‘How much?’ He said: ‘Fifty dinars.’ He said: ‘Badīḥ! Bring the purse.’ I brought it, and he said: ‘Count it out for him.’ People did not weigh money then, so I counted, and I trilled and varied my voice. When I reached fifty, I stopped. He said: ‘Continue.’ I continued until I had emptied the purse. He said: ‘If only the purse had remained, and your voice too!’” Hishām said: “How much was in the purse?” “Four hundred dinars,” Badīḥ replied. “Who was the man?” Hishām asked. “I will not tell you,” he said. “Why not?” “I fear you will take it from him.” Hishām said: “You wretch! ʿAbd Allāh gives it to him, and I take it back from him?” Badīḥ replied: “Yes, by God, you would.”

They related that Ibn Jaʿfar was preparing to travel and ordered a man to make preparations for him. The man did so and brought him the account. Ibn Jaʿfar said to him: “What do you need the account for?” The man replied: “So you can read it and know the amount, may God preserve you!” Ibn Jaʿfar said: “I have no need of it. If there is a surplus in your favor, tell us so that we may give it to you; and if we owe you something, tell us so that we may decide what we think proper to do about it.” The man said: “I would like you to read it.” He read it, and the first thing he read was:

“Rope, for fifty dirhams.” He said: “Ropes have become expensive.” The man replied: “It is mottled.” Ibn Jaʿfar said: “If it is mottled, then pay him for it.” To this day it is a saying in Medina. They related that a man among the pilgrims’ caravan lost his camel. He came to Marwān, who was then governor of Medina, and complained to him, but Marwān did nothing. The man went to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar and said: (al-ṭawīl)

Abū Jaʿfar, the pilgrims have departed,

and, as you know, I have no mount for my baggage.

Abū Jaʿfar, the emir has been stingy with his wealth,

while you are master of what lies in your hands.

Abū Jaʿfar, you are of a true and blessed house;

your prayers are a support to the Muslims.

A fine mount, saddled and equipped, had been brought to Ibn Jaʿfar, with a sword-sheath on it. He said: “Take the mount and what is on it, and keep the sword safe, for I bought it for a thousand dinars.” The man said: (al-ṭawīl)

ʿAbd Allāh bestowed on me—may I be his ransom—

a swift, gray-white camel, strong in motion, with long, supple lips,

and a blade of iron, bright as water,

like a meteor appearing while night’s armies are dark.

Among the generous were al-Saffāḥ, who was ʿAbd Allāh the Younger ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās; Muḥammad ibn Jaʿfar ibn ʿUbayd Allāh; Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayya, who slaughtered a number of camels every day and fed people with them, and was much praised; ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿĀmir ibn Kurayz ibn Rabīʿa ibn Ḥabīb ibn ʿAbd Shams, who was one of Quraysh’s young men; and Ḥamza ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām was generous and much praised. Mūsā Shahawāt said of him: (al-ramal)

Ḥamzah, who buys generosity with money,

and sees that he has been cheated in the sale.

Yaʿqūb ibn Ṭalḥah ibn ʿUbayd Allāh al-Taymī, and ʿUmar ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Maʿmar ibn ʿUthmān al-Taymī. There are reports about his generosity, including this one: ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān wanted to humble him. ʿUmar had been ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr’s governor in Baṣrah, where he had displayed such generosity that people spoke of it throughout the lands. When power passed to ʿAbd al-Malik, ʿUmar came to him, and the two rode alongside one another. ʿUmar was on a camel slightly larger than ʿAbd al-Malik’s, so the people looked up at him. This angered ʿAbd al-Malik, who ordered him to go to Medina and remain there, since it was home to the leading men of Quraysh. When the people of Medina heard that he was coming, they went out several miles to meet him. He dismounted and walked, and the people dismounted with him. He continued on foot, with the men walking alongside him, until he entered Medina. Upon entering, he distributed garments among them. On that day, no one entered the Mosque of the Messenger of God ﷺ—for the noon prayer without wearing a garment from ʿUmar. This reached ʿAbd al-Malik, who said:

“I wanted to humble him, but his own nature would have none of it except to rise.”

It is said that they asked ʿUmar, “Will you walk when you have more riding beasts than anyone?” He replied, “I will not ride while a Qurashī walks.” It is also said that what prompted ʿAbd al-Malik to act as he did was that ʿUmar had been steadfast in his work for Ibn al-Zubayr. Ibn al-Zubayr appointed him over Baṣrah, but ʿAbd al-Malik did not approve of how firmly he held it. He said to him, “You are a keenly whetted sword for Ibn al-Zubayr, but a blunt blade for me. By God, I shall send you to a town where your stature will dwindle.” So he sent him to Medina, and what I have recounted came to pass.

Abū Ḥazābah al-Tamīmī had a slave girl called Basbāsah, whom he loved. He was compelled to sell her, and ʿUmar ibn ʿUbayd Allāh bought her from him for a great sum. When ʿUmar handed over the money and took possession of her, she went to enter and he caught hold of her garment. Then he said: (al-ṭawīl)

“Do you remember, Basbāsah, a need of yours today,

which came in anguish from the need of one who remembers?

Were the passage of time not keeping me from you,

nothing would part us but death—so forgive me.

I bear the grief of your painful separation,

with which I commune with a heart long given to thought.

Peace be upon you; there will be no visit between us,

and no reunion unless Ibn Maʿmar so wills.”

He said, “We have willed it. She is yours, and so is her price.”

Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Asīd ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah was the most generous man in Syria, a man of distinction and much praised.

Ṭalḥah “Generosity,” son of ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAwf ibn ʿAbd ʿAwf ibn ʿAbd ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Zuhrah: a man would come to him asking for something, and Ṭalḥah would seat him; then another would come, and he would seat him; then a third. When their number matched the number of garments Ṭalḥah was wearing, he would go in and throw his cloak to the first, his shirt to the second, and, when he reached the third, say, “Bring me a garment,” then hand his waist-wrap to him. The Umayyads used to send him on tax-collecting missions among Asad and Ghaṭafān. He would return with great sums of money, then he would make the payment from it seem acceptable to them and would lavish favors, grant gifts, give, and distribute. One day, a Bedouin who had recently suffered an illness and been battered by hardship was told, “Why don’t you approach this Qurashī? He may do you some good.” The man approached him wearing a red garment. When he came up to him, he said, “Help me against hardship.” “Yes,” Ṭalḥah replied. Then he turned to his steward and asked, “How much do you have with you?” The steward said, “A surplus of money.” Ṭalḥah said, “Pour it into his garment.” The steward poured it in until the weight forced the Bedouin to sit down. Ṭalḥah looked at him for a long while, then began to weep. The man asked, “Why are you crying? Did you think what you gave me too little?” He replied, “No. But I thought about what the earth will consume of your generosity, and I wept.”

Muṣʿab ibn ʿAbd Allāh related that Ṭalḥah’s wife said to him:

“I have never seen friends like yours. When you are well-off, they are in your house and at your doorstep; but when time turns against you, they shun you.”

He replied, “I have only praised them: when I am able to bear their needs, they are at ease and behave well; when I am unable to help them, they make things easier and excuse me.”

Ṭalḥah ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Abī Bakr al-Ṣiddīq ﵃—was known as Ṭalḥah “of the Dirhams.” He was exceedingly generous. Al-Ḥazīn al-Kinānī said of him: (al-mutaqārib)

“If, Ṭalḥ, you have given me a swift she-camel

that makes the sands seem light,

your benefit to me has come not once

nor twice, but time and again.

Your father was the one who affirmed the Chosen One,

and journeyed with the Chosen One wherever he journeyed.

Your mother is a fair Taimī woman,

and, when people are counted, she is pure gold.”

Ṭalḥah “the Good”: Ṭalḥah ibn al-Ḥasan ibn ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵉. His mother was Umm Isḥāq, daughter of Ṭalḥah ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿUthmān. He was a generous provider of food and left no descendants.

Al-Azraq was ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd Shams ibn al-Mughīrah ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm. Ibn al-Zubayr appointed him governor of Yemen, where he gave away great sums of money, until he was dismissed. Abū Duḥbul al-Jumaḥī said of him: (al-basīṭ)

“He gave both as governor and after being dismissed; yet his noble qualities were not dismissed from him: they still enveloped him, and he did not cast them off.”

Abū Duḥbul also said of him: (al-kāmil)

“May women be barren, that they bear none like him—

women are barren of his like.

Gentle of speech from modesty, as though

he were afflicted, though his body is free of illness.”

“Cheerful in prosperity, yet never turning away—

to him abundance and want are alike.

Indeed, houses are mines; his stock is

gold, and all his forebears are mighty.”

Al-Ḥakam ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn Ḥanṭab ibn ʿUbayd al-Makhzūmī was generous and much praised.

Al-Mughīrah “the One-Eyed,” son of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah al-Makhzūmī, outdid the generous men of Kūfah in feeding people, until they left the field to him alone. In Kūfah at the time, those who provided food included ʿĪsā ibn Mūsā ibn Ṭalḥah al-Taymī, ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Bishr ibn Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam, Khālid ibn Khālid ibn al-Walīd ibn ʿUqbah ibn Abī Muʿayṭ, and the sons of ʿUmārah ibn ʿUqbah ibn Abī Muʿayṭ. Al-Aʿwar outdid them all. He spread out leather dining-cloths in Kūfah and put ḥays on them; the food was eaten by those mounted, standing, or seated. Everyone else who had been feeding people in Kūfah stopped. Al-Aqayshir al-Asadī said of him: (al-ṭawīl)

“The sea has come upon you, overflowing against Quraysh—

O Mughīrah! For Ibn Bishr has been alarmed.

And the young man has been alarmed—the young man of Taym—when

he saw that what he gave freely was no small amount.

And the vengeance I sought against ʿUqbah’s sons has healed me,

as have the Ḥāṭibī clan and the clan of Ṣakhr.

Do not let their fine attire deceive you,

nor a saddle in Byzantine brocade and a tiger-striped cloak.”

By “the Ḥāṭibī,” he meant Muḥammad ibn [al-Ḥāṭib ibn] al-Ḥārith ibn Maʿmar ibn Ḥabīb al-Jumaḥī. He was a generous provider of food. By “Ṣakhr,” he meant Ṣukhayr ibn Abī al-Jahm al-ʿAdawī, who was a generous provider of food. Among the generous men was Nahshal ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Wahb al-Fihrī, who was a generous provider of food.

Arbiters in Quraysh’s boasts and contests for precedence

Ibn al-Kalbī said: “There were four men among Quraysh to whom people resorted for judgments because of their sound judgment, and who arbitrated between people in contests of boasting. All had lived into the Islamic period. They were ʿAqīl ibn Abī Ṭālib ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib; Makhzamah ibn Nawfal ibn Uhayb ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhrah; Ḥuwayṭib ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Abī Qays ibn Naṣr ibn Mālik ibn Ḥisl ibn ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy; and Abū al-Jahm ibn Ḥudhayfah ibn Ghānim al-ʿAdawī. The one they disliked most was ʿAqīl ibn Abī Ṭālib. The other three would count the merits of the two men who came before them in a contest of boasting, and give precedence to whichever had more merits. ʿAqīl, however, would count their faults, and put back whichever had more faults. The two men would say, ‘We wish we had not gone to him: he has brought to light our faults that had been hidden from people.’”

Those who harmed the Messenger of God ﷺ

Abū Lahab, ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib; al-Ḥakam, the exile, ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah; ʿUqbah ibn Abī Muʿayṭ ibn Abī ʿAmr ibn Umayyah; and al-Naḍr ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Kaladah of Banū ʿAbd al-Dār.

Those among Quraysh who mocked the Prophet and died unbelievers, each by a different death

Al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil ibn Hāshim al-Sahmī, and al-Ḥārith ibn Qays ibn ʿAdī al-Sahmī, who was a devotee of idols: whenever he passed a stone more attractive than the one he had, he took it and threw away the one he had. It was concerning him that this was revealed: “Have you seen the one who takes his own desire as his god?” (Qurʾān 45:23). Al-Aswad ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā; al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm; and al-Aswad ibn ʿAbd Yaghūth ibn Wahb ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhrah.

As for the cause of their deaths: al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil went out on a rainy day on his mount with two of his sons, to enjoy himself and have a meal. He dismounted in one of those ravines. When he set his foot on the ground, he cried out. They searched around but saw nothing. His leg swelled until it became like a camel’s neck, and he died from a bite in the earth. As for al-Ḥārith ibn Qays, he ate salted fish and was seized by thirst. He kept drinking water until he burst and died, saying, “The Lord of Muḥammad has killed me.” As for al-Aswad ibn al-Muṭṭalib, he had a dutiful son called Zamʿah, whose business took him to Syria. Whenever Zamʿah left his father on a journey, he would say, “I shall travel for such-and-such a time, and reach the town on such-and-such a day.” Then he would set out on that day, and nothing he said would fail to come to pass. The Messenger of God ﷺ—had prayed against al-Aswad that his eyesight be taken and that he lose his son. On the day Zamʿah had promised to return, al-Aswad went out with a servant boy. Gabriel ﵇—came to him as he sat in the shade of a tree and began striking his head and forehead with a green leaf. His sight was taken, and Gabriel struck his face with thorns. His servant cried out for help, but al-Aswad said, “I see no one doing anything to you except yourself.” God took his sight and caused him to lose his son.

As for al-Walīd, he passed a man from Khuzāʿah who had arrowheads that he had feathered. An arrow caught on him. The story of al-Walīd and his death has already been mentioned in the book.

As for al-Aswad ibn ʿAbd Yaghūth, he left his family and was struck by the poisonous wind, whereupon he turned black. He came to his family, but they did not recognize him and shut the door against him. He died saying, “The Lord of Muḥammad has killed me.” Ibrāhīm ibn Saʿd related that Gabriel ﵇ came to the Messenger of God ﷺ—while he was circumambulating the House. Al-Aswad ibn al-Muṭṭalib passed by, and Gabriel struck his face with a green leaf, blinding him. Then al-Aswad ibn ʿAbd Yaghūth al-Zuhrī passed by, and Gabriel pointed to his belly. He developed dropsy and died of it; al-ḥaban means dropsy. Al-Walīd passed by, and Gabriel pointed to the trace of a wound on the bottom of his heel, which he had received years earlier while driving his camels. He passed a man from Khuzāʿah, and an arrow from the man’s quiver caught on his garment and scratched him—a slight scratch, nothing serious. When Gabriel ﵇ pointed to him, that scratch reopened and killed him. Al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil passed by, and Gabriel pointed to the hollow of his foot. He rode out on a donkey, intending for al-Ṭāʾif. His donkey knelt on a shabriqah, and a thorn from it entered the hollow of his foot and killed him.

Quraysh’s Heretics

Ṣakhr ibn Ḥarb became Muslim. ʿUqbah ibn Abī Muʿayṭ was beheaded by the Messenger of God ﷺ Ṣakhr ibn Ḥarb became Muslim. ʿUqbah ibn Abī Muʿayṭ was beheaded by the Messenger of God ﷺ—as a captive on his return from Badr at al-Ṣafrāʾ. Ubayy ibn Khalaf was killed by the Messenger of God ﷺ—with his own hand on the day of Uḥud: he stabbed him with a spear. He did not kill anyone with his own hand ﷺ except this Ubayy. Abū ʿAzzah was also beheaded by his own hand ﷺ on the day of Uḥud. The Prophet had taken him captive at Badr. Abū ʿAzzah complained to him of his dependents and poverty, so the Prophet ﷺ took pity on him, released him, and took his pledge that he would not go out against him. But he went out on the day of Uḥud, urging people to fight the Messenger of God. The Messenger of God ﷺ—beheaded him with his own hand. Al-Naḍr ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Kildah, of the Banū ʿAbd al-Dār, was also killed by the Messenger of God as a captive; he had been hostile to him. Nubayh and Munabbih, the two sons of al-Ḥajjāj ibn ʿĀmir al-Sahmiyyān, were killed at Badr. Al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil al-Sahmī and al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah al-Makhzūmī learned heresy from the Christians of al-Ḥīrah.

Those of Quraysh Who Fed the Army during the Battle of [Badr]

Abū Jahl, whose name was ʿAmr ibn Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah, slaughtered ten camels on the first day. Then Umayyah ibn Khalaf slaughtered nine. Then Suhayl ibn ʿAmr, of the Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy, slaughtered ten. Then Shaybah ibn Rabīʿah slaughtered ten. Then Munabbih and Nubayh, the two sons of al-Ḥajjāj, slaughtered ten. Then Abū al-Bukhtarī al-ʿĀṣ ibn Hishām ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Asad slaughtered ten. Then al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, who had been taken out to Badr against his will, slaughtered ten. Muḥammad ibn ʿUmar related that Quraysh did not provide al-ʿAbbās with food because they knew of his affection and inclination toward the Messenger of God, and that he had been taken out unwillingly.

The Fools of Quraysh, Their Stories, and Those among Them Who Had or Had No Children

ʿAbd al-Dār ibn Quṣayy had children; Kurayz ibn Rabīʿah ibn Ḥabīb ibn ʿAbd Shams had none. Kurayz’s father, Rabīʿah, had been killed by the Banū Jusham ibn Muʿāwiyah ibn Bakr of Hawāzin. He was killed by Ṣarīḥ ibn Naḍlah ibn Ṭarīf ibn Kulafah ibn al-Aḥmar, of the Banū ʿIṣmah. Kurayz would climb Abū Qubays and shoot an arrow into the air, having bound a headband around himself. His son ʿĀmir ibn Kurayz ibn Rabīʿah had children. ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān ﵁ appointed ʿĀmir’s son ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿĀmir governor of Baṣrah. ʿĀmir asked ʿUthmān’s permission to visit his son, and ʿUthmān gave it to him. He set out to see him. When ʿAbd Allāh mounted the pulpit—he was an orator—ʿĀmir began talking about himself and kept saying to those beside him, “Do you see this governor of yours? Did he come out of this one?” ʿAbd Allāh did not let him remain, but treated him well, provided for his journey, and sent him back to Medina for fear of disgrace. Al-ʿĀṣ ibn Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah had children; he was killed on the Day of al-Fijār. Al-ʿĀṣ ibn Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah had children; he was killed as an unbeliever at Badr. He had gambled with Abū Lahab and won all his property and his person, making him a qayn. When Quraysh set out to protect their caravan from the Messenger of God ﷺ—they took the Banū Hāshim out against their will. Whoever did not go out was replaced by another man, whom they sent in his place. Abū Lahab sent al-ʿĀṣ as his substitute, and he was killed as an unbeliever at Badr. Al-ʿĀṣ ibn Saʿīd and al-ʿĀṣ ibn Hishām were both called the fools of Quraysh. Suhayl ibn ʿAmr, of the Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy, had children. Muḥammad ibn Ḥāṭib ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Muʿammar ibn Ḥabīb al-Jumaḥī: Muʿāwiyah ibn Abī— Sufyān divorced Maysūn bt. Baḥdal al-Kalbiyyah, the mother of his son Yazīd. Muḥammad ibn Ḥāṭib came to him, and Muʿāwiyah said, “What do you need, Ibn Ḥāṭib?” He replied, “I have come to ask for a woman’s hand.” Muʿāwiyah said, “Whom have you named?” He replied, “Maysūn bt. Baḥdal al-Kalbiyyah, the mother of Yazīd.” Muʿāwiyah fell silent. He said, “What does the Commander of the Faithful say about this?” Muʿāwiyah said, “I say that you are a donkey.” He left him and kept repeating, “He said, ‘You are a donkey’; he said, ‘You are a donkey,’” until he reached his home.

ʿAmr ibn Ḥurayth al-Makhzūmī had no children. ʿUtbah ibn Abī Sufyān had no children. Muʿāwiyah appointed him governor of Egypt, and he would go out to the Nile with the notables of his administration to show them how he could swim while bound. ʿAmr ibn Suhayl ibn ʿAmr had no children. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muʿāwiyah left no descendants.

Muʿāwiyah ibn Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam had children. He said: “While this Muʿāwiyah was waiting for ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān in Damascus, at the door of a miller whose donkey was turning the millstone with a bell around its neck, he said to the miller, ‘Why have you put this bell around your donkey’s neck?’ The miller replied, ‘Sometimes I am distracted and neglect it. If I do not hear the bell, I know it has stopped, so I call to it.’ Muʿāwiyah said, ‘What if it stopped and then moved its head like this and like this?’—and he moved his head—‘How would you know?’ The miller said, ‘How could a donkey have the same intelligence as the prince?’”

Khālid ibn Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiyah used to mock this Muʿāwiyah ibn Marwān. One day he said to him, “The Commander of the Faithful has appointed his brothers by his father: he appointed ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz to Egypt, Bishr to Iraq, and Muḥammad to al-Jazīrah. If only you would ask him to appoint you!” Muʿāwiyah asked, “What should I ask him for?” Khālid said, “Ask him for Bayt Lāhyā, a village near Damascus.” Muʿāwiyah went in to see him and said, “Commander of the Faithful, am I not your mother’s son?” “Indeed,” he replied, “and the dearest of people to me.” Muʿāwiyah said, “You appointed your brothers but did not appoint me.” “Ask, Abū al-Mughīrah,” said ʿAbd al-Malik, “for whatever you wish.” Muʿāwiyah said, “The estate of Lāhyā.” ʿAbd al-Malik asked, “When did you see Khālid?” “Yesterday,” he replied. ʿAbd al-Malik said, “Then do not speak to him.” Then Khālid came in just after this exchange and said, “How are you this morning, Abū al-Mughīrah?” Muʿāwiyah said, “He has forbidden us to speak to you.”

Al-Khayrah bt. Anīf ibn Zabān al-Kalbī was married to this Muʿāwiyah. When he consummated the marriage and the next morning came, ʿAbd al-Malik visited him to congratulate him, accompanied by Anīf, her father. ʿAbd al-Malik said to him, “How did you find your wife?” He replied, “She afflicted us with her blood last night.” Her father Anīf said, “She is one of those women who keep that hidden from their husbands. May God and His angels curse whoever deceived me about you!”

The Kalb called Abū Bakr ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān “the Yellow Fool” because of his stupidity. Bakkār ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān, who was Abū Bakr, had no children. Al-Sukkarī said: “I think he meant this Muʿāwiyah ibn Marwān; that is how he had informed us.” He said: “ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān used to forbid Bakkār from keeping company with Khālid ibn Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiyah, because he knew how foolish Bakkār was. One day he sat with him, and Khālid said, ‘By God, this is the man passed around among Quraysh: his mother is so-and-so, her mother is so-and-so, and his wife is so-and-so.’ Bakkār replied, ‘By God, I am just as the poet said:’ [basīṭ]

A man passed around among the baseborn, passed around and around.

When this reached ʿAbd al-Malik, he became angry and said, “Did I not forbid you from keeping company with Khālid?”

Bakkār’s falcon flew away, so he sent word to the keeper of the gate of Damascus: “Close the city gate, for a falcon has flown away; it must not get out.”

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Qays ibn Makhramah ibn al-Muṭṭalib: the Banū al-Muṭṭalib used to be called the fools. ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz appointed this ʿAbd Allāh governor of Mecca. ʿAbd Allāh wrote to ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, beginning with himself: “From ʿAbd Allāh ibn Qays to ʿUmar, Commander of the Faithful.” He was told, “Woe to you! You put yourself before the Commander of the Faithful.” He replied, “We have the seniority over them.” When the letter and what he had said reached ʿUmar, he said, “By God, he is more foolish than the rest of his foolish family.”

Al-Aḥwaṣ ibn Jaʿfar ibn ʿAmr ibn Ḥurayth had no children. He married a woman from Quraysh, and a dispute arose between him and her brothers concerning her. She appointed one of her brothers to litigate against him. He came before Ibn Abī Laylā, the judge, and the case was discussed in the judge’s presence. Al-Aḥwaṣ said, “May God set you right! By God, her testicles are in my hand; let them do whatever they like.” Her brothers said, “By God, we will never litigate against you again.”

This al-Aḥwaṣ used to keep company with Ḥamzah ibn Bayḍ, Jamīl ibn Ḥumrān, Mālik ibn ʿUyaynah ibn Asmāʾ ibn Khārijah, and al-Mughīrah ibn Aʿshā ibn Abī Rabīʿah. One of them said, “Come, let us have a laugh at al-Aḥwaṣ.” He came to them the next morning. Ibn Bayḍ asked, “Are you feeling unwell?” He replied, “No, by God.” He said:

“Then why is your face yellow?” He then met Jamīl and said the same to him, then met Mālik and said the same to him, then met al-Mughīrah and said the same to him. Al-Aḥwaṣ returned to his home and said, “O sons of misfortune! I am ill, and you have not told me! Throw some clothes over me, for I am sick, and send for the doctor to treat me.” He feigned illness, and his companions visited him. He did not speak. His family said, “You told us he was ill; by God, he is indeed as sick as he feels.”

Then Sharāʿah ibn ʿUbayd ibn al-Zandabūdh al-Fārisī arrived. He had Persian impudence and was a client of the Banū Taym Allāh ibn Thaʿlabah. He was the most charming man in Kūfah. He asked permission to visit. His family said, “If he does not speak when he sees Sharāʿah, he is surely dying.” Sharāʿah had a companion with him. He spoke to al-Aḥwaṣ, but al-Aḥwaṣ did not answer. Sharāʿah felt his pulse but found nothing wrong, and saw no sign of illness on his face. He looked at his companion and said, “Yesterday we were in al-Ḥīrah and got wine: thirty bottles for a dirham. Wine then cost three bottles to a dirham.” Al-Aḥwaṣ raised his head and said, “The liar—may his mother’s privates be burned!—would believe that,” and sat upright. His family showered Sharāʿah with sugar. Sharāʿah said, “Sit down—you’ll never sit up or prosper—and bring your drink.” It was brought, and the two of them drank the whole day.

Names of Qurayshites Who Were Flogged

The Messenger of God ﷺ—flogged Misṭaḥ ibn Uthāthah ibn ʿAbbād ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn ʿAbd Manāf, who was Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq’s maternal cousin ﵁ for accusing ʿĀʾishah ﵂ of the lie. ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵁ flogged Sulayṭ ibn ʿAmr ibn— ʿAbd Shams ibn ʿAbd Wadd, of the Banū Sāmah ibn Luʾayy, for drinking wine, after a group of people testified that he had drunk it. ʿUmar also flogged ʿIkrimah ibn ʿĀmir ibn Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn ʿAbd al-Dār, who had slandered Wahb ibn Rabīʿah ibn al-Aswad. ʿUmar also flogged Rabīʿah ibn Umayyah ibn Khalaf al-Jumaḥī for drinking wine. He was a libertine and a rogue; he became angry and fled to the Byzantines, converted to Christianity, and died there a Christian.

A Muslim who had taken part in a raid against the Byzantines met him and recognized him. He said, “Woe to you, Rabīʿah! Have you become a Christian, and turned foreign after having been an Arab, and exchanged the Gospel for the Qurʾān?” He replied, “Yes.” The man said, “What remains of the Qurʾān in your breast?” He answered, “One verse: ‘Perhaps those who disbelieved will wish that they had been Muslims’” (15:2). The man said, “Woe to you! By God, you are ruined.”

ʿUmar also flogged his son Abū Shaḥmah, son of ʿUmar, who had committed fornication with ʿUmar’s stepdaughter. He flogged him as a legal punishment. As he flogged him, Abū Shaḥmah said, “Father, you have killed me.” ʿUmar replied, “My son, when you meet your Lord, tell Him that your father upholds the prescribed punishments.”

ʿUmar also flogged his son ʿUbayd Allāh, who was killed at Ṣiffīn, for drinking wine. Afterward ʿUbayd Allāh swore that he would eat neither grapes nor anything made from grapes, nor dates nor anything made from dates. ʿUmar also flogged Qudāmah ibn Maẓʿūn al-Jumaḥī for drinking wine. Al-Jārūd al-ʿAbdī testified that he had drunk it, and ʿAlqamah ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Khaṣī al-Tamīmī testified that he had vomited it up.

ʿUmar also flogged Abū Jandal ibn Suhayl ibn ʿAmr, of the Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy, for drinking wine. ʿUmar also flogged Makhramah ibn Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhrah for a slander that he had made against a man from Quraysh; evidence was established against him for it before ʿUmar. ʿUmar also flogged Abū al-Jahm ibn Ḥudhayfah ibn Ghānim al-ʿAdawī for the same kind of offense. ʿUmar also flogged al-Nuʿmān [ibn— ʿAdī— ibn Naḍlah ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn [Ḥarthān ibn—] ʿAwf ibn ʿUbayd ibn ʿUwayj ibn ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb. ʿUmar appointed him governor of Maysān, where he fell in love with a Persian woman. He is the one who said: (Ṭawīl)

Has the fair one heard that her husband,At Maysān, is served in glass and ḥantam?

If you are my drinking companion, pour for me from the larger [vessel];Do not pour for me from the smaller, chipped one.

Whenever you wish, the landowners of a village sing to me ...And a cymbal-player dances upon every track.

Perhaps the Commander of the Faithful will be displeased by it,That we should drink together in the ruined palace.

When ʿUmar heard what he had said, he replied, “Yes, by God! It surely displeases me, and displeases my Lord, by God; and I shall punish you too.” ʿUmar also punished a man for making a false accusation against another man. Abū ʿUbaydah ibn al-Jarrāḥ, while governor of Syria, also punished Abū Jandal ibn Suhayl ibn ʿAmr, one of the Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy, for wine; Jandal was notoriously addicted to wine. Abū ʿUbaydah also punished Ḍirār ibn al-Khaṭṭāb al-Fihrī. ʿUmar also punished al-Ṣalt ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wābiṣah ibn Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm, for wine. He took offense and became angry, joined the Byzantines, converted to Christianity, and died there as a Christian. He had descendants among the Byzantines.

ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān ﵁ punished ʿĀṣim ibn ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb for wine. This was because al-Ḥusayn ibn ʿAlī ﵄ brought a charge against him and testified against him before ʿUthmān. This was the first enmity to arise between the houses of ʿUmar and ʿAlī ﵄. ʿUthmān also punished Hāshim ibn ʿUtbah ibn Abī Waqqāṣ for wine, on the testimony of a group of people from Kūfah. ʿUthmān also punished al-Musayyib ibn Ḥazn ibn Abī Wahb al-Makhzūmī for wine; he was the father of the jurist Saʿīd ibn al-Musayyib.

Muʿāwiyah ibn Abī Sufyān appointed ʿAbd Allāh ibn Khālid ibn Asīd ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ governor of al-Ṭāʾif. ʿAnbasah ibn Abī Sufyān was brought before him drunk on wine, so he punished him. Muʿāwiyah became angry at this and dismissed him. Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah, Muʿāwiyah’s governor in Medina, punished ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Ḥakam ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ for wine. Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam, while governor under Muʿāwiyah, punished his brother ʿAbd al-Raḥmān for his slander of the Anṣār, by order of Muʿāwiyah. Marwān, also while governor of Medina, punished Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Abī Bakr al-Ṣiddīq after he was brought before him drunk on wine. He sent to ʿĀʾishah to consult her, and she sent back this reply: “This is God’s prescribed punishment; you must deal with him.” So he punished him.

Marwān also punished Suhayl ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf for wine. Marwān also punished Ibn Abī ʿAtīq, whose name was ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Abī Bakr, for wine. Abū Qatādah ibn Ribʿī al-Anṣārī met him after he had been beaten and said, “Nephew, what did they beat you with?”

“Nothing at all, by God, ʿAmr! It was a tawny wine from Dārūm, or a Babylonian wine, or wine from Balās, a place where wine is made.” Abū Qatādah said, “Then I see that they did not wrong you.”

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Khālid ibn Asīd punished ʿUmar ibn Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ. ʿUmar became angry and went to Muʿāwiyah, complaining to him about ʿAbd Allāh ibn Khālid and what he had done to him. He told him that ʿAbd Allāh had wronged him, asked Muʿāwiyah to exact retribution from him and to secure his right for him. Muʿāwiyah said, “Nephew, by God, you found him staunch in his loyalty to the Banū ʿAbd Shams.” ʿUmar replied, “Commander of the Faithful, by God, you started this when he beat your brother ʿAnbasah at al-Ṭāʾif and then you did not exact retribution from him.”

Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam punished al-Miswar ibn Makhzamah ibn Nawfal [ibn Uhayb—] ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhrah for slandering Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiyah while Yazīd was caliph. Yazīd wrote to Marwān ordering him to inflict the prescribed punishment on al-Miswar, saying, “Punish him as his father was punished.” Abū Ḥurrah al-Ḍamrī said of this: (Ṭawīl)

Does Abū Khālid drink it pure, breaking its seal,While al-Miswar is made to suffer the prescribed punishment?

ʿAmr ibn Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ punished ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān for wine. [At this,] Yaḥyā ibn al-Ḥakam ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ said: (Ṭawīl)

I wished—by the Lord of the House—that I could ransom him,ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, while he was being flogged for wine.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr punished Khālid ibn al-Muhājir ibn al-Walīd al-Makhzūmī when Khālid was pledged allegiance to, for wine found in his possession. ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān punished Hāshim ibn al-Miswar ibn Makhzamah, who had slandered a man from Quraysh in Medina. ʿAbd al-Malik’s governor in Medina wrote to inform him of this, and ʿAbd al-Malik wrote back, “Punish him as his father and grandfather were punished before him.”

ʿAbd al-Malik also punished Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Ḥakam. Yaḥyā’s governor in Medina wrote to seek his permission, and ʿAbd al-Malik wrote back, “Punish him; he is a reprobate, the son of a man who was punished.” So he punished him. Abū Bakr ibn ʿAmr ibn Ḥazm al-Anṣārī, ʿAbd al-Malik’s governor in Medina, punished Hishām ibn ʿUrwah ibn al-Zubayr for making a false accusation against a man from the Banū Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Ḍaḥḥāk ibn Qays al-Fihrī, al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik’s governor in Medina, punished Hishām ibn ʿUrwah ibn al-Zubayr for slandering a man from the Banū al-Mughīrah ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm.

Ibrāhīm ibn Hishām, while governor of Medina, flogged Muṣʿab ibn ʿUrwah ibn al-Zubayr with the prescribed punishment for wine. He also punished Ḥamzah ibn Muṣʿab ibn al-Zubayr for wine, and also ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUrwah ibn al-Zubayr for wine. ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz punished Yaʿqūb ibn Salamah ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah, who had slandered his brother Ayyūb ibn Salamah.

Ibrāhīm ibn Hishām—or Muḥammad ibn Hishām—who was Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik’s governor in Medina, punished Ismāʿīl ibn ʿUthmān ibn al-Arqam, then al-Makhzūmī, for wine. ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz punished Isḥāq ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar ibn Abī Ṭālib for wine. Isḥāq said to ʿUmar, “ʿUmar, I wish everyone had been flogged,” by which he meant his father, ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, since he had been punished for wine.

And ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān ... through Marwān, when he was Muʿāwiyah’s governor ...

. ..

Liars among Quraysh

. .... ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAnbasah ibn Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah; Ayyūb ibn Salamah ibn al-Walīd al-Makhzūmī; Ibrāhīm ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muṭīʿ ibn al-Aswad al-ʿAdawī; and ʿĀṣim ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿĀṣim ibn ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb. It was said that the Antichrist would not appear while even one of these men was alive, because they were impostors, and the Antichrist is the liar.

Sons of Abyssinian women among Quraysh

Naḍlah ibn Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Quṣayy, whose mother was Ṣahāl; Nufayl ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā al-ʿAdawī, whose mother was also Ṣahāl; ʿAmr ibn Rabīʿah ibn Ḥabīb, of the Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy, whose mother was also that Ṣahāl; al-Khaṭṭāb ibn Nufayl al-ʿAdawī, whose mother was Ḥayyah; al-Ḥārith ibn [ʿAbd Allāh ibn—] Abī Rabīʿah al-Makhzūmī, whose mother was Sabḥāʾ; ʿUthmān ibn al-Ḥuwayrith ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā; and Ṣafwān ibn Umayyah ibn Khalaf al-Jumaḥī; Hishām ibn ʿUqbah ibn Abī Muʿayṭ; Mālik ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿUthmān al-Umawī; ʿUmayr ibn Judʿān al-Taymī; al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵄; Aḥmad ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Abī Marwān ibn Abī ʿAffān, a descendant of ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān ﵁; Aḥmad ibn Muḥammad ibn Ṣāliḥ al-Makhzūmī and al-Arqamī, whose name is not known; al-ʿAbbās ibn al-Muʿtaṣim; Hibah Allāh ibn Ibrāhīm ibn al-Mahdī; Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Isḥāq ibn al-Mahdī, nicknamed Naffāṭah; and al-ʿAbbās ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Wahhāb ibn Ibrāhīm ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib.

Sons of Sindhi women

Hishām said: Muḥammad ibn ʿAlī ibn al-Ḥanafiyyah ﵄. Kharrāsh ibn Ismāʿīl al-ʿAjalī claimed that she was from the Banū Ḥanīfah, who were living among the Banū Asad. During the caliphate of Abū Bakr ﵁ a group of Arabs raided them and took Khawlah. They brought her to Medina, where Usāmah ibn Zayd bought her; then ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵁ bought her, and ʿAlī ﵁ had a child by her. They say that her kinsmen came and said, “This woman is one of us, so give her the marriage payment customary for our women.” Then he married her, and she bore him only Muḥammad.

Also ʿAlī ibn al-Ḥusayn ibn ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵃; Saʿīd ibn Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān; Zayd ibn ʿAlī ibn al-Ḥusayn ibn ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵃; and Isḥāq ibn al-Mahdī—namely, Muḥammad, the Commander of the Faithful—whose mother was Makhzamah al-Udhn, called Sukkar.

Sons of Nabatian women among Quraysh

Muslim ibn ʿAqīl ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵄ whose mother was Khalīlah of the family of Fihriyadhī; ʿUmar ibn ʿUmārah ibn ʿUqbah ibn Abī Muʿayṭ; Ziyād, son of his father, whose mother was a Nabataean woman from Kaskar; ʿAqīl ibn Jaʿdah ibn Hubayrah al-Makhzūmī, whose mother was a Nabataean woman from the people of Sūrā—her brother Sammāk was in Kūfah; and Salamah [ibn Hishām] ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Hishām, whose mother was a Nabataean woman from Dūmat al-Jandal.

Sons of Jewish women among Quraysh

Ṣayfī and Abū Ṣayfī, the sons of Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf; and Makhzamah ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn ʿAbd Manāf: their mother was the same woman, from Khaybar. Also Qays ibn Makhzamah ibn al-Muṭṭalib and Musāfiʿ ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn ʿUmayr ibn [Uhayb—] al-Jumaḥī: their mother was the same woman, from Khaybar. Abū ʿAzzah al-Jumaḥī, the poet—he was ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd Allāh; al-Khayyār ibn ʿAdī ibn Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf; and al-Ḥuṣayn ibn Sufyān ibn Umayyah ibn ʿAbd Shams: their mother was one woman called al-Rabāb, from Yathrib, and her mother was a noble Jewish woman. Also ʿĀṣim ibn al-Walīd ibn ʿUtbah ibn Rabīʿah ibn ʿAbd Shams; ʿAmr ibn Qudāmah ibn Maẓʿūn, whose mother was from the Jews of the Anṣār; Tuwayt ibn Ḥabīb ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā, whose mother was from the Jews of the Anṣār; ʿĪsā ibn ʿUmārah ibn ʿUqbah ibn Abī Muʿayṭ, whose mother was a Jewish woman from Dawrān; and Hāshim and ʿĀmir, the sons of ʿUtbah ibn Nawfal al-Zuhrī, whose mother was a Nabataean Jewish woman called Qāmā. She was the grandmother of Ḥammād ibn Yūnus al-Zuhrī.

Sons of Christian women among Quraysh

Al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Rabīʿah al-Makhzūmī, whose mother was a Christian Abyssinian woman. called Sabḥāʾ; ʿUthmān son of ʿAnbasah son of Abū Sufyān son of Ḥarb son of Umayyah; and al-ʿAbbās son of al-Walīd son of ʿAbd al-Malik son of Marwān.

. The kawāsijah [hairless-cheeked men] and thuṭṭ [sparsely bearded men] of Quraysh

ʿAbd Allāh son of Judʿān al-Taymī; ʿAbd Allāh son of al-Zubayr son of al-ʿAwwām; ʿIkrimah son of Abū Jahl son of Hishām; ʿAbd al-Raḥmān son of al-Ḥakam son of Abū al-ʿĀṣ son of Umayyah; Muḥammad son of Sulaymān son of ʿAlī son of ʿAbd Allāh son of al-ʿAbbās son of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib; and al-ʿAbbās son of ʿUbayd Allāh son of al-ʿAbbās son of Muḥammad son of ʿAlī son of ʿAbd Allāh son of al-ʿAbbās son of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib.

. The blind men of Quraysh

Kilāb son of Murrah son of Kaʿb son of Kaʿb son of Luʾayy; Zuhrah son of Kilāb son of Murrah; ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib son of Hāshim son of ʿAbd Manāf son of Quṣayy; al-ʿAbbās son of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib; ʿAbd Allāh son of al-ʿAbbās son of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib; Umayyah son of ʿAbd Shams; Abū Sufyān, who was Ṣakhr son of Ḥarb son of Umayyah son of ʿAbd Shams; al-Ḥakam son of Abū al-ʿĀṣ son of Umayyah; Makhramah son of Nawfal son of ʿAbd Manāf son of Zuhrah; Saʿīd son of Yarbuʿ al-Makhzūmī; al-Fākih son of al-Mughīrah al-Makhzūmī; Abū Quḥāfah, who was ʿAbd Allāh son of ʿUthmān al-Taymī; ʿAmr son of Umm Maktūm—she was his mother—and he was ʿAmr son of Qays son of Zāʾidah son of al-Aṣamm, the brother of Banū ʿĀmir son of Luʾayy; al-Ḥārith son of al-ʿAbbās son of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib; and Muṭʿim son of ʿAdī son of Nawfal son of ʿAbd Manāf; Abū Bakr son of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān son of al-Ḥārith son of Hishām son of al-Mughīrah al-Makhzūmī; Abū al-Jahm son of Ḥudhayfah son of Ghānim al-ʿAdawī; Hārūn son of Sulaymān son of al-Manṣūr, Commander of the Faithful; and Mūsā son of Mūsā al-Hādī, Commander of the Faithful.

. The one-eyed men of Quraysh

Abū Sufyān son of Ḥarb, who later became blind; Umayyah son of ʿAbd Shams, who later became blind; Hāshim son of ʿUtbah son of Abī Waqqāṣ; ʿUtbah son of Abū Sufyān; Saʿīd son of ʿUthmān son of ʿAffān; al-Mughīrah son of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān [son of—] al-Ḥārith son of Hishām al-Makhzūmī; and al-Wāthiq, Hārūn son of Muḥammad son of Hārūn son of Muḥammad son of al-Manṣūr.

. The cross-eyed men of Quraysh

ʿUmar son of al-Khaṭṭāb, al-Fārūq ﵁; Abū Lahab son of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib; Abū Jahl son of Hishām; Ziyād son of his father; Hishām son of ʿAbd al-Malik son of Marwān; Abān son of ʿUthmān son of ʿAffān; Abū Ḥudhayfah son of ʿUtbah son of Rabīʿah son of ʿAbd Shams; ʿAmr son of ʿUtbah son of Abū Sufyān son of Ḥarb, it is said of him; ʿUbayd Allāh son of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān son of Samurah son of Ḥabīb son of ʿAbd Shams; and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān son of ʿAbd Allāh son of Abī Rabīʿah al-Makhzūmī.

The men with protruding upper teeth among Quraysh

ʿAmr son of Saʿīd son of al-ʿĀṣ son of Saʿīd son of al-ʿĀṣ; Yazīd son of ʿAbd al-Malik son of Marwān; Yazīd son of Hishām son of ʿAbd al-Malik; and ʿAmr son of al-Zubayr son of al-ʿAwwām.

. The lame men of Quraysh

ʿAbd Allāh son of Judʿān al-Taymī; Abū Ṭālib son of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib; ʿAbd al-Ḥamīd son of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-ʿAdawī; and Sulaymān son of ʿAbd al-Malik son of Marwān.

. The names of the horses of Quraysh

The Messenger of God ﷺ—had horses, among them al-Ḍarib, Lizāz, al-Sakb, and al-Murta jiz, so named for the beauty of its neighing; al-Sakb was a dark bay, with a white star and white stockings, but with its right foreleg unmarked; Dhū al-Limmah; al-Laḥīf; and the horse of Ḥamzah son of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ﵁—had a horse called al-Ward. Of it Ḥamzah says: (khafīf)

I have nothing but a weapon and a rose-colored steed … a mature horse from the stock of Dhū al-ʿIqāl

With my life I ward off death for its sake … while it, before me, charges into the ranks of the lances

A huge beast, untouched by war;

When its champions blaze, it does not care

A youthful, comely steed, as though it were a bull’s horn;

That, and nothing else, is all my wealth.

/ And when I die, it will be my inheritance,

And fine lambs among my lambs

Jaʿfar son of Abī Ṭālib ﵁—had a chestnut mare called Sabḥah. He was martyred while mounted on her at Muʾtah; he hamstrung her, and she was the first horse hamstrung in Islam. It is said that the Khārijites adopted the practice of hamstringing horses on that account. The first man to tether a horse in God’s cause was Saʿd son of Muʿādh; and the first to ride a horse at a gallop in God’s cause was al-Miqdād, the ally of Banū Zuhrah son of Kilāb. Al-Zubayr son of al-ʿAwwām had a horse called al-Yaʿsūb, a horse on which he was present at Khaybar called Maʿrūf, a horse called Dhū al-Khimār on which he was present on the Day of the Camel, and a horse called Dhāt al-Bighāl. The horse of ʿUbayd Allāh son of ʿUmar son of al-Khaṭṭāb was al-Laṭīm. Al-Miqdād’s horse was called Dhū al-ʿAtq, and he was mounted on it at Badr; he had another horse on which he was present during the raid at Medina, called Baʿzajah. I have included al-Miqdād among Quraysh because the clients of a people belong to them, and their allies belong to them, [as has been related—] from the Messenger of God ﷺ. Abū Jahl’s horse was Maḥāj, and the horse of Ubayy son of Khalaf al-Jumaḥī was al-ʿAwd. He used often to say to the Prophet ﷺ—in Mecca: “Muḥammad, I feed al-ʿAwd a madd every day; I shall kill you on it.” The Prophet ﷺ—would say to him, “Rather, “I shall kill you on it, God willing.” The Prophet killed him with his own hand while he was on al-ʿAwd. / The horse of Musāfiʿ son of ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā, one of Banū ʿĀmir son of Luʾayy, was al-Naʿāmah. Of it he says:

(ṭawīl)

[And—] by God, I shall never forget al-Naʿāmah on that night … nor that day until my wrist is laid beneath me

A horse sleek as the open plain, and swift in foaling … when urged onward, as though it were branded by a cautery

The horse of Muḥriz son of Naḍlah, an ally of Banū ʿAbd Shams, was al-Sarḥān; he rode it during the raid. The horse of ʿUtbah son of Abū Sufyān was al-Fayḍ. He fled on it on the Day of Ṣiffīn, and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān son of al-Ḥakam said: (wāfir)

By your father’s life, ʿAmr, and by the sons who grow in number,

You have fled far away, ʿUtbah.

Was it because you were given a coat of mail and a colt,

Called al-Fayḍ, pouring forth in torrents,

That you abandoned the noble chiefs

When you saw that war had brought forth a foal?

The horse of ʿUbayd Allāh son of ʿUmar son of al-Khaṭṭāb was al-Laṭīm. Of it he said: (ṭawīl)

If my sword is Dhū al-Wishāḥ and my mount

Is al-Laṭīm, then no blood that I seek has been shed in vain.

The horse of ʿUqbah son of Abī Muʿayṭ was Janāḥ. The horse of Khālid son of al-Walīd son of al-Mughīrah was al-ʿAyyār. Muḍarris son of Anas al-Muḥāribī said: (kāmil)

I witnessed the cavalry on the Day of al-Yamāmah,

As the squadrons were led forth by the rider of al-ʿAyyār.

The horse of Ḍirār son of al-Khaṭṭāb al-Fihrī was al-Ḥawāʾ. The horse of Quṭbah son of ʿAbd/ al-ʿUzzā son of ʿAbd Manāf son of Asʿad son of Jābir, brother of Banū Taym son of al-Adram son of Ghālib, was al-Balqāʾ. He was one of Quraysh’s horsemen. The horse of Maslamah son of ʿAbd al-Malik son of Marwān was al-Raṭl.
