The horse of al-Walīd son of ʿAbd al-Malik son of son of Marwān was al-Baṭṭān son of al-Ḥarūn son of al-Athāthī son of al-Khazazz son of Dhū al-Ṣūfah son of Aʿwaj. And Marwān son of Muḥammad al-Ashqar had [a horse] that was one-eyed. It was descended from the horse of Hishām son of ʿAbd al-Malik, al-Dhāʾid son of al-Buṭayn son of al-Baṭṭān son of al-Ḥarūn son of al-Athāthī.

. The swords of Quraysh

The sword of the Messenger of God ﷺ—was Dhū al-Fiqār. It had belonged to al-ʿĀṣ son of Munabbih son of al-Ḥajjāj son of ʿĀmir al-Sahmī. ʿAlī ﵁—killed him at Badr and brought his sword to the Messenger of God ﷺ—who awarded it to him. Of it he says: (rajaz)

There is no sword but Dhū al-Fiqār,

And no young hero but ʿAlī.

The sword of Ḥamzah son of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib was al-Liyyāḥ. On the Day of Uḥud, when he killed ʿUthmān son of Abī Ṭalḥah, who carried the banner, he ﵁—said: (basīṭ)

ʿUthmān tasted, on the day of war at Uḥud,

The blow of al-Liyyāḥ, and perished, disgraced.

And ʿUtbah tasted, at Badr, its blow.

Shame on the death of a sheikh, then disgraced.

And all of Fihr, marked for battle, gathered;

Had none warded them off, death would have marked them all.

The sword of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib son of Hāshim was al-ʿAṭshān. He said: (basīṭ)

Whoever’s sword fails him on a day of battle,

Al-ʿAṭshān neither faltered nor betrayed.

How many times have I severed an arm, a head,

a Qirdamānī helmet, and a body.

The sword of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAttāb ibn Asīd ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ: Walwal. He said on the Day of the Camel: (rajaz)

I am the son of ʿAttāb, and my sword is Walwal;

death lies before the Camel, covered in armor.

The sword of Hubayra ibn Abī Wahb al-Makhzūmī: al-Hadhulūl. He said: (ṭawīl)

How many a warrior have I stripped of his weapon,

leaving al-Hadhulūl to make him stumble, fallen.

I have witnessed an unrelenting war whose encounters

you, Hunayda, met head-on with your spear.

The sword of al-Ḥārith ibn Hishām ibn al-Mughīra: al-Akhīras. He said in Syria in the time of ʿUmar: (ṭawīl)

My horses did not turn aside at Faḥl or falter,

nor did I reproach al-Akhīras’s stroke on the day of terror.

The sword of ʿIkrima ibn Abī Jahl: al-Nazīf. He said on the day of Badr, when he had killed the two sons of ʿAfrāʾ and a man of the Anṣār, and struck Muʿādh ibn ʿAmr ibn al-Jamūḥ on the shoulder, severing his upper arm with his hand until it hung by a strip of skin at his flank: (ṭawīl)

Whoever had come to evening praising my deed,

for their two mothers had become, in Yathrib,

bereft, weeping for two youths cut down;

will you weep for slain men they did not expect?

Before them, al-Nazīf had brought down a noble warrior,

whose house and station were in the heights of glory.

And you, Ibn al-Jamūḥ, I struck you with a blow

that split apart head and shoulder.

The sword of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵁ was Dhū al-Wishāḥ. Its chape was silver. ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿUmar was with Muʿāwiya at Ṣiffīn, and a man of Bakr ibn Wāʾil from Banū ʿĀyish, a man of al-Baṣra called Muḥriz ibn al-Ṣaḥṣāḥ, killed him and took the sword. When matters had settled in Muʿāwiya’s favor, he took it from Taym Allāh, then sent it to the Banū ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb in Medina. ʿUbayd Allāh said: (ṭawīl)

If my sword Dhū al-Wishāḥ and my mount, the

lame one, are with me, then no blood I seek shall go unavenged.

He who ends the day my bitter enemy

shall know that, as long as I live, I will pursue him.

The sword of ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd Wudd al-ʿĀmirī, killed on the Day of the Trench: al-Malid. ʿAmr said: (basīṭ)

Al-Malid is a sword: whenever I strike with it,

at any time, it cleaves—or breaks.

How many a great man has death given to drink at midday … and a youth who never attained [in it—] old age.

The sword of Ḍirār ibn al-Khaṭṭāb al-Fihrī: al-Saḥāb. He said: (basīṭ)

At dawn of battle’s heat, al-Saḥāb was not among those

who falter at the sword’s edge when they saw Ghassān.

I left among them, beside the hollow, a battlefield—

fallen men. “They were not our equals, O May, our slain!”

Had you seen them, with the horses pressing them down,

while the swords took them, two by two and one by one,

you would have known that Banū Fihr and their kin

were horsemen by the hollow on the day of terror.

The sword of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil al-Sahmī: al-Laḥj. He said during the wars in Syria: (rajaz)

I strike them with the blade

until they leave the pass

for whoever walks and marches heavily.

The sword of ʿUmar ibn Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ: al-Milāʾ. Abū al-Nuwayʿim al-ʿĀmirī lamented him when al-Mukhtār ibn Abī ʿUbayd killed him: (ṭawīl)

May God bless the eyes of whoever saw a young man like him,

when war blazed and its sparks flew far.

He would bare himself for it, with al-Milāʾ in his hand,

to quench its flames, which flared and spread.

The sword of Khālid ibn Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiya: al-Ghamr. He said of it: (ṭawīl)

A position where, at noon or at evening,

no one is safe with his flank exposed.

Beneath my cloak I cut through,

and over my shirt, al-Ghamr—sharp, with its stripes.

Khālid ibn al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīra had three swords: al-Mursib, which was Dhū al-Qurṭ; another called al-Adlaq; and another called al-Qurṭabī. He said on the day of Muʾta: (rajaz)

I am Abū Salmān; my sword is al-Mursib,

son of al-Walīd, descended from the noble.

With it I rise above every denier

of Aḥmad, the purified, the good.

He said, after killing a Byzantine general: (rajaz)

With al-Mursib I struck the general’s head,

and came down upon the place where his veins converge,

with a sharp sword of keen force, that cleaves.

He said: (mutaqārib)

With Dhū al-Qurṭ I have killed among the men

grey-haired men, tall and foreign, and Arabs.

He said: (rajaz)

I strike them with al-Adlaq,

like an embittered young man strikes—

with a gleaming sword.

He said: (basīṭ)

With al-Qurṭabī I struck the head of Ibn Ḍāriya,

ʿAmr, and left him sprawled in the trench.

The sword of Zamʿa ibn al-Aswad ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā: Lisān al-Kalb.

It passed to his son ʿAbd Allāh, and with it he killed Hudba ibn Khashram. When Hudba was killed with it, al-Miswar ibn Ziyāda said: (wāfir)

Lisān al-Kalb has cut the vein of my vengeance;

it has relieved my anguish and healed my soul.

He said: When Jaʿfar ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵀ came to the Negus, the Negus gave him a sword called al-Ghamām. He fought with it on the day of Muʾta, saying: (rajaz)

Fihr and Fihr’s ruler know

that I am among its people, in the heights and at the summit.

How many a warrior’s flank and skull have I cut through!

The sword of ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Nawfal ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib: al-Shaqīq. Muʿāwiya offered him a high price to sell it, but he refused and said: (ṭawīl)

I have sworn I will not sell al-Shaqīq for any price.

O Muʿāwiya, I am loath to part with al-Shaqīq.

Jarīr said to al-Farazdaq when Sulaymān ibn ʿAbd al-Malik handed him a Roman prisoner to behead, but his sword did nothing: (ṭawīl)

Had you struck him with al-Shaqīq of al-Nawfalī,

you would have cleft him in two; the sword is no coward.

But with the sword of your smith, Ghalib,

you struck, you worst of barefoot and shod men.

The sword of Khālid ibn Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayya: Dhiʿlūq. He said in Syria while fighting the Byzantines: (rajaz)

My father is Saʿīd; my sash is Dhiʿlūq.

With it I strike the crown of every general.

Not a day has passed when my beard was wet with saliva.

Saʿīd ibn Zayd ibn ʿAmr ibn Nufayl al-ʿAdawī had two swords: al-Fāʾiz and al-Khalīl: (rajaz)

I strike with al-Fāʾiz and al-Khalīl,

like a noble, generous, excellent, brave man,

seeking the pleasure of the Merciful and the Messenger,

until I die or find my way.

The sword of Khālid ibn al-Muhājir ibn Khālid ibn al-Walīd al-Makhzūmī: Dhū al-Kaff. He said, after killing Ibn Athāl, Muʿāwiya’s physician, who was known as Abū al-Ward: (ṭawīl)

Ask Ibn Athāl whether I struck above his nape

with Dhū al-Kaff, until he fell unsheltered.

Had my sword bitten Ibn Hind, it would have made

my drink go down; I would not have cared when my staff rose again.

The sword of Abū Duhbul al-Jumaḥī, Wahb ibn Wahb ibn Zamʿa ibn Asad ibn— Khalaf: al-Mustalib. He said: (rajaz)

I am Abū Duhbul, Wahb ibn Wahb.

I inherit glory, father after father.

My spear is Radīnī, and my sword is al-Mustalib.

The sword of Muḥammad ibn Abī al-Jahm al-ʿAdawī: al-Qāʾim al-Qāʿid. Muḥammad ibn Abī al-Jahm said of it: (mutaqārib)

I have two swords: one for a wound that reaches the brain,

and one that is al-Qāʾim al-Qāʿid.

Take this upon your head, a wound that reaches the brain,

and beware, beware, Khālid!

The Horsemen of Quraysh

Ḥamza ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib; al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām ibn Khuwaylid; Hubayra ibn Abī Wahb [ibn ʿAmr—] ibn ʿĀʾidh ibn ʿImrān ibn Makhzūm; Khālid ibn al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīra ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm; ʿIkrima ibn Abī Jahl ibn Hishām ibn al-Mughīra; and ʿAmr, the horseman of Yalīl, ibn ʿAbd Wudd ibn Abī Qays, of Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy, who was— The horseman of Quraysh: ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵇ killed him on the Day of the Trench. He was one hundred and forty years old. He was Dhū al-Thudayya. Also among them were Busr ibn Abī Arṭāʾ ibn ʿUwaymir ibn ʿImrān al-ʿĀmirī, who killed the two sons of ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib; Quṭfah ibn Rabīʿah, the brother of Banū Sāmah ibn Luʾayy; and Quṭbah al-ʿĀqid, the horseman of al-Balqāʾ, “the White, Forelocked One,” son of ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Manāf, one of Banū Taym al-Adram ibn Ghālib. Also: Ḍirār ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ibn Mirdās al-Fihrī; Ḥabīb ibn Maslamah al-Fihrī; al-Ḥārith ibn Hishām al-Makhzūmī; Ubayy ibn Khalaf al-Jumaḥī; Abū Lubayd ibn ʿAbdah ibn Jābir ibn Wahb, the brother of Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy; and Abū al-ʿAjlān ibn al-Ḥuṣayn ibn Sayyār ibn Nizār ibn Muʿayṣ ibn ʿĀmir, who was the champion of the people on the Day of Dhū Dawrān against Juhaynah. Also: al-Walīd ibn Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik; Ibrāhīm ibn ʿĀʾishah al-ʿAbbāsī; and the Commander of the Faithful, al-Muʿtaṣim.

. Names of Qurashīs whose hands Quraysh amputated for theft

Mudrik ibn ʿAwf ibn ʿUbayd ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm stole repeatedly in the pre-Islamic period, so Quraysh amputated his hand. He then stole again, and they stoned him until he died. Al-Khayyār ibn ʿAdī ibn …

Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf stole in the pre-Islamic period, so his hand was amputated. Mulayḥ ibn Shurayḥ ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Sabbāq ibn ʿAbd al-Dār had his hand amputated in connection with the gazelle of the Kaʿbah. Muqays ibn Qays ibn ʿAdī ibn Saʿd ibn Sahm had his hand amputated in connection with the gazelle. ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAmr ibn Kaʿb ibn Saʿd ibn Taym had his hand amputated [in] the pre-Islamic period for stealing camels. Wābiṣah ibn Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm …

. The leading houses of Quraysh

The honor and leadership of Quraysh belonged to Banū Quṣayy. No one disputed it with them, nor could any boaster boast over them; they remained in that position, and others submitted to them. In the pre-Islamic period, Quraysh had six distinctions—all of them belonging to Banū Quṣayy alone, to the exclusion of the rest of Quraysh: custodianship of the Kaʿbah, provision of water to the pilgrims, provision of food to the pilgrims, the standard, the council, and leadership. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib undertook what Hāshim had undertaken. When ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib died, and Ḥarb ibn Umayyah died, the leadership and honor were divided. Among ʿAbd Manāf’s descendants were al-Zubayr, Abū Ṭālib, Ḥamzah, and al-ʿAbbās, the sons of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ibn Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf; Abū Uḥayḥah Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah ibn ʿAbd Shams ibn ʿAbd Manāf; ʿAbd Yazīd ibn Hāshim ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn ʿAbd Manāf—ʿAbd Yazīd being of pure stock, with no blemish in him—and al-Muṭʿim ibn ʿAdī ibn Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf. Among Banū Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Quṣayy were Khuwailid ibn Asad and ʿUthmān ibn al-Ḥuwayrith ibn Asad. Quraysh’s distinctions in Islam were three: prophethood, the caliphate, and consultation. Two belonged exclusively to Banū ʿAbd Manāf …

and they shared the third with Zuhrah, Taym, ʿAdī, and Asad: consultation. The caliphate became the exclusive possession of Banū ʿAbd Manāf after the two Elders ﵏.

. Those among Quraysh who forbade intoxicating drink, wine, and divining arrows in the pre-Islamic period

ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ibn Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf; Shaybah ibn Rabīʿah ibn ʿAbd Shams, who practiced ḥanīfism at Ḥirāʾ; Waraqah ibn Nawfal ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā; Abū Umayyah ibn al-Mughīrah and al-Ḥārith ibn ʿUbayd, both Makhzūmīs; Zayd ibn ʿAmr ibn Nufayl ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā al-ʿAdawī, who practiced ḥanīfism at Ḥirāʾ and would not eat what had been slaughtered for idols; ʿĀmir ibn Ḥudhaym al-Jumaḥī; ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān al-Taymī; Muqays ibn Qays ibn ʿAdī al-Sahmī; ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān ﵁—ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah; and al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm. Hishām his son was flogged for it.

. Those whose hearts were reconciled among Quraysh

Abū Sufyān Ṣakhr ibn Ḥarb; his son Muʿāwiyah; Ḥakīm ibn Ṭulayq ibn Sufyān ibn Umayyah; Khālid ibn Usayd ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah; al-Ḥārith ibn Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah al-Makhzūmī; Saʿīd ibn Yarbuʿ al-Makhzūmī; and Ṣafwān ibn Umayyah ibn …

Khalaf al-Jumaḥī; Suhayl ibn ʿAmr, the brother of Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy; Ḥuwayṭib ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Abī Qays al-ʿĀmirī; Ḥakīm ibn Ḥizām ibn Khuwailid ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā; Abū Sufyān ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib; and al-ʿAlāʾ ibn Jāriyah al-Thaqafī, an ally of Banū Zuhrah ibn Kilāb. The Messenger of God ﷺ—gave each of these men one hundred camels, except Saʿīd ibn Yarbuʿ and Ḥuwayṭib ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā, to each of whom he gave fifty camels.

. The disciples of the Messenger of God ﷺ—from Quraysh

Al-Musayyibī related, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muʿādh al-Ṣanʿānī, from Maʿmar, who said: “Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, ʿAlī, Ḥamzah, Abū ʿUbaydah ibn al-Jarrāḥ, ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān, ʿUthmān ibn Maẓʿūn al-Jumaḥī, ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf, Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ, Ṭalḥah ibn ʿUbayd Allāh, and al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām.” Ibn al-Kalbī related that al-Zubayr alone was a disciple.

. Those of Quraysh described as handsome

Abū Lahab, namely ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ibn Hāshim—he was given the kunya Abū Lahab because his face shone like a flame—and he was cross-eyed; al-Sajjād, Muḥammad ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib: when he intended to make the pilgrimage and passed through Medina, the women, male slaves, and female slaves would stand watching him. Abū Miskīn al-Madanī said: “I asked him, ‘How does your stature compare with your father’s?’”

He said: “I used to stand beside my father ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh, and my head came level with the end of his shoulder. My father used to say, ‘I used to stand beside my father ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās, and my head came to that same place on him.’ ʿAbd Allāh said, ‘I stand beside my father al-ʿAbbās, and my head comes to that same place on him.’” Abū Bakr said: “Al-Mudhahhab, namely al-ʿAbbās ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. He was also al-Aʿnaq, ‘the long-necked’; his neck was beautiful and perfectly formed, like a silver ewer. He was generous. Al-Akhṭal praised him, and he awarded him a thousand dinars. Once he was riding past, and a woman caught his eye; his horse stumbled beneath him, and he died. Al-Muṭarraf, namely ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān, and his son al-Dībāj, namely Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿUthmān; also al-Muṭarraf, ʿAmr ibn al-Zubayr al-ʿAwwām ibn Khuwailid ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā; al-Muṣawwar, namely ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ibn Nufayl; and Wafd. He was a youth when he visited Muʿāwiyah and stayed with him for a month. One day he said to him, ‘Commander of the Faithful, grant my request.’ Muʿāwiyah replied:

“‘I have granted you this: you are the most handsome of people’.”

He granted his requests and gave him a generous gift.

. Those of Quraysh who resembled the Messenger of God ﷺ

Al-Ḥasan ibn ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵄—resembled the Prophet ﷺ from the crown of his head to his navel. Al-Ḥusayn resembled him from his navel to his feet. Also Jaʿfar ibn Abī Ṭālib, to whom he said, “You resemble me in my physical form and my character”; Muḥammad ibn Jaʿfar ibn Abī Ṭālib; Abū Sufyān ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, who was born on the same night as the Prophet ﷺ; ʿAbd Allāh ibn Nawfal ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib; Muslim ibn Muʿtab ibn Abī Lahab; and al-Sāʾib ibn …

ʿUbayd ibn ʿAbd Yazīd ibn Hāshim ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn ʿAbd Manāf; Qutham ibn al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib; and Kābis ibn Rabīʿah ibn Mālik ibn ʿAdī ibn al-Aswad ibn Jusham ibn Rabīʿah ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Sāmah ibn Luʾayy ibn Ghālib. ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿĀmir ibn Kurayz, when he was Muʿāwiyah’s governor over Baṣrah, wrote to him to report that there was a man in Baṣrah from Banū Nājiyah who resembled the Messenger of God ﷺ. He wrote back, ordering him to send the man to him. When the man arrived before Muʿāwiyah, and Muʿāwiyah saw him approaching, he rose from his couch, kissed him between the eyes, [and] asked him, “Whose descendant are you?” He replied:

“I am from Banū Sāmah ibn Luʾayy.”

Muʿāwiyah said, “Why, then, did he write to me that you were from Banū Nājiyah?”

“By God, Commander of the Faithful, she did not give birth to me, though people do attribute me to her.”

So he granted him al-Marghāb, [which is] a canal that branches from the Maʿqil canal, three farsakhs from Baṣrah.

. The first to have two Hāshimī parents

Ṭālib, ʿAqīl, Jaʿfar, and ʿAlī, the sons of Abū Ṭālib. Their mother was Fāṭimah bt. Asad ibn Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf, and their father was Abū Ṭālib ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ibn Hāshim.

. The first man born to three Hāshimī women

ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Nawfal ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib.

His mother was Khālidah bt. Muʿtab ibn Abī Lahab ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, and her mother was ʿĀtikah bt. Abī Sufyān, namely al-Mughīrah ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, whose mother was Umm ʿAmr bt. al-Muqawwim ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib.

. Those whose maternal uncle and paternal uncle were caliphs

There were only two: ʿUthmān ibn ʿAnbasah ibn Abī Sufyān ibn Ḥarb ibn Umayyah, and Yaḥyā ibn ʿUrwah ibn al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām. As for ʿUthmān, his mother was Zaynab bt. al-Zubayr; his paternal uncle was Muʿāwiyah, and his maternal uncle was ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr. As for Yaḥyā ibn ʿUrwah, his mother was Umm Yaḥyā bt. al-Ḥakam ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah; his paternal uncle was ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr, and his maternal uncle was Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam.

. A Qurashī woman whose father, grandfather, and husband fought at Badr

She was Umm Kulthūm bt. ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵁. Her maternal grandfather was the Master of Mankind, Muḥammad ﷺ; her father was ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵁; and her husband was ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵀.

And a man from Quraysh whose father, paternal uncle, maternal grandfather, maternal uncle, maternal uncle of his father, and maternal uncle were martyrs: Zayd ibn ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb. His father, ʿUmar, and his paternal uncle, Zayd ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, were martyred during the apostasy wars. His maternal grandfather was ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib; his maternal uncle was Jaʿfar ibn Abī Ṭālib; his father’s maternal uncle was Ḥamzah ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib; and his maternal uncle was al-Ḥusayn ibn ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵉.

. This is the end of Al-Munammaq, as transmitted from Ibn Ḥabīb. Abū Saʿīd al-Sukkarī said: “This is not from Ibn Ḥabīb.”

Quraysh’s delegation to Sayf ibn Dhī Yazan, including their notables

Abū Saʿīd al-Sukkarī related to us: Abū Bakr Muḥammad ibn al-Mughīrah ibn Bassām related to us: ʿAlī ibn Zurayq related to us: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Maymūn ibn Mihrān related to me, from Ibn ʿAbbās ﵄—who said:

“Sayf ibn Dhī Yazan raided the Negus, attacked them, killed a great many of them, and took many captives. Then he returned to his land. The Arabs would travel to him from distant regions to congratulate him, and the poets would praise him. A delegation of Quraysh set out to visit him. Among them were ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ibn Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf; Umayyah ibn ʿAbd Shams ibn ʿAbd Manāf; ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān al-Taymī; and Riyāḥ ibn ʿAbd Allāh. When they reached his door, they requested permission to enter, and permission was granted. They entered to find him in a palace called Ghumdān. Umayyah ibn Abī al-Ṣalt al-Thaqafī says of it: (basīṭ meter)

Drink, and be glad, with the crown raised high above you …Upon Ghumdān’s summit, a dwelling made yours to inhabit.”

The men entered to find him perfumed with ambergris, his skin sticking, white musk upon his parting. He wore one mantle around his waist and another over his shoulders. Before him lay his sword, and to his right and left were kings and maqāwil. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib asked his permission to speak. The king said to him, “If you are one who speaks in the presence of kings, then speak.”

ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib said: “God has placed you, O king, in a lofty and exalted station, and caused you to spring from a noble source whose root is good and whose stock is mighty. Its foundation is firm and its branches rise high, in the finest dwelling place and the noblest origin. You, O king—may you never be cursed—are the lion of the Arabs, who cannot be broken; their springtime and their fertility, by which their growth lives. You are the head of the Arabs and their pillar, upon which they rely, and their stronghold, to which people flee for refuge. Your forebear was the best of forebears, and you are the best successor to him for us. The memory of the one whose forebear you are will never fade, and the one whose successor you are will never perish. We, O king, are the people of God’s sanctuary and the keepers of His House. We have come to you because of your protection, which has encompassed us, and because of your repelling the distress that has weighed upon us. We are neither a delegation of congratulations nor a delegation of calamity.” The king said to him, “Who are you, O speaker?” He replied, “I am ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib b. Hāshim b. ʿAbd Manāf.” The king said to him, “The son of our sister!” He replied, “Yes, O king.” The king said to him, “Welcome! A she-camel and a saddle, a smooth resting place, and a great kingdom, with generous gifts. The king has heard your words, accepted your request, and recognized your standing and kinship. You are people of night and day. You shall have honor for as long as you stay, and gifts when you depart.”

Then he took the men to the guest house, where what was customarily provided for people like them was provided. They stayed for a month, and he did not ask after them. Then, sometime afterward, he sent for ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. He came to him, and when he entered, the king cleared his own seat for him and drew him close. He said, “O elder! I am entrusting to you a portion of my secret knowledge, which I would not have disclosed to anyone other than you. But I have found you to be its repository. Let it remain concealed with you until God permits it to be revealed.”

“Within the hidden Book and the stored knowledge that we have chosen for ourselves and kept concealed from others, I find a momentous report and a grave matter, containing the honor of life and the virtue of death for all people, for your people generally, and for you in particular.”

ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib said, “A man like you, O king, is noble and virtuous. What is it? May all the people of the encampments, company after company, be your ransom.”

The king said to him, “When a boy is born in Tihāmah with a mark between his shoulders, he will hold the imamate until the Day of Resurrection.”

ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib said to him, “O king—may you never be cursed! You have brought news unlike any brought by another before you. Were it not for my awe of the king, his majesty, and the reverence and respect due to him, I would ask the king about the glad tidings he has given me, so that I might rejoice still more.”

The king said to him, “This is the time when he is to be born—or he may already have been born. His name is Muḥammad. His eyes are wide, his legs are shapely, and his face is like a piece of the moon. His father will die while he is still young, and his mother will die; his grandfather and uncle will care for him. We have borne him many times. God will send him forth openly and make supporters for him from among us. Through them He will strengthen His friends and humiliate His enemies. Through them he will unlock the treasures of the earth, strike down people indiscriminately, shatter the idols, drive away Satan, and worship the Merciful. He will command what is right and do it; he will forbid what is wrong and abolish it. His speech is decisive and his judgment just.”

ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib said to him, “May your glory be exalted, may your standing rise, and may your kingdom endure and your life be long! Is the king a bearer of glad tidings concerning clear signs, since he has made some of them clear?” The king said to him, “By the Lord of the House of veils, signs, and sacrificial stones, you are truly his grandfather—no lie.” ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib fell prostrate before the king. The king said to him, “Raise your head, O elder!” He raised his head, and the king said, “May your heart be gladdened and your fame exalted! Have you sensed anything of what I have told you?” ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib said to him:

“I had a son, the tenth of ten, the youngest of them. I was tender toward him and delighted in him. I married him to a woman of the noblest of my people, Āminah bt. Wahb al-Zuhriyyah. She bore a boy; his father died, and his mother has just lived to see him reach two years of age. He bears the signs you described. I and his uncle have cared for him.”

The king said to him, “Matters are as you have described, O elder! Guard your grandson and beware of the Jews, for they are the most hostile of people toward him, but God will grant them no way over him. Keep what I have told you concealed from these men of your people who are with you, lest jealousy over your leadership seize them and they seek treachery against you.”

“Long may your life endure! Is the king a bringer of glad tidings of clear signs, since he has made some of them clear?” The king said to him, “By the Lord of the House of veils, signs, and sacrificial stones, you are truly his grandfather—no lie.” ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib fell prostrate before the king. The king said to him, “Raise your head, O elder!” He raised his head, and the king said to him, “May your heart be gladdened and your fame exalted! Have you sensed anything of what I have told you?” ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib said to him:

“I had a son, the tenth of ten, the youngest of them. I was tender toward him and delighted in him. I married him to a woman of the noblest of my people, Āminah bt. Wahb al-Zuhriyyah. She bore a boy; his father died, and his mother has just lived to see him reach two years of age. He bears the signs you described. I and his uncle have cared for him.”

The king said to him, “Matters are as you have described, O elder! Guard your grandson and beware of the Jews, for they are the most hostile of people toward him, but God will grant them no way over him. Keep what I have told you concealed from these men of your people who are with you, lest jealousy over your leadership seize them and they seek treachery against you.”

“They will lay snares for you and seek to ensnare you; they and their sons will do so. Their power in this matter is indeed overwhelming, and their destruction in it is manifest. Were it not that I know death will overtake me before his mission begins, I would have moved with my cavalry and infantry to Yathrib and made it my home. For I find in the speaking Book and the foreknowledge that the establishment of his affair, the proclaiming of his fame, the people who will support him, and the place of his grave are in Yathrib. I find that love and affection for him have entered my heart. Were I not protecting him from misfortunes and guarding him against afflictions, I would make the Arabs follow in his footsteps despite his young age. But I shall entrust this to you, without neglecting those who are with you.”

Then he ordered that each man be given ten black slaves, ten black slave women, a brick of gold, and a wineskin full of ambergris, and a musk-filled pouch. He ordered ten times as much for ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. Quraysh envied him, and ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib used to say, “People of Quraysh! If you knew of the king’s glad tidings to me, this would seem little to you.”

The book is complete. Praise be to God, Lord of the worlds. Blessings upon Muḥammad, the best of His creation, and upon his family. May God have mercy on whoever reads it and prays for its owner to enjoy a long life, and for its scribe to be granted well-being in both abodes; may the All-Guardian suffice him in both, and all Muslims. Amen.

The printing of al-Munammaq was completed for the first time on Thursday, the eleventh of Rabīʿ al-Ākhir, 1384 AH, 20 August 1964 CE, at the press of the Ottoman Council of the Dāʾirat al-Maʿārif in Hyderabad, Deccan, India. It was printed for the second time in Beirut, collated against the first, Indian edition. Praise be to God, Lord of the worlds. May God bless our master Muḥammad, and all his family and Companions.

Notes

1
The text’s “kallā yā rabb” is retained as written; its sense in this exchange is uncertain. ↑
2
The source repeats “more eloquent” for both brothers; the reading is retained. ↑
3
The first verse is corrupt in the original; its word division is unclear. ↑
4
The opening word is corrupt; the manuscript reads la-khiṣāl. ↑
5
The original has the isolated letter hāʾ; the intended reference is unclear. ↑
6
The Arabic wording of this verse is corrupt in the original; the accompanying editorial note records variant readings. ↑
7
The wording “من منجبي الحمقى فكنّ في يده” is unclear in the original; the translation of the passage is uncertain. ↑
8
The wording of Jābir’s statement is corrupt or unclear in the original. ↑
